Assembled 2026-09-23 from the PDFs in literature. 45 sources annotated: 24 books in literature/Books, 11 added since in literature/Expanded (7 books or book-length works, a dissertation, 2 articles, and 2 files that are book reviews rather than the books they are named after), and 10 fulldome/planetarium papers and decks. Three of the 45 are extracts rather than whole works (Bligh, Feld, Lofland), and two more (White, Kendon) are reviews — the entries say so at the top.

What this covers

The review's subject is practice-based research in fulldome performance, along the spectrum from theatrical work in a dome, to the performance lecture, to the plain lecture. The corpus as supplied splits in two, and the split is the main thing the corpus tells you:

  • The field's own writing on the dome (10 items). Conference papers, festival material, slide decks, one handbook chapter. It describes the medium, its venues, its production problems and its technical vocabulary. It contains no scholarship on dome performance, no audience study, and no research methodology.
  • Everything else (24 books). Media theory, art history, phenomenology, performance studies, political theory, research methodology. None of it is about domes. It is the apparatus the dome literature lacks, and it has to be imported deliberately.

The gap the review has to fill is not a missing citation — it is the absence of a body of work that treats a dome as a performance situation and a place where research happens. Several sources in the first group state that absence themselves.

How these entries were made

  1. Every PDF was converted to page-marked text (pdftotext; PyMuPDF and pdfminer for those pdftotext garbled; RapidOCR for the six with no usable text layer at all).
  2. Each source was read by targeted search over that text — locating where a theme lives, then reading around it — rather than skimmed whole. Long books were sampled, not read end to end, so a passage may exist that these entries do not mention.
  3. Every quote was machine-verified against the extracted source text (verbatim, or whitespace/hyphenation-normalised) and checked against the page it is attributed to. 445 quotes were checked and 436 matched their source exactly; the nine that did not are either sub-12-character scan fragments recorded for transparency (in Mark Fisher's damaged scan) or single terms, and are labelled as such. Nothing here is a paraphrase presented as a quote.
  4. Page numbers are given as PDF page (the page of the file in your folder) and, where the printed folio survives in the text, as printed page as well. Where extraction was damaged, the entry says so rather than guessing. Quotes are reproduced with ligature characters (fi, fl), hyphenation and quote-mark style normalised for readability; nothing else is altered.

How to read an entry

Each entry has: Citation (with the source of the bibliographic detail — from the file, or flagged as checked externally); Coverage (what the file actually contains, which is not always the whole book); Argument in brief; Key ideas in the author's own terms; Quotes with page references; Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review (which argument it can carry, and which it cannot); Limits; and Adjacent sources in this folder.

Two things to distrust. First, several files are not the whole work — Steve Dixon is an MIT preview containing only chapter 1 plus the bibliography of a 776-page book, Muñoz is chapter 1 only, two items are conference slide decks whose content is in the images, the Lofland file is a partial preview, and two of the newly added files turn out to be book reviews rather than the books they are named after (Gareth White's Audience Participation in Theatre and Adam Kendon's Conducting Interaction) — so the arguments of those two books cannot be cited from this folder at all. Second, several scans have damaged text layers; the affected words are flagged in each entry and quotes were drawn from undamaged passages only.

Read this first, in this order

If you have an hour: Grau on what immersion has historically been (Virtual Art), the Remann & Francis manifesto chapter for the field describing itself, Schlemmer's "Man and Art Figure" for the Bauhaus origin of treating a whole constructed space as the instrument of a performance, Sloterdijk's "celestial domes have burst" passage (PDF p.12 / printed p.24) for the argument that a dome is a replacement shell rather than a venue, Barrett & Bolt for the research method, and Manovich's planetarium anecdote (PDF p.32) — the only passage in the whole corpus that puts a performance inside a dome on the record.

1. The dome as a medium: the field describing itself

Five items, and they are the closest thing in the corpus to a literature of fulldome. Read together they argue that the dome is a medium in its own right rather than a curved cinema screen — Remann and Francis supply the historical claim (the frame was an interval, the dome returns to surround), von Chamier-Waite supplies the formal consequence (the frame is gone, so montage has to become spatial and somatic), Saper and Tomlinson supply the public-scholarship case, Ka Chun Yu supplies the research agenda the others lack, and Carss and Hazelden supply the vocabulary problem: 'immersion' is used so loosely in the field that it means nothing in an academic context. Between them they also state the absence this review has to work around — Yu notes that no dome data exists, and the only fulldome research project studying non-cognitive audience response he can name is one. There is no performance scholarship here at all: live work appears as festival examples, not as a subject of study.

Micky Remann and Kelley M. Francis, Towards a Fulldome Manifesto: Tour d’Horizon for the Immersive-Inclined (2020)

Citation

Remann, Micky, and Kelley M. Francis. Towards a Fulldome Manifesto: Tour d'Horizon for the Immersive-Inclined. In Handbook of Research on the Global Impacts and Roles of Immersive Media, edited by Jacquelyn Ford Morie and Kate McCallum, 79–108. Hershey, PA: IGI Global, 2020. DOI: 10.4018/978-1-7998-2433-6.ch005.

Author details are printed on the chapter title page (PDF p.1): Micky Remann / Fulldome Festival Foundation, Germany and Kelley M. Francis / Independent Researcher, USA. The extracted PDF names no publisher, editor, series or year anywhere in its 34 pages: it is the pre-publication final proof of chapter 005 of an edited handbook — that is what 005 and final proof in the original filename mean. The book-level details above come from the publisher's record for the DOI (IGI Global, © 2020, pp. 79–108), checked externally; treat them as bibliographic metadata, not as text found in this file.

Original PDF filename: Fulldome Articles\005 Towards a Fulldome Manifesto - final proof.pdf.

Coverage

Book chapter (complete), pre-publication proof. 34 PDF pages. PDF p.34 is blank; PDF pp.2, 8, 20, 30 are largely figure/reference pages and thin on prose (68–105 words each). There are no printed folios anywhere in the file, so every page reference below is a PDF page. Text layer is clean — no OCR noise, no garbled words — but structurally damaged: figure captions run straight into the following paragraph with no line break, and some captions are clipped mid-string (Figure 4, PDF p.9, where the figure credit is spliced into the caption). The figures themselves are photographs and screenshots; this annotation quotes only the prose.

Despite the title the chapter contains no numbered manifesto — no articles, no theses list, no numbered demands. It is continuous prose under all-caps headings. The only enumerated material is: (a) a four-item bullet list of hypotheses quoted from Mohammad Jaradat's 2017 Bauhaus-Universität Weimar thesis Dome without Chairs (PDF p.25); (b) a short bullet list of the conditions of Jing Augusto-Wuethrich's dome visualisation (PDF pp.23–24); (c) a five-item telegram style summary of the authors' saltwater-flotation dome proposal (PDF p.26); and (d) the Key Terms and Definitions glossary (PDF pp.31–32). The manifesto register is rhetorical throughout: Kant, Marx, Gropius, Buckminster Fuller and Schlemmer are paraphrased as if they were writing about fulldome (might have proclaimed), with the substitutions flagged in endnotes 2–6.

Argument in brief

The chapter claims fulldome has arrived as a medium in its own right — immersive, surround, communal — and that its arrival deserves a manifesto-like programme rather than more technical documentation. It is built as a Tour d’Horizon: a history of image-making from Paleolithic cave painting through photography, film sound and classical Hollywood choreography to hemispheric projection; a set of medium definitions (Fulldome is like Tofu); a celebration of the Jena FullDome Festival and the Janus Award as the field's institutional centre; and two concrete proposals — dome-to-dome real-time interactive networking, and a redesign of dome seating up to and including a warm saltwater flotation dome. Its strongest recurring claim is that the frame is a historical restriction of vision rather than a natural format, and that the dome restores the surround conditions of ordinary embodiment; the sensory argument turns on the asymmetry between the 180-degree eye-sphere and the 360-degree ear-sphere. It also acknowledges dome risk (vertigo, nausea, the difficulty of distancing oneself from an overpowering experience). Evidence is anecdote, festival history and master's theses quoted at length: there is no study, no data and no method.

Key ideas

  • Avant-Immersive — the authors' name for the present phase of forefront experimentation in immersive media (a keyword of the chapter).
  • Frameless Frenzy — the slogan (borrowed from the 10th FullDome Festival programme, 2015) for the shift out of the frame age; the chapter's historical hinge between cave and dome.
  • Frame age — the long interval in which images were caged in rectangles and separated from viewers; the condition fulldome claims to end.
  • Immersivists / Immersivevistas — the terms the chapter adopts (attributed to unnamed media mavens) for the dome's new audience-tribe and what it consumes.
  • Janus 2.0 / Janus deficiency syndrome — Janus (double-faced, guardian of transitions) as fulldome's patron deity, award statue and satirical diagnosis of flat-media habit.
  • Eye-sphere vs. ear-sphere — 180-degree vision against 360-degree hearing, proposed as a compositional problem for dome content.
  • Third space — what is revealed in the overlaps of the mind-field and the domefield when dome and mind become undistanced.
  • Gesamtkunstwerk — the Bauhaus total-artwork idea (Schlemmer, Gropius, Moholy-Nagy, Kandinsky) re-read as a programme for fulldome content and architecture.
  • Liquid Sound — underwater audio system and spa practice proposed as the fusion partner for fulldome above a warm saltwater pool.
  • Fulldome (authors' glossary definition) — immersive dome-based video projection environments.

Quotes

  • “When dome and mind become undistanced, as is common in a relevant fulldome exposure, a third space is revealed in the overlaps of the mind-field and the domefield, inviting us to question the semi-illusionary nature of our reality constructs and to participate with media differently.” (PDF p.3) — the chapter's core claim about dome spectatorship
  • “For an experience to be deemed immersive, it must tickle the senses and captivate the imagination! Simply surrounding the population with environments of triviality doesn’t live up to the uplifting potential of the dome, so a rise in narrative and engagement is in order. Shallow is an enemy to wonderful, and masterful experiential storytelling is imperative for immersion that satisfies.” (PDF p.3) — definition-by-demand: immersion as a narrative standard, not a technical spec
  • “Then came the dark ages. This was a time of caged images—a time of broadening distance between vision and viewer: the frame age!” (PDF p.4) — the frame/sphere historical hinge, in the field's own rhetoric
  • “A problem with any new medium is that it cannot be described or understood by the means of the old medium that it evolved out of; it exists beyond what it was. What is performed across a theatre stage is not the same as what is revealed in a movie. Fulldome, the next step in this evolution, has become a paradigm-shifting medium of its own.” (PDF p.5) — medium-specificity argument — direct support for treating dome performance as its own format
  • “All of reality is surround; all sensory input is immersive!” (PDF p.6) — the chapter's most compact claim about the dome as a return to ordinary embodiment
  • “It’s a mistake to simply view the dome as a curved screen. Imagine the dome as a device to transport the audience to new worlds” (PDF p.19) — Warik Lawrance (Melbourne Planetarium), quoted as the venue-director position on the dome as device
  • “Most seating arrangements in planetariums and dome theaters have a regretful tendency to reintroduce the limitation of the field of vision that the 360-degree projection technology has overcome.” (PDF p.25) — the body/seating argument — the dome's own furniture reinstating the frame
  • “Immersion, true immersion, is only complete when people get really wet!” (PDF p.26) — the authors' punchline from the IMERSA Denver dinner (endnote 20) — useful as a marker of the chapter's rhetorical mode

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the source to cite for the field's own self-description of fulldome as a medium, venue and genre — for the frame-to-sphere historical claim, for the body/seating problem, for the communal-social claim about the dome audience, and for the institutional map (Jena FullDome Festival, Janus Award, IMERSA, SAT/IX Symposium, Domelab at FCMoD, FDDB). It also supplies a quotable counter-position to reception-science talk: it asserts the immersive experience as a cultural and perceptual paradigm shift rather than a measurable effect. What it cannot carry: any claim about audience perception, learning, evaluation, or research method — none is discussed — and any argument about a performance or lecture practice inside the dome, since live work appears only as festival examples (concerts, Résonances Boréales, IX Symposium interactives).

Limits

State of the art as of 2019–20: the chapter says We have reached the year 2019; references stop at 2019; there is no mention of the Las Vegas Sphere or of any venue built after 2019, and dome-to-dome interactivity is still described as in its infancy. Register drifts between manifesto, festival promotion and personal anecdote, so individual claims should not be treated as findings. The flotation-dome proposal and the get really wet line are provocations. Copy-editing is loose: the Virginia Woolf source is cited as Wolf, V. (1931) and named Virginia Wolf in endnote 1; Gropius is dated 1919 in the text against a (1935) reference entry; the IAIA-type geographical slips are absent here but Santa Fe, Mexico appears in the sibling Saper & Tomlinson chapter. There is no bibliography of the fulldome literature proper — no Shedd, no Yu, no Carss, no Lantz. Nothing on practice-based research, teaching method or evaluation.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Fulldome_Articles_The_Cine_poetics_of_Fulldome_Cinema_pdf — the same frame-to-sphere claim argued as film form (montage) rather than as manifesto rhetoric.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Fulldome_Immersive_Modalities_and_Materiality_Toward_A_Poetics_of_the_Fi — shares the expanded-cinema prehistory (VanDerBeek, movie-drome) and the pedagogical case for domes.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_future_directions_for_research_media_aesthetics_and_fulldome_film — the empirical counterweight: what a research agenda for dome media aesthetics would look like.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_ips_2010_gaston_rodigast_pdf (The Sound of Space) — fills the spatial-audio gap the manifesto flags and does not develop.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Fulldome_immersive_creation_A_panorama_Soci_t_des_arts_technologiques_SA — the SAT dossier, i.e. the venue and curatorial practice the manifesto cites as the art-dome pole.

Clea von Chamier-Waite, The Cine-poetics of Fulldome Cinema (2013)

Citation

von Chamier-Waite, Clea. The Cine-poetics of Fulldome Cinema. Animation Practice, Process & Production 3, no. 1 (2013): 219–233. Intellect.

The author line and affiliation are printed on the opening page of the PDF (PDF p.1): Clea von Chamier-Waite / iMAP / University of Southern California, School of Cinematic Arts clea.waite@usc.edu. No venue, journal, volume, year or DOI appears anywhere in the extracted text — this is an author manuscript: it has no journal header, no running footer, no abstract and no keywords, and the running heads carry only the author's name and a folio. The journal details above come from Crossref for DOI 10.1386/ap3.3.1-2.219_1 (Animation Practice, Process & Production, vol. 3, issue 1, pp. 219–233, published 1 December 2013, publisher Intellect, author affiliation University of Southern California); checked externally, not present in the file. Note the published version has an abstract that this manuscript lacks, and its pagination (219–233) does not match the folio numbers printed in this PDF (1–12).

Original PDF filename: Fulldome Articles\The Cine-poetics of Fulldome Cinema.pdf.

Coverage

Journal article (author manuscript / preprint of the complete text). 13 PDF pages; PDF p.13 is blank. Prose is on PDF pp.1–12; the five numbered figures sit on PDF pp.2–4 and 10–11, and footnote text (the definition of feature length, the film URLs) is run into the prose on PDF pp.7–10. The printed folios visible in the running heads run 2–12 and coincide with PDF pages, so printed page below is the folio in this file, not the journal's pagination (219–233). Text layer is clean; the only extraction artefacts are footnote markers and figure captions sitting inline with body text, and one paragraph hyphenated across a line break. About 3,700 words.

No abstract and no method statement: it is a critical/historical essay by a practitioner, drawing on her own fulldome film Moonwalk (2010) and on a viewing survey of the field (Of the seventy plus fulldome features this author has watched). Five block quotations carry the theoretical argument (Arnheim 1957 on montage, Eisenstein 1949 twice, Rodowick 2007, Pingree & Gitelman 2003).

Argument in brief

The essay argues that fulldome is at the same stage of development as cinema in the 1920s and that its cinematic language is being held back by the planetarium culture it grew up inside. It sets out the medium first (hemispheric projection, fisheye optics, spatial sound), then tells two histories in parallel: planetarium shows (Zeiss star projectors, 1960s slide projectors, 1970s lasers, 1980s video patchwork, 1990s fulldome video) and projection media (the magic lantern, silent-film narration, the benshi, Griffith and Porter's parallel editing, Eisenstein's montage, Bazin's long take). Its diagnosis: fulldome inherits the star-projector show's continuous wide shot and the benshi/presenter's attention-guiding role, is normalised to the science museum and its school-age audience, sticks to photorealistic computer rendering and a first-person rollercoaster camera, and therefore has no montage. Because the dome has no frame and chronology does not cut well on a hemisphere, it proposes the answer: spatial montage — rhythm made by the eye's movement across the dome rather than by succession in time, producing what she calls a somatic montage that is different for every viewer. The conclusion poses the open question of what the media-specific cine-poetics of the immersive film might be.

Key ideas

  • Cine-poetics — the essay's term for the cinematic language specific to the immersive film; the object the essay says does not yet exist.
  • Frame-less / no frame — the dome's defining formal difference from 2D media; also why the world reads as inverted (We are always looking up).
  • Spatial montage — rhythm produced by space rather than time: montage cells distributed across the dome and displayed concurrently.
  • Somatic montage — the resulting structure, individual to each viewer, created in the viewer's own eye movement.
  • Proto-montage — her term for the star-projector show and narrated silent film, both long wide shots with a live attention guide.
  • Total cinema / realist mise-en-scène — Bazin's long-take aesthetic, which she says current fulldome features default to.
  • Quadratic image projection — 1980s video patchwork that destroyed the illusion of limitless space created by the dome.
  • Rollercoaster — fulldome's pet trope, the present-day stand-in for the chase scene; first-person, forward-moving, uncut.
  • Feature length — in fulldome, 20 minutes or more (her footnote), the longest being about forty-five minutes.

Quotes

  • “Fulldome refers to an immersive motion picture medium that is a dome-based, digital video projection environment.” (PDF p.1) — the medium defined — the essay's own working definition
  • “The Japanese benshi not only narrated the films, but also directed the audience’s attention within a wide shot to certain details on the screen, conceiving a conceptual precursor to the cinematic close-up.” (PDF p.5 / printed p.5) — the attention-guiding narrator as the historical precedent for live dome address
  • “Of the seventy plus fulldome features this author has watched, not one comes to mind that employs a dynamic, parallel montage.” (PDF p.6 / printed p.6) — the empirical kernel of the argument: montage is absent from dome film
  • “Fulldome films, with their giant resolution and huge screens, frequently adhere to this same, hyperrealistic approach to mise-en-scène. These over-dimensional films are typically more interested in exploring spatial relations rather than temporal relations.” (PDF p.7 / printed p.7) — why dome storytelling differs from flat cinema — space over time, mise-en-scène over cut
  • “Because of the technical impediments of the medium – one requires a dome and the specialized projection system - fulldome is heavily restricted to planetaria and hence both technically and socially normalized to the culture of the science museum” (PDF p.7 / printed p.7) — the institutional explanation for dome conventions (science museum, school audience)
  • “Fulldome differs most dramatically from other two-dimensional image media in that it has no frame. In a fulldome theater, the world is conceptually inverted. We are always looking up. You can’t look down without flipping your universe, which is one of the reasons that outer space and underwater are the two most popular motifs in fulldome films.” (PDF p.8 / printed p.8) — the frame/field claim stated head-on; the source's best short statement of dome-specific form
  • “What is missing from the cinematic language of fulldome films is exactly this notion of evocative, rhythmic montage.” (PDF p.9 / printed p.9) — the gap claim, quotable as the field's formal deficit
  • “The result is a somatic montage structure that is lyrical, non-linear, and individual to each viewer.” (PDF p.9 / printed p.9) — her proposed alternative and its spectator model — a viewer-generated, non-repeatable form

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the source to cite for the form of the dome image: for the frame/field relation (there is no frame; the field is the whole visible hemisphere), for why dome storytelling does not work like flat cinema (no cut-based parallelism, no close-up in the ordinary sense, the horizon and the theatre's architecture doing the organising), and for the live presenter as the historical predecessor of dome attention-guiding. It is also the sharpest available statement that the medium's film-making is under-formalised and that its institutional home (science museums, school audiences) has determined its conventions. What it cannot carry: anything about performance-as-research, theatre staging in a dome, or the educational/lecture address — the presenter appears only as historical analogy — and nothing about audience response, which she explicitly leaves as an open question.

Limits

A decade old at the time of writing this bibliography: the count of approximately 80 theaters in 2004 to 1028 theaters in 2012 is the latest datum, the essay pre-dates accessible 4K live-action dome capture (which it notes is only then arriving), the Led/Sphere-scale venues, and real-time dome engines. Evidence for the formal claims is the author's own viewing (Of the seventy plus fulldome features this author has watched) — a survey, not a study; no method is given and no other viewer's judgement is reported. The theoretical frame is film history and auteur practice, not reception or phenomenology, and somatic montage is asserted from her own film rather than demonstrated with audience data. It also treats the dome audience as one undifferentiated body (school children), and uses cinema itself as the unexamined norm against which the dome is measured.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Fulldome_Articles_005_Towards_a_Fulldome_Manifesto_final_proof_pdf — the same frame-to-sphere claim made as cultural polemic; the manifesto also names Moonwalk only indirectly, via its own festival examples.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_future_directions_for_research_media_aesthetics_and_fulldome_film — Yu (2008) asks the empirical question next to her formal one, and both cite Arnheim and Zettl-adjacent aesthetics.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_thompson_pdf (Brad Thompson, IPS 2004 Fulldome Standards Summit) — the production-standards side: action/text safe areas for the dome image she discusses as frame-less.
  • Books_The_Virtual_Window_pdf and Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf — the frame/screen and intermediality literature that this essay's no frame argument would need to be tested against.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Fulldome_Immersive_Modalities_and_Materiality_Toward_A_Poetics_of_the_Fi — Saper & Tomlinson (2024) take up the same term poetics for the dome, but from electronic literature rather than film history.

Craig Saper and Lynn Tomlinson, Fulldome Immersive Modalities and Materiality: Toward A Poetics of the Firmament (2024)

Citation

Saper, Craig, and Lynn Tomlinson. Fulldome Immersive Modalities and Materiality: Toward A Poetics of the Firmament. MATLIT: Materialidades da Literatura 11, no. 1 (2024): 85–95. ISSN 2182-8830. DOI: 10.14195/2182-8830_11-1_6.

Original PDF filename: Fulldome Articles\Fulldome Immersive Modalities and Materiality Toward A Poetics of the Firmament..pdf.

Coverage

Journal article (complete, published version). 12 PDF pages; PDF p.12 is blank. Printed pages are 85–95, i.e. printed page = PDF page + 84 for PDF pp.2–11 (PDF p.2 = printed 86, PDF p.11 = printed 95); PDF p.1 is the title page. Full text present, including the reference list (PDF pp.10–11), the dual English/Portuguese abstract, and the CC BY notice. Text layer is clean with two artefacts: the journal sets names and running heads in letter-spaced small capitals, so the extraction inserts spaces inside words (M A R Y L A N D, C R A I G S A P E R), and one stray BEL control character appears at the start of footnote 1 on PDF p.3. About 4,600 words.

The article's evidentiary base is a curated screening programme, not a study: in July 2023 the authors curated fulldome projections at the Hemispherium of Coimbra's Exploratório for the Electronic Literature Organization (ELO) conference and media festival, and the essay works through that programme work by work — Larry Cuba's Two Space (1979), Michaela French & Adrian Lahoud's Climate Crimes (2019), Millie Young's Elephant Elephant Elephant (2023), Tomlinson's Reverie de Giverny, Ben Shedd's IAIA student project 60-50 IAIA, Tomlinson's Aqua Dome and Ignite!, Pedro Rodolpho Ramos's Saravá (2021), and the authors' own CoDomeDigo (2023) and Global Reading Supplement (2023).

Argument in brief

The article argues two things: that the specific technology of the dome moves immersive media out of the headset and back into a shared public room, and that the dome form enables a new genre of visual e-poetry and e-literature that can carry massively interconnected narratives (climate and migration flows, non-human perspectives, sign language, the poem as spherical advert). It supplies a condensed pre-history — domes as architecture, church domes, VanDerBeek's movie-drome and expanded cinema, the Zeiss planetarium's Bauhaus visitors, Monet's Nymphéas — and argues that the shift to digital projection is what made all this possible, analogous to hypertext on personal computers. The final section turns to teaching: the authors want university fulldome production labs making original student and faculty content, an open-source distribution network between institutions, and domes used for public-facing scholarship beyond astronomy. The poetics of the firmament is their name for the proposal that the dome is a communal mode for knowledge production, not a delivery format for astronomy. The taxonomy of modalities is presented through the works rather than as a table: 2D hand-drawn composited into 3D dome space, painterly mixed-media stop-motion, sound-led multi-channel composition with a near-black screen, concrete/spherical poetry, and data/flows visualisation.

Key ideas

  • Fulldome as a social technology — the dome as a shared public space, contrasted with the usually socially isolating headset experience.
  • Modality / materiality — used deliberately in the McLuhan/Pingree–Gitelman sense: the medium's physical character (hemisphere, dome master, spatial sound) is a constituent of meaning, not a container for it.
  • Dome master — the circular grid defining a hemisphere within a square; the file format that makes fulldome production distributable.
  • Fulldome-essay / spherical knowledge design — Saper's terms for writing and reading designed for the dome instead of the flat page; Global Reading Supplement as prototype.
  • Expanded cinema pre-history — VanDerBeek's movie-drome, Jordan Belson, Henry Jacobs, E.A.T. as the 1960s–80s precursor modality.
  • e-lit / e-poetry in the dome — the new genre claim, built on CoDomeDigo's adaptation of Augusto de Campos's concrete poem CODIGO.
  • Poetics of the firmament — the essay's closing proposal: the dome as the poetic, globally inclusive curve of the sky, repurposed for collective reading, teaching and scholarship.
  • Curated exhibition as argument — method here is programming and commentary, not measurement.

Quotes

  • “first, the specific technology of fulldome projections moves a usually socially isolating experience of immersive media into a shared public space” (PDF p.2 / printed p.86) — the article's first thesis: dome as social technology
  • “fulldome shows engage audiences in communal and public ways to experience immersive media without donning VR headsets” (PDF p.2 / printed p.86) — the communal audience claim, quotable for the performance/participation argument
  • “it is useful to understand how and why the term fulldome describes not only the spherical visual moving media, but also the multi-channel surround sound that creates a visceral and collective immersive experience” (PDF p.7 / printed p.91) — materiality: the dome medium is visual and spatial sound
  • “little research has been conducted to establish a technology-independent principle for 360º creative storytelling” (PDF p.5 / printed p.89) — the field's research gap, quoted by the authors from Millie Young's 2020 Silpakorn dissertation
  • “The fulldome project translates the qualities of the printed essay into an immersive move through texts.” (PDF p.8 / printed p.92) — the fulldome-essay as a transfer of the page's qualities into the dome — their genre proposal in one line
  • “Most importantly, the fulldome is an important space for necessary public-facing scholarship across interdisciplinary sciences, literature, the humanities more generally, social sciences, health and medicine, the arts, and data visualization.” (PDF p.9 / printed p.93) — the pedagogical/lecture argument: the dome as a site of address and knowledge production
  • “At present, most university-based planetarium shows are either created in real-time within the planetarium presentation system, or are pre-packaged products licensed from third parties.” (PDF p.9 / printed p.93) — the state of university dome practice — useful for framing what practice-based research would have to change
  • “Today, the word firmament is mostly literary, used to poetically describe the visual curve of the sky as globally inclusive.” (PDF p.10 / printed p.94) — the title concept — why the word firmament is chosen and what the dome image is taken to mean

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

Use this source for the current (2020s) academic framing of fulldome as a humanities and electronic-literature medium rather than a science-visualisation tool: the communal-versus-headset argument, the pedagogy case for university domes, the dome-master/technical materiality, and the expanded-cinema lineage that connects the dome back to performance and projection art. It is also the most recent of these five and the only one that discusses framing and distributing the same dome work between festival, conference and classroom. What it cannot carry: any account of a live performance practice inside a dome (the works discussed are pre-rendered films, with the one exception of Ignite!, a live choir filmed in the dome and replayed as recording), and any evidence about audience response — the audience is asserted to be communal, not studied.

Limits

The modalities of the title are not delivered as a systematic taxonomy — no table, no typology, no criteria; the reader has to reconstruct the categories from the ten or so works discussed, and the older modalities (star-ball, laser, slide, Omnimax) are narrated historically rather than classified. The article is short (10 pages of text) for the ground it covers and repeatedly defers its own main promise, including a stated future essay on animage, CAVEs and computer-assisted virtual environments. Its institutional claims about student labs and distribution networks are proposals with no reported outcomes. Reference apparatus is thin in places (Millie Young's Elephant Elephant Elephant and Tomlinson's Aqua Dome are listed with no venue or festival; the reference list mixes author–date and title-only entries). There is at least one geographical error in the published text: the IAIA project is placed in Santa Fe, Mexico (PDF p.6) where New Mexico is meant. The section on teaching reports intentions, not practice-based findings.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Fulldome_Articles_The_Cine_poetics_of_Fulldome_Cinema_pdf — the other source here that claims the word poetics for the dome; hers is film-formal, theirs is literary/pedagogical.
  • Fulldome_Articles_005_Towards_a_Fulldome_Manifesto_final_proof_pdf — shares the expanded-cinema prehistory, the communal-dome claim and the Bauhaus/Zeiss anecdote; the manifesto is louder, this article is better sourced.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Fulldome_immersive_creation_A_panorama_Soci_t_des_arts_technologiques_SA — the SAT dossier names the same non-science dome venues (SAT, Market Hall Plymouth, CULTVR Cardiff) and the same festival/network ecology.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_fulldome_101_danintroduction_pdf — the other end of the same community: the what is fulldome terminology slide deck (seamless image, covers the entire dome, democratizes show creation).
  • Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf — Grau's history of immersion as illusion is the theoretical backdrop for their modality/materiality move.
  • Books_Digital_Performance_Steve_Dixon_pdf — for the digital-performance lineage their expanded-cinema prehistory implies but does not follow up.

Ka Chun Yu, Future directions for research: Media aesthetics and fulldome filmmaking (2008)

Citation

Yu, Ka Chun. Future directions for research: Media aesthetics and fulldome filmmaking. Paper presented at the 2008 Fulldome Summit, Chicago, Illinois, July 3rd, 2008.

Both details are printed in the file itself. Author and affiliation on PDF p.1: Ka Chun Yu / Denver Museum of Nature & Science / 2001 Colorado Blvd., Denver CO, 80205 USA (e-mail a kcyu@dmns.org on the same page). Venue and date in the footer repeated on every page: 2008 Fulldome Summit, Chicago, Illinois, July 3rd, 2008. The running head on PDF pp.2–9 is Ka Chun Yu. No journal, publisher, volume or DOI appears anywhere.

Original PDF filename: Fulldome Articles\Imersa\future directions for research media aesthetics and fulldome filmmaking by ka chun yu.pdf.

Coverage

Conference paper (complete). 10 PDF pages; PDF p.10 is blank. Prose on PDF pp.1–7, references on PDF pp.7–9 (45 numbered entries), running footer with venue and date on every page. No printed folios, so page references are PDF pages. Text layer is clean: the only artefacts are the inline placement of figure captions (Figures 1–4, PDF pp.3–5, including a caption numbering slip — the text calls the upcoming two figures Fig. 3a/3b but the caption below them is labelled Figure 4) and the author's e-mail being run into the first page's footer line. There is one abstract, three figure captions and no method section: the paper is a research-agenda proposal. About 3,500 words.

Argument in brief

The paper argues that fulldome research has so far aimed at cognition (learning gains in planetarium programmes) and should now turn to affect: the emotional response produced by specific filmmaking choices. Yu takes media aesthetics as studied for film and television (Arnheim, Zettl) and asks which of its elements transfer to the dome — object placement relative to the (absent) frame, the tilted horizon in a tilted unidirectional theatre, story-intensity curves, colour, pacing, montage rhythm — and which do not. He then borrows the psychological model of emotion as valence/arousal/dominance, with its measurement toolkit (Self-Assessment Manikin, EMG, heart rate, skin conductance) and the findings of affective-media research (motion raises arousal; negative imagery improves subsequent memory), to propose a programme of dome experiments. The payoff claimed is practical: affective response feeds attention, memory and learning, so aesthetics research is also education research. The paper closes with a list of open questions rather than results, and names one existing project (Maya Skies at Chabot) as the only dome study of non-cognitive audience response it knows of.

Key ideas

  • Media aesthetics — the study of how specific elements that make up an artistic medium affects us when we digest that content; here applied to dome-specific elements.
  • Media elements — composition, mise-en-scène, lighting, camera motion, scene length, editing, pacing, rhythm, colour, image quality; the variables of the proposed research.
  • Valence / arousal / dominance — the dimensional model of emotion Yu imports from psychology (dominance largely set aside) as the dependent variables.
  • Affective fulldome research — his name for the gap: dome studies of emotional response as distinct from learning-gain studies.
  • Frame-less — he accepts the dome is arguably frame-less, then tests which frame-based rules survive (object tension high in the dome vs. low; the unstable, seat-dependent horizon).
  • Story intensity — narrative graph (after Block) tracking intensity over a film's running time, offered as a tool the dome can also use.
  • Immersion as confound — the question he poses but leaves open: how the immersive display itself helps or hinders each aesthetic effect.
  • Objective measurement — self-report, physiological and behavioural methods proposed as the means, with the caveat that self-report cannot be taken during the experience.

Quotes

  • “In this paper I will argue for a direction for future research lying not in cognition but in the affective realm, looking specifically at how creative filmmaking choices in the presentation of specific media elements can result in emotional reactions in the viewer.” (PDF p.1) — the paper's thesis in its own words
  • “These media elements have been studied as media aesthetics in traditional cinema, but have never been properly applied to the fulldome realm.” (PDF p.1) — the gap claim — the field has no worked-out dome aesthetics
  • “Objects have different amounts of tension depending on where they are with respect to the frame. For objects near the upper edge of the frame, they appear to hang against the force of gravity.” (PDF p.2) — dome-specific form: what happens to frame-based composition rules without a frame
  • “However it is uncertain how important tilted horizons are for fulldome films in tilted uni-directional domes, since a horizon may appear perfectly level from one seat, but may appear not only tilted but warped from elsewhere in the theater.” (PDF p.2) — the horizon and seat-dependence problem — the image is not the same from every position
  • “It may be very threatening and dominating, because the spaceship does appear to fly literally over the audience’s heads, instead of disappearing off the top of the frame in traditional film.” (PDF p.6) — embodied dome effect: the overhead image acts on a seated, looking-up body
  • “Maya Skies is the only fulldome research project that we are aware of that explicitly studies non-cognitive factors in the audience response.” (PDF p.6) — the evidence base in 2008 — one project
  • “How can media aesthetic variables be used to help elicit specific responses from an audience?” (PDF p.7) — the research question — quotable as the agenda
  • “How does the immersive display (or other parameters that make the fulldome space unique) hinder or help?” (PDF p.7) — his framing of the dome itself as a variable rather than a container

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

Cite this for the field's own research agenda: what dome filmmaking lacks (no worked-out media aesthetics, only one known non-cognitive audience study, no controlled comparison of dome against flat presentation), and what a practice-based or empirical programme in the dome could measure. It is the best bridge in this corpus between the formal claims made by practitioners (Cine-poetics, the Manifesto) and any actual evidence, and it is the one source here that names the affect–attention–learning chain explicitly. It can also carry the description of dome form from a scientifically literate film practitioner: horizon behaviour in tilted domes, object tension overhead, the intensified overhead pass-by. What it cannot carry: findings (there are none), any account of live performance or the lecture as a form, and any treatment of the presenter or of a pedagogical address — its unit of analysis is the pre-rendered film and the individual viewer's measured affect.

Limits

It is a proposal, not a result: every empirical claim is imported from flat-screen media research and is probably applicable or uncertain for the dome. Nothing in the paper reports dome data; the future directions are unexecuted, and no follow-up is cited in the text. It pre-dates the real-time/game-engine dome pipeline, 4K live-action capture, head-mounted measurement and modern eye-tracking, so its instrumentation list (EMG, heart rate, skin conductance, SAM) now looks conservative. Its model of the audience is a single measured subject; collective, co-present dome spectatorship is not theorised, even though the dome is what makes the co-presence possible. It also takes the science-museum audience and the planetarium show as the given context, so festival, artistic and performance work sits outside its scope, and the learning-gain literature it wants to move past is cited only in passing.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Fulldome_Articles_The_Cine_poetics_of_Fulldome_Cinema_pdf — the same questions about dome-specific film form, argued historically and asserted rather than operationalised; her somatic montage is exactly the kind of claim Yu proposes to test.
  • Fulldome_Articles_fulldomesummit_08_the_perception_of_domed_environments_by_pete_carss_and — the other 2008 Fulldome Summit paper in this folder; Carss & Hazelden take the presence/perception route Yu does not, and also cite Noë and Varela on embodied perception.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_thompson_pdf and Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_ips_2010_gaston_rodigast_pdf — the production-standards and spatial-audio counterparts to his media-element list.
  • Books_Immersed_in_Technology_Moser_and_MacLeod_pdf — the art/science anthology context in which dome affect-and-cognition arguments were being made at the same period.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_fulldome_101_danintroduction_pdf — Dan Neafus's terminology deck; the venue-side vocabulary Yu assumes the reader already has.

Pete Carss and Katina Hazelden, The Perception of Domed Environments (2008)

Citation

Carss, Pete, and Katina Hazelden. The Perception of Domed Environments. Paper presented at the 2008 Fulldome Summit, Chicago, Illinois, July 3rd, 2008.

Both details are printed in the file. Author line and affiliation on PDF p.1: Pete Carss and Katina Hazelden / EL CETL / i-DAT / University of Plymouth, Devon, UK, with the running head Pete Carss, et al. on every page. Venue and date in the footer repeated on every page: 2008 Fulldome Summit, Chicago, Illinois, July 3rd, 2008. No journal, publisher, volume or DOI appears anywhere.

Original PDF filename: Fulldome Articles\fulldomesummit_08 the perception of domed environments by pete carss and katina hazelden.pdf. An identical duplicate sits at Fulldome Articles\Imersa\fulldomesummit_08 the perception of domed environments by pete carss and katina hazelden.pdf (also extracted as Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_fulldomesummit_08_..._by_pete_ca): same file size (100,955 bytes) and a byte-identical extracted text (md5 b24b41a853cbe572b27157e40442766e), so it is the same PDF filed twice, not two sources.

Coverage

Conference paper (complete), but a review/proposal rather than a study. 6 PDF pages; PDF p.6 is blank, prose on PDF pp.1–5 (the second half of PDF p.5 is the reference list). No printed folios, so page references are PDF pages. Text layer is clean; artefacts are minor — footnote text broken into single-line blocks (PDF pp.3–4), figure captions running into body text, and a broken word (absection for section/absorb on PDF p.3). About 2,200 words. References are a numbered list [1]–[10]; no abstract keywords, tables or data.

Important: despite filing under the perception of domed environments, the paper reports no findings about dome perception — it is explicitly a secondary-research review that ends by proposing the study: The above represents the end of a preliminary phase of secondary research. It also does not discuss orientation, the horizon, or the dome seam: searches for horizon, seam, orient, peripheral and seamless return no matches anywhere in the text. The only line about the dome's physical configuration is the refurbishment sentence quoted below (tilted 25º, 40 forward-facing seats, single SXGA+ channel plus an inset QXGA projector). Its first phase is scoping the presence literature for what might transfer to domed augmented virtual environments (DAVEs).

Argument in brief

The paper argues that domed immersive environments have been left out of two bodies of research that should apply to them — the presence/VR measurement community, which it says is anecdotally unaware of dome theatres, and perceptual/cognitive psychology — and that this is why immersivity remains vague in dome practice. Its first move is terminological: reject immersion as over-used and adopt presence as the measurable construct, defined as being there. Its second move is theoretical: cognition is socially distributed and embodied (Noë, Varela, Hutchins), so the dome's seat-bound, non-interactive audience cannot be dismissed as passive acquisition of knowledge — perception is an activity, and Gregory's finding that perception is largely memory means viewers see what their prior experience predicts. Its third move is empirical-by-proxy: Blake et al.'s (2007) habitualisation result, in which presence is highest for the naïve viewer and decays with expertise (the Spin/Spinoza model rather than Coleridge's suspension of disbelief), which the authors flag as a problem for frequent dome viewers and for a medium whose capture tools are becoming cheap. Its fourth move is pedagogical: co-located domes have a social advantage that CAVEs and HMDs lack, which is what makes them suited to presenter-led learning, framed through Vygotsky's Zone of Proximal Development. It closes with a methods plan (self-report, behavioural observation, biofeedback) and its known hazards, noting the Plymouth dome's USB sockets in every seat as the hardware for a biofeedback study on a scale not yet been attempted.

Key ideas

  • DAVE (domed augmented virtual environment) — the authors' term for what is now called a fulldome theatre; placed on Milgram's virtuality continuum alongside CAVE and HMD.
  • Presence over immersion — their key terminological demand: immersivity is meaningless in academic use, presence is definable and measurable.
  • Perceptual immersion — quoted definition from Biocca & Delaney: the degree to which the environment submerges the perceptual system; measurable by counting senses supplied and physical inputs shut out.
  • Suspension of disbelief (SoD) vs. Spin — the two competing models of acclimatisation-to-simulation borrowed from Blake et al. (Coleridge vs. Spinoza); the paper leans to the Spinoza model.
  • Perception as activity — Perception is not something that happens to us, it is something we do (Noë), and Gregory's 90% memory: the viewer's prior experience determines what is seen.
  • Distributed and embodied cognition — the paper's unifying principle (Hutchins, Varela), used to argue the seated dome audience is still perceptually active.
  • Social presence / being there together — the co-located advantage of the dome over headset VR, tied to collaborative learning.
  • Zone of Proximal Development (ZPD) — Vygotsky's construct used to frame the presenter-led planetarium, including collaboration among peers and via the artefacts of absent others.
  • Presence measurement — subjective (self-report), behavioural (observation), physiological (biofeedback); the paper lists the failure modes of each.

Quotes

  • “The refurbishment included ‘tilting’ the dome to 25º, and moving from concentric to 40 forward facing seats.” (PDF p.1) — the venue: tilted dome, 40 forward-facing seats — the physical configuration the paper works from
  • “In technological practice, immersion is a much-overused phrase - the ubiquity of use has rendered it quite meaningless in an academic context. Instead, the term presence carries more value.” (PDF p.2) — the terminological intervention: immersion out, presence in
  • “The degree of presence in a given environment inversely correlates with the degree of awareness of the mediation.” (PDF p.2) — the paper's operational claim about the dome — mediation awareness versus presence
  • “There has to date been very little academic research into perception in DAVEs.” (PDF p.3) — the paper's own statement of the perceptual-research gap for domes
  • “'Perception is not something that happens to us, it is something we do' [6].” (PDF p.3) — embodied perception, quoted from Alva Noë — the anti-passive-audience warrant
  • “Perception, according to Gregory, is 90% memory.” (PDF p.3) — the paper's most portable perceptual claim — relevant to recurring dome audiences and to teaching
  • “Blake el at [1] concluded that the Spinoza model – start as a naïve viewer and lose presence when expert - was more accurate.” (PDF p.4) — the habitualisation result the paper flags (with the caveat that it contradicted established wisdom)
  • “Further, as technologies for fulldome video capture become more accessible, we need to be aware that we could be placing people in environments in which the viewer is already expert.” (PDF p.4) — the practical consequence for a medium with a recurring, habituated audience — directly relevant to repeated festival or lecture programming

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the source for the claim that the dome is a shared, co-present medium rather than a headset medium, and for the embodied/distributed-cognition justification of a seated dome audience — useful against the passive spectator objection that any performance-in-a-dome project will meet. It also supplies the vocabulary and the caution that a dome audience can be too expert to be present (habitualisation), which is a live problem for recurrent festival programming and for teaching. Use it for method framing too: it is the only one of these five that lays out measurement options honestly. What it cannot carry: any dome-specific perceptual finding (it has none), any account of orientation, the horizon, the seam or seating geometry as perceptual variables, and nothing on performance, theatre or the lecture as form — the presenter appears as a pedagogical role inside a Vygotskian frame, not as a performer.

Limits

Not a perception study, and the framing as one is misleading. Everything about perception is imported from headset VR, CAVE and psychology literature; the only dome-specific content is the description of the Plymouth facility. The experimental plan is future tense throughout and nothing in the text reports whether it ran. Two caveats the authors raise themselves and which should be repeated: the Blake et al. result runs counter to established wisdom, and biofeedback measurement at dome scale has, to our knowledge, not yet been attempted. The paper also carries visible copy-editing damage (Blake el at [1], set them aside form other immersive environments, look to absection existing bodies of work, this is appears to be true) and cites a 2007 presence workshop paper [7] for the claim that other research fields are unaware of domes — an anecdotal claim about the literature, not a finding about audiences. It predates seated-audience eyetracking, the modern fulldome literature (Yu's own paper is not cited; no fulldome sources at all beyond the venue), and any real-time dome engine.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_future_directions_for_research_media_aesthetics_and_fulldome_film — the other 2008 Fulldome Summit paper here; Yu wants affective measurement where Carss & Hazelden want presence measurement, and neither has data.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_ips_2010_gaston_rodigast_pdf — planetarium audio history/futures; relevant to the multi-sensory and spatial-audio strand of presence the paper only gestures at.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_aes_amsterdam_planetarium_presentation_pdf — Gaston's survey of 100+ planetariums, including seating arrangements, i.e. the venue variability the ZPD discussion assumes away.
  • Fulldome_Articles_005_Towards_a_Fulldome_Manifesto_final_proof_pdf — the assertive, unmeasured opposite of this paper's methodological caution about the dome audience; useful as a contrast pair.
  • Books_Phenomenology_of_Perception_Maurice_Merleau_Ponty_pdf and Books_Intercorporeality_and_Interaffectivity_Thomas_Fuchs_pdf — the embodied-perception literature that grounds the perception is something we do line far more fully than this paper's two-sentence citation can.

2. Dome practice and production: how the work is actually made

The production side of the field, and the most useful material for anyone making work in a dome: what the vocabulary means, what the standard formats are, what fails across venues, and what the industry has and has not agreed on. Neafus gives the terms (dome master, equidistant polar fisheye, unidirectional versus concentric seating, the neighbour terms fulldome film / planetarium show / immersive cinema / digital dome) but the deck's definitions live in its images. Thompson gives the production constraints that matter for a lecture in the round — gravity-level horizon, seating tilt, safe areas for text and action, colour calibration, star decimation — and states that his findings apply only to unidirectional theatres, which excludes any concentric setup an experiment might want. Gaston and Rodigast give audio: no speaker standard, no seating standard, 46 percent of venues dissatisfied, an IPS standards proposal from 2010 that had not been adopted. Two of these five are image-only slide decks and one is a web-page export of a curatorial dossier, so the recoverable text is thin; that is a property of how this field publishes, and worth saying so in a review.

Dan Neafus, Fulldome 101 (slide deck; no date, venue or affiliation in the text layer)

Citation

Neafus, Dan. Fulldome 101. Slide deck. Title slide reads WELCOME! / Fulldome 101 / Dan Neafus (PDF p.1); the final slide reads Thank You / Introduction to Fulldome (PDF p.40). No date, conference, institution or venue appears anywhere in the extracted text — the deck is the standard orientation talk of the immersive-dome circuit, circulated in the Imersa/IPS material. Original PDF: Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_fulldome_101_danintroduction_pdf.

Coverage

Slide deck, 41 PDF pages, ~410 extracted words in total — about ten words per page. The text layer contains only slide titles, bullet labels and diagram captions. Nine pages (8, 9, 16, 25, 26, 36, 37, 38, 41) yield no text at all; on most others a single phrase sits over an image. The visual content — dome photographs, projection-rig diagrams, screenshots, producer and title slides — is not recoverable from the text layer. Two consequences for this review: (1) the deck can supply the field's terms but never its definitions — there are no definitions in the text layer, only labels; (2) any paraphrase of what the deck shows (dome configurations, markets, producers, film titles) would be invention, so nothing of that kind is asserted here.

Key ideas — the deck's vocabulary for the review

Terms as they appear, with the page that carries them (labels only — the deck's own definitions are in images and cannot be quoted): - Fulldome Film / Planetarium Show / Immersive Cinema / Digital Dome — four competing names presented side by side under the question What is it called? (PDF p.10). - Fulldome — glossed on the slide as Seamless image, Covers the entire dome, Digital Immersive experience, Democratizes show creation (PDF p.12). - dome master — the name of the master-format slide, appearing twice (PDF pp.20, 22), immediately before the projection jargon. - equidistant polar fisheye — the projection/format term (PDF p.23); this is what a dome master is in projection terms. - IMAX dome vs Fulldome — the two dome-screen systems named together for comparison (PDF p.21). - Digital projection / Film projection / Dome Theaters — the delivery taxonomy (PDF pp.13–15); the deck's history runs from analogue film projection to digital. - Dome configurations / Dome markets — the deck organises the field by geometric configuration and by market (PDF pp.5–6). - Isolated / Communal — the two terms used to characterise the first dome (PDF p.4). - Unidirectional / Concentric — seating/plan configuration terms (PDF p.7); the same pairing that structures Thompson's and Gaston's material. - Flight of the Pixel — the production pipeline named as a sequence: digital audio and video creation, manipulation, storage/compression, distribution, slice and dice, play, display (PDF p.24). - Systems and Pixels taxonomy — single channel, fisheye; mirror dome; dual channel; many channel; combination; hybrid; 3D stereo; movie playback; realtime shows (PDF pp.27–35). - Audio formats — Audio, Mixing and workflow, 5.1, 7.1, 15.1, and more (PDF p.39). - Neighbour terms — Virtual reality, Computer graphics, Digital dome, 360 cinema, Numerical simulation (PDF p.19). - Agenda terms with no surviving text: Technology, Jargon, Pushing Pixels, Standards (PDF p.2) — the four headings of the talk, announced but not extractable.

Argument in brief

The deck is an orientation: it defines what a digital dome is, lists its configurations and markets, names the format (fulldome) against its rivals, walks the production pipeline from pixel creation to display, lists the hardware system types, and closes on audio channel counts. Its only evaluative claim in the text layer is on the definition slide, where fulldome is said to democratise show creation. Everything else is taxonomy plus unextractable images, so the deck functions as a vocabulary sheet for the field rather than an argument.

Quotes

  • "• What is a digital dome? • Technology • Jargon • Pushing Pixels • Standards" (PDF p.2) — the deck's agenda and, in effect, the standard contents of a fulldome orientation talk.
  • "What is it called? • Fulldome Film • Planetarium Show • Immersive Cinema • Digital Dome" (PDF p.10) — the naming problem of the medium, in the field's own parallel terms.
  • "What is Fulldome? • Seamless image • Covers the entire dome • Digital Immersive experience • Democratizes show creation" (PDF p.12) — the field's compact self-definition, including its democratisation claim.
  • "Equidistant polar fisheye" (PDF p.23) — the jargon term for the dome master projection format, the vocabulary a review must use precisely.
  • "• Mirror dome • Dual channel • Many Channel • Combination • Hybrid • 3D stereo • Movie playback • Realtime shows" (PDF p.27) — the system taxonomy (this is the continuation of the slide headed Systems and Pixels, whose first item is Single Channel, Fisheye).
  • "• Audio • Mixing and workflow • 5.1 • 7.1 • 15.1 • More!" (PDF p.39) — where the field itself places audio: channel counts as an open-ended variable.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the vocabulary source: it supplies the terms (fulldome, dome master, equidistant polar fisheye, unidirectional/concentric, isolated/communal, Systems and Pixels, Flight of the Pixel, 5.1/7.1/15.1, and the naming dispute fulldome film / planetarium show / immersive cinema / digital dome) that a fulldome-performance review has to define before it can argue anything. It can carry the point that the field describes itself first as a format and only second as a medium of performance. It cannot carry arguments about spectatorship, dramaturgy or practice: it has no performance content and, in this file, no definitions.

Limits

No date, no affiliation, no references; the definitions of every term are in images that the extraction cannot reach. The deck is technology- and standards-facing — dome configurations, markets, producers, pixels, channel counts — and never discusses audience, staging, narration or authorship. Any claim about what the deck shows must be checked against the PDF images or a transcript, not this text layer.

  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_thompson_pdf — Brad Thompson's 2004 paper on dome master safe areas, tilt and gamma: the production detail behind this deck's format slides.
