Brad Thompson, Four Issues to Consider When Producing Fulldome Content for Wide Distribution (2004)

Citation

Thompson, Brad. Four Issues to Consider When Producing Fulldome Content for Wide Distribution. Paper presented at the IPS 2004 Fulldome Standards Summit, Valencia, Spain, 7–8 July 2004. Author credited as Brad Thompson, Spitz Creative Media (PDF p.1); the conference line runs as a head on pages 1–6. Original PDF: Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_thompson_pdf.

Coverage

Conference paper, complete — 7 PDF pages, ~2,950 words, with abstract, four numbered sections and no reference list; page 7 is empty. No figures and no data tables in the text layer: all results are reported in prose, including the four test theatres with their channel counts, resolution, codec, projector type and dome tilt (PDF p.1) and the resampling-filter comparison (PDF p.5). Extraction is clean enough to quote directly. Minor damage and source errors to know about: the list of horizon types in §1 is internally inconsistent (the paper says it created three types then lists four, and then refers to the three test sequences); a duplicated relative pronoun appears on p.6 (stars treated as points whose diameter...); and the words beyond of the field-of-view of the viewer on p.3 are the author's own wording, preserved verbatim in the quote below.

Argument in brief

Spitz Inc., at the request of the Denver Museum of Nature & Science and with National Science Foundation funding, ran production tests to make fulldome shows transferable across dissimilar projection systems. Four issues are examined. (1) Camera tilt: there is no standard dome tilt, producers render for a gravity-level horizon by subtracting dome tilt from camera tilt, a non-level horizon makes viewers feel off-balance, and a 15-degree tilt is the emerging consensus for looking correct in the most theatres. (2) Action and text safe areas: unlike flat-screen practice, the risk is not cropping but placing information outside the viewer's field of view or text too small or too large to read; the safe area is content dependent and the conclusion is that art directors must understand their choices when producing dome master frames. (3) Gamma: producers, manufacturers and operators use the word for far more than gamma — colour temperature, white point, black point and faithful reproduction generally — a standard exists (2.2 curve, 6500 K white point) but education, the cost of spectroradiometers and graphics-projector defaults set near 9000–10000 K work against it, so per-theatre correction remains necessary and should be done at the slicing stage. (4) Star decimation: fine stars vanish when a high-resolution master is scaled to lower-resolution systems, no resampling filter is objectively better, 2.5–4 pixel stars are the useful range, 4.0 pixels held up in preliminary tests at both 3600x3600 and 1024x1024, and a two-layer star/foreground render is the fallback. The paper is explicitly a work in process, and states that some of these issues are production aesthetics rather than objectively testable properties. It confines itself to theatres with unidirectional seating.

Key ideas

  • gravity level horizon — the horizon producers must fake by subtracting dome tilt from camera tilt so that an immersed viewer does not lose balance (PDF p.2).
  • dome tilt, no standard — test tilts of 0, 10, 20 and 30 degrees; the emerging consensus is that 15 degrees looks most correct in the greatest number of theatres (PDF pp.2–3).
  • over-rendering — the rejected option of rendering a 210-degree polar master and cropping to 180 degrees to apply tilt in post; rejected for resolution cost, production complexity, impossible test review and loss of artistic control (PDF pp.2–3).
  • action and text safe area — borrowed from flat-screen production but re-motivated for the dome: not cropping, but content placed outside the viewer's field of view, and text that is too large or too small to read; the safe area is content dependent (PDF p.3).
  • dome master — the master frame format for which title and action safe areas are defined (PDF p.3).
  • gamma (as used loosely in the field) — shorthand for gamma, colour temperature, white point, black point, i.e. accurate representation of source material on the dome; target 2.2 curve and 6500 K white point against graphics-projector defaults of 9000–10000 K (PDF p.4).
  • slicing/splitting — the per-theatre conversion step, identified as the best moment to apply gamma correction, and argued to be a standard feature of slicing software (PDF p.5).
  • star decimation — the loss of fine stars when scaling a high-resolution master down; filters compared (Hermite, triangle, Mitchell, Bell, B-spline, Lanczos, EWA) with no objective winner (PDF pp.5–6).
  • star size in practice — DMNS renders stars at 2.5 pixels for a 3600x3600 polar dome master and accepts up to 4 pixels; 4.0 pixels held up in preliminary tests when rescaling to 1024x1024 (PDF p.6).
  • unidirectional seating as scope condition — the paper deliberately excludes omni-directional theatres, which it judges to be a small share of fulldome video theatres with special production requirements (PDF p.1).
  • aesthetic versus objectively testable — the paper's own methodological caveat: some issues raised are production aesthetics that cannot be objectively tested, so the best available outcome is awareness and, at most, a recommended approach (PDF p.1).

Quotes

  • "At the request of the Denver Museum of Nature & Science, and with grant support from the National Science Foundation, Spitz Inc. performed a set of production tests to help ensure wide distribution and cross-compatibility of future fulldome shows with many different fulldome projection systems." (PDF p.1) — the institutional basis of the field's early standards work.
  • "You will notice that we have consciously chosen to address only theaters with unidirectional seating designs in our research." (PDF p.1) — the seating configuration that quietly defines almost all of this literature.
  • "There is no standard dome tilt or sweep for fulldome video theaters, but content producers often wish to present viewers with a “gravity level horizon”." (PDF p.2) — the dome's geometry as a production constraint and the field's own term for it.
  • "Because the viewer is immersed in the frame, a non-gravity level horizon can make the viewer feel off-balance." (PDF p.2) — the body of the spectator entering the argument, via the inner ear.
  • "Our problem is not that the edges may get cropped in projection, but that since the field-of-view that we have available is so wide, it’s possible to place important information beyond of the field-of-view of the viewer." (PDF p.3) — the composition problem specific to a dome screen, and why safe areas are redefined.
  • "The goal of this test was to create a “Dome Master” format reference frame that shows recommended title and action safe areas." (PDF p.3) — dome master as both a technical format and a compositional frame.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

The video-side counterpart to the audio papers, and the best source here for how the dome's geometry constrains what can be composed and seen: tilt and the gravity-level horizon, safe areas for titles and action, the dome master frame, and the fact that the field's standard screen geometry is unidirectional seating. For a review of practice — including the performance lecture — it supplies the technical constraints under which text, titles and figures can be placed in a dome, and it shows the field's own boundary between measurable production questions and untestable aesthetics. It cannot carry spectatorship theory: the viewer appears only as someone who may feel off-balance.

Limits

Dated 2004 and tied to the projection systems of that moment (CRT, DLP, MPEG-2 and wavelet codecs, specific 1280x1024 and 3600x3600 pipelines), with no conclusions on two of its four issues; star-size figures are era- and venue-specific. No figures or tables survive in the text layer, so the test imagery and measurements cannot be checked. Omni-directional (concentric) theatres are excluded by design, which is a real gap for live performance work in domes. No performance content and no audience research.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_fulldome_101_danintroduction_pdf — Neafus's orientation deck, where dome master and equidistant polar fisheye appear as vocabulary without definitions.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_aes_amsterdam_planetarium_presentation_pdf — Gaston on the audio equivalent: no standard speaker placement or seating, and a call for loudspeaker correction.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_ips_2010_gaston_rodigast_pdf — Rodigast and Gaston, 2010: the same standards agenda continued for sound.
  • Fulldome_Articles_The_Cine_poetics_of_Fulldome_Cinema_pdf — von Chamier-Waite, who takes up the compositional question Thompson raises as a poetics rather than a specification.

Leslie Gaston, Methods for Sharing Stereo and Multichannel Recordings among Planetariums (AES Amsterdam; year not stated)

Citation

Gaston, Leslie. Methods for Sharing Stereo and Multichannel Recordings among Planetariums. Slide presentation, Audio Engineering Society (AES) convention, Amsterdam. Title slide credits the presenter as Leslie Gaston, Assistant Professor of Recording Arts, University of Colorado at Denver, Denver, Colorado USA, with contact address (PDF p.1); a footer repeats Leslie Gaston and the same address on every slide. No year, session number or paper number appears anywhere in the text layer — the Amsterdam venue comes from the original PDF filename (Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_aes_amsterdam_planetarium_presentation_pdf) and the folder's AES material. The same author's 2010 IPS paper cites a 2008 IPS Chicago paper of near-identical title, which brackets the likely date but does not establish it.

Coverage

Slide deck (PowerPoint export), 25 PDF pages, page 25 blank, ~890 extracted words — roughly 35 words per slide. The text layer holds slide titles, short bullets, chart axis/legend labels and the footer; the substance of many slides is in images and is not recoverable from the text: speaker-arrangement diagrams and photographs (p.8), microphone-position measurement photos (p.9), frequency/phase/impulse response plots (pp.13–14), the aural-immersion bar charts (p.5), the dissatisfaction factor chart (p.11) and the live/prerecorded signal-flow block diagram (p.21), whose box labels survive as bare words (Mixer, Lake Huron 3D, Live production, Router, D/A, Loudspeakers, SEOS Media Player) with no connections. Extraction damage worth knowing before quoting: stray closing quote marks end many lines; some words are split by a demoted space (slide 2 extracts the five areas of concern as Acoustics, Production, D elivery, E quipment, S eating Arrangements); the fi ligature appears as a single glyph in Findings, films and satisfied; the source itself misspells surprisingly (p.11). Numbers on slides are reliable; sentence-level prose is scarce.

Argument in brief

The deck argues that dome audio cannot currently travel between venues and that the fix is measurement-based correction plus standardisation. Evidence: a survey of over 100 planetariums (103 respondents on some questions) plus production and listening work at Gates Planetarium (Denver) and Chabot (Oakland). Findings reported: prerecorded shows are already passed between planetariums despite no standardisation of speaker placement or seating; 46% of those surveyed are dissatisfied with their venue's audio; dissatisfaction clusters on fidelity/intelligibility, localisation and panning, acoustics, and difficulty playing shows from outside sources. Loudspeaker correction with the Trinnov Optimizer is presented as the first step toward transferability, demonstrated by informal listening tests (a soloed right channel perceived at multiple locations without correction, one location with it). Delivery is discussed as 5.1 remapped to the dome's 16 loudspeakers, with stems for dialog replacement and varied channel layouts. It closes with recommendations and the finding that 92.5% favour standardisation for sharing and 91.1% for audio quality, while IPS has no audio standards at all.

Key ideas

  • Areas of concern — the deck's five-part frame: Acoustics, Production, Delivery, Equipment, Seating Arrangements (listed on PDF p.2; the words are extraction-damaged there). Seating arrangement is treated as an audio variable throughout, not a comfort variable.
  • survey of over 100 planetariums worldwide — the deck's evidence base (PDF p.2); the only research instrument described.
  • Visual vs aural immersion — respondents were asked to rate immersion of the visual and the aural experience separately on 1–10 scales (PDF p.5), which is the deck's one framing of the dome as an experience rather than a system.
  • Three modes of planetarium audio — star shows with a single announcer, prerecorded shows, live music (PDF p.6); the deck's own taxonomy of dome programming sound.
  • Loudspeaker correction — equalisation of the loudspeaker/room system (Gates uses none, PDF p.9); implemented with the Trinnov Optimizer, measuring phase, impulse and frequency response (PDF pp.13–14); proposed as the prerequisite for content transferability (PDF p.12).
  • Precedence effect — the dome listening test's explanatory mechanism: a soloed channel reproduced from multiple loudspeakers is localised differently depending on where the listener sits, unless the system is corrected (PDF p.15).
  • Dome acoustics for audiences — no loudspeakers behind listeners; viewers turn head and body to see images, which affects HRTFs; all speakers above except forward speakers (PDF p.16).
  • Loudspeaker remapping — traditional 5.1 is remapped to the dome's 16 loudspeakers; spatialisation via Trinnov Optimizer, Lake Huron 3D, or VBAP (PDF p.18).
  • Stems and track layout — mixes split into stems so dialog can be replaced or remixed (PDF p.22); 5.1 plus an occasional top channel, 6.1/7.1 uncommon, channel orders inconsistent across venues (PDF p.22).
  • Narration address — at Chabot narration is delivered as a god-voice from the centre, at Gates from a front narration channel (PDF p.19); the deck treats this as an unsolved production question created by differing seating arrangements.
  • Audio fingerprints / mastering houses — recommendations to characterise each venue acoustically and create specialist mix facilities (PDF p.23).

Quotes

  • "Prerecorded shows are produced at one planetarium, and shared with others, despite a lack of standardization of speaker placement and seating arrangements." (PDF p.7) — the deck's central problem statement, and the clearest evidence of what domes do and do not share.
  • "Not suprisingly… when asked, “Are you satisﬁed with the audio in your Planetarium?” Forty-six percent (46%) of the 103 surveyed are not satisﬁed with the audio in their venue." (PDF p.11) — survey headline; note the source's own typo and the slide's stray quote marks, preserved verbatim.
  • "Based on these ﬁndings, loudspeaker correction should be considered the ﬁrst step towards achieving the goal of transferability of content between planetariums." (PDF p.12) — the deck's thesis in one sentence.
  • "No loudspeakers behind listeners, • Viewers turn their heads and bodies to see images (impacting HRTF’s), • All speakers are above, except for forward speakers." (PDF p.16) — dome geometry as a listener problem: the seated spectator moves to see the image.
  • "As in feature-length ﬁlms, some planetariums replace the main dialog track with another language, so they need a mix separated into “stems”, with the dialog on one set of tracks (mono or stereo) and the music and effects on another (mono, stereo, cube, 5.1, etc.)." (PDF p.22) — how multichannel dome delivery is actually packaged, and why.
  • "There is a widespread sentiment among planetarium operators that some sort of standardization should be implemented to promote ease of sharing (92.5% in favor) and audio quality (91.1% in favor)." (PDF p.23) — the field's own demand for standards, with figures.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the production-infrastructure source: it can carry arguments about the dome as an acoustic and seating architecture, about what counts as a portable work in a dome, about narration address (centre god-voice versus front channel) and about the absence of shared standards across venues. It supplies the review's technical vocabulary for delivery — 5.1 remapped to N loudspeakers, stems, channel layouts, precedence effect, HRTF. It cannot carry arguments about spectators' experience beyond two survey rating questions, and it has no performance-theory content.

Limits

No date, no session details, no methodology section: the survey's sampling, questions and response rate are not given. Listening tests are explicitly informal, with formal tests deferred. All measurements, charts and speaker layouts are images and cannot be read from this file. It predates object-based/immersive audio standards (Dolby Atmos, MPEG-H) as a planetarium topic, and treats audio purely as an engineering problem.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_ips_2010_gaston_rodigast_pdf — the follow-up paper by the same author with René Rodigast, which cites this line of work and adds WFS and object-based formats.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_fulldome_101_danintroduction_pdf — Neafus's orientation deck, which lists dome audio formats (5.1, 7.1, 15.1) as an open variable.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_thompson_pdf — Thompson's 2004 standards paper: the video-side equivalent of Gaston's audio-side compatibility problem.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_fulldomesummit_08_the_perception_of_domed_environments_by_pete_ca — Carss and Hazelden on perceiving domed environments, the audience-side counterpart to this system-side deck.

René Rodigast and Leslie Gaston, The Sound of Space: A Look at the History and Future of Audio in Planetariums (2010)

Citation

Rodigast, René, and Leslie Gaston. The Sound of Space: A Look at the History and Future of Audio in Planetariums. Paper presented at the 2010 Fulldome Summit, Alexandria, Egypt, June 26–30, 2010. Title page gives the authors as Rodigast, René, Fraunhofer IDMT, Ehrenbergstrasse 31, 98693 Ilmenau, Thüringen, Germany, and Gaston, Leslie M., University of Colorado Denver, Campus Box 162, PO Box 173364, Denver, CO 80217 USA (PDF p.1); a footer on every page repeats the summit and dates. Original PDF: Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_ips_2010_gaston_rodigast_pdf.

Coverage

Conference paper, complete — 9 PDF pages, ~2,750 words, with abstract, six numbered sections, five figures and a numbered reference list; page 9 is empty. All five figures are images and not recoverable from the text layer (WFS principle, animation of audio objects, a Spatial Pan SPL simulation, the directional-mixing principle, and the object-versus channel-based comparison). Extraction damage to watch before quoting: the 1986 journal name comes out as The Plantarian (for The Planetarian); on p.2 the text gives a high-pass filter at 250 kHz (24dB/oct), where the unit is very likely Hz and the figure should be checked against the PDF before being reused; on p.5 the figure caption collides with the running text as a single garbled string (Directional hearing printed over an image), so the opening words of that paragraph are unreliable — the sentence resumes cleanly at the words For the definition of specific directional areas in the dome. Prose elsewhere extracts cleanly.

Argument in brief

The paper reviews planetarium audio historically (1920s acoustic treatment and perforated domes, Jena 1926 and Hamburg, a separate sound console at Mueller in 1947, stereo from the 1960s, live music programmes, the digital workstation turn of the 1990s) and describes present technology, noting that terms such as ambisonics, 3D audio and wavefield synthesis are entering the planetarium vocabulary. It then sets out three routes to giving every seat a comparable spatial experience — Wave Field Synthesis, the cheaper Spatial Pan variant with fewer loudspeakers, and directional mixing built on the precedence effect — and argues that sharing audio between planetariums is blocked because existing channel-based delivery formats (tape, disc, files, DAW sessions, AC3) carry no flexible metadata; the fix proposed is an object-based format describing position, timing, level, processing and acoustic environment. It closes with a preliminary standards proposal for IPS: unspatialised centre narration, sound tied to its visual source and stable for all seats, room acoustics within a defined range, an object-based format with unlimited channels, and accommodated downmixes to mono/stereo/5.1 and upmixes to 3D. The stated driving forces are the variety of production styles, the desire to share shows, operator demand (92% of surveyed planetariums) and the need to improve audio quality.

Key ideas

  • Audio as the neglected half of the dome — the paper's premise is that planetarium audio has no standards while its picture formats do; the survey of over 100 planetariums is its evidence base (PDF p.1).
  • Perforated dome — the historic technical fix for dome acoustics: acoustic transparency achieved by perforating the dome shell, tried at Jena and Hamburg with steel, aluminium, cork and glass wool (PDF pp.1–2).
  • Reverberant dome — the acoustic condition treated as the field's defining problem; the paper's remedies are high-absorption walls and floors, directed loudspeakers, and reduced low-frequency bandwidth, with one separate subwoofer channel or more (PDF p.2).
  • Wave Field Synthesis (WFS) — a closed loudspeaker array at the height of the horizontal line emulating virtual sources by Huygens' principle; each speaker driven by a unique signal; MPEG 3D-Audio profile for source positions (PDF p.3).
  • Spatial Pan — a reduced-speaker-number WFS variant giving 2D horizontal movement and near-individual spatial sound per seat, described as a cost-effective alternative (PDF pp.4–5).
  • Precedence effect — the psychoacoustic basis of directional mixing: the first arriving wavefront fixes localisation, so amplitude- and time-scaled loudspeaker groups are driven to steer the image (PDF p.5).
  • Object-based versus channel-based — the paper's key distinction for sharing: an audio event with position, active time, level, processing and environment (object) versus fixed channel assignments with phantom images (PDF pp.6–7).
  • Uniformity for all seats — the recurring requirement that every audience member have a similar experience, which is what forces either WFS-class systems or narration rules (PDF p.3, p.7).
  • Narration placement — proposal that dialog be unspatialised and emitted from the centre, with sound effects originating from their visual source and stable for all seats (PDF p.7).
  • Room-acoustics standard — a defined planetarium range in RT60, early reflections, low-frequency response, speech transmission index, C50 and C80 (PDF p.7).

Quotes

  • "Planetarium designers recognized as early as the 1920’s that acoustic treatment was necessary to help with intelligibility, and very soon thereafter recognized the need for perforated domes to achieve acoustic transparency." (PDF p.1) — dome acoustics as a hundred-year design problem, not an afterthought.
  • "Although reverberant domes pose a unique challenge acoustically, optimization for them is possible." (PDF p.2) — the paper's thesis for the acoustics section.
  • "With Wave Field Synthesis (WFS) technology all listeners in the area of the dome can determine the exact position of sound sources." (PDF p.4) — the seat-independence claim that a review can compare against actual dome spectatorship.
  • "This sound reinforcement technology is based on the “precedence effect” (or “law of the first arriving wavefront”) and provides the basis for directional hearing" (PDF p.5) — the psychoacoustic term the dome's directional mixing rests on (sentence continues in the source).
  • "Issues such as loudspeaker orientation, audience orientation, number of channels, and type of playback media were discussed." (PDF p.6) — what actually blocks sharing content between venues.
  • "Narration and other dialog elements should come from center (if a loudspeaker exists there) and not be spatialized." (PDF p.7) — the proposed standard that speaks directly to narration as an address position in the dome.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the acoustics-and-address source for the dome as a performance space: it can carry arguments that the dome is an acoustic instrument in which seating position changes what a spectator hears, that the placement and spatialisation of narration is a design decision with a proposed standard, and that the field's practice knowledge circulates through IPS standards work rather than through journals. It is also the clearest evidence that the community's own research agenda is transferability and standardisation of content between venues. It cannot carry arguments about image, dramaturgy or authorship.

Limits

Engineering paper, no performance theory: live work appears only as live music programming, and audiences appear only as seats requiring uniform coverage. Figures are unavailable in the text layer and one unit (250 kHz) is suspect. The survey instrument is not described here — it points to the author's earlier paper for method. It predates the current object-based/immersive formats (Atmos, MPEG-H) as planetarium practice, and its standards proposal is explicitly preliminary.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_aes_amsterdam_planetarium_presentation_pdf — Gaston's earlier sharing deck, cited here as reference [1] and the origin of the survey and loudspeaker-correction findings.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_thompson_pdf — Thompson's 2004 standards paper: the same community problem (tilt, gamma, resolution) on the video side.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_fulldome_101_danintroduction_pdf — Neafus's orientation deck, which ends on the same 5.1 / 7.1 / 15.1 channel-count question.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_fulldomesummit_08_the_perception_of_domed_environments_by_pete_ca — Carss and Hazelden on how dome environments are actually perceived, the empirical counterpart to this paper's uniformity requirement.

Argument in brief

The dossier announces itself as the SAT creation team's survey of the evolution of artistic practices in fulldome creation and of the medium's possibilities. Its two surviving sentences frame the field historically and commercially: the 1923 Zeiss Model I is named as the first projection device for a geodesic dome, and the Las Vegas Sphere is named as a content-creation-oriented dome venue that sets precedents for immersive entertainment technology. The file therefore documents that a Montreal artist-run centre with its own dome produced a dated 2024 state-of-the-field dossier, and preserves its two opening claims — but the dossier's actual analysis of creation practice is invisible in this export. Nothing about method, works or artists can be extracted without OCR.

Key ideas

  • Fulldome creation as an artistic field — the dossier's stated subject is the evolution of artistic practices in fulldome creation, i.e. practice and authorship rather than astronomy programming (PDF p.1).
  • Model I centenary (1923) — the planetarium community's own origin date, given as October 21, 1923, Deutsches Museum; the medium's first hundred years used as the dossier's timeframe (PDF p.1).
  • content-creation-oriented dome venues — the dossier's category for new venues (Sphere, Las Vegas) built to consume created content instead of serving astronomy; implicitly contrasted with planetarium programming (PDF p.1).
  • Dossier as publication form — a dated institutional web dossier with site navigation baked into every page; evidence of how this sector publishes practice knowledge outside peer review (PDF pp.1–22).

(PDF p.1) — the commercial/entertainment pole of dome production, in the SAT's own wording.

These four quotes are the whole of the recoverable text and appear identically on every text-bearing page (PDF pp.1–22).

It can carry an argument about institutional production of dome work and about the sector's self-periodisation. It cannot carry any argument about aesthetics, spectatorship or method — that content is in the images and is not in this file.

3. Immersion, illusion and the image space

The historical and theoretical spine for the word the dome literature uses most and defines least. Grau is the key text: immersion is not a technology but a recurrent strategy of image spaces, defined by installing the observer inside a totality that fills the field of vision and conceals its own apparatus, and its history runs from Roman wall painting through the panorama to IMAX. Moser and MacLeod's anthology is the 1996 record of what artists actually did when they got access to VR, and the moment when artists stopped being users of borrowed tools. Manovich supplies the distinction a literature review needs most — the database and navigable space as forms against narrative — and, almost incidentally, the corpus's only description of a performance staged under a dome. Friedberg supplies the opposing pole: the window and the screen as frames that organise perception, and the argument that the abolition of the frame is a historical event with an aesthetics of its own.

Oliver Grau, Virtual Art: From Illusion to Immersion (2003)

Citation

Grau, Oliver. Virtual Art: From Illusion to Immersion. Translated by Gloria Custance. Cambridge, MA: MIT Press, 2003. (Leonardo series; ISBN 0-262-07241-6.) Revised and expanded translation of Virtuelle Kunst in Geschichte und Gegenwart: Visuelle Strategien (Berlin: Reimer, 2001). Source: title page, copyright page and Library of Congress CIP block, PDF pp.1-5. Original PDF: Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf (432 PDF pages).

Coverage

Book, complete. 432 PDF pages / ~130,600 words. Front matter: Leonardo series foreword (Roger F. Malina), foreword by Joel Slayton, acknowledgments; nine chapters (1 Introduction; 2 Historic Spaces of Illusion; 3 The Panorama of the Battle of Sedan; 4 Intermedia Stages of Virtual Reality in the Twentieth Century; 5 Virtual Art-Digital! The Natural Interface; 6 Spaces of Knowledge; 7 Telepresence; 8 Evolution; 9 Perspectives); References (printed pp.352-401), author index, subject index.

Text layer: clean and quotable; no OCR garbling in the passages used. Extraction artifacts to know about: (a) ligatures survive as single glyphs, so words such as artificial and field carry the fi ligature and the quotes below reproduce it; (b) the degree sign after 360 was lost in extraction and comes through as a control character (U+0014, 36 occurrences), so those phrases are not quoted byte-clean and are avoided in the quotes below; (c) printed folio = PDF page minus 15 throughout the body (e.g. PDF p.40 = printed p.25), which is how the printed-page numbers below were derived. No duplicated pages; no missing chapter.

Argument in brief

The book's thesis is that computer virtual reality is not a new medium but the current stage of a long art-historical drive to install the observer inside a hermetically closed image space, and that this drive belongs to the core of the human relation to images rather than to the history of technology. It is built as a comparative history of illusion spaces: classical fresco rooms (Villa dei Misteri, ca. 60 b.c.; Villa Livia, ca. 20 b.c.), the devotional environments of the Sacri Monti, Gothic and Renaissance rooms, Baroque ceiling panoramas, then the panorama of 1789 onward, which Grau treats as the high point of illusionism and suggestive power available to painting, followed by the twentieth-century intermedia line (stereopticon, Cineorama, Cinerama, Sensorama, IMAX, Omnimax, CAVE, HMD) into digital virtual art (Osmose, Place, World Skin, The Home of the Brain), telepresence art, and A-Life/genetic art. Method: art history written as media history, with artists treated as the leading theorists and technicians of each epoch's image. The analytic hinge is Grau's definition of immersion as a process of diminishing critical distance and increasing emotional involvement, arising when artwork and apparatus are perceived to merge so completely that the medium itself falls below the observer's perceptual threshold. A long case study of the Sedan panorama (Anton von Werner, 1883) shows how the effect was engineered through architecture, lighting, faux terrain and viewing platform, and shows the economics and state interest behind it. The recurrent claim across all chapters is that new image media serve power, propaganda and markets far more often than art, and that immersion reliably works against distanced, critical reflection.

Key ideas

  • Immersion - a process, not a state: diminishing critical distance to what is shown plus increasing emotional involvement in what is happening; explicitly not a simple either/or with critical distance, since the relation is dialectical and depends on the observer's disposition.
  • Immersive image space / 360-degree image space - image media that seal the observer off from external visual impressions and address the eye with a totality of images. The book's period limit is deliberately 360-degree images, not cave painting, so virtuality is not claimed as an anthropological constant.
  • Calculated totalization and the invisible medium - the moment when artwork, apparatus, message and medium are perceived to merge inseparably; the principle of immersion is to withdraw the apparatus from perception so that "the medium becomes invisible".
  • Aesthetic distance - what immersion removes. Immersed in a high-resolution 360-degree illusion space the observer can no longer objectify the work as an autonomous aesthetic object; media competence (the learned ability to objectify a medium) is exactly the faculty the installation works against.
  • Immersion effect - Grau's own term, used once for the stereoscopic effect that Knowbotic Research's Dialogue with the Knowbotic South deliberately frustrates with a one-eyed display, leaving the user inside the image space but with a distanced, bifocal perspective: his worked example of an immersion counter-model (PDF p.231 / printed p.216).
  • Polylogue - his vocabulary for reception in speech-and-image spaces; in The Home of the Brain the moving interactor can compile an individual collage of polylogical statements out of the four media philosophers installed in the space (PDF p.236 / printed p.221).
  • Telepresence - the classic position of an observer in front of a material work is replaced by a participatory relation across distance that still appears to be immediately present in the work; the work loses its locatability, and Grau treats this as an aesthetic paradox rather than as a break with immersion.
  • Apocalyptists versus utopists - his recurring pattern for the discourse around every new illusion medium (the panorama's critics, print, film, VR): those who see a danger to perception and consciousness against those who welcome a space for fantasy and fusion with all-pervasive image worlds (PDF p.79 / printed p.64).
  • Panorama as presentation apparatus - rotunda, balustrade, faux terrain, overhead lighting and darkened viewing platform are engineering to keep the picture's limits invisible and to make the painted image appear as the source of the real; the technical model for all later immersive projection.

Quotes

  • "Immersion is undoubtedly key to any understanding of the development of the media, even though the concept appears somewhat opaque and contradictory. Obviously, there is not a simple relationship of ‘‘either-or’’ between critical distance and immersion; the relations are multifaceted, closely intertwined, dialectical, in part contradictory, and certainly highly dependent on the disposition of the observer. Immersion can be an intellectually stimulating process; however, in the present as in the past, in most cases immersion is mentally absorbing and a process, a change, a passage from one mental state to another. It is characterized by diminishing critical distance to what is shown and increasing emotional involvement in what is happening." (PDF p.28 / printed p.13) — definition of immersion: diminishing critical distance
  • "The intention is to install an artiﬁcial world that renders the image space a totality or at least ﬁlls the observer’s entire ﬁeld of vision" (PDF p.28 / printed p.13) — what makes an image space immersive: totality of the field of vision
  • "The overall effect is to break down barriers between the observer and what is happening in the images on the walls. This is accomplished by a suggestive appeal to the observer from all sides that utilizes illusionism techniques." (PDF p.40 / printed p.25) — Villa dei Misteri: hermetic address from all sides
  • "for all that, it is clearly a palpable testimony to a virtual reality, which not only sought to involve the observer through its subject but also, through the use of panoramic images, speciﬁc colors, and dramatic gestures, aimed at emotionally arousing the observer to ecstatic participation: the psychological fusion of observer and image in the cult." (PDF p.44 / printed p.29) — Villa dei Misteri: psychological fusion, ecstatic participation
  • "The resulting effect of illusion is that of ‘‘a form of second reality,’’27 which opens into the space of illusion but does not evoke a feeling of presence or immersion in it." (PDF p.47 / printed p.32) — illusion without immersion: the Odyssey frieze as the negative case
  • "The panorama installs the observer in the picture." (PDF p.72 / printed p.57) — panorama: observer installed inside the image
  • "Building on the traditions and mechanisms of illusionistic landscape spaces, the panorama developed into a presentation apparatus that shut out the outside world completely and made the image absolute." (PDF p.74 / printed p.59) — panorama as presentation apparatus that hides itself
  • "With curved screens of up to 1000 m2, spectators are literally in the images." (PDF p.175 / printed p.160) — IMAX / Omnimax: dome-adjacent large-format spectatorship
  • "It is well-nigh impossible to perceive it as an autonomous aesthetic object. If media competence results from the faculty or learned ability to objectify a given medium, then this mechanism is diminished in virtual installations." (PDF p.217 / printed p.202) — aesthetic distance / media competence
  • "This organization of perception must be construed as a countermodel to image worlds that address all the senses to form a sphere of illusion and, with the aid of intuitive interfaces and anthropomorphic agents, curtail the inner distance of the observer." (PDF p.231 / printed p.216) — immersion as withdrawal of the observer's inner distance
  • "The examples discussed here demonstrate that a constant characteristic of the principle of immersion is to conceal the appearance of the actual illusion medium by keeping it beneath the perceptive threshold of the observer to maximize the intensity of the messages that are being conveyed. The medium becomes invisible." (PDF p.355 / printed p.340) — immersion hides its own apparatus: the medium becomes invisible
  • "through real-time computation, interaction, and evolution, the observer is attaining a power to form the image that is unparalleled in history. At the same time, the observer is subjected to the greatest ever suggestive potential of images, which are now dynamic, interactive, evolutionary, and ‘‘alive’’ in immersive image spaces." (PDF p.363 / printed p.348) — interactivity and the 'living' image

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the source that lets a fulldome review say that the dome is a late instance of a recurring format rather than a new technology, and it supplies the vocabulary the review needs for the immersion question: immersion as diminishing critical distance, calculated totalization, the invisible medium, aesthetic distance versus media competence, and the apocalyptists/utopists pattern that every fulldome debate re-enacts. It can carry the genealogy argument (360-degree fresco room -> panorama -> Cinéorama/Omnimax/IMAX dome -> CAVE -> digital virtual art) and the argument that immersive formats are historically bound to power, propaganda and market interests rather than to art, which is a useful counterweight to fulldome advocacy writing. It also gives the review a defensible negative case: Grau's analysis of the Odyssey frieze, where a "form of second reality" opens into an illusion space without evoking presence or immersion, defines what a merely illusionistic image lacks. What it cannot carry: live performance, acting, the performer's body, rehearsal, or anything about practice-based research method - the body appears only as the immersant's sensorium in interactive installation art, and the theatre appears only as Futurist/Bauhaus scenospace and the Gesamtkunstwerk lineage.

Limits

  • Predates the fulldome boom and the digital planetarium. Planetarium is named once, in a list of large-scale illusion spaces (PDF p.364 / printed p.349); there is no discussion of dome projection, dome masters, fisheye, or planetarium practice.
  • No engagement with performance studies, liveness, or spectatorship as a social situation; reception is analysed as individual perception and seduction.
  • Artist chapters (Osmose, The Home of the Brain, Place, World Skin, A-Life) are largely descriptive-exegetical, with long quotations from the artists' own statements and less critical distance than the historical chapters.
  • Claims about how historical spectators actually felt are inferential; Grau himself notes that we judge earlier illusion media with our own media competence and may badly underestimate their suggestive force.
  • Translated German academic prose, sometimes dense; the book is also strongly German- and Europe-centred in its case selection (Sedan panorama, Sacri Monti, Chambre du Cerf).

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_The_Language_of_New_Media_Lev_Manovich_pdf - same MIT Leonardo series and the obvious companion claim about the database/narrative and screen lineages Grau cites.
  • Books_Immersed_in_Technology_Moser_and_MacLeod_pdf - the Banff collection of 1990s immersive work that Grau names as the overview he builds his history behind.
  • Books_The_Virtual_Window_pdf - the other main art-historical account of the frame, window and screen that Grau's "image space" argument runs against.
  • Fulldome_Articles_005_Towards_a_Fulldome_Manifesto_final_proof_pdf and the other Fulldome_Articles_* files - the field's own advocacy writing, which Grau's history can be used to historicise.
  • Books_Artificial_Hells_Claire_Bishop_pdf - participation and the social turn, the critical counterpart to Grau's account of the participating observer.
  • Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf and Books_Digital_Performance_Steve_Dixon_pdf - the intermediality and performance terms Grau does not supply.

Moser, Mary Anne, with Douglas MacLeod, eds., Immersed in Technology: Art and Virtual Environments (1996)

Citation

Moser, Mary Anne, with Douglas MacLeod, eds. Immersed in Technology: Art and Virtual Environments. Cambridge, MA: MIT Press, for the Banff Centre for the Arts, 1996. Details from the title and copyright pages inside the file (PDF pp. 2–3): "IMMERSED IN TECHNOLOGY / ART AND VIRTUAL ENVIRONMENTS / EDITED BY MARY ANNE MOSER WITH DOUGLAS MACLEOD / FOR THE BANFF CENTRE FOR THE ARTS / THE MIT PRESS CAMBRIDGE, MASSACHUSETTS LONDON, ENGLAND"; "© 1996 Massachusetts Institute of Technology"; ISBN 0-262-13314-8; Library of Congress "95-385 CIP" and class mark N72.T4 (the file's OCR prints it as "N72.HI48"). Original PDF filename: Books_Immersed_in_Technology_Moser_and_MacLeod.pdf.

Coverage

Book, apparently complete: 370 PDF pages, 106,803 words. Preface (ix), acknowledgments (xv), introduction (xvii–xxv), 11 essays, 10 artists' statements, artwork production credits, contributors, index, and a plate section at the end. It is a scanned "Copyrighted Material"-watermarked copy; the text layer is OCR with real damage: letter-spaced words ("audie n c e" for audience), lost spaces ("ofdisembodiment", "ofRitllal" for Objects of Ritual, "ofRitual"), line-break hyphens and mis-rendered quotation marks. Plates and captions are mangled ("YUxweluplun"). Chapter authors were recovered from the contents pages plus the running head on each chapter's pages; the contents page's two columns are misaligned in the OCR, so the title→author pairing is inferred from the author column's order and confirmed by running heads (all 11 confirmed except where noted). Quotes below were whitespace-normalised only (line-break hyphens rejoined, OCR letter-spacing respaced); word content is unchanged and each quote was verified present in its source page.

Argument in brief

The book documents and theorises the Banff Centre's Art and Virtual Environments Project (1991–94), which put artists into a production lab with engineers and gave them access to VR hardware on the artists' own terms. Its editorial position is that "virtual reality" is not a break in the history of technology but one more technology on a continuum, and that the interesting questions are social, cultural, ethical and political rather than technical. The essays divide between critical theory (Hayles, Bailey, Tenhaaf, Dyson, Stone, Ronell, Milthorp, Randolph, Todd, Morse, Huhtamo) and artists' own production statements about specific works made at Banff (Objects of Ritual, Archeology of a Mother Tongue, Dancing with the Virtual Dervish, Bar Code Hotel, VR on $5 a Day, Placeholder, Field Recording Studies, Topological Slide, Inherent Rights Vision Rights). The recurring editorial claim is that images which implicate the viewer — interactive or immersive media — are "unbounded" and demand experiential cognition, which puts the critic inside the thing being criticised. Several contributors attack the head-mounted, single-user, helmeted model of VR and build instead for groups, rooms, projections and performance space. The book is therefore a first-wave record of what it looked like when artists, not engineers, set the terms of immersive production.

Contents — chapters and artists' statements (printed page numbers)

Editors/editors' matter - Preface — Douglas MacLeod (Project Director), p. ix - Acknowledgments — Mary Anne Moser, p. xv - Introduction — Mary Anne Moser (the contents page credits Douglas MacLeod and Mary Anne Moser; the introduction's first page is headed "Mary Anne Moser"), p. xvii

Essays - N. Katherine Hayles, "Embodied Virtuality: Or How to Put Bodies Back into the Picture", p. 1 - Cameron Bailey, "Virtual Skin: Articulating Race in Cyberspace", p. 29 - Nell Tenhaaf, "Mysteries of the Bioapparatus", p. 51 - Frances Dyson, "When Is the Ear Pierced? The Clashes of Sound, Technology, and Cyberculture", p. 73 - Allucquère Rosanne Stone, "Cyberdammerung at Wellspring Systems", p. 103 - Avital Ronell, "A Disappearance of Community", p. 119 - Rob Milthorp, "Fascination, Masculinity, and Cyberspace", p. 129 - Jeanne Randolph, "A City for Bachelors", p. 151 - Loretta Todd, "Aboriginal Narratives in Cyberspace", p. 179 - Margaret Morse, "Nature Morte: Landscape and Narrative in Virtual Environments", p. 195 - Erkki Huhtamo, "Time Traveling in the Gallery: An Archeological Approach in Media Art", p. 233

Artists' statements - Will Bauer and Steve Gibson, "Objects of Ritual", p. 271 - Toni Dove and Michael Mackenzie, "Archeology of a Mother Tongue", p. 275 - Diane J. Gromala and Yacov Sharir, "Dancing with the Virtual Dervish: Virtual Bodies", p. 281 - Perry Hoberman, "Bar Code Hotel", p. 287 - Ron Kuivila, "VR on $5 a Day", p. 291 - Brenda Laurel and Rachel Strickland, "Placeholder", p. 297 - Michael Naimark, "Field Recording Studies", p. 299 - Marcos Novak, "Dancing with the Virtual Dervish: Worlds in Progress", p. 303 - Michael Scroggins and Stewart Dickson, "Topological Slide", p. 309 - Lawrence Paul Yuxweluptun, "Inherent Rights, Vision Rights", p. 315

Back matter: Artwork Production Credits p. 319; Contributors p. 329; Index p. 333. Author attributions: confirmed against running heads for Hayles, Bailey, Tenhaaf, Dyson, Stone, Milthorp, Randolph, Todd, Morse, Huhtamo and all ten artist statements; Ronell's running head ("AVITAL RONELL", PDF p. 145) confirms her as the author of "A Disappearance of Community". Nothing in the text layer contradicts this list.

Key ideas

  • virtual environments — the term Banff adopted instead of "virtual reality"; also "artificial reality, virtual space, immersive media, experiential images". The editorial point is that the term's common denominator is an apparent dematerialisation of experience.
  • unbounded image / experiential cognition — Moser's (via Timothy Druckrey) distinction: the bounded image is seen from a distance and offers perceptual experience; interactive or immersive media are unbounded and require the viewer to be inside them to comprehend them.
  • bioapparatus — Nell Tenhaaf's term (from the 1991 Banff residency) for the human body plus technology as a single circuit; the "fully immersive" interface is expected to make that circuit perceptually immediate and so seem unmediated.
  • immersion as illusion — Cameron Bailey: cyberspace's spatial metaphor promises that "one gets inside"; cinema only permits an illusion of participation, cyberspace is predicated on it. He then argues the immersion model conceals a "Self behind a 'camera'".
  • immersive group experience — Bauer and Gibson's design aim for Objects of Ritual: immersive real-time media without helmets or tracking gear, deliberately social and multi-participant, blurring performer and audience.
  • disembodiment / recorporealisation — Gromala and Sharir: a dancer wearing an HMD feels immersed and disembodied at once; the immersive environment was built from the artist's own MRI body so that "the experiential" and "the visceral" return through the interface.
  • the room as interface — Hoberman's Bar Code Hotel: a whole room covered in bar code as "a responsive membrane, an immersive interface", multiple simultaneous participants, stereoscopic projection, no head-mounted display and no first-person point of view.
  • virtual landscape / liminal space — Margaret Morse: virtual environments are subjunctive spaces "like the cave or sweat lodge"; the notion of immersion itself carries a spiritual register ("an amniotic ocean"), and is available to seduction as much as to critique.
  • spectator become inhabitant — Marcos Novak: in a virtual environment the spectator no longer suspends disbelief from outside; the spectator-become-inhabitant is suspended inside.
  • the sphere as a spatial figure — Naimark's Field Recording Studies tiles forty-two video stills into "a single panoramic dome" and then texture-maps panoramas onto a hollow sphere the visitor navigates in and around; Morse reads the result as a landscape seen as "a design on a ball" from outside and as "partially immersive" from inside.

Quotes

  • "Artists no longer sit on the sidelines eventually to become grateful users of borrowed tools but have become active in development, creating a disturbance in the field with new contingencies." (PDF p.7 / printed p.ix) — Douglas MacLeod's preface; the book's claim that artists are producers of immersive technology, not its consumers. Good for the practice-based-research framing.
  • "the Art and Virtual Environments Project was not about hardware and software, but rather about the need for a new perceptual structure for the arts." (PDF p.11 / printed p.xiii) — MacLeod, preface; the project's own statement of aim, usable as an early formulation of practice-based enquiry into new perceptual formats.
  • "Images that implicate the viewer in some way, however, as with interactive or immersive media, are unbounded. They require experiential cognition." (PDF p.16 / printed p.xix) — Moser's introduction, summarising Timothy Druckrey; names the methodological problem of the critical viewer inside the immersive image.
  • "The cut of the film gives way to the continuum of interactive and immersive experience." (PDF p.292 / printed p.275) — Toni Dove and Michael Mackenzie, "Archeology of a Mother Tongue"; the move from edited film to continuous immersive space, in a statement that explicitly tracks performer, space and audience.
  • "We wanted to create a piece that blurred or erased some conventional boundaries between performer and audience. To this end, we decided to "just say no to helmets" and to eschew the use of conventional VR tracking and visualization equipment." (PDF p.289 / printed p.272) — Will Bauer and Steve Gibson, "Objects of Ritual"; the clearest early argument for group, room-scale, non-headset immersion with a performing audience.
  • "As my perception accommodates itself to the three-dimensional illusion, I accrue a sense of being in another, additional skin; I feel immersed. Yet as I experience immersion in the simulation, I simultaneously lose the sense of being grounded in physical space, I experience the sensation of disembodiment." (PDF p.301 / printed p.284) — Diane J. Gromala and Yacov Sharir, "Dancing with the Virtual Dervish"; the dancer's first-person account of immersion and disembodiment in the same moment.
  • "An entire room is covered with printed bar code symbols, creating an environment in which every surface becomes a responsive membrane, an immersive interface that can be used simultaneously by a number of people to control and respond to a projected real-time, computer-generated, stereoscopic, three-dimensional world." (PDF p.305 / printed p.289) — Perry Hoberman, "Bar Code Hotel"; projection-based, multi-participant immersion — the closest thing in the book to a dome-room argument.
  • "The resulting images then were texture-mapped over a stationary hollow sphere that the visitor to its virtual space could navigate in and around. Seen from outside, the resulting landscape is like a design on a ball; from inside, the sphere is a partially immersive virtual landscape" (PDF p.230 / printed p.212) — Margaret Morse, "Nature Morte", describing Michael Naimark's Field Recording Studies; the anthology's one extended treatment of a spherical image environment, and a direct ancestor of dome thinking. (Sentence continues in the source.)
  • "Virtual environments replace the spectator with the inhabitant, and consequently the terms are reversed: the spectator does not suspend disbelief, but rather the spectator-become-inhabitant is held suspended within the arms of unbelievable space." (PDF p.320 / printed p.305) — Marcos Novak, "Dancing with the Virtual Dervish: Worlds in Progress"; the strongest formulation of the shift from spectatorship to inhabitation.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

It is the historical precedent section of the review: the first sustained attempt to let artists define immersive media practice in a production lab, with published production statements rather than only theory. It can carry arguments about the terminology shift from "virtual reality" to "virtual environments", about immersion as experiential rather than perceptual cognition, about group/projected immersion versus the headset, about the body (disembodiment, viscerality, the dancer), and about the sphere as an image environment (Naimark via Morse). It cannot carry spectator-response data, dome-specific projection geometry or dome-master practice, and it has no methodology chapter — the practice-based research dimension has to be argued from the preface and the statements, not cited.

Limits

Predates fulldome as a format (no dome masters, no fisheye, no planetarium production workflow). "Immersion" is discussed almost entirely in terms of head-mounted displays; the book's dome-relevant material is incidental, in Naimark's statements and Morse's reading of them. The theory essays are largely about identity, race, gender, sound and community in cyberspace rather than about performance or staging; "theatre" appears once or twice and "performance" barely at all outside the artist statements. The scan's OCR damage means any quote should be spot-checked against the printed edition; the quotations above were whitespace-repaired (letter-spacing and line-break hyphens), which is stated here rather than marked in every quote. The contributors' list is a period document; several of these artists were writing before the works existed as finished pieces.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf — the later canonical history of immersion in art, which treats this anthology's moment as a chapter.
  • Books_The_Virtual_Window_pdf — screen/window/immersion genealogy; supplies the image-surface history the Banff statements assume.
  • Books_Spheres_Bubbles_pdf — Sloterdijk's spheres; a philosophical counterpart to the book's talk of amniotic immersion and enclosing virtual space.
  • Books_Digital_Performance_Steve_Dixon_pdf — performance-and-digital-media history that overlaps the artists' statements here (Placeholder, Bar Code Hotel, Dove).
  • Fulldome_Articles_005_Towards_a_Fulldome_Manifesto_final_proof_pdf — the later fulldome field making its own claim on the word "immersion".
  • Fulldome_Articles_Fulldome_Immersive_Modalities_and_Materiality_Toward_A_Poetics_of_the_Fi — dome-specific account of what immersion means in a fixed dome; the counterpoint to this book's headset framing.
  • Fulldome_Articles_fulldomesummit_08_the_perception_of_domed_environments_by_pete_carss_and — later empirical work on how a domed field of view is perceived.
  • Books_Barrett_Estelle_Bolt_Barbara_eds_Practice_as_Research_Approches_to_Creative_Arts_Enq — the methodology literature this anthology lacks; use it to convert the Banff production model into a research claim.

Lev Manovich, The Language of New Media (2001)

Citation

Manovich, Lev. The Language of New Media. Cambridge, MA: MIT Press, 2001. [The file has no title page, no imprint and no copyright page — the extracted text opens directly on the prologue's first plate. Coverage

Book (complete), 308 PDF pages, 127,699 words extracted. Present and continuous: Contents (PDF pp.3–5); the Prologue "Vertov's Dataset" (PDF pp.7–26), a series of roman-numbered stills from A Man with a Movie Camera, each carrying a quotation from the book itself with its page reference in brackets; Acknowledgments (PDF p.27); a note on previously published articles (PDF pp.28–29); Introduction (PDF pp.29–35); I What is New Media? Extraction damage — moderate. This is OCR of a scanned book; the prose is continuously readable but peppered with misspellings that would corrupt a quote taken too literally: "staring points" for starting, "continuos", "diffirent" / "diffirent", "instlltion", "momevement", "equisation", "literara", "Nethertheless", "dwn" for down, "Archigram" as "Arhigram", "Schockwave", "QyickTime" for QuickTime, "Jeffrew Shaw", "Nietzche", "Silvester Stallone", "Camridge", "Septermebr", "exellent", "%95" for 95%, "muli-tasking", "Vkutemas" for Vkhutemas. Em dashes and curly quotes survive intact (unlike the Friedberg file). A second page-number series from the original scan is also interleaved into the text stream (numbers such as 226, 236, 310, 342, 373 appear mid-page), and the prologue repeats each quote's page reference in parentheses. Quotable, but with the misspellings in mind.

Argument in brief

Manovich sets out to write down the language of new media while it is still forming, on the model of film historians who reconstructed the grammar of early cinema. The book's spine is five principles held to be general tendencies of a culture undergoing computerization rather than laws: numerical representation, modularity, automation, variability, transcoding — the last being the most consequential, because computerization doubles every media object into a cultural layer and a computer layer that reshape each other. From there he argues that cultural form is now organised by two new forms, the database and navigable space, and that narrative — including cinema — has to be re-described as one trajectory through a database rather than as the default shape of culture. He then treats the computer screen not as a break with cinema but as the continuation of a centuries-old classical screen, whose rectangular frame, frontal viewing position and immobile viewer new media inherits; VR turns out to reproduce the cinematic frame rather than escape it. The final chapter draws the consequence for cinema: once images can be generated and composited, cinema loses its indexical identity and becomes "a sub-genre of painting", and the loop, not linear progression, becomes a candidate narrative engine for the computer age. The book is built as six thematic chapters with numbered sections, each ending in endnotes; a running motif is Vertov's A Man with a Movie Camera, treated as a database-driven prototype of new media. The stance is deliberately present-tense and materialist ("digital materialism") — a record and theory of the present, not a prophecy.

Key ideas

  • Numerical representation (principle 1) — every new media object is composed of digital code; media becomes programmable (PDF p.49).
  • Modularity (principle 2) — the "fractal structure of new media": images, sounds, shapes and behaviours are stored as discrete samples and assembled into larger objects while keeping their separate identity (PDF p.51).
  • Automation (principle 3) — principles 1 and 2 let media creation, manipulation and access be automated; the later part of this section argues that the key cultural problem shifted from making objects to finding them, i.e. from creation to access (PDF pp.52–55).
  • Variability (principle 4) — a new media object is not something fixed once and for all but can exist in different, potentially infinite, versions; hence the interface becomes separable from the content, and a work is one or more interfaces to a database of multimedia material (PDF pp.56, 200).
  • Transcoding (principle 5) — the mutual reshaping of a cultural layer (encyclopedia, story, composition, mimesis) and a computer layer (process and packet, sorting and matching, function and variable); the result is a composite culture, "a blend of human and computer meanings" (PDF pp.63–64).
  • Database and narrative as natural enemies — database represents the world as an unordered list, narrative as a cause-and-effect trajectory; "creating a work in new media can be understood as the construction of an interface to a database" (PDF pp.199–200).
  • Navigable space — the second key form of new media, along with the database; the computer becomes its perfect medium for a form that predates computers (PDF pp.216, 219). Manovich argues most computer culture spatialises representation, and puts the motion simulator, the 3-D fly-through and Hale's Tours in a single lineage with the shooter and adventure game.
  • The navigator and the explorer — two subject positions, or phenotypes (his term), of the new media user: the Baudelairean flâneur (culture, crowd, city) and the Cooper/Twain wilderness scout (nature, obstacle, conflict) (PDF pp.231–232).
  • The classical screen — a flat rectangular surface meant for frontal viewing (as against a panorama), holding a bounded virtual space inside our own; the computer screen continues this tradition, which is why the screen, not the computer, is what makes VR, interactivity and telepresence real (PDF pp.99–100). VR's frame, he argues, produces an experience closer to cinematic perception than to unmediated sight (PDF p.89).
  • Cultural interfaces — the HCI of windows, folders, menus is a hybrid built from three traditions: print, cinema and human–computer interface; the computer screen therefore becomes a battlefield of incompatible definitions — depth and surface, opaqueness and transparency, image as illusionary space and image as an instrument for action (PDF pp.80–99). Manovich treats interfaces as representations that privilege particular models of the world and the user.
  • Cinema as a sub-genre of painting — once images can be synthesised and composited, cinema can no longer be distinguished from animation: "It is no longer an indexical media technology but, rather, a sub-genre of painting" (PDF p.250).
  • Compositing — the new default operation of image-making; the image is assembled from independently rendered layers (live plates, blue-screen footage, 3-D elements) aligned into a seamless virtual space. he breaks it into three steps: construction of a seamless 3D virtual space from different elements; optional simulation of a camera move through that space; optional simulation of the artifacts of a particular medium (PDF pp.130–131).
  • Loop as a narrative engine — the loop gave birth to cinema (nineteenth-century pro-cinematic devices, the Kinetoscope) and to computer programming; Manovich asks whether it can become a new narrative form for the computer age, against narrative cinema's avoidance of repetition (PDF pp.264–265).
  • Spatial montage — instead of one image replacing another, images coexist on the screen; the computer screen becomes a record of memory rather than a record of perception, and spatial montage is the aesthetics of the GUI's multiple windows and of multitasking (PDF p.273).
  • Society of a screen — his term for the present: we may debate whether our society is a society of spectacle or of simulation, but it is undoubtedly a society of a screen (PDF p.99).

Quotes

  • "All new media objects, whether they are created from scratch on computers or converted from analog media sources, are composed of digital code; they are numerical representations." (PDF p.49 / printed p.49) — principle 1 stated in one sentence; the opening claim of the book's taxonomy.
  • "This principle can be called "fractal structure of new media.” Just as a fractal has the same structure on different scales, a new media object has the same modular structure throughout." (PDF p.51 / printed p.51) — principle 2; note the mismatched quote marks (straight opening, curly closing) are the OCR's. The relevant point for a dome argument: an asset-based work is a collection of interchangeable parts, not an integral object.
  • "The last, fifth principle of cultural transcoding aims to describe what in my view is the most substantial consequence of media’s computerization. As I have suggested, computerization turns media into computer data." (PDF p.63 / printed p.63) — principle 5; the claim that media and computer are now two layers of one object.
  • "In summary, the computer layer and media/culture layer influence each other. To use another concept from new media, we can say that they are being composited together. The result of this composite is the new computer culture: a blend of human and computer meanings, of traditional ways human culture modeled the world and computer’s own ways to represent it." (PDF p.64 / printed p.64) — the composite claim; the passage that most directly underwrites "the digital image is composite."
  • "As a cultural form, database represents the world as a list of items and it refuses to order this list. In contrast, a narrative creates a cause-and-effect trajectory of seemingly unordered items (events). Therefore, database and narrative are natural enemies. Competing for the same territory of human culture, each claims an exclusive right to make meaning out of the world." (PDF p.199 / printed p.199) — the database/narrative opposition in full; the cleanest way to state why a programme of images is not a narrative.
  • "In general, creating a work in new media can be understood as the construction of an interface to a database." (PDF p.200 / printed p.200) — one sentence; the form of a new-media work, and the test a performance lecture with images either passes or fails.
  • "To summarize, along with a database, navigable space is another key form of new media. It is already an accepted way for interacting with any type of data; an interface of computer games and motion simulators and, potentially, of any computer in general." (PDF p.219 / printed p.219) — navigable space as a cultural form, not a game genre; the sentence that separates a spatial work from a screen-bound one.
  • "Interactive virtual worlds, whether accessed through a screen-based or a VR interface, are often discussed as the logical successor to cinema, as potentially the key cultural form of the twenty-first century, just as cinema was the key cultural form of the twentieth century." (PDF p.89 / printed p.89) — the "screen's successor" claim; note that he immediately undercuts it, VR still being cut by a rectangular frame (PDF p.89).
  • "Consequently, cinema can no longer be clearly distinguished from animation. It is no longer an indexical media technology but, rather, a sub-genre of painting." (PDF p.250 / printed p.250) — cinema's demotion once compositing is normal; the claim a projection-based dome practice is structurally implicated in.
  • "Cinema’s birth from a loop form was reenacted at least once during its history. In one of the sequences of A Man with a Movie Camera, Vertov shows us a cameraman standing in the back of a moving automobile." (PDF p.265 / printed p.265) — the opening of "New Temporality: Loop as a Narrative Engine"; in context he goes on to say a loop, repetition, gives birth to a progression of events. Directly usable for a dome piece built on cyclical time.
  • "Like the rest of the festival, the performance took place in the Planetarium." (PDF p.32 / printed p.32) — the book's only planetarium; the venue is being rented out to an art festival, i.e. treated as a room.
  • "Under the black semi-spherical ceiling with mandatory models of planets and stars, a young artist was methodically painting an abstract painting." (PDF p.32 / printed p.32) — the single most useful sentence in either source for a fulldome review: a dome used as a room, by a performer whose act has no relation to the dome, witnessed by a puzzled audience. It is Manovich's own parable of incompatible historical and conceptual layers composited together.
  • "discussions of virtual reality (VR) in a Planetarium located in this classical city which, like Venice, is obsessed with its past—what for me, coming from the West, were incompatible historical and conceptual layers were here composited together, with the Nintendo Dataglove being just one layer in this mix." (PDF p.32 / printed p.32) — the analytic point of the same anecdote: the dome as a container in which incompatible media layers coexist rather than fuse. The early fulldome problem stated from outside the field.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the source that gives the review its formal vocabulary and its sharpest distinction. It supplies the five principles as a checklist for what makes a work computed rather than merely projected; the database/narrative opposition and "interface to a database" as a way of classifying programmes of images; navigable space as a named cultural form distinct from a screen-bound one; compositing and spatial montage as the operations that produce the multiply-layered image; and the "classical screen" genealogy which explains why an immersive apparatus still delivers a framed, frontal view. It can carry the central argument that a lecture-with-images is a database with an interface and a narrative imposed on it, whereas a genuinely spatial form is one the audience navigates. It cannot carry anything about the dome as a venue type, dome production practice, or audience research.

Limits

Written 1998–99, published 2001: it describes "new media" as Web sites, CD-ROMs, VRML worlds and computer games, and its examples stop at Star Wars: Episode I, QuickTime and Windows 98. Nothing on real-time GPU rendering, networked live performance, projection mapping or fulldome. Its only planetarium is the St. Petersburg anecdote, and it treats the dome as a rented hall rather than as a medial form — so it never asks what a dome is for. "Navigable space" is defined around games and simulators, not around performance or audience; there is no spectatorship theory, no body, no liveness, and no practice-based method. The five principles are asserted as tendencies and are not derived from data; the "database vs narrative" opposition has since been widely contested. The scan is complete but its OCR introduces misspellings inside sentences, so any quote should be checked against a printed copy before publication; no index is included.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_The_Virtual_Window_pdf — the historical counterpart: Friedberg supplies the frame/screen genealogy Manovich's "classical screen" compresses into a page.
  • Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf — the immersion history Manovich's "representation versus simulation" distinction (PDF p.41) is arguing with.
  • Books_Digital_Performance_Steve_Dixon_pdf — names Manovich in chapter 1 as the position it disputes (technology for technology's sake), and supplies the performance-side material Manovich omits.
  • Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf — intermediality as the performance-studies answer to Manovich's media-forms approach.
  • Books_Immersed_in_Technology_Moser_and_MacLeod_pdf — the art/VR anthology from the same period; the practitioner-side counterpart.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Fulldome_Immersive_Modalities_and_Materiality_Toward_A_Poetics_of_the_Fi — the fulldome application of the spatial/navigable question.

Anne Friedberg, The Virtual Window: From Alberti to Microsoft (2006)
