Argument in brief

Friedberg argues that the architectural window — the aperture that gives light and air — supplies the governing metaphor for every framed image in Western visual culture, and that it has been progressively replaced in fact by the screens of film, television and the computer. Her starting point is Alberti's 1435 instruction in De pictura to regard the painted rectangle as an open window (aperta finestra), and her endpoint is the Microsoft Windows trademark and the multi-window desktop. She revises the term "virtual": it is not a synonym for the digital but an ontological register — a substitute that is functionally or effectively, but not formally, of the same materiality — and it has a long prehistory in painting, photography and cinema. Against the rhetoric of an immersive, unframed virtual reality she sets the frame: framed images "organize and structure perception and cognition in a very different way" (p.26), and the screen's frame is an "ontological cut" that separates material spectatorial space from the virtual space it holds. The book closes on convergence and multiplication: screens replacing real windows as image-bearing glass skins, computer windows nested on a desktop, and the postcinematic, postperspectival viewer positioned in front of a fractured, multiply windowed screen. It is built as a sequence of themed chapters with four short "Lens" interludes (Descartes, Heidegger, Bergson, Virilio) rather than a continuous chronological history.

Key ideas

  • "virtual window" — Friedberg's composite term, deliberately double: a metaphoric window (the frame as a window onto a depicted world) and a literal window that now delivers a virtual view (the screen that has replaced the aperture in the wall). She insists the second is not merely figurative: the screen has become an actual substitute for the window (PDF p.26).
  • The window as architectural and computer trope — one figure running from Alberti's aperta finestra to the Microsoft "Windows" trademark; the computer window breaks the metaphor's singularity because it is not one frame onto one world but one of many inset frames nested on a desktop (PDF p.16).
  • The frame / "ontological cut" — the moving-image screen's frame separates the material surface of the wall (or of the room) from the view inside the aperture; the frame is the threshold where immobile spectator meets mobile image (PDF pp.20–21).
  • Perspective after perspective — perspective may have met its end on the computer desktop (PDF p.17): on the computer display, above/below/ahead/behind are simultaneous, and each element is composed with no systematic spatial relation to the others. Her phrase for the resulting condition is "post-Cartesian, postperspectival, postcinematic, and posttelevisual" (PDF p.22).
  • Immersion vs. the frame — Friedberg (drawing on Oliver Grau's Virtual Art) treats immersive spaces of illusion — panorama, cinéorama, Sensorama, 3-D IMAX — as a separate tradition from framed images, and refuses to let "virtual" collapse into "immersive" (PDF pp.26, 185). The 1920s–30s theatre projects she reports (Pilichowski's encircling panoramic screen, Kiesler's endless theatre) were designed precisely to abolish the proscenium frame for the sake of immersion.
  • The screen as architecture — chapter 4's relocation of the argument: not architecture depicted inside film, but the screen functioning as a building element, "an expansion of material built space" (PDF p.166); the film screen as a surface — "a picture plane caught in a cone of light" (PDF p.181).
  • The Multiple — chapter 5's term for split-screen and multiple-screen composition in film and television, today made vernacular by the GUI's inset windows: a screen that is multiple, adjacent, postperspectival (PDF p.37).
  • Multitasking and the "apperceptive apparatus" — Benjamin's notion of reception in a state of distraction (der Zerstreute) re-read as a model for the multi-window computer user; the flâneur succeeded by the multitasker (PDF p.262).
  • Screenless images — the Conclusion's forecast: image-bearing glass skins on buildings, screens substituting for real windows, and a windows environment giving way to virtual window-walls (PDF pp.37, 257).

Quotes

  • "The window is an opening, an aperture for light and ventilation. It opens, it closes; it separates the spaces of here and there, inside and outside, in front of and behind" (PDF p.16 / printed p.1) — the definition of the window as aperture; sentence continues in the source: The window opens onto a three-dimensional world beyond. The review's first line of definition for the dome as an enclosing hatch.
  • "The window reduces the outside to a two-dimensional surface; the window becomes a screen. Like the window, the screen is at once a surface and a frame-a reflective plane onto which an image is cast and a frame that limits its view." (PDF p.16 / printed p.1) — the shift from window-as-opening to screen-as-surface, and the screen as simultaneously surface and frame. The single most useful sentence in the book for a dome argument.
  • "In his 1435 treatise on painting and perspective, De pictura, Leon Battista Alberti-painter, architect, scholar of antiquity, quintessential Renaissance man-famously instructed the painter to "regard" the rectangular frame of the painting as an open window (aperta finestra)." (PDF p.16 / printed p.1) — the origin point of the whole frame tradition; note the hyphens for em dashes are the extraction's.
  • "The computer "window" shifts its metaphoric hold from the singular frame of perspective to the multiplicity of windows within windows, frames within frames, screens within screens." (PDF pp.16–17 / printed pp.1–2) — the multiple-screen/multi-frame desktop; this sentence crosses the page break, beginning on PDF p.16.
  • "Above, below, ahead, and behind are simultaneous on the computer display, where each element in composition is seen separately with no systematic spatial relationship between them." (PDF p.17 / printed p.2) — the end of perspectival space on the desktop; the claim a dome work that offers one centred image should be measured against.
  • "Like the frame of the architectural window and the frame of the painting, the frame of the moving-image screen marks a separation-an "ontological cut"-between the material surface of the wall and the view contained within its aperture." (PDF p.20 / printed p.5) — the frame as separation, not decoration. Grounds any claim that a projected image in a dome is still framed (by the dome's own edge, by the seam, by the mask).
  • "As the beholders of multiscreen "windows," we now receive images-still and moving, large and small, artistic and commercial-in spatially and temporally fractured frames. This new space of mediated vision is post-Cartesian, postperspectival, postcinematic, and posttelevisual, and yet remains within the delimited bounds of a frame and seen on a screen." (PDF p.22 / printed p.7) — the essential correction to "immersive": even fractured, multiplied, postcinematic vision is still "within the delimited bounds of a frame and seen on a screen."
  • "Framed images organize and structure perception and cognition in a very different way." (PDF p.26 / printed p.11) — one sentence, the frame vs immersion crux; in context it is the distinction she draws between Grau's immersive "spaces of illusion" and framed pictures.
  • "Instead of describing the use of architecture within the filmic or televisual image, the following account will consider the screen as architecture, as an expansion of material built space through the "virtual window" of the film, television, or computer screen." (PDF p.166 / printed p.151) — the method statement for treating a projection surface as a building element; directly transferable to the dome as architecture.
  • "The film screen is a surface, a picture plane caught in a cone of light, dark and empty until projected images are caught on its veneer. Despite variations in theater architecture and films projected, what remains-constant and haunting-is the screen." (PDF p.181 / printed p.166) — the surface/materiality of the screen against the ephemerality of what is projected on it; useful against accounts that treat the image as the only object of study.
  • "These plans-whether with multiple mobile projectors and a spectator encircled by the screen, or a theater with walls, ceiling, and an adjustable main screen as projection surfaces-were designed to abolish the proscenium frame and allow the spectator to be lost in the imaginary space of the screen: not in distraction but immersion." (PDF p.185 / printed p.170) — the historical statement of the dome programme, written about 1930s cinema projects (Pilichowski, Kiesler): encircling screen, multiple mobile projectors, spectator lost in the image. The direct precedent for fulldome as an immersive architecture.
  • "Just as the chemically based, analog images of photography have been replaced by digital images, the "cinema" has been displaced by systems of circulation and transmission that abolish the projection screen and link the screens of the computer and television with the dialogic interactivity of the telephone." (PDF p.257 / printed p.242) — the book's closing position on the projection screen's displacement; the sentence a dome argument has to answer, since the dome reinstates a single large projection surface rather than abolishing it.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the review's theoretical backbone for the frame/screen argument and the best available vocabulary for separating a lecture-with-images from a genuinely spatial form. It gives: the window as the origin-figure of the framed image; the frame as an "ontological cut" rather than a neutral border; the screen as architecture rather than as a delivery surface for content; the multiple/fractured screen as the vernacular of the postcinematic desktop; and the immersion-vs-frame opposition, with the 1930s encircling-screen projects as the dome's direct ancestors. It can carry the argument that the dome is a frame-legible architecture, not a frame-abolishing one, and that "virtual" is an ontological register rather than a digital effect. It cannot carry spectatorship data, dome-specific production practice, or practice-based research method — and it never mentions planetaria.

Limits

Predates the named field: no occurrence of the terms fulldome, dome master or fisheye, and no planetarium discussion — the immersive tradition is handled at the level of panorama, cinéorama and IMAX. The final chapter stops at Microsoft Windows and the early 2000s (postlogue dated 2005); nothing on VR headsets, real-time rendering in performance, or fulldome cinema. Method is historical and theoretical exegesis; there is no fieldwork, no audience, no practitioner interview, and no practice-based research model. The frame/immersion opposition is asserted more than tested, and the author's own position (the frame is inescapable) is arguably a frame for reading the dome rather than a finding about it. Finally, the text layer is dirty enough that no quote from the "Lens" interludes or the Conclusion's plates can be verified — OCR errors are frequent and a few passages are unusable.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_The_Language_of_New_Media_Lev_Manovich_pdf — the new-media complement: the five principles, the database/narrative opposition and navigable space give the form argument Friedberg's screen history sets up.
  • Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf — the immersive-spaces history Friedberg cites at PDF p.26 and deliberately separates from the framed tradition.
  • Books_Digital_Performance_Steve_Dixon_pdf — projection and technology in live performance; supplies the performance history and the interactivity taxonomy Friedberg's book omits.
  • Books_Immersed_in_Technology_Moser_and_MacLeod_pdf — the art/VR precursor volume; the immersion-side literature.
  • Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf — the media-relations vocabulary for the intermedial claim Friedberg implies but never makes about theatre.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Fulldome_Immersive_Modalities_and_Materiality_Toward_A_Poetics_of_the_Fi — applies the frame/immersion question to fulldome specifically.

4. Performance, theatre and the mediated body

The performance studies the dome literature does not have. Schlemmer is the historical origin the fulldome manifesto chapter itself reaches for: the theatre whose materials are form, colour and the constructive fusion of space and building, and whose subject is the transfiguration of the human form — the Bauhaus ancestor of treating an entire constructed space as the instrument of a performance. Dixon is the standard history of digital performance and the place to cite for the definition of the term and for the theatre-versus-technology arguments; note that the file in this folder is a preview containing only chapter 1 and the bibliography, so his chapters on scenography, VR and interactivity are cited from the contents page, not read. Bay-Cheng gives the working vocabulary of intermediality — its separation from multimedia, its link to hypermediacy rather than transparency, the projection designer as a new theatrical role, and spectatorship as a self-reflective act. Fischer-Lichte gives the smallest usable unit: performance as an event constituted by the bodily co-presence of performers and spectators, generated by an autopoietic feedback loop neither side controls.

Oskar Schlemmer, "Man and Art Figure"

Citation

Coverage

Single essay plus plates — not the whole volume. 33 PDF pages, ~3,400 words of text. Text pages: Schlemmer's essay "Man and Art Figure" (PDF pp.2, 4–19 = printed pp.17–32), including a fold-out-style diagram, "SCHEME FOR STAGE, CULT, AND POPULAR ENTERTAINMENT ACCORDING TO …" (PDF p.6), and the translator's footnotes on Hoffmann and Kleist and on Gordon Craig (PDF p.15). Plate/production pages (PDF pp.20–33 = printed pp.33–46): "The Triadic Ballet" programme note, "The Figural Cabinet I" and "II", "Circus", sets for "Meta or the Pantomime of Places" and for Hindemith–Kokoschka's Mörder, Hoffnung der Frauen, closing with "Variations on a mask. OCR condition — quotes are word-accurate but the scan is damaged. This PDF has no text layer; the text was OCR'd from an image-only scan, so spaces inside and between words are dropped and some bracket punctuation is wrong. I have restored spacing and rejoined hyphenated line breaks only; no word or letter has been changed (so "naivete", "Ubermarionette", "Bihne" appear as the scan/OCR gives them). Beyond spacing, text is missing or dropped in several places: - PDF p.5 (printed 18): the section's opening sentence is cut off mid-clause ("The theater, which should be the image of our time and perhaps the one" → jumps), and a later sentence clearly has a dropped span ("Neither … tion abstracted from the natural and directing its effect at the human being"). Nothing is quoted from those gaps. - PDF p.6: the stage diagram survives only as scattered single words (cult / theater / arena / "picture frame" / commedia dell'arte etc.) — usable as a taxonomy, not as prose. - PDF p.9 (printed 22): "Color and form reveal their elementary values within the constructive" breaks off; a span is missing before "In painting and sculpture…". Same page, "Man, the animated being, would be banned from" / "at the central switchboard" — the middle of the sentence is gone. - PDF p.10 (printed 23): "This happens in the theater of illusionistic realism." followed by "This happens on the abstract stage." — a line has dropped between them. - PDF pp.11 and 17, and 20, 22–26, 28, 30, 32: no OCR text (sketch and plate pages). Continuity of the argument is unbroken across PDF p.17, so it is a plate page, not a missing text page. - PDF p.16 (printed 29): dropped spans around the variable-scale passage and in the list of oppositions ("… sensical, from the sublime to the comic"). - PDF p.18 (printed 31): dropped span where the text turns from religion to society.

Argument in brief

Schlemmer writes the history of theatre as the history of the transfiguration of the human form, and locates the arena of that transfiguration in the architect's constructive fusion of space and building — so the performance is made by three sets of materials: form and colour (painter/sculptor), the body (actor/dancer), word and sound (author). He sorts the stage first by its position between religious cult and popular entertainment, then by its material of address, giving three stage forms — sound, play and visual — each with a representative (author, actor, designer) and a universal regisseur who weights them against each other "with mathematical precision". The essay's central problem is the collision of two orders of law: the invisible planimetric/stereometric network of cubical stage space versus the organic laws of the human body. Schlemmer resolves it temporarily in "Man as Dancer" (Tanzermensch), who obeys both laws at once and is the medium of transition into the theatre; and he then surveys the means of transforming the body — costume, costume geometrics, mechanisation — ending with the Kunstfigur (automaton, marionette) as the figure that escapes gravity and allows any movement, any scale, any duration. The essay closes on the artist's three options in a theatre that has lost the feeling for play, and on the spectator as the last condition of realisation.

Key ideas

  • Transfiguration of the human form — the object of theatre history; man as actor of physical and spiritual events, ranging "from naivete to reflection, from naturalness to artifice."
  • The arena — "the constructive fusion of space and building, the realm of the architect": not a backdrop but one of the three elementary materials of performance, alongside form/colour and word/sound.
  • Emblems of our time — abstraction (which generalises into "a new totality") and mechanisation ("everything which can be mechanized is mechanized"), plus the new potentials of technology and invention.
  • Three stage forms and their representatives — the oral or sound stage (Sprech- oder Tonbühne) for a literary or musical event, the play stage (Spielbühne) for a physical-mimetic event, the visual stage (Schaubühne) for an optical event; their representatives are the author, the actor, the designer.
  • Universal regisseur / director — the one who combines the forms by weight distribution, perfected "with mathematical precision".
  • The absolute visual stage — theoretically, kaleidoscopic, infinitely variable yet strictly organised play of form, colour and transformable architectonic structures, with Man banned from it and seated "at the central switchboard" to direct a feast for the eyes: the spectacle pole of the essay.
  • Man and Space, "whose shall prevail?" — either abstract space is bent back into the imitation of nature (the theatre of illusionistic realism) or the stage is left abstract.
  • Two sets of laws — the laws of cubical space (invisible linear network of planimetric and stereometric relationships; the geometry of calisthenics, eurhythmics and gymnastics) versus the laws of organic man (heartbeat, circulation, respiration; movements that create an imaginary space, so cubical space becomes only the "horizontal and vertical framework" for the flow).
  • Man as Dancer (Tanzermensch) — obeys the law of the body and the law of space; works on the bare stage or in a scenic environment constructed for him; the medium of transition into theatre.
  • Costume and mask — the agencies of metamorphosis, distinguishing genuine stage costume (Harlequin, Pierrot, Columbine) from "native costume". Schlemmer's four decisive transformations produce: ambulant architecture (laws of cubical space transferred to the body), the marionette (functional body laws), a technical organism (laws of motion — spinning top, snail, spiral, disk), and dematerialisation (metaphysical forms of expression).
  • The Kunstfigur — the mechanical human figure (automaton, marionette) that replaces the organism; carries Schlemmer's citations of Hoffmann, Kleist, Gordon Craig's Übermarionette and Brjusov's "mechanized dolls", and permits any movement, any position, any duration and any relative scale.
  • Paper and model — the option of isolating oneself from the existing theatre; the projects then remain "materials for demonstration lectures and exhibitions of theater art", their realisation deferred to "the new theater of glass, metal, and the inventions of tomorrow".

Quotes

  • "The history of the theater is the history of the transfiguration of the human form. It is the history of man as the actor of physical and spiritual events, ranging from naivete to reflection, from naturalness to artifice. The materials involved in this transfiguration are form and color, the materials of the painter and sculptor. The arena for this transfiguration is found in the constructive fusion of space and building, the realm of the architect. Through the manipulation of these materials the role of the artist, the synthesizer of these elements, is determined." (PDF p.4 / printed p.17) — the thesis line and the ancestor claim: space and building are one of the materials of performance, and the architect is a performance author. Note: the first two paragraphs are run together here as one continuous extraction passage.
  • "One of the emblems of our time is abstraction. It functions, on the one hand, to disconnect components from an existing and persisting whole, either to lead them individually ad absurdum or to elevate them to their greatest potential. On the other hand, abstraction can result in generalization and summation, in the construction in bold outline of a new totality." (PDF p.4 / printed p.17) — the period diagnosis behind both abstraction in dome imagery and the "new totality" of an enclosure.
  • "A further emblem of our time is mechanization, the inexorable process which now lays claim to every sphere of life and art. Everything which can be mechanized is mechanized. The result: our recognition of that which cannot be mechanized." (PDF p.4 / printed p.17) — mechanisation as period emblem, with the residual human remainder as its by-product.
  • "This confrontation of passive spectator and animate actor preconditions also the form of the stage, at its most monumental as the antique arena and at its most primitive as the scaffold in the market place. The need for concentration resulted in the peep show or "picture frame," today the "universal" form of the stage. The term theater designates the most basic nature of the stage: make-believe, mummery, metamorphosis." (PDF p.5 / printed p.18) — spectator/actor polarity as the generator of stage form, with arena, scaffold and picture frame as its historical shapes: directly citable for the dome as an arena form against the framed screen.
  • "Each of these stage forms has its corresponding representative, thus: the author (as writer or composer) who is the creator of the word or musical sound; the actor whose body and its movements make him the player; the designer who is the builder of form and color." (PDF p.7 / printed p.20) — the three-way division of labour (word / body / form-colour) that a dome production redistributes.
  • "Yet when the word is silent, when the body alone is articulate and its play is on exhibition — as a dancer's is — then it is free and is its own lawgiver." (PDF p.7 / printed p.20) — the autonomy of non-verbal performance: the warrant for treating movement, not text, as the primary material.
  • "Man, the human organism, stands in the cubical, abstract space of the stage. Man and Space. Each has different laws of order. Whose shall prevail?" (PDF p.9 / printed p.22) — the essay's central question stated as a bare opposition; the sentence a fulldome paper can put at the top of a section.
  • "Invisibly involved with all these laws is Man as Dancer (Tanzermensch). He obeys the law of the body as well as the law of space; he follows his sense of himself as well as his sense of embracing space. As the one who gives birth to an almost endless range of expression, whether in free abstract movement or in symbolic pantomime, whether he is on the bare stage or in a scenic environment constructed for him, whether he speaks or sings, whether he is naked or costumed, the Tanzermensch is the medium of transition into the great world of the theater (das grosse theatralische Geschehen)." (PDF p.12 / printed p.25) — the fullest statement of the body-in-constructed-space relation, and of the performer as the medium of transition between the two orders of law.
  • "The endeavor to free man from his physical bondage and to heighten his freedom of movement beyond his native potential resulted in substituting for the organism the mechanical human figure (Kunstfigur): the automaton and the marionette." (PDF p.15 / printed p.28) — the man/figure distinction in Schlemmer's own terms; the line that Kleist, Craig and later puppetry/mocap arguments get attached to.
  • "Or he may isolate himself altogether from the existing theater and cast his anchor far out into the sea of fantasy and distant possibilities. In this case his projects remain paper and model, materials for demonstration lectures" (PDF p.18 / printed p.31) — the sentence continues over the page break with "and exhibitions of theater art." Highly relevant here: unrealisable, model-stage work is explicitly designated as lecture material — the performance-lecture lineage stated by Schlemmer himself.

(Also on PDF p.19 / printed p.32, not quoted above: "This realization will come with the construction of the new theater of glass, metal, and the inventions of tomorrow", and the closing condition that the Utopia depends "upon the inner transformation of the spectator — Man as alpha and omega of every artistic creation".)

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the origin text for the claim that a constructed space, not a picture, is the instrument of the performance: Schlemmer assigns the arena to the architect as one of his three elementary materials and makes the performer the medium who obeys the law of the body and the law of space simultaneously. It can carry (a) the historical argument that immersive/arena staging is a recurring answer to the spectator-actor relation, (b) the acting/dance distinction that legitimises non-verbal work in a dome, and (c) the explicit sanction for unrealised stage projects being presented as demonstration lectures — which is exactly the genre the researcher is studying. It cannot carry: any planetarium/dome, projection, screen or digital-image argument — the word projection here means optical stage effects, and the technology Schlemmer imagines is glass, metal and machinery.

Limits

  • Predates everything in the dome lineage: no projection, no screen, no lens, no planetarium, no computation; "visual stage" means coloured forms in motion under illumination.
  • Written as Bauhaus programme, 1925 (English 1961); the utopian register and the "boldest fantasies" of technology are period rhetoric and should be cited as such.
  • The prescriptions are strongly anti-psychological and anti-illusionistic: the Kunstfigur argument explicitly proposes replacing the actor's body, which a participatory/interactive fulldome argument has to argue against rather than inherit.
  • Text-quality caveats: this copy is OCR of an image-only scan with dropped spaces throughout and genuine missing spans (PDF pp.5, 9, 10, 16, 18) and no front/back matter, so no publisher/date can be cited from it. Quotes above are word-accurate against the scan, but the scan itself should be checked before publication; the p.6 diagram and the plate pages carry no usable prose.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf — the modern version of Schlemmer's arena/immersion lineage, with the art-history bridge between them.
  • Books_The_Virtual_Window_pdf — the "picture frame" option in Schlemmer's p.5 opposition, treated at length (window vs. immersion).
  • Books_The_Society_of_the_Spectacle_Guy_Debord_pdf — Schlemmer's "passive spectator" becomes Debord's constitutive separation.
  • Books_Digital_Performance_Steve_Dixon_pdf — the body/technology substitution (the Kunstfigur line) updated as digital performance.
  • Books_Artificial_Hells_Claire_Bishop_pdf — the participatory counter-argument to Schlemmer's Ban-the-human-from-the-abstract-stage position.
  • Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf, Books_Immersed_in_Technology_Moser_and_MacLeod_pdf, Books_Phenomenology_of_Perception_Maurice_Merleau_Ponty_pdf — the body-in-space and intermedial staging literature the Tanzermensch passage plugs into.
  • Fulldome_Articles_005_Towards_a_Fulldome_Manifesto_final_proof_pdf — the current field's own manifesto writing, which can be read against Schlemmer's Bauhaus manifesto form.

Steve Dixon, Digital Performance: A History of New Media in Theater, Dance, Performance Art, and Installation (2007)

Citation

Dixon, Steve. Digital Performance: A History of New Media in Theater, Dance, Performance Art, and Installation. Leonardo series. Cambridge, MA: MIT Press, 2007. [title page and copyright page of the PDF: "© 2007 Massachusetts Institute of Technology"; the series list on PDF p.3 places the book in the Leonardo series, Roger F. Malina series editor, Sean Cubitt editor-in-chief, and lists it as the 2007 entry, alongside Grau's Virtual Art: From Illusion to Immersion, 2003.] Original PDF filename Books_Digital_Performance_Steve_Dixon_pdf.pdf — an MIT Press online "bookpreview-pdf"; every page carries the watermark "Downloaded from http://direct.mit.edu/books/monograph/1945/bookpreview-pdf/2425316 by guest on 30 August 2025".

Coverage

Book (preview only — front matter, chapter 1, and bibliography), 94 PDF pages: - Front matter, PDF pp.1–19: title page, Leonardo series list, copyright, Contents (PDF pp.6–7), Series Foreword, Preface (printed ix–xii), Acknowledgments (printed xiii–xvi). - Chapter 1, "Introduction" — complete, PDF pp.20–53 = printed pp.1–34, text running continuously page to page (printed p.15 = PDF p.34 is a figure plate with captions only, which is why one sentence of chapter 14's description breaks across it). - The complete Bibliography — PDF pp.54–93 = printed pp.737–776 (folios 745, 751, 766 and 775 visible on the pages). - Absent: chapters 2–26 and all of the Notes and Index. The Contents pages (PDF pp.6–7) list every chapter title, but none of that text is in the file: no ch.2 genealogy of digital performance, ch.4 multimedia theater 1911–1959, ch.6 liveness, ch.10 virtual bodies, ch.14 digital theater and scenic spectacle, ch.15 VR and immersion, ch.16 liquid architectures and site-specific fractures, ch.17 telematics or ch.23 performing interactivity. The Notes (printed pp.671–736) are missing, so the introduction's numbered references cannot be resolved. - Extraction: clean. Type is good; the only artefacts are the "fi" ligature glyph (deﬁne, ﬁeld, ﬁlm) and running heads/folios interleaved into the text stream. Quotable. - Warning for a dome review: the words dome, planetarium and panorama occur nowhere in the extracted text, and nor do the phrases for the flesh/virtual-body opposition used elsewhere in the literature — those arguments sit in the chapters this preview does not include.

Argument in brief

Dixon's book is a survey history and taxonomy of performance that uses computer technology centrally rather than decoratively, built as reference rather than thesis: histories (avant-garde genealogy, film-theater, art-and-technology since 1960), theories and contexts (liveness, postmodernism/posthumanism, the digital revolution, digital dance software), then sections on the body, space, time and interactivity. Chapter 1 lays out the definitions and the book's stance: digital performance is additive, it extends rather than replaces theater, and it is an "emergent avant-garde" rather than one more symptom of postmodernism. Dixon explicitly attacks the tendency (naming Manovich) to fetishise the technology and to romanticise or demonise it, and insists on assessing content, dramaturgy, and audience-performer relations first. He also documents the field's internal argument — Grotowski's "poor theatre" and the critics of Monsters of Grace on one side, the practitioners of projection, telematics and VR on the other — and treats that argument as the field's recurring structure rather than a settled question.

Key ideas

  • digital performance — defined maximally: any performance where computer technology plays a key role rather than a subsidiary one in content, technique, aesthetics or delivery. The definition explicitly includes digitally created or manipulated projections inside live theater (PDF p.22).
  • via positiva vs via negativa — Dixon's structural contrast between Grotowski's ascetic stripping-away of theater and digital performance as an additive process: extra technologies, effects, interactions, prostheses, bodies (PDF pp.46–47).
  • "the solely live" — the position (Grotowski, Peter Brook) that theater's essence and its competitive advantage is the actor–spectator communion in the room; in Dixon this is a position to be reported and tested, not adopted (PDF pp.44–48).
  • Interactive paradigms ranked by openness — Navigation, Participation, Conversation, Collaboration, "in ascending order in relation to the openness of the system and the consequent level/depth of user interaction" (PDF p.39).
  • Digital dance / motion sensing — custom-built artist software (Isadora, EyeCon) and motion tracking driving real-time sound and image; Life Forms and Character Studio for choreography; Riverbed's work with Cunningham and Bill T. Jones as the high point of the virtual body (PDF pp.20, 31).
  • Cyborgic performance / the digital double — the book's body section: virtual bodies, the four categories of double (reﬂection, alter-ego, spiritual emanation, manipulable mannequin), robots, cyborgs, framed through a camp sensibility and the fear/fascination of mechanical embodiment (PDF pp.32–34).
  • Telematics — networked performance linking remote performers in real time, from 1970s satellite and telex experiments and Hole-in-Space (1980) to 1990s telematic theater (PDF pp.36–37).
  • emergent avant-garde — Dixon's polemical label: digital performance has begun but not completed the historical avant-garde's demand that art transform social life; it is therefore outside dominant postmodern paradigms (PDF pp.26–27).
  • Bibliography as a resource — the included bibliography (printed pp.737–776) names the adjacent literature a dome review needs: Reaney, "The Theatre of Virtual Reality: Designing Scenery in an Imaginary World" (1992) and "Virtual Reality and the Theatre: Immersion in Virtual Worlds" (1999); Char Davies, "Osmose: Notes on Being in Immersive Virtual Space" (1998); Grau, Virtual Art: From Illusion to Immersion (2003); Gromala and Sharir, "Dancing with the Virtual Dervish" (1996).

Quotes

  • "We deﬁne the term “digital performance” broadly to include all performance works where computer technologies play a key role rather than a subsidiary one in content, techniques, aesthetics, or delivery forms." (PDF p.22 / printed p.3) — the definition; state it and argue with it.
  • "This includes live theater, dance, and performance art that incorporates projections that have been digitally created or manipulated; robotic and virtual reality performances; installations and theatrical works that use computer sensing/ activating equipment or telematic techniques" (PDF p.22 / printed p.3) — projection in live performance sits inside Dixon’s definition, not beside it.
  • "immersive and kinetic theater scenography. Three companies are highlighted to demonstrate different technical and artistic approaches: the deep-perspective, illusory effects of George Coates Performance Works; the inventive visual eclecticism of Robert Lepage’s Ex Machina company; and the post-Brechtian aesthetics of The Builders Association" (PDF p.35 / printed p.16) — the sentence describing his own scenography chapter (giant projection screens making immersive, kinetic space).
  • "Chapter 15 provides a detailed discussion of a ﬁeld of computer technology that would seem to offer theater and performance unique and compelling possibilities, but where surprisingly few, though notable experiments have so far materialized: “Virtual Reality.”" (PDF p.35 / printed p.16) — the closest thing in this preview to a statement about a dome-like apparatus.
  • "categories of interactive art and performance ranked in ascending order in relation to the openness of the system and the consequent level/depth of user interaction—Navigation, Participation, Conversation, and Collaboration" (PDF p.39 / printed p.20) — his grading of interactive work: navigation, participation, conversation, collaboration.
  • "Opponents ﬁercely contest that there is a mismatch of media and a corruption of theater’s purity as a live form" (PDF p.45 / printed p.26) — the charge any projected or dome production has to answer.
  • "No matter how much theatre expands and exploits its mechanical resources, it will remain technically inferior to ﬁlm and television. Consequently I propose poverty in the theatre." (PDF p.46 / printed p.27) — the anti-technology position, quoted at length by Dixon.
  • "digital performance is by deﬁnition an additive process. New technology is added to performance, a new ingredient that is delicious for some but unpalatable for others." (PDF p.47 / printed p.28) — one-line answer to the purity charge.
  • "The real-time linking of performers working in remote locations has been one of the most popular uses of the Internet for live performance" (PDF p.36 / printed p.17) — the lineage of multi-site, distributed live performance.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the review's anchor for the history of projection and technology in live performance: it supplies the definition of digitally-mediated performance, the additive/via positiva frame, the taxonomy of interactivity, the telematics lineage, and the standing objections (Grotowski, the Monsters of Grace critics) that a dome production inherits. It can carry the argument that the dome is a scenographic and immersive apparatus in a century-long line of film-theater, projection and VR experiments, and that this line has repeatedly failed on content rather than technique. It cannot carry anything specific about domes, planetariums or panoramic architecture, and it gives no methodology for practice-based research.

Limits

The file is a preview: chapters 2–26 are absent, so nothing on liveness (ch.6), digital scenography (ch.14), VR and immersion (ch.15), site and liquid architecture (ch.16) or interactivity (ch.23) can be quoted — only chapter 1's summary of them. No dome, planetarium or panorama term occurs anywhere in the extracted text. Notes are missing, so the introduction's citations cannot be followed. Dixon's survey stops around 2005–6 and predates fulldome as a named field; its unit of analysis is the individual artwork or company, not a venue or an audience population. The book has no first-person or practice-based research method — it is art history written about practitioners, not by one.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf — the same Leonardo series, and the standard history of immersion and the panorama that Dixon's Space section assumes.
  • Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf — the theory counterpart: the media-relations vocabulary Dixon's survey works without.
  • Books_The_Language_of_New_Media_Lev_Manovich_pdf — the position Dixon singles out to dispute in chapter 1 ("technology for technology’s sake" — the avant-garde as software).
  • Books_Immersed_in_Technology_Moser_and_MacLeod_pdf — also in the Leonardo series (1996); the art-and-VR precursor volume Dixon's bibliography draws on (Gromala/Sharir, Dancing with the Virtual Dervish).
  • Books_Capitalist_Realism_Mark_Fisher_pdf — the political frame for why an immersive cultural form so easily reads as an accommodation rather than a critique.

Sarah Bay-Cheng, Chiel Kattenbelt, Andy Lavender and Robin Nelson (eds.), Mapping Intermediality in Performance (2010)

Citation

Bay-Cheng, Sarah, Chiel Kattenbelt, Andy Lavender, and Robin Nelson, eds. Mapping Intermediality in Performance. MediaMatters series. Amsterdam: Amsterdam University Press, 2010. [title page and copyright page of the PDF: "© S. Bay-Cheng, C. Kattenbelt, A. Lavender, R. Nelson / Amsterdam University Press, Amsterdam, 2010"; ISBN 978 90 8964 255 4, e-ISBN 978 90 4851 314 7; cover illustration credited as "Participant in an immersive performance by CREW".] Original PDF filename Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf.pdf. The volume is the successor to Chapple and Kattenbelt (eds.), Intermediality in Theatre and Performance (2006), and grew out of the IFTR Intermediality, Theatre & Performance research group.

Coverage

Book (complete), 306 PDF pages, ~109,800 extracted words. All front matter, the introduction ("Prospective Mapping and Network of Terms"), the five portals they announce (performativity and corporeal literacy; time and space; digital culture and posthumanism; networking; pedagogic praxis), the retrospection on the historical avant-garde, the endnotes (PDF pp.261–283), the contributor biographies (PDF pp.284–292) and the index (PDF pp.293–305) are present. Page arithmetic is a constant offset of 1 (PDF p.30 = printed p.29; PDF p.219 = printed p.218), so printed pages given below are derived from the surviving folios. Extraction: good, with structural artefacts. The prose is clean; what survives badly is the book's apparatus. The in-text cross-reference markers, printed as arrows and icons, are mangled into stray quotation marks and brackets, so a term link extracts as a bracket, a quotation mark, the word term, a colon and the term name (for example ( term: presence)); a portal link as portal: corporeal literacy; an instance link as instance: Granular Synthesis; a prospective-mapping link as prospective mapping. Running heads and footers interleave with body text (mapping intermediality in performance, portal: performativity and corporeal literacy, instances). Headings are run into the following paragraph, so a section title and its first sentence appear on one line. No garbling of words was found, and no mention of domes or planetariums occurs anywhere in the text; the nearest material is CREW/EUX omnidirectional video (pp.70–72) and the index entry "panoramic video".

Argument in brief

This is not a single-argument book but an edited map: a network of nodes (modes of experience; dimensions; actuality–virtuality; interrelations), each illustrated by a cluster of terms and by instances (short reports on specific productions), several written by the practitioners themselves. Its working definition, taken from Kattenbelt, is that intermediality is not the co-existence of media but an interplay that produces both a redefinition of the media involved and a resensibilisation of the senses; multimediality (media side by side) and transmediality (transposition of one art form into another) are distinguished from it. The claims that bind the volume: theatre is a hypermedium that stages other media, and intermediality is best understood as a mode of performativity; media relations are ultimately matters of perception and interpretation by observers; the intermedial stage troubles the standard definition of theatre as corporeal co-presence in the here and now; liveness must therefore be re-thought (Auslander) while the live remains the site where intermedial provocation becomes possible; and permanent-media integration is not synthesis — the book is explicitly against Gesamtkunstwerk fusion and for separation, discrepancy and the audience’s labour of negotiation.

Key ideas

  • Intermediality (Kattenbelt's definition) — co-relations between media "that result in a redefinition of the media that are influencing each other, which in turn leads to a fresh perception" (PDF p.20); the operative pair of terms throughout the book is redefinition of media + resensibilisation of the senses.
  • Multimediality / transmediality / intermediality — a three-way distinction (endnote 61, PDF p.265) that is the book's explicit answer to the conflation of intermediality with multimedia.
  • Jensen’s three levels — intermediality as combination of material vehicles (level one, “sometimes called multimedia”), as communication through several sensory modalities at once (level two), and as interrelations between media as social institutions — convergence, conglomeration (level three); the book works at all three (PDF p.17).
  • Theatre as hypermedium — theatre's distinctive capacity to stage other media without itself becoming them; “the other media become ‘signs of signs’ as opposed to ‘signs of objects’” (PDF p.20).
  • Hypermediacy vs immediacy (Bolter and Grusin) — intermediality is aligned with hypermediacy (diversity, discrepancy) against immediacy (unity, harmony, media transparency) — the anti-Gesamtkunstwerk argument (PDF p.36).
  • Separation — the modernist condition in which elements are held apart: “Separation is inextricably linked with intermediality, since the intermedial is only conceivable if two or more inherently fused elements (media) are also seen to be separable from each other” (PDF p.189).
  • Recursion / remediation — recursion as "the operating logic of remediation, the ongoing refunctioning of media" (PDF p.189); remediation as the refunctioning of an older medium inside a new one, e.g. photography inside digital media in The Builders Association's Super Vision (PDF p.153).
  • Presence, in three registers — classical presence (co-presence in time and space), virtual (digital, mediatised) presence, and intermedial presence as the "both-and" intertwining of the two (PDF p.65); telepresence as temporal rather than spatial proximity (PDF p.47).
  • The immersant / omnidirectional video (CREW, EUX) — the book's one sustained account of an immersive apparatus: head-mounted surround video in which the spectator becomes a user or "immersant" and the virtual space coincides with the embodied space of the self (PDF pp.70–72).
  • Experiencer / corporeal literacy — the preferred term for the audience member, chosen to mark that perception is something performed and learned, with several sensory modalities at work at once (PDF pp.39, 219).
  • Practice as research — flagged as the UK route by which practitioner-researchers have produced first-person, non-traditional accounts inside the volume (PDF p.11).

Quotes

  • "My aim is to emphasise the performativity of intermediality by arguing that intermediality is very much about the staging (in the sense of conscious self-presentation to another) of media, for which theatre as a hypermedium provides pre-eminently a stage (see Kattenbelt 2006)." (PDF p.30 / printed p.29) — Kattenbelt’s definition in his own words.
  • "Following Chiel Kattenbelt, we define intermediality as performance and performative practices in which media not only exist next to each other, but through their interplay result in both a redefinition of media and resensibilisation of the senses (2008, 25)." (PDF p.219 / printed p.218) — the volume’s operative definition.
  • "intermediality is more closely connected to the idea of hypermediacy (diversity, discrepancy) than immediacy (unity, harmony, and media transparency) of Bolter and Grusin (1999). Intermediality thus conceived assumes interrelations in terms of mutual affects." (PDF p.36 / printed p.35) — the anti-fusion, anti-transparency line.
  • "Kattenbelt distinguishes between multimediality, transmediality and intermediality, employing the concept of intermediality exclusively to denote the interplay between media within a performance that results in a redefinition of media and resensibilisation of the senses (2008)." (PDF p.265 / printed p.264) — the explicit separation of intermediality from multimediality and transmediality.
  • "While, historically, theatre audiences observed a constructed world in actual space, immersive environments, actual and virtual, allow us both to see space and move into and through it. In respect of virtual environments, however, the space is not already there, as it is in theatre buildings and actual site-specific environments, but is created in the process of our moving through, and playing within, it." (PDF p.20 / printed p.19) — the book’s nearest approach to dome spectatorship.
  • "The space of intermediality, in this regard, is not already there but can only be understood as a temporal, dynamic and highly complex spatial configuration, which is created within the process of the performance." (PDF p.95 / printed p.94) — space and place: the intermedial stage against site-specific theatre.
  • "We will argue that in intermedial performances spectatorship in itself becomes a self-reflective act and in this process of becoming, is able to entail a politics of spectating." (PDF p.219 / printed p.218) — the spectator’s role, stated as a thesis.
  • "Indeed, a new role within theatre is emerging, that of the projection designer, and since the screen image is often brighter, larger and more intense than the human figure on stage, some have expressed concern that mediated imagery threatens to undermine the unique liveness of performance." (PDF p.64 / printed p.63) — practitioner account of screen versus live body.
  • "ODV is a human-machine interface that leads to unprecedented levels of presence and intimacy as well as novel ways of mixing and experiencing different levels of reality. By means of a headmounted display, the spectator is afforded a surround video environment and becomes a user or an ‘immersant’." (PDF p.71 / printed p.70) — the one immersive apparatus described in the book (CREW, EUX).

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the review's theory spine for saying what a dome performance is in media terms: it supplies the definition of intermediality and its three-way separation from multimedia and transmediality, the hypermedium claim about theatre staging other media, the taxonomy of intermedial relations (separation, recursion/remediation, hypermediacy), the three-register account of presence, and the argument that the spectator of an intermedial work has to negotiate actively between live and mediatised bodies. It also gives the review a named precedent for an immersion apparatus in a performance context (CREW's omnidirectional video) and an explicit warrant for including practitioner accounts as research. It cannot supply dome or planetarium specifics, and it offers no empirical audience data.

Limits

A 2010 European theatre-studies volume: no dome, planetarium, planetarium-adjacent or fulldome material, and its immersive example is head-mounted omnidirectional video rather than a shared public image environment — so the dome's peculiar problem (one apparatus, many bodies, no headset) is not addressed. Its network form means definitions are deliberately provisional and no single author's argument is sustained across the book; several chapters are production reports rather than analysis. Its liveness argument is largely a commentary on Auslander and Phelan rather than fieldwork. Practice as research is acknowledged in one paragraph and not theorised as a method.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_Digital_Performance_Steve_Dixon_pdf — cited throughout the volume (as Dixon and Smith 2007) for the "digital double" and for the extended sense of space; the survey history this book theorises.
  • Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf — the historical account of immersion and the panorama that the intermediality map assumes but does not narrate.
  • Books_The_Language_of_New_Media_Lev_Manovich_pdf — the new-media vocabulary (transcoding, compositing) used in the time-and-space portal.
  • Books_Immersed_in_Technology_Moser_and_MacLeod_pdf — the earlier art-and-VR collection behind the book's immersion material.
  • Books_How_We_Became_Posthuman_Katherine_Hayles_pdf — Hayles's "condition of virtuality" is imported directly in the Super Vision instance (PDF p.150).
  • Books_The_Society_of_the_Spectacle_Guy_Debord_pdf — the spectacle account of the spectator that the book's "politics of spectating" argues with.

Erika Fischer-Lichte, "Culture as Performance" (2009)

Citation

Fischer-Lichte, Erika. "Culture as Performance." Modern Austrian Literature 42, no. 3, special issue: Performance (2009): 1-10. Association of Austrian Studies. JSTOR stable URL: https://www.jstor.org/stable/24649950. [JSTOR cover page, PDF p.1, and the article's first page] Original PDF filename is not recoverable from the text layer; the slug Books_Culture_as_Performance_Erika_Fischer_Lichte_pdf is the only record of it.

Coverage

Article (JSTOR PDF) - confirmed, not a book. 12 PDF pages = JSTOR cover page + printed pp.1-10 + one trailing download page. c.4,800 words. The Books_ prefix in the slug is a filing artefact of this corpus. Text layer is clean: no ligature damage, no dropped spaces. The JSTOR OCR does leave a few slips - the byline reads "Fischer-Uchte" for Fischer-Lichte, "modern" appears as "modem", "In the last years" as "fn the last years" - so those words should not be quoted as printed. Printed page = PDF page minus 1 (printed p.3 = PDF p.4).

Argument in brief

A report on the ten-year Berlin research programme Kulturen des Performativen, stated as a series of theoretical claims. Fischer-Lichte first collapses the old dichotomy in which European culture is textual and non-European culture performative: performativity and textuality are not opposites, and the metaphor of culture as text gives way to culture as performance. Performance is then defined technically - it comes into being as an autopoietic feedback loop out of the bodily co-presence of participants, so that all participants co-create a process nobody controls. From this she derives consequences for subjectivity (the autonomous subject is negated), for agency (the social sciences' manipulation thesis fails), and for method (emergence, contingency and the hiatus make hermeneutic reading insufficient). The article closes by proposing performance as a laboratory for cultural dynamics and performance research as a new Enlightenment.

Key ideas

  • performative turn / performative shift - paradigm breaks in the media conditions for communication (medieval to early modern; nineteenth century to the present) that reconfigure the relation between performativity and textuality.
  • culture as performance - the metaphor that replaces culture as text; the same forces are at work in performance as in culture at large.
  • autopoietic feedback loop - the definition of performance: a self-generating process arising from the reciprocal perception and reaction of all present.
  • bodily co-presence - the minimal condition of performance; without co-present bodies there is no performance.
  • co-creators - spectators are not recipients but co-authors of the event; all participants act as co-creators.
  • emergence / contingency / hiatus - what appears without plan or intention; the liminal moment in which a performative process can turn.
  • manipulation thesis - the social-science claim that staged events manipulate passive participants; rejected because a performance is not at anyone's disposal.
  • performance vs mise en scene - the plan (staging) is not the event (performance); a performance can never match its plan.
  • laboratory - performance as an experimental model for studying cultural dynamics, and performance research as a new Enlightenment.

Quotes

  • "Here the terms "performative shift" and "performative turn" seemed appropriate since such junctures in the conditions for communication crucially changed the relationship between performativity and textuality, therefore describing fundamental paradigmatic shifts." (PDF p.3 / printed p.2) — performative shift / performative turn as a media-historical paradigm change.
  • "Increasingly, textuality revealed itself as a sub-category of performativity. Thus the metaphor "culture as text" increasingly blended into that of "culture as performance."" (PDF p.4 / printed p.3) — 'culture as text' gives way to 'culture as performance'.
  • "It emerges out of the bodily co-presence of different groups of participants and their confrontation and interaction." (PDF p.4 / printed p.3) — bodily co-presence as the minimal condition of performance.
  • "In this sense performances are generated and determined by the actions and behavior of all participants, no matter whether they are performers or spectators. All participants act as co-creators of the performance which, in many respects, remains unpredictable and spontaneous to a certain degree." (PDF p.4 / printed p.3) — the autopoietic feedback loop: all participants co-create, none controls.
  • "For it has become clear that no individual or group of individuals/specialists will succeed in manipulating the "innocent" participants of such events according to their intention via minutely planned and calculated strategies of staging." (PDF p.6 / printed p.5) — the manipulation thesis fails: staging cannot determine its participants.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

The review's positive theoretical instrument, set against Debord. The same bodily co-presence that Debord reads as separation is here the mechanism of co-creation and unpredictability, which gives a defensible vocabulary for the dome as an event rather than an image (feedback loop, co-presence, emergence) and for the performance lecture's claim that the live encounter generates something the recording cannot. It also supplies the counter-argument to any account of immersive media as manipulation: participants are not at the disposal of the staging. What it cannot carry: nothing on mediation, recording, projection or the image - staging is explicitly set aside - and no politics, no case study, no dome.

Limits

Ten pages; an English summary of a German research programme, with the substance in Aesthetik des Performativen (2004). No examples and no analysis of any actual performance, so every claim is programmatic. Its illustrative examples are dated to 2009 (swarms, cell-phone dating services, internet communities, terror networks). The umbrella term performance is stretched to cover theatre, ritual, festival, games, political rallies and sports competitions, so performance and culture risk becoming coextensive and the concept loses its edges as an analytic tool. The emergence and future argument is speculative and explicitly quasi-futurological.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_Artificial_Hells_Claire_Bishop_pdf - Chapters 8-9 work with exactly this actor/spectator model while insisting that the non-participating secondary audience cannot be dissolved into the event.
  • Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf - how performance absorbs media without losing the live event.
  • Books_Digital_Performance_Steve_Dixon_pdf - the liveness-versus-mediation case this article's feedback loop brackets.
  • Books_The_Society_of_the_Spectacle_Guy_Debord_pdf - the opposing reading of co-presence as spectacular separation.

Gropius, Walter, and Arthur S. Wensinger (eds.), The Theater of the Bauhaus (1961; German original 1925)

Citation

Gropius, Walter, and Arthur S. Wensinger, eds. The Theater of the Bauhaus. Translated by Arthur S. Wensinger. Middletown, CT: Wesleyan University Press, 1961. ISBN 0-8195-6020-0 (Wesleyan Paperback 1971; third printing 1987). German original: Die Bühne im Bauhaus, Bauhausbücher 4, Munich: Albert Langen Verlag, 1925. — Title page, copyright page and translator's note, PDF pp.4-5, 106. Original PDF filename: Expanded_Gropius_The_Theater_of_the_Bauhaus_pdf.pdf.

Note on pages: PDF page = printed folio + 1 for the whole body of the book. Verified twice against the book's own cross-references: the contents list gives Gropius p.7 / Schlemmer p.17 / Moholy-Nagy p.49 / Molnár p.73 / Schlemmer p.81, which are PDF pp.8 / 16 / 48 / 72 / 80; and Schlemmer's footnote "(Kugeltheater, page 89)" on PDF p.89 points at the spherical theater plate on PDF p.90, while "(illust. page 78)" on PDF p.89 points at the plate on PDF p.79. What is in the file: authors, essays, proportions

The volume is four pieces by three authors (Gropius, Schlemmer, Moholy-Nagy, Molnár) plus Gropius's 1961 Total Theater statement and Wensinger's apparatus — not one text. Word share of the file (PDF pages, then % of the 17,118 words):

Attribution traps in this file: - Gropius writes two different things: the Introduction proper (PDF pp.8-11) and a separate statement on the Total Theater project (PDF pp.13-15) which is dated June 1961 and is not 1925 text. All the architectural, light-keyboard and "flat picture" argument is in the 1961 statement, not in the Introduction. The Introduction is also a 1961 retrospective (it cites Herbert Read's Icon and Idea, 1955, Fitch's Walter Gropius, 1960, the Piscator productions of 1926 and the 1935 Volta Congress). - Schlemmer writes two pieces here: the famous Man and Art Figure (already annotated from another file) and the 1927 lecture-demonstration Theater (Bühne), which is a different text with a different argument. All Schlemmer quotes below are from the 1927 lecture. - Molnár's U-Theater is not an essay but a numbered specification of a building (1. The Division of the Stage, 2. Subsidiary Parts of the Stage, 3. The Auditorium) with plates. There is no Molnár essay on utopia in this volume. The utopia material (the passage beginning "Utopia? It is indeed astonishing how little has been accomplished so far in this direction", PDF p.30, and, three pages later, the closing line that any realization is doomed to remain utopia until it finds intellectual and spiritual receptivity and response, PDF p.33 / printed pp.29 and 32) is inside Schlemmer's Man and Art Figure — i.e. in the essay already annotated from Books_oskar_schlemmer_bauhaus_man_and_art_figure_pdf. One line of it is repeated below only so that it is not mistaken for Molnár; for the full passage take it from that annotation, not from this one. - Signed plate-caption statements by other hands inside the volume: Kurt Schmidt (PDF pp.55-57), Alexander Schawinsky (PDF p.67), Heinz Loew signed "H. L." (PDF p.85), Andreas Weininger signed "A. W." (PDF p.90 - the spherical theater), plus Wensinger's translator's notes (footnotes signed "(Translator)", PDF pp.29, 51, 53, 83, 89, 93). - One plate caption is unsigned and its author is not determinable from this file: The Building as Stage (PDF p.79), which states that the modern building with its series of traversable roof areas, terraces, and verandas will make the ideal outdoor stage of the future. Schlemmer's lecture cross-refers to that plate ("illust. page 78"), but the caption itself carries no name. Not used as a quote for that reason; it is the volume's clearest statement that the building itself is the stage, and it is worth chasing in the German original.

Argument in brief

The book is Bauhausbücher 4, the Bauhaus's public argument that the stage is not a decorated box but an apparatus — a constructed space with light, sound, form, motion, film and the performer's body all holding equal rank as media. Moholy-Nagy supplies the polemic: the historical theater transmitted literature; the "theater of totality" (Gesamtbühnenaktion) is a dynamic-rhythmic organism of media, and man, once a mere interpreter, becomes one productive element among others. Gropius supplies the architectural version of the same claim: the playhouse should be a "keyboard for light and space" whose deep/proscenium/arena forms can be switched, and the "flat picture effect of the customary stage" replaced by an animated house. Schlemmer's 1927 lecture supplies the demonstration vocabulary — the stage as an architectonic-spatial organism including the auditorium, the actor as a "space-bewitched" creature once he steps onto it, and an explicit admission that the wall-behind/wings-at-the- sides arrangement is what produced the peep show. Molnár, in 644 words, turns all of this into a building specification: a stage visible from three sides (up to 270 degrees), a sliding platform at auditorium level, a suspended stage, and rings of adjustable seats. The volume is the founding document for treating a whole constructed space, rather than a picture plane, as the instrument of a performance.

Key ideas

  • Gestaltung / Bühre — the Bauhaus word for creative formation as a totality; the stage (Bühne) is a "Bau", an orchestral complex produced only by cooperation of heterogeneous forces (Schlemmer, PDF p.82).
  • Theater of Totality / TOTAL STAGE ACTION (Gesamtbühnenaktion) — Moholy's term for a dynamic-rhythmic process compressing all media into elementary form (PDF pp.61-65).
  • Stage and spectator merged — Moholy's demand that the audience stop being passive; repeated as "adjustable and rotating seats provide the best possible view of any action" (Molnár, PDF p.77) and as the stage being built out toward the audience on bridges and platforms (PDF p.69).
  • The peep-show stage — the volume's name for the frontal, rectangular stage; rejected as unsuitable for organized motion (Moholy, PDF p.69), described as the origin of theater in a wall + wings + spectator arrangement (Schlemmer, PDF p.96), and structurally overcome in Gropius's interchangeable forms and Molnár's 270-degree stage.
  • Space stage (Raumbühne) / space theater — Gropius's and Schlemmer's term for staging in the whole building; realized as a project only in the spherical theater (Kugeltheater).
  • Kugeltheater (Spherical Theater) — Andreas Weininger's dome project, plate + signed text at PDF p.90 / printed p.89: spectators on the inner wall of a sphere, "concentric, eccentric, multidirectional" space-stage phenomena; the volume's only drawn dome.
  • Light as a medium, and film/projection as part of the set — Moholy's "ACTION OF LIGHT" equal in rank to all other media; Schlemmer's flats turned into projection screens and translucent walls, lit for their own colors (PDF pp.68, 97-98).
  • Mechanized Eccentric (die mechanische Exzentrik) — Moholy's figure of a stage action concentrated to pure form, from which man was first excluded and then re-admitted "on an equal footing with the other formative media" (PDF pp.53-58).
  • Man and Art Figure (Kunstfigur) — Schlemmer's automaton/marionette figure, discussed in the separately annotated essay and quoted by Gropius in the Introduction (PDF p.9).
  • Schau-Spiel — literally "visual play"; Schlemmer's argument that the term becomes true only when space, form, color and light are comprehended as a totality (PDF p.89).
  • The book as an object — Wensinger on the 1925 original as a typographic manifesto: heavy and spaced type, capitals, dashes, exclamation points, diagrams, sketches, photos, plus Schlemmer's handwritten dance scores (PDF pp.106, 87-88) — the volume's form is part of its argument about constructed space.

Quotes

  • "From his paintings, as well as from his stage work for ballet and theater, it is apparent that he experienced space not only through mere vision but with the whole body, with the sense of touch of the dancer and the actor." (Walter Gropius, Introduction, PDF p.9 / printed p.8) — the body as the instrument that measures space; useful against purely optical accounts of immersion.
  • "I myself felt that the modern stage and theater which would do justice to these new interpretations of theatrical space was still to be created." (Walter Gropius, Introduction, PDF p.11 / printed p.10) — Gropius's own verdict that the theater of spatial performance did not yet exist; the project statement behind the whole book.
  • "The contemporary theater architect should set himself the aim to create a great keyboard for light and space, so objective and adaptable in character that it would respond to any imaginable vision of a stage director; a flexible building, capable of transforming and refreshing the mind by its spatial impact alone." (Walter Gropius, statement on the Total Theater, dated June 1961, PDF p.13 / printed p.12) — the ancestor formulation of the whole constructed space as the instrument of performance.
  • "the whole house would be animated by three-dimensional means instead of by the 'flat' picture effect of the customary stage. This would also greatly reduce the cumbersome paraphernalia of properties and painted backdrops. "Thus the playhouse itself, made to dissolve into the shifting, illusionary space of the imagination, would become the scene of action itself." (Walter Gropius, statement on the Total Theater, PDF p.15 / printed p.14) — the rectangle named as the thing to be overcome; the building itself becomes the scene. (The stray opening quotation mark is in the source, where the whole statement is quoted.)
  • "Films can also be projected onto various surfaces and further experiments in space illumination will be devised. This will constitute the new ACTION OF LIGHT, which by means of modern technology will use the most intensified contrasts to guarantee itself a position of importance equal to that of all other theater media." (László Moholy-Nagy, Theater, Circus, Variety, PDF p.68 / printed p.67) — the earliest point in the corpus where projection is written into the performance space as a medium rather than a backdrop.
  • "It is clear that the present peep-show stage is not suitable for such organized motion." (László Moholy-Nagy, Theater, Circus, Variety, PDF p.69 / printed p.68) — the concise rejection of the framed stage, three pages after the demand to abolish the separation of stage and spectator.
  • "Action on the stage proper is visible from three sides, i.e., up to 270 degrees, depending on the requirements of any given action." (Farkas Molnár, U-Theater, PDF p.74 / printed p.73) — the frame broken by specification rather than by argument.
  • "A sphere as architectonic structure in place of the customary theater. The spectators, on the inner wall of the sphere, find themselves in a new relationship to space. Because of their all-encompassing view, because of centripetal force, they find themselves in a new psychic, optical, acoustical relationship; they find themselves confronted with new possibilities for concentric, eccentric, multidirectional, mechanical space-stage phenomena." (Andreas Weininger, signed "A. W.", plate and text on the Spherical Theater, PDF p.90 / printed p.89) — the volume's dome, and the only place where the audience stands inside the architecture; OCR letterspacing in the source normalized.
  • "The art of the stage is a spatial art, a fact which is bound to become clearer and clearer in the future. The stage, including the auditorium, is above all an architectonic-spatial organism where all things happening to it and within it exist in a spatially conditioned relationship." (Oskar Schlemmer, Theater (Bühne), 1927 lecture-demonstration, PDF p.86 / printed p.85) — stage and auditorium as one organism; the strongest sentence in the volume for a dome argument.
  • "The human figure, the actor, naked or in white tights, stands in space. Before him, the receptive spectator, awaiting every motion, every action. Behind him, the security of a wall; at each side, the wings for his entrance and exit. This is the situation which any person creates who instinctively steps back from a group of two or more curious spectators in order to "act out" something for them. It is the basic situation which produced the peep show. It might even be called the origin of all theatrics." (Oskar Schlemmer, Theater (Bühne), PDF p.96 / printed p.95) — names the rectangle's conditions (wall, wings, single front) as the origin of theater, not as a neutral convention.
  • "Since, moreover, we are not concerned with imitating nature and for this reason use no painted flats or backdrops to transplant a kind of second-rate nature onto the stage - since we have no interest in make-believe forests, mountains, lakes, or rooms - we have constructed simple flats of wood and white canvas which can be slid back and forth on a series of parallel tracks and can be used as screens for light projection." (Oskar Schlemmer, Theater (Bühne), PDF p.97 / printed p.96) — planes in tracks used as projection surfaces: the rectangle converted from scenery into a screen.
  • "the visual attraction and the shock techniques of an angular, hyperelliptical, engineered, and sparse new German manifesto style: the dashes, the dots, the exclamation points; the spaced type, the heavy type, and the capitals of Moholy's typography; the staccato paragraphing; the sketches, graphs, and photos by masters and students - and now and again a singular lyric touch or unexpected metaphor." (Arthur S. Wensinger, Translator's Note, PDF p.106 / printed p.105) — the book's own typographic and diagrammatic form as part of the argument.
  • "Utopia? It is indeed astonishing how little has been accomplished so far in this direction." (Oskar Schlemmer, Man and Art Figure, PDF p.30 / printed p.29) — the volume's utopia point, quoted here only to fix its attribution: it belongs to Schlemmer's essay (annotated separately), not to Molnár's U-Theater, which contains no utopia discussion.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the pre-history of the dome argument: the point where the performance space stops being a framed picture and becomes a total apparatus, and where projection enters the space as a medium (Moholy, PDF p.68). It carries the claim that the rectangle/peep-show/proscenium is a contingent convention that the space stage sets out to overcome (Gropius PDF p.15, Moholy PDF p.69, Schlemmer PDF p.96), and it contains the corpus's earliest drawn dome — Weininger's spherical theater, spectators on the inner wall of the sphere. It also carries the body-in-space argument from the theatre side (Gropius PDF p.9; Schlemmer's floor geometry, taut wires and "space-bewitched" actor, PDF pp.93-94). What it cannot carry: it never uses the word immersion, it never describes a built dome in use, and its audience is conceived as a mass to be activated rather than as perceivers in a specific optical environment.

Limits

Nothing here was performed in a dome; the spherical theater is explicitly "a utopian project not feasible at the present" (Schlemmer's footnote, PDF p.89). There is no empirical spectator, no perception research, no account of what it feels like to be inside; the rhetoric is manifesto and specification. The word rectangle never appears — the critique is carried by "peep show", "flat picture effect", "proscenium" and "box", so a researcher looking for the term must bring it. The volume predates planetarium projection practice entirely (Moholy's projected films and Gropius's twelve screens are analogue, front-projected, and unspecified in optics). Gropius's architectural texts here are 1961 retrospective reconstructions of a 1927 project, so they are evidence of the argument's later framing, not of 1925 language. The English edition's interleaved plates and captions by other hands (Schmidt, Schawinsky, Loew, Weininger) make attribution unstable unless the plate signature is checked. Extraction quality is moderate: the dance-score pages and the Spherical Theater caption are partly garbled.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_oskar_schlemmer_bauhaus_man_and_art_figure_pdf — the same volume's central essay (printed pp.17-46 here), including the "Utopia" passage and the Kunstfigur/material law material; already annotated, do not double-count.
  • Expanded_Schlemmer_Vordruck_The_Making_of_pdf — Schlemmer's own documentation of staging and the making of the Triadic Ballet, the practical counterpart to the lecture-demonstration here.
  • Books_The_Virtual_Window_pdf and Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf — the long history of the frame/window as the alternative to the total space this volume argues for.
  • Books_The_Language_of_New_Media_Lev_Manovich_pdf — the screen/interface vocabulary that picks up where projection-as-medium (Moholy, PDF p.68) arrives in the digital era.
  • Fulldome_Articles_005_Towards_a_Fulldome_Manifesto_final_proof_pdf — the modern manifesto form of the same claim (the whole space as instrument, the audience inside the image).

Marcia Fae Feuerstein, Oskar Schlemmer's Vordruck: The Making of an Architectural Body for the Bauhaus (1998)

Citation

Feuerstein, Marcia Fae. Oskar Schlemmer's Vordruck: The Making of an Architectural Body for the Bauhaus. PhD dissertation in Architecture, University of Pennsylvania, 1998. UMI Microform 9829896, Ann Arbor. [Established from the title page ("A DISSERTATION in Architecture Presented to the Faculties of the University of Pennsylvania in Partial Fulfillment of the Requirements for the Degree of Doctor of Philosophy", 1998), the copyright page ("COPYRIGHT MARCIA FAE FEUERSTEIN / 1998") and the UMI microfilm front matter — not from a running head. Supervisor of dissertation: David Leatherbarrow; graduate group chairperson: Marco Frascari. Original PDF filename: Expanded_Schlemmer_Vordruck_The_Making_of.pdf]

Coverage

Dissertation, text complete: 243 PDF pages, 85,159 words. UMI microfilm reproduction of the University of Pennsylvania copy — UMI "INFORMATION TO USERS" licence pages, UMI microform/copyright page (9829896), dedication, acknowledgments (naming the archives and their staff), abstract (scanned three times on PDF p.8), table of contents, two lists of illustrations, Introduction, Chapter One "Vordruck Descriptive Analysis", Chapter Two "Vordruck Origins", Chapter Three "Vordruck and Tafel I", Chapter Four "Type", Chapter Five "Training and Mazdaznan", Conclusion and Afterthought, chapter-end endnotes, bibliography, index, and the UMI "IMAGE EVALUATION TEST TARGET" sheet at the back.

Two coverage facts to know before using any of it:

  • The illustrations are not in this file. Images requiring the Schlemmer family's permission and fees were omitted from the published version, and the ones that were included were "reinterpreted and/or altered by the author as interpretive illustrations" (PDF p.18); the full set exists only in the unpublished copy held by the University of Pennsylvania and by the author. Every bracketed figure reference in the argument ([1.2], [2.3], Tafel I, [3.19], [3.20]) points to an image absent from this PDF. The lists of illustrations (PDF pp.10–11) are the only inventory of them.
  • The text layer is microfilm OCR and is damaged throughout. German words and names break up ("Diirer", "Hoelzel", "Kiinste", "Biihne", "Normalmafie"), the space in "of" is often lost ("image o f a male human body"), and Vordruck appears as "titledVordruck", "V ordruck" and "V'ortfrncJfc". The quotes below are copied verbatim from the extracted text and keep that damage; do not tidy them when citing.

Argument in brief

An architectural history of one printed sheet.
