Schlemmer's Vordruck is an image of an ideal male body shown from the front, side and back, printed for his Bauhaus course Der Mensch (Dessau, 1928–29) as a ready-made canon of human proportion that students were to draw over and work from; Schlemmer also used it himself as the base for anatomical studies and as a two-dimensional model on which he drew and hung costumes for his stage characters. Feuerstein reads the sheet architecturally rather than theatrically — as an orthographic drawing whose views disagree with one another, and as a composition that implies and provokes a space — and argues it is more than a form handed out: it is a standardised spatial composition. Her thesis is that this emphatically male body and space have female origins, descending from Tafel I, a standing woman in Adalbert Goeringer's 1893 booklet on the Golden Section, whose proportions Schlemmer traced and redrew, passing her through a genderless intermediate drawing before reaching the male canon. Around that finding the dissertation widens into type and Typisierung in German design, the long history of proportion canons (Vitruvius, Dürer, Leonardo, Zeising, Carus, Schmidt, Fritsch), and the exclusion any "ideal type" performs — of women, and of every man who does not match it — which she carries through the Bauhaus's Mazdaznan milieu to the racial politics of the period. It is one scholar's argument on archival evidence, and candid about its own limits: authorship of the original drawing is given as unknown, the female-to-male transformation is offered as inference ("it appears", "one suspects", "may be speculated upon"), and the sheet's distribution history is hedged.

Key ideas

  • Vordruck — Literally a printed form or template. In this text: the printed sheet carrying one male figure in front, side and back view, made for the course Der Mensch — canon, proportion demonstration, drawing base and costume pattern at once.
  • Der Mensch ("The Human Being") — Schlemmer's Bauhaus course at Dessau, 1928–29. Two components: the obligatory "theoretical" section and the optional practical section ("Instruction on Man" — drawing the nude, figure drawing); the Vordruck appeared in both, and also in his Theater Workshop.
  • Homo — Schlemmer's earlier male figure (1916), seated in right profile with an embedded proportional cross; more elaborate than the Vordruck and repeatedly reused across his paintings and architectural projects.
  • Bauhaus Canon of Human Proportion — Feuerstein's own coinage for the proportional system the Vordruck embodies, formalised in printed and reproduced form for the course. She treats the sheet as a document of standardisation, not as a study.
  • Tafel I / the Golden Circle — Goeringer's 1893 plate of a woman, and his proportional instrument (a modified pantograph), both found by the author among Schlemmer's Der Mensch papers. Tafel I is the Vordruck's female origin; the Golden Circle is the tool that let proportion be applied without mathematics.
  • lineaments (lineamenta) — Alberti's term, used here for the lines and proportions passing between the woman of Tafel I, a neutral intermediate drawing and the male Vordruck — the sameness that purges difference.
  • type / Typisierung — Standardisation and typification in German design (the Muthesius–Van de Velde debate). The Vordruck is read as the production of an "ideal type", and the exclusion of difference is the dissertation's political stake.
  • laws of cubical space — Schlemmer's phrase for the "invisible linear network of planimetric and stereometric relationships" — the drawn web the stage figure moves inside (PDF pp.82–83).
  • lecture-demonstration — Schlemmer's illustrated stage talk on the new Bauhaus stage (Bühne, March 1927), which became the essay "Stage" and, with "Man and Art Figure", the material in Theater of the Bauhaus. A performance that demonstrates rather than narrates.
  • Mazdaznan — The reform-cult religion (Ha'nish) that Itten and Muche brought into the Bauhaus; Chapter Five uses it to move from body-canon to body-politics and exclusion.

Quotes

  • "This dissertation focuses on the image o f a male human body designed by Schlemmer, titledVordruck, or "form ." which Schlemmer made into a standardized form that was handed out and used in his Bauhaus course, Der Mensch —the Human Being, in the late 20s." (PDF p.8 / printed p.v) — what the Vordruck is, who it was made for, and where it sits in Der Mensch.
  • "Although there were many copies of Vordruck made for Schlemmer's course on the human being, the original seems to be drawn in ink. Whether Schlemmer actually drafted the original is unknown.7 These images were supposed to have been used by Schlemmer's students who, he complained, were unable to draw the human figure." (PDF p.47 / printed p.34) — copies against an uncertain original; students who could not draw the figure.
  • "On the page distributed to the students appears a drawing o f a male figure shown from the front, side, and back, and the word Vordruck." (PDF p.47 / printed p.34) — the handout described as an object: three views of one male body on a sheet.
  • "Schlemmer himself used this pre-printed "form" to describe both the inner and outer body. It became his template onto which the human anatomical systems were drawn: muscles [1.4] , “inner” organs [1.5], and bones [1.2].9 He used it to explore other canons of the human body [1.6], and he used it as a two dimensional "model" upon which he designed, drew, and hung costumes for characters in his later ballets and theatrical characters." (PDF p.47 / printed p.35) — the same sheet reused as an anatomical template and as a costume pattern.
  • "The Schlemmer family forbids photocopying or microfilming this original source material, and, while they allow scholarly access to the work, they continue to maintain strict control over its dissemination." (PDF p.18 / printed p.5) — who holds the material and under what conditions.
  • "The only documentation of the performance consists of a few black and white photographs, as well as a partially completed information form about the dance, and a drawing for the stage set-up." (PDF p.115 / printed p.103) — what survives of a live dance: photographs, a half-filled form, a set drawing.
  • "A drawing of the plan may have been used as a guide rather than as the template for the dance." (PDF p.119 / printed p.106) — drawing as guide rather than instruction - the score/event gap in one sentence.
  • "It appears that Schlemmer has transformed the female in Tafel 1 into the male shown in Normalmafie e in es..., by tracing and redrawing Goeringer’s female model’s proportions along the vertical axis, drawing horizontal lines through this vertical axis and onto the sheet of paper entitled Einige wesentliche Punkte, which became a series of fragments of accurately placed mobile bodily parts." (PDF p.106 / printed p.94) — transmission by tracing: a female model re-made as the male canon.
  • "In the Laban system, the body is segmented into its moving parts and given a code for each possible direction and range of motion o f that part - in relation to the total body. It is a complete language o f gestural movement which is used to record a dance as it is being performed, to compose dance on paper —creating the dance from one's own mind and body —and, of course, to do both: a combination o f lived and written movement. Schlemmer developed his own system o f dance notation and human movement, which was both drawn in the Der Mensch manuscript and utilized by Schlemmer as a graphic record of his various Bauhaus Dances of this time (1927/28)." (PDF p.40 / printed p.27-28) — dance notation, writing movement down, Schlemmer's own graphic record.
  • "The staged lecture-demonstration format might be compared to and understood in relation to an ideal space inhabited by an ideal body, translating Vitruvius’s description of the first building (iheAedium ) as stage and the perfectly proportioned body (III. 1) as performer." (PDF p.81 / printed p.68-69) — the illustrated lecture-demonstration as a format with its own history.
  • "Schlemmer's workshop and performances happened on stage, rather than in a theater." (PDF p.81 / printed p.68-69) — the stage as experimental space, not a theater.
  • "while the series of costumes "spoke" their "message" statically - as drawings, as a series of parts, or hung from manikins (which is how we currently view them) before they were worn, they also changed their message once they were put into motion, following choreographed, musical, and improvised scores." (PDF p.147 / printed p.135) — the drawn figure against the body that wears and animates it.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

It can carry the document problem, and carry it well: how a live bodily practice is converted into a transferable object — a sheet printed in many copies, drawn over, reused for anatomy, for costume and for teaching — and what that conversion does to the body it claims to describe (a woman's proportions end up as a male canon; a dance ends up as photographs, a half-completed information form and a set drawing). It can also carry the archive question, since it states openly who owns the material and who may photograph it. For a practice-as-research argument it supplies one strong hinge: the stage treated as an experimental place for studying space and movement rather than as a theatre — and the lecture-demonstration named as a format in its own right. It cannot carry anything about spectatorship, projection, immersion or the dome; Feuerstein writes as an architectural historian about a drawing, and her viewer is a viewer of drawings.

Limits

Not a performance-studies source and not a notation treatise. The Vordruck is a proportion canon, not a score for an event: there is no developed theory of reconstruction here, only scattered material — the circle-dance chapter, one paragraph on Laban notation, one sentence on costumes viewed static against costumes worn. Nothing on how performers were instructed or rehearsed; dancers appear as collaborators (Manda von Kreibig on the circle dance) or as bodies in photographs, never as recipients of directions. The Triadic Ballet is mentioned and not analysed; for Schlemmer's own stage theory go to the essays in Theater of the Bauhaus. The female origin rests on tracing evidence and is explicitly presented as speculation — treat the causal claim as the author's inference rather than a finding. Distribution is soft: the abstract says the sheet was "handed out and used", the body text says the prints were "supposedly" given to students (PDF p.14), and an endnote records the daughter's December 1992 recollection that the students bought the sheets themselves (PDF p.66) — a small inconsistency in the one fact the rest leans on. Finally the period: everything here is 1920s–30s Germany and the body in architecture; it predates and does not touch dome, planetarium or screen-based immersive work.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Expanded_Gropius_The_Theater_of_the_Bauhaus_pdf (111 pp) — Schlemmer's own "Man and Art Figure" and "Stage" essays plus the other Bauhaus theatre texts; the primary sources this dissertation quotes and the place to get what the Vordruck cannot give.
  • Books_oskar_schlemmer_bauhaus_man_and_art_figure_pdf (33 pp) — the art-figure and costume essay alone, in translation.
  • Expanded_Turner_The_Ritual_Process_pdf (223 pp) — ritual, liminality and communitas; sits beside Feuerstein's chapter on the Bauhaus's cultist climate and its festivals.
  • Books_Being_Singular_Plural_Jean_Luc_Nancy_pdf — body, space and the singular-plural body; pairs against the claim that an "ideal type" erases the bodies it cannot hold.
  • Books_Immersed_in_Technology_Moser_and_MacLeod_pdf and Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf — immersion and illusion; the dome-adjacent half of the corpus, against which Schlemmer's "laws of cubical space" read as an early attempt to specify an enveloping space geometrically.
  • Expanded_Goffman_Forms_of_Talk_pdf (343 pp) — talk as a performed format, the lecture included; the natural neighbour for the lecture-demonstration material.

Sanela Nikolić, The Bauhaus Theater – Oskar Schlemmer's Design in Motion Concept (2014)

Citation

Nikolić, Sanela. "The Bauhaus Theater – Oskar Schlemmer's Design in Motion Concept." S A J — running head as printed on every page: "S A J _ 2014 _ 6 _". Header block: original scientific article approval date 03 02 2014 / UDK BROJEVI: 7.038.16:792.027.5(430)”1925/1933” ID BROJ: 207128332. Author affiliation printed under the title: "Sanela Nikolić / Faculty of Music, University of Arts in Belgrade." Printed page range 43–62. Original PDF: Expanded_SAJ_2014_1_Nikolic_pdf.pdf.

Journal identification caveat: the file prints no volume/issue statement beyond the running head "S A J _ 2014 _ 6 _", and the journal title is never spelled out ("Serbian Architectural Journal" is inferred from the SAJ running head, not stated in the file). The filename encodes 2014_1. I could not confirm from the file whether 6 is the volume and 1 the issue of that year — treat the volume/issue as unverified. Title, author, affiliation, category line, UDK and ID numbers are taken directly from the first page.

Kind of article: not a design-theory essay and not a practice-based study — it is a descriptive historical study of a design theory, published by the journal under its own category line "original scientific article". Method is exegetical/bibliographic: exposition of Schlemmer's stage theory built almost entirely from already-published English translations of Schlemmer, Gropius and Wingler plus secondary literature; no archival work, no drawings or plans analysed, no reconstruction, no fieldwork.

Coverage

Journal article, complete: abstract + keywords + body (printed pp. 43–57) + NOTES and Works Cited (printed pp. 58–62). 21 PDF pages, 8,571 words. PDF p.21 is blank / image-only; nothing follows the reference list on PDF p.20. Page mapping is consistent throughout: printed folio = PDF page + 42 (PDF 1 = p.43, PDF 12 = p.54, PDF 20 = p.62), so this is an extracted article, not a whole issue.

Text layer is clean, well-ordered and legible — no OCR damage, nothing unquotable. Artifacts only: em dashes render as "–", a sentence-final period is missing after "variables" on PDF p.5, running heads and page folios interleave with body text in the column order (the folio "47" is interposed mid-sentence on PDF p.5, which is why no quote below spans that break), and italics are flattened.

Two figures exist as captions only: "Figure 1." (PDF p.3 / printed p.45) and "Figure 2-3." (PDF p.8 / printed p.50). The images themselves and any further caption text are not in the extraction, so the article's visual evidence (stage plans, diagrams, photographs) is invisible in this file.

Argument in brief

A documented account of the Bauhaus stage under Oskar Schlemmer, read through an explicitly architectural frame: the stage is treated as designed, mathematically determined space, not as drama and not as dance. Nikolić reconstructs Schlemmer's own theory (chiefly from "Man and Art Figure", 1925, and "Mathematics of the Dance") and argues that his central problem was the law of motion of the human body in space, and that stage work was for him one branch of the Bauhaus programme of designing the totality of the space in which man exists. The performer's body, enclosed in geometrical costume, becomes an artificial figure (Kunstfigur) that both obeys the planimetric and stereometric geometry of the stage and metaphysically animates it — mechanic and metaphysic held together. The essay moves from Bauhaus ideology (Gropius's total architecture; the Stage Workshop founded in 1921 and taken over by Schlemmer in October 1925), through the Dessau stage built into the school building and the Triadic Ballet, to a closing claim that the stage space was interpreted by analogy with architectural space, sharing its three-dimensionality, optics, acoustics and "movements of mechanical and organic bodies within a limited space". The organising term is "design in motion" — the stage as organised by mechanical-choreographic motion plus form and colour — which makes Schlemmer's stage the "absolute visual stage".

Key ideas

  • design in motion — the article's own term for Schlemmer's concept: organisation of the stage space by specific mechanical-choreographic and spatial design, working with form and colour. It is what distinguishes the stage from the fixed formative arts and makes the stage "architectonic".
  • absolute visual stage (Schaubühne) — Schlemmer's third stage type, after the oral/sound stage and the play stage: a purely optical event, handled by the designer as "builder of form and colour". It is a mathematically determined but empty space that man fills.
  • artificial figure / Kunstfigur — the human body on stage transformed by sculptural costume into an abstract geometric body, at once symbol of man and element of space construction. Costume is the key instrument of that transformation.
  • mechanic / metaphysic — the keyword pair from the abstract and from Schlemmer's own vocabulary: precise, almost mechanical movement in geometrically determined space, plus the non-mathematisable inner/emotional element that animates that space. The article quotes Schlemmer placing the artist between the danger of the mechanical age and "exact metaphysics", and records his negative position on the contemporary "mechanic theater".
  • space mathematics / planimetric and stereometric relations — the stage defined as a limited space of planimetric and stereometric links; performers are given visual and physical points of orientation in order to engage "in the space mathematics" consciously.
  • stage space by analogy with architectural space — the article's central claim (PDF p.5); the stage is not a picture frame but a built volume, the "constructive fusion of space and building, the realm of the architect".
  • body architecture / performer as kinetic sculpture — the performer's body is designed with the same elements as buildings and useful objects: form, colour, material. Costume material directly conditions motion (glass in Glastanz, wood in Stäbetanz), which raises the question of how space and the perception of space change with the material defining it.
  • the Dessau stage — the experimental theatre built as an integral part of the Dessau Bauhaus building. Described in the article's prose (no quotation possible across the page break): frontally open, deliberately small and controlled with few variables, its only mechanism a four-row system for opening the side walls and moving suspended requisites; Schlemmer is quoted as pleased that Gropius was building him a stage he could use as he saw fit, and as rarely using its opening onto both the main hall and the canteen, doing his experiments on the frontal opening.
  • lecture-demonstration — Schlemmer's extensive 1927 report on the objectives and methods of the Bauhaus Theatre, also delivered as a lecture-demonstration before "The Circle of Friends of the Bauhaus"; the article calls the 1927 theatre lecture the place where he put man's existence in relation to space as the central problem.

Quotes

  • "Bauhaus Theater obtained its most complete form through Oskar Schlemmer’s artistic, pedagogical and theoretical work. The key problem for Schlemmer was the law of motion of the human body in space." (PDF p.1 / printed p.43) — the abstract’s own statement of the article’s problem; opens any use of the source.
  • "When in October 1925 Oskar Schlemmer assumed the management of the Bauhaus Stage Workshop, the theatre became one of the central fields of school work, with regards to the potential of integrating within itself all other artistic fields. The stage space was understood as per the analogy with architectural space, and in compliance with Gropius’ ideas on integrated artwork which unifies various arts into oneness of the new harmony." (PDF p.5 / printed p.47) — the article’s key claim — stage space read as architectural space; it also dates the October 1925 takeover that the UDK code names.
  • "The work in the Bauhaus Stage Workshop since 1925 has proceeded in significantly improved conditions with regards to the fact that the experimental theater stage was built as an integral part of the Bauhaus Dessau building which until 1929 resulted in the program of twelve conducted Schlemmer’s creations." (PDF p.5 / printed p.47) — the Bauhaus theatre’s relation to its building: the stage is a built-in part of the Dessau school.
  • "For him performing represented the matter of architecture in motion, where the human bodies – by dressing in costumes were transformed into artificial figures – and the stage object created the variable forms in constant motion." (PDF p.6 / printed p.48) — Schlemmer’s stage work read as architecture, not dance — the single most useful sentence for a performer-and-space argument.
  • "The materials involved in this transfiguration are form and color, the materials of the painter and sculptor. The arena for this transfiguration is found in the constructive fusion of space and building, the realm of the architect." (PDF p.7 / printed p.49) — Schlemmer quoted from “Man and Art Figure”: the stage arena defined as built space, the architect’s realm.
  • "Schlemmer believed that the limited space of the theater stage was determined by the linear network of planimetric and stereometric links, whereby that stage geometry was inherent to the mathematical aspect of the human body." (PDF p.9 / printed p.51) — the geometry of the performer’s body made homologous with the geometry of the stage plan.
  • "In his significant lecture on the theater in 1927, as a problem Schlemmer particularly emphasized the event of the existence of man in relation to space." (PDF p.10 / printed p.52) — the 1927 theatre lecture as the site where the theory was stated — closest this source comes to a performance-lecture lineage.
  • "The performers had physical and visual points of orientation which enabled them to consciously and physically engage in the space mathematics. On the Dessau stage Schlemmer used also the staircases, mobile skeleton platforms and small mobile articles such as hoops, stickers, cubes, balls and sticks." (PDF p.13 / printed p.55) — how the designed volume is actually operated by bodies, plus an inventory of the built stage apparatus.
  • "A specific design of body architecture was in practice which comprised those same elements relevant for designing the buildings and utilitarian objects - the form, color and material." (PDF p.14 / printed p.56) — explicit equivalence between designing the performer’s body and designing buildings — the body as designed volume.
  • "It concerned the equivalent approach in interpretation of the stage space and architectural space which united the aspects of three-dimensionality of the optical effects, acoustics, as well as the “movements of mechanical and organic bodies within a limited space.”" (PDF p.15 / printed p.57) — the closing synthesis: stage space and architectural space treated as the same kind of designed enclosure for moving bodies.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This source can carry the historical-theoretical argument that theatrical space has been designed as an architectural volume with the moving body as its measuring instrument — the Bauhaus stage as a remote ancestor of the domed performance environment, where the performer's movement is both constrained by a geometry and constitutive of the space. It is also the best source in this folder for the claim that a stage can be treated as an apparatus calibrated to produce a specific relation between bodies and space, and it documents Schlemmer's lecture-demonstration practice, giving the performance lecture a Bauhaus pedigree. It cannot carry anything about domes, immersion, projection environments or audience experience.

Limits

  • No dome, no planetarium, no immersion. Searched for dome, planetarium, immersive, immersion, fulldome: zero occurrences in the file. The only "projection" is inside a quotation describing the later Bauhaus dances as "involving a strong emphasis on light projection" (PDF p.12). The space described is a small frontal proscenium, the opposite of an enclosing projection surface.
  • The audience is essentially absent. The word "audience" occurs once in the whole file and only inside a long quoted passage on the 1928–29 tours ("ready to be shown to even the most sophisticated audiences", PDF p.14). The spectator's relation to the space is never analysed; the argument runs entirely from the maker/theorist side. The only concrete audience datum is the stage's double opening onto hall and canteen, which the article notes Schlemmer barely used.
  • Reconstruction and documentation are named, not discussed. "McCall's Reconstructions" appears only as a bibliography entry (Moynihan and Odom, TDR 28/3, 1984, PDF p.20). The documents that record the space are named only as the fourth Bauhaus book Die Bühne im Bauhaus (1925) and the third issue of the Bauhaus magazine (1927); the article does not examine plans, drawings, photographs or archives, and its own two figures are not recoverable from this text layer.
  • Secondary sourcing. Much is quoted at second hand: the Dessau stage description is taken from Trimingham, Theatre Research International (note 15, PDF p.17), and Schlemmer's "Neue Formen der Bühne" is cited through Trimingham's book, not from the original (note 46, PDF p.18). Quotations from Schlemmer come from English translations, not the German originals.
  • The UDK's 1925–1933 frame is only partly honoured. A large share of the discussion is the Triadic Ballet (1912–1923) and pre-Dessau Weimar; the Dessau years are covered through the stage's construction, the 1927 report and the 1929 departure. The article does not address the mismatch between its own period code and its material.
  • The English is the author's non-native prose. Quotations are accurate, but the connecting argument is sometimes awkward or repetitive, with typos in the body ("Schelemmer", "Bauhus", "mechanicalchoreographical", "from" for "form") and in the reference list ("Mogoly-Nagy", "Bauhau", "Exibition", "Stonard … 465–464"). Content is reliable; wording of the surrounding prose is not quotable with confidence.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_oskar_schlemmer_bauhaus_man_and_art_figure_pdf — Schlemmer's own "Man and Art Figure" essay, the primary text this article quotes most and the source of its key stage/architecture formulation.
  • Expanded_Gropius_The_Theater_of_the_Bauhaus_pdf — the 1961 English edition of Die Bühne im Bauhaus (1925), the "fourth Bauhaus book" this article names as the record of the stage programme.
  • Expanded_Schlemmer_Vordruck_The_Making_of_pdf — further Schlemmer primary material, useful for checking what this article paraphrases.
  • Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf — theory of performance space and intermediality, the bridge from the Bauhaus stage to projection environments.
  • Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf and Books_Digital_Performance_Steve_Dixon_pdf — the immersion and performance-technology lineage this article predates and does not address.

Erving Goffman, Forms of Talk (1981)

Citation

Goffman, Erving. Forms of Talk. University of Pennsylvania Publications in Conduct and Communication. Philadelphia: University of Pennsylvania Press, 1981. [From the title page and copyright page in the extraction: ERVING COFFMAN / UNIVERSITY OF PENNSYLVANIA PRESS PHILADELPHIA / 1981 (the cover signature scans as COFFMAN for GOFFMAN); Copyright @ 1981 by Erving Goffman All rights reserved Printed in the United States of America; series page: University of Pennsylvania Publications in Conduct and Communication / Erving Goffman and Dell Hymes, General Editors; ISBNs scan as ISBN o-8122-779o-2 (0-8122-7790-2) and ISBN o-8122-1112-x (pbk.).] Original PDF: (University of Pennsylvania Publications in Conduct and Communication) Erving Goffman - Forms of Talk-University of Pennsylvania Press (1981).pdf.

Coverage

Book, complete. 343 PDF pages, 125,598 words in the extraction. No preview, no watermark, no missing chapter. Page offset is fixed and verified: PDF page = printed folio + 6 (PDF p.166 = printed p.160; PDF p.203 = printed p.197; PDF p.341 = printed p.335). Contents as printed: Introduction (pp.1-4; PDF 7-10); 1. Replies and Responses (pp.5-77; PDF 11-83); 2. Response Cries (pp.78-123; PDF 84-129); 3. Footing (pp.124-159; PDF 130-165); 4. The Lecture (pp.160-196; PDF 166-202, with REFERENCES on PDF p.202); 5. Radio Talk (pp.197-330; PDF 203-336); INDEX (pp.331-335; PDF 337-341); publisher's series list (PDF 342-343, unnumbered).

Two things to be clear about. (1) 'The Lecture' is one chapter among five papers, not the book. The other four - Replies and Responses, Response Cries, Footing, Radio Talk - are separate essays with their own bibliographies; the Introduction says the five were written 1974-1980, the first three previously published as journal articles, the last two (The Lecture and Radio Talk) printed here for the first time (PDF p.7). (2) The chapter is itself a performed lecture converted to print: PDF pp.166-167 state that it was originally presented as the Katz-Newcomb Memorial Lecture, University of Michigan, 1976, designed to be spoken, and delivered by aloud reading from a typed text, with all the spontaneous elaborations of the occasion omitted, plus the author's own disclaimer that the printed version adds still more that was never spoken.

The Lecture chapter is complete and not thin: 37 PDF pages (printed 160-196), 13,930 words, running heads alternating The Lecture / Forms of Talk. The extraction is pdftotext over an already-OCR'd scan, so the text layer carries systematic scan damage: digits as letters and vice versa (8o-528o6 for 80-52806; the Response Cries chapter number scans as z; RADIO TALI<), tildes for hyphens in a few words (a~rangement, parenthe~ical), quotation marks sometimes rendered as 11 (11Contextualizing, 11meet, 11on/11off cues), and line-break hyphens sometimes swallowed so words join (textparenthetical, reengagernent). Every quote below was string-verified against the extraction and taken from spans the scan left clean; a few otherwise ideal sentences were avoided because of this damage (A platform a~rangement is often involved, Text parenthe~ical remarks), and those are noted in Key ideas rather than quoted.

Argument in brief

Goffman treats a lecture not as a method of transferring information but as a bounded speech event with its own frame, and asks what is left over once you subtract the text. He starts from the social arrangement: an occasion with sponsors and a celebrative purpose, an introduced speaker, a gathered audience seated and held in staring attention, and an institutional monopoly on the floor. He then analyses what the speaker does with himself in that slot. Words can be animated in three ways - memorization, aloud reading, fresh talk - and each is a production mode that puts the speaker on a different footing with the audience; lectures move between them, and much of the craft of lecturing is managing those switches so the seams read as spontaneity. Goffman calls the result a fresh-talk illusion, and his own chapter is an exhibit of it: an aloud-read text with keying, bracketing and parenthetical remarks layered on top. His conclusion is that the text transmission is the manifest business while the ritual work - self-exposure, giving access to a person, committing to this one occasion, affirming intellectual authority - is what the audience actually attends to; hence his description of every lecturer as a functionary of the cognitive establishment. The method is naturalistic and reflexive rather than theoretical: close attention to the mechanics of actual speaking, with his own podium conduct offered as data, and no audience-side ethnography at all.

Key ideas

  • text vs. talk - a lecture carries a text that could equally have been printed or spoken in conversation; what is distinctive to lecturing as a form is the delivery - the interactional encasement. He is explicit that he is left with the form, not the content, and that the content of a lecture is not to be understood as characteristic of lecturing (PDF pp.178-179).
  • game and spectacle - following Pike, the lecture is analysed as a laminated affair: the business at hand embedded in the enveloping social fuss that surrounds it, with preplay and postplay in between, and the word 'lecture' obscuring whether it means the spoken text or the whole social event (PDF pp.173-174). This is the closest he comes to naming the lecture as a staged event with its own frame.
  • immediate audience / platform arrangement - an audience gathered, typically seated, holding the speaker's whole body in attention as they would an entertainer's, and restricted at first to back channel. Note the scan damage: the sentence opening this passage reads A platform a~rangement is often involved (PDF p.171).
  • animator / author / principal - three separable functions of the word 'speaker'; the lecture is defined by the claim that one person fills all three, is assumed to have intellectual (not merely institutional) authority, and does not have to fight for the floor (PDF p.173, with the definitions in a footnote at PDF p.232). The split between these functions is what a stand-in reader or a recording makes visible.
  • footing - from ch.3, then applied throughout the lecture chapter: the term is defined at PDF p.134 / printed p.128 (quoted below). A change of footing is a change of frame, and lectures are full of them.
  • production modes and production shifts - memorization, aloud reading, fresh talk. Shifts between them are, in his words, a crucial part of lecturing, and are heard as changes in the speaker's relationship to the audience (PDF pp.177-178).
  • fresh-talk illusion - most lectures, and still more broadcast talk, depend on sounding formulated on the spot; fresh talk is itself somewhat illusory, since utterances are composed in clause-length segments ahead of delivery (PDF p.178).
  • bracket expressions and text-parenthetical remarks - openings and closings plus qualifying asides delivered in a changed voice; the asides let the speaker act as broker of his own statements, and the introduction and closing let him come down off the horse and rejoin the audience as one of them (PDF pp.181-183). Question time is an official production shift, marked by small rituals - sitting down, lighting a cigarette, drinking water (PDF p.182).
  • noise / equipment and encoding faults - everything that intrudes on the text: bodies, coughs, mispronunciations, feedback, disturbances in the auditorium. Each implies a self the audience must be licensed to notice, and which the speaker may acknowledge in a remedial aside; the audience must otherwise disattend it (PDF pp.189-192).
  • access, ritual character, celebrative occasion - a lecture gives preferential contact with a person and, through him, with his subject; the sponsors' interests include the photographic, taped and textual record of the talk as much as the talk itself, and the speaker's prestige is lent to the auspices (PDF pp.175, 192-194).
  • functionary of the cognitive establishment - lecturing presupposes and supports the proposition that the world has a reportable structure and that speaking before an audience and listening to a speaker are reasonable things to be doing (PDF p.201).
  • broadcast talk / absent addressees (ch.5) - the announcer performs to listeners who are not there, gets no ongoing back channel, and works with a narrower frame space than conversation: a fixed footing is not required in talk, whereas broadcasting constrains the shifts available (PDF pp.247, 328-330).

Quotes

  • "So I ask that these papers be taken for what they merely are: exercises, trials, tryouts, a means of displaying possibilities, not establishing fact." (PDF p.7 / printed p.1) — method, stated by the author in the Introduction: naturalistic description as tryout, not proof
  • "A change in footing implies a change in the alignment we take up to ourselves and the others present as expressed in the way we manage the production or reception of an utterance. A change in our footing is another way of talking about a change in our frame for events." (PDF p.134 / printed p.128) — footing defined (ch.3), the term the lecture chapter then applies wholesale
  • "A lecture is an institutionalized extended holding of the floor in which one speaker imparts his views on a subject, these thoughts comprising what can be called his "text." The style is typically serious and slightly impersonal, the controlling intent being to generate calmly considered understanding, not mere entertainment, emotional impact, or immediate action." (PDF p.171 / printed p.165) — definition of the lecture; a lecture has a text, and it is not framed as entertainment
  • "I mean a gathered set of individuals, typically seated, whose numbers can vary greatly without requiring the speaker (typically standing) to change his style, who have the right to hold the whole of the speaker's body in the focus of staring-at attention (as they would an entertainer), and who (initially, at least) have only the back channel through which to convey their response." (PDF p.171 / printed p.165) — platform arrangement, immediate audience, back channel: the physical frame of the lecture room
  • "A lecture, then, purports to take the audience right past the auditorium, the occasion, and the speaker into the subject matter upon which the lecture comments. So your lecturer is meant to be a performer, but not merely a performer." (PDF p.172 / printed p.166) — performer-but-not-just-performer: the double claim of the lecture form
  • "Memorization, aloud reading, and fresh talk are different production modes. Each presupposes its own special relation between speaker and listener, establishing the speaker on a characteristic "footing" in regard to the audience. Switches from one of the three forms to another, that is, "production shifts," imply for the speaker a change of footing, and, as will be seen, are a crucial part of lecturing. The critical point that will later be addressed is that a great number of lectures (because of my incompetence, not including this one) depend upon a fresh-talk illusion." (PDF p.178 / printed p.172) — the three modes as footings; production shifts; the fresh-talk illusion of lecturing
  • "Responding to questions, after all, requires fresh talk. In other words, question answering requires a production shift from aloud reading to fresh talk, with the speaker often marking the shift by means of bracket rituals, such as lighting a cigarette, changing from a standing to a sitting position, drinking a glass of water, and so forth." (PDF p.182 / printed p.176) — bracket rituals and the shift into question time: the lecture's staged transition to conversation
  • "There is an irony here. There are moments in a lecture when the speaker seems most alive to the ambience of the occasion and is particularly ready with wit and extemporaneous response to show how fully he has mobilized his spirit and mind for the moment at hand. Yet these inspired moments will often be ones to most suspect." (PDF p.184 / printed p.178) — extemporaneity as rehearsed effect; the speaker's self-reference is itself scripted
  • "In any case, all of these changes in footing cut too deep; they project the self of the animator all right, but this time not the author of the text, thereby widening a split that is just the one that successful lecturing heals. Such an arrangement, then, strikes at the ritual elements of the presentation." (PDF p.187 / printed p.181) — the stand-in / recorded reader: when animator and author split, the ritual frame of the lecture breaks
  • "For what is noise from the perspective of the text as such can be the music of the interaction-the very source of the auditors' satisfaction in the occasion, the very difference between reading a lecture at home and attending one." (PDF p.192 / printed p.186) — why the live occasion is not text delivery: everything not in the text is what the audience came for
  • "And this ritual work is done under cover of conveying his text. No one need feel that ritual has become an end in itself. As the manifest content of a dream allows a latent meaning to be tolerated, so the transmission of a text allows for the ritual of performance." (PDF p.197 / printed p.191) — the central claim: text transmission is the manifest cover for ritual performance
  • "It is in this sense that every lecturer, merely by presuming to lecture before an audience, is a functionary of the cognitive establishment, actively supporting the same position: I repeat, that there is structure to the world, that this structure can be perceived and reported, and therefore, that speaking before an audience and listening to a speaker are reasonable things to be doing, and incidentally, of course, that the auspices of the occasion had warrant for making the whole thing possible." (PDF p.201 / printed p.195) — lecturing as social membership and institutional affiliation, not just information transfer
  • "In brief, announcers must conjure up in their mind's eye the notion of listeners, and act as though these phantoms were physically present to be addressed through gaze, body orientation, voice calibrated for distance, and the like. In a fundamental sense, then, broadcasting (whether announcing news, giving a political address, or whatever) involves self-constructed talk projected under the demands, gaze, and responsiveness of listeners who aren't there." (PDF p.247 / printed p.241) — the media frame (ch.5): talk performed to absent addressees, i.e. the recorded lecture shown to a later audience

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the source that lets a lecture be described as a performance rather than a delivery system: it supplies the vocabulary (text, footing, animator/author/principal, production shift, bracket, fresh-talk illusion, ritual work under cover of conveying a text) for saying that a speaker on a platform is producing an event for a present audience, and for reading a performance lecture's switches between reading and speaking as structural moves rather than lapses of delivery. It also carries the argument that a recorded or relayed lecture is a different speech event from the live one: the Radio Talk analysis of talk projected to listeners who are not there, plus the claim that separating the animator from the author of the text breaks the ritual frame of the presentation (PDF p.187), is the strongest warrant in this corpus for treating a lecture replayed in a dome as a changed form rather than the same lecture on a different screen. What it cannot carry: nothing on domes, projection, image or architecture, no audience-side evidence, and no theory of media or recording beyond the broadcast talk chapter.

Limits

  • Written 1974-1980 for a US academic audience; no digital, no video, no dome, no planetarium, nothing on the projected image. 'Architecture' here means the auditorium, the podium and the seating, not the enclosure.
  • He refuses the audience half of the analysis on the very page where the platform arrangement is described (PDF p.171), so the chapter is speaker-side only. The audience appears as warm or unresponsive, as back channel, as witnesses and as recipients of access - never with its own conduct described in any detail.
  • The chapter is not extemporaneous talk and says so: it was aloud-read from a typed text, spontaneous additions omitted, and the print version adds material never spoken (PDF pp.166-167). As evidence about lecturing it therefore sits at the scripted end of the spectrum.
  • He labels his own concepts provisional (PDF p.7, quoted below) and admits the book repeats itself heavily; the opening of the chapter is a parody of podium self-reference, which is a stylistic costume, not data.
  • The extraction is OCR-derived, so a handful of otherwise ideal sentences cannot be quoted cleanly (e.g. A platform a~rangement, Text parenthe~ical remarks); nothing from those spans is quoted above.

Adjacent sources in this folder

What's the Use of Lectures? (Bligh) — the empirical teaching-effectiveness literature that Goffman's speech-event analysis displaces. Conducting Interaction (Kendon) — the micro-ecology of co-present interaction (F-formations): how bodies in a room arrange themselves, the seated/staring arrangement Goffman treats only in passing. Culture as Performance (Fischer-Lichte) — performance-as-event, and the performance studies vocabulary for what Goffman calls ritual work. The Ritual Process (Turner) — ritual, liminality and the celebrative occasion; Goffman cites Durkheim, not Turner, for the ritual character of access. Audience Participation in Theatre (White) — the audience-side account missing from Goffman's chapter. Mapping Intermediality in Performance and Digital Performance (Dixon) — where the recorded/relayed performance argument goes after 1981. The Theater of the Bauhaus (Gropius ed.) and Oskar Schlemmer — the staging and architectural counterpart to the podium arrangement.

5. Participation, spectatorship, spectacle

What to think with when the room is asked to do something. Bishop is essential and counter-intuitive: participatory art is not automatically democratic or good, the ethical justification of a project is not an aesthetic judgement of it, and the artist-as-facilitator role has to be argued for rather than assumed. Her chapter on Beuys and experimental pedagogy speaks directly to the lecture form as an artistic situation. Debord supplies the opposing accusation — the spectacle as a social relation mediated by images, spectators isolated by their one-way link to the centre, which is a description worth holding beside any claim that a dome creates community. Fisher supplies the ambient version of the same problem: a condition in which no alternative can be imagined, and a cultural form that pre-empts dissent rather than answering it. Muñoz supplies the counter-move, a hermeneutics of the not-yet-here; note that this file is chapter 1 only.

Claire Bishop, Artificial Hells: Participatory Art and the Politics of Spectatorship (2012)

Citation

Bishop, Claire. Artificial Hells: Participatory Art and the Politics of Spectatorship. London: Verso, 2012. ISBN 978-1-84467-690-3 (pbk), 978-1-84467-796-2 (ebook). [title page and copyright page of the PDF] Original PDF filename is not recoverable from the text layer; the slug Books_Artificial_Hells_Claire_Bishop_pdf is the only record of it.

Coverage

Book (complete). 387 PDF pages, c.149,700 words: title page, imprint, contents, Introduction, nine chapters, Conclusion, Notes (printed pp.285-362), Acknowledgements, Illustration Credits, Index, colophon. No preview watermark, no scan gaps, no missing chapters. Text layer is good; damage is limited to (a) fi/fl ligatures, (b) a scatter of dropped spaces ("ofcontemplation", "itselfas"). In the quotes below I have expanded ligatures to plain ASCII and restored dropped spaces; nothing else has been altered. Printed folios are legible and run four behind the PDF page number (printed p.277 = PDF p.281). Chapter list: Introduction; 1 The Social Turn: Collaboration and Its Discontents; 2 Artificial Hells: The Historic Avant-garde; 3 Je participe, tu participes, il participe; 4 Social Sadism Made Explicit; 5 The Social Under Socialism; 6 Incidental People: APG and Community Arts; 7 Former West: Art as Project in the Early 1990s; 8 Delegated Performance: Outsourcing Authenticity; 9 Pedagogic Projects; Conclusion.

Argument in brief

Bishop surveys participatory art from the Italian Futurist serate to 2011 and refuses the field's default value judgement: that work involving people is automatically more democratic, more ethical and more real than work that makes objects. She calls the post-1989 formation the social turn (her own 2006 coinage) but argues it is more accurately a return to the social, with three moments - 1917, 1968, 1989 - each carrying a utopian re-imagining of art's relation to the social. Her central complaint is methodological: participatory art is explained sociologically (community, agency, empowerment, demonstrable outcomes) and adjudicated ethically (which artist collaborated generously, which exploited collaborators), and the work itself is never examined as art. Historical case studies - Paris Dada, GRAV, the Situationist International, Lebel's Happenings, Tucuman Arde, Boal's Invisible Theatre, Czechoslovak happenings, the Collective Actions Group, the Community Arts Movement, the Artist Placement Group, Culture in Action, Project Unite, delegated performance, pedagogic projects - build the argument that participation has no fixed politics: it served Italian Fascism, Soviet mass spectacle and state socialism as readily as the anti-capitalist left. The conclusion argues that participation and democracy are not interchangeable, that participatory practice now dovetails with neoliberal labour (networks, mobility, project work, affective labour), and that the tension between artistic and social critique should be sustained rather than collapsed. Method is deliberately interdisciplinary, importing political philosophy (Ranciere above all), theatre history and performance studies.

Key ideas

  • social turn / return to the social - the post-1989 wave of collaborative, socially embedded art, historicised against two earlier avant-garde moments.
  • participation - strict definition: people constitute the central artistic medium and material, in the manner of theatre and performance (PDF p.6). Not interactivity, not audience engagement in general.
  • the ethical turn - judgement of participatory work by the artist's intentions and procedures rather than by the work's artistic identity; a diagnosis she shares with Ranciere's Malaise dans l'esthetique.
  • aisthesis / the aesthetic regime - Ranciere's terms as Bishop adopts them: the aesthetic as an autonomous regime of experience, and the ground of a politics of spectatorship (le partage du sensible).
  • antagonism / consensus - Bishop's own version of antagonism is thin and occasional: art can operate in a space of antagonism or negation vis-a-vis society. Her consensus/dissensus argument is carried entirely by Ranciere; Laclau does not appear anywhere in the book, and Mouffe appears once only, in an endnote, quoted through Jodi Dean on post-politics (PDF p.366 / printed p.362). There is no agonistic-democratic theory here, and a review that wants Mouffe's agonism must import it.
  • audience as co-producer - the third term of the social-turn triad: artist becomes collaborator, work becomes project, viewer becomes co-producer. Bishop tracks the historical sequence crowd (1910s) - masses (1920s) - people (1960s/70s) - the excluded (1980s) - community (1990s) - volunteers (2000s).
  • delegated performance - hiring non-professionals to perform on the artist's behalf; outsourcing authenticity (Ch.8).
  • pedagogic projects - art converging with education; Beuys, the Free International University and the lecture-action as precedent (Ch.9).
  • artistic vs social critique - Boltanski and Chiapello's distinction, used in the conclusion to frame the whole field as a clash of incommensurable value systems.
  • documentation problem - participatory art emphasises process over a definitive image and depends on first-hand experience over long duration, which forecloses critical distance for the critic as much as for the viewer (PDF p.10).

Quotes

  • "the artist is conceived less as an individual producer of discrete objects than as a collaborator and producer of situations; the work of art as a finite, portable, commodifiable product is reconceived as an ongoing or long-term project with an unclear beginning and end; while the audience, previously conceived as a ‘viewer’ or ‘beholder’, is now repositioned as a co-producer or participant." (PDF p.6 / printed p.2) — definition of participation: people as medium, artist as producer of situations, audience as co-producer.
  • "The book ends with a consideration of the changing identity of the audience across the twentieth century, and suggests that artistic models of democracy have only a tenuous relationship to actual forms of democracy." (PDF p.9 / printed p.5) — the book's closing claim that participation and democracy are not the same thing.
  • "the urgency of this social task has led to a situation in which socially collaborative practices are all perceived to be equally important artistic gestures of resistance: there can be no failed, unsuccessful, unresolved, or boring works of participatory art, because all are equally essential to the task of repairing the social bond. While sympathetic to the latter ambition, I would argue that it is also crucial to discuss, analyse and compare this work critically as art" (PDF p.17 / printed p.13) — the 'no failed participatory art' problem and the demand to judge the work as art.
  • "Artists and works of art can operate in a space of antagonism or negation vis-à-vis society, a tension that the ideological discourse of creativity reduces to a unified context and instrumentalises for more efficacious profiteering." (PDF p.20 / printed p.16) — art's capacity for antagonism/negation, against the creativity discourse.
  • "One of the aims of this book, then, is to emphasise the aesthetic in the sense of aisthesis: an autonomous regime of experience that is not reducible to logic, reason or morality." (PDF p.22 / printed p.18) — aisthesis: the aesthetic as an autonomous regime of experience.
  • "instead of turning to appropriately social practices as points of comparison, the tendency is always to compare artists’ projects with other artists on the basis of ethical one-upmanship" (PDF p.23 / printed p.19) — ethical one-upmanship as the substitute for art-critical judgement.
  • "Her comparison is based on an ethics of authorial renunciation: the work of Oda Projesi is better than that of Thomas Hirschhorn because it exemplifies a superior model of collaborative practice, one in which individual authorship is suppressed in favour of facilitating the creativity of others." (PDF p.26 / printed p.22) — the critique of authorial renunciation / artist-as-facilitator.
  • "This line of thinking has led to an ethically charged climate in which participatory and socially engaged art has become largely exempt from art criticism: emphasis is continually shifted away from the disruptive specificity of a given practice and onto a generalised set of ethical precepts." (PDF p.27 / printed p.23) — participatory art exempted from art criticism.
  • "It is Joseph Beuys, however, who remains the best-known point of reference for contemporary artists’ engagement with experimental pedagogy; in 1969 he claimed that ‘to be a teacher is my greatest work of art’." (PDF p.247 / printed p.243) — Beuys: the lecture-format performance and teaching as art.
  • "Far from being oppositional to spectacle, participation has now entirely merged with it." (PDF p.281 / printed p.277) — 'participation has now entirely merged with' spectacle.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the load-bearing text for the review's negative claim: that participation, immersion and audience activation are not in themselves democratic, ethical or good. It supplies the vocabulary for criticising fulldome work that treats audience involvement as self-validating (the no-failed-works problem), the argument for judging such work as art rather than by its social or ethical yield, and a documented history in which participatory formats served Fascism and state socialism as readily as the left. Chapter 9 sits directly on the review's spectrum: Beuys's lecture-actions and the pedagogic-project genre are the art-historical precedent for the performance lecture and the plain lecture as art formats. What it cannot carry: no fulldome, planetarium, projection or immersive-media case study, no technical account of apparatus, and no agonistic political theory beyond Ranciere.

Limits

European focus (Bishop states she includes Eastern Europe and South America but not Asia, and only a few US examples). Written 2006-2011: predates the immersive-media boom and post-2015 participation culture; her example of participation's collapse into spectacle is Gormley's One and Other (2009), not platform media. It is a polemic with a moving position - she notes the shift between the 2006 polemic and the 2011 conclusion. No empirical audience research, no data; the judgements are art-critical. Bishop also concedes the visual-analysis problem without solving it: she argues participatory work cannot be grasped from images alone, yet her own evidence is largely documentary.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_The_Society_of_the_Spectacle_Guy_Debord_pdf - named on Bishop's opening page of Chapter 1 as the most-cited reference in participatory-art literature, and the target of her claim that participation has merged with spectacle.
  • Books_Culture_as_Performance_Erika_Fischer_Lichte_pdf - the actor/spectator feedback-loop model Bishop's Chapters 8-9 assume.
  • Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf - intermediality in performance, adjacent to her theatre-history method.
  • Books_Digital_Performance_Steve_Dixon_pdf - the delegated and live-versus-mediated performance field her Chapter 8 opens.
  • Books_Cruising_Utopia_Jose_Esteban_Munoz_pdf - a rival account of performance's social and political efficacy.
  • Books_Capitalist_Realism_Mark_Fisher_pdf - shares the diagnosis that anti-market and participatory rhetorics are absorbed by neoliberalism, which is Bishop's conclusion.

Guy Debord, The Society of the Spectacle (1967; Ken Knabb translation, 2014)

Citation

Debord, Guy. The Society of the Spectacle. Translated and annotated by Ken Knabb. Berkeley, CA: Bureau of Public Secrets, 2014. Originally published as La Societe du Spectacle, Paris: Editions Buchet-Chastel, 1967; reissued Champ Libre 1971 and Gallimard 1992. ISBN 978-0-939682-06-5. [title page, copyright page, contents and back cover of the PDF] Original PDF filename is not recoverable from the text layer; the slug Books_The_Society_of_the_Spectacle_Guy_Debord_pdf is the only record of it.

Translation warning. This file is Ken Knabb's 2014 translation, not Donald Nicholson-Smith's (Zone Books) or Fredy Perlman's (Black and Red). Thesis 1 therefore does not read as it is usually cited: the familiar wording about the whole life of those societies in which modern conditions of production prevailing and presenting itself as an immense accumulation of spectacles is Nicholson-Smith/Perlman, whereas Knabb has In societies where modern conditions of production prevail, life is presented as an immense accumulation of spectacles (see the quote below, PDF p.11). Any review that quotes Debord should name the translation. The thesis numbers (1-221) are stable across translations and are the correct citation anchor; page numbers are not.

Coverage

Book (complete, with the translator's annotations). 161 PDF pages, c.47,900 words: title page, copyright page, contents, Knabb's Preface, the nine chapters (theses 1-221), Notes keyed to the numbered theses, Index, back-cover blurb. Printed folios are legible and run nine behind the PDF page (printed p.90 = PDF p.99). Text layer is OCR-derived and uneven: 938 soft hyphens inside words at line breaks, plus frequent dropped spaces ("ofunification", "itselfas", "ofthis"). In the quotes below I have removed the soft hyphens, re-joined the broken words and restored dropped spaces; nothing else has been altered. Chapters: 1 Separation Perfected; 2 The Commodity as Spectacle; 3 Unity and Division Within Appearances; 4 The Proletariat as Subject and Representation; 5 Time and History; 6 Spectacular Time; 7 Territorial Management; 8 Negation and Consumption Within Culture; 9 Ideology Materialized.

Argument in brief

221 numbered theses arguing that the spectacle is not mass media and not visual excess but the present stage of capitalist society, in which everything directly lived has receded into representation. Debord builds from separation as the origin and substance of the spectacle, through the commodity as spectacle, the proletariat as subject and representation, spectacular time as the consumption of pseudo-cyclical time, territorial management (urbanism as the technology of separation, producing atomised pseudo-communities), culture's negation and consumption, to ideology materialised as the spectacle's own self-justification. The mode of writing is detournement: Marx, Hegel, Feuerbach, Lukacs and Lefebvre are rewritten, and Knabb's annotations document each borrowing. The register is declarative and totalising rather than empirical; there are no case studies and no data.

Key ideas

  • the spectacle - not a collection of images but a social relation between people mediated by images (thesis 4); a materialised worldview (5); the heart of real society's unreality (6).
  • separation - the alpha and omega of the spectacle (25); everything directly lived recedes into representation (1).
  • image / appearance - appearances are socially organised and monopolised (10, 12); the spectacle's sole message is that what appears is good and what is good appears (12).
  • spectacular time and space - Chapters 5-7: time becomes a consumable commodity; tourism circulates people to banalised places (168); urbanism separates (171) and reconstitutes isolated individuals as pseudo-community (172).
  • unilateral communication - media concentration produces a one-way relation: spectators are linked solely by their one-way relationship to a centre that isolates them from each other (29). Directly usable against the assumption that surround or shared viewing is inherently mutual.
  • detournement - the writing practice itself, and the Situationist method of turning the culture industry's language against itself; documented thesis by thesis in Knabb's notes.

Quotes

  • "In societies where modern conditions of production prevail, life is presented as an immense accumulation of spectacles. Everything that was directly lived has receded into a representation." (PDF p.11 / printed p.2) — thesis 1: the founding image of the accumulated spectacle (Knabb translation).
  • "The spectacle is not a collection of images; it is a social relation between people that is mediated by images." (PDF p.11 / printed p.2) — thesis 4: the spectacle as a social relation, not a collection of images.
  • "The spectacle cannot be understood as a mere visual excess produced by mass-media technologies. It is a worldview that has actually been materialized, that has become an objective reality." (PDF p.11 / printed p.2) — thesis 5: spectacle as materialised worldview, not just media.
  • "Its sole message is: "What appears is good; what is good appears." The passive acceptance it demands is already effectively imposed by its monopoly of appearances, its manner of appearing without allowing any reply." (PDF p.13 / printed p.4) — thesis 12: the spectacle's demand for passive acceptance.
  • "Separation is the alpha and omega of the spectacle." (PDF p.17 / printed p.8) — thesis 25: separation as the alpha and omega.
  • "Spectators are linked solely by their one-way relationship to the very center that keeps them isolated from each other. The spectacle thus reunites the separated, but it reunites them only in their separateness." (PDF p.19 / printed p.10) — thesis 29: one-way relation, isolation, reunification in separateness.
  • "The spectacle is a permanent opium war designed to force people to equate goods with commodities and to equate satisfaction with a survival that expands according to its own laws." (PDF p.26 / printed p.17) — thesis 44: spectacle as permanent opium war (commodity/satisfaction).
  • "Tourism-human circulation packaged for consumption, a by-product of the circulation of commodities-is the opportunity to go and see what has been banalized." (PDF p.99 / printed p.90) — thesis 168: tourism, circulation, the banalised place.
  • "While all the technical forces of capitalism contribute toward various forms of separation, urbanism provides the material foundation for those forces and prepares the ground for their deployment. It is the very technology of separation." (PDF p.100 / printed p.91) — thesis 171: urbanism as the technology of separation.
  • "Urbanism is the modern method for solving the ongoing problem of safeguarding class power by atomizing the workers, who had been dangerously brought together by the conditions of urban production." (PDF p.100 / printed p.91) — thesis 172: urbanism, atomisation and pseudo-community.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the negative pole of the review. Any fulldome work that surrounds, addresses or absorbs an audience can be read as spectacular in Debord's sense, and thesis 4 forecloses the easy defence that immersion is not merely images. Bishop's argument that participation has merged with spectacle is unreadable without this text, and theses 168-172 give a language for the dome or planetarium as a built environment of circulation and separation rather than a neutral venue. Cite thesis number plus translation; do not carry Debord page numbers across editions. What it cannot carry: any account of the apparatus of projection, of an individual work, or of an audience that is anything other than duped.

Limits

1967: predates the dome, digital media, the planetarium revival and any empirical audience research. No account of the spectator's experience beyond alienation, no ambivalence about participation, no counter-example. The argument is totalising to the point of being untestable, since the spectacle explains both submission and resistance. The Knabb translation differs in wording from the version most readers know, and the text layer of this PDF is damaged enough that only cleaned quotes should be used.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_Artificial_Hells_Claire_Bishop_pdf - treats Debord as the reference point of participatory-art discourse and attacks the assumption that participation escapes the spectacle.
  • Books_The_Practice_of_Everyday_Life_Michel_de_Certeau_pdf - supplies the tactical, everyday counter-term Debord's system leaves out.
  • Books_Capitalist_Realism_Mark_Fisher_pdf - post-1989 restatement of the spectacle thesis under neoliberalism.
  • Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf - the immersion tradition Debord would read as spectacular.

Mark Fisher, Capitalist Realism (2009)

Citation

Fisher, Mark. Capitalist Realism. [title page, PDF p.2, which carries only the two words "Capitalist Realism"; author biography on the same page]. Original PDF filename Books_Capitalist_Realism_Mark_Fisher_pdf.pdf. Publisher, place, year and subtitle cannot be read from this file: there is no title verso/copyright page in the scan (PDF p.1 is blank, PDF p.2 is the title plus author bio, PDF p.3 the dedication), and the back cover (PDF p.88) yields only damaged fragments — the words "•• III" (over the Philosophy category line), a price block, and the barcode digits "9 78184 43171". The cover carries the imprint URL, itself damaged, as "www.o- books.net" — which points to Zero Books, the publisher of Mark Fisher, Capitalist Realism: Is There No Alternative? (2009). Publisher, place, year and subtitle are not stated anywhere in this file; cite them from a copy, not from this scan.

Coverage

Book (complete text), 89 PDF pages, ~25,100 extracted words. All nine chapters are present and continuous, from printed p.1 to printed p.79, followed by the back cover (PDF pp.87–88): It's easier to imagine the end of the world than the end of capitalism; What if you held a protest and everyone came?; Capitalism and the Real; Reflexive impotence, immobilization and liberal communism; October 6 1979: 'Don't let yourself get attached to anything'; All that is solid melts into PR: market Stalinism and bureaucratic anti-production; …capitalist realism as dreamwork and memory disorder; 'There's no central exchange'; Marxist Supernanny. Folio/PDF offset is a constant 6 (PDF p.23 = printed p.17, PDF p.39 = printed p.33, PDF p.85 = printed p.79), so printed pages below are derived arithmetically and confirmed against the folios that do survive. Extraction quality — read this before quoting. The text layer of this scan is character-level unreliable. Body text contains recognisable OCR substitutions that change real words: the film Children of Men is printed as Childrell of Mell, and in places Childml of Mell; the book’s most-quoted sentence reads it is now impossible even to imagille a coherent alternative to it; Picasso’s Guernica is Cuemica; P.D. James is P.O. James; lifeworlds appears as Iifeworlds; there are stray capitals inside words (noW, Wisely). Running heads are mangled throughout (It's easier to imagine the end of the worLd .., What if you held a protest...7, / Capitalist Realism, I Capitalist Realism). Every quotation below was checked character by character against the extracted string; the defective word in the first quote is flagged. Argue from this text, but do not type a Fisher quotation from memory into the review.

Argument in brief

Fisher's claim is that capitalism has ceased to be one position among others and has become the horizon of thought itself: the widespread sense that capitalism is the only viable political and economic system and that no coherent alternative can even be imagined. He calls that condition capitalist realism and treats it as a cultural atmosphere, not a doctrine — realism "in itself" rather than a particular realism. His evidence is cultural and clinical: contemporary dystopian film, gangster rap and neo-noir, the anti-globalisation protest that becomes a media spectacle (Live 8), the culture of auditing and targets in public services, and the epidemic of depression and anxiety among the young, which he reads as a privatised form of political depression. Two mechanisms do the analytical work: precorporation — the formatting of desires and hopes by capital before they can form as opposition — and the installation of a business ontology in which running hospitals, schools and universities as businesses appears simply obvious. He closes by arguing that mental distress should be converted into "effective antagonisms" directed at capital, and that the pervasiveness of capitalist realism is precisely why small alternative possibilities can have a disproportionate effect.

Key ideas

  • Capitalist realism — not a genre or an aesthetic but "the widespread sense that not only is capitalism the only viable political and economic system, but also that it is now impossible even to imagille a coherent alternative to it" (PDF p.8). It is self-reinforcing rather than argued: it works by making its own presuppositions the ground of realism.
  • Business ontology — the naturalisation of market logic across every institution, "including healthcare and education" (PDF p.23); public services defended only in the terms of the business they are told to become.
  • Precorporation — the replacement of subversion-and-incorporation with prior formatting: "'Alternative' and 'independent' don't designate something outside mainstream culture; rather, they are styles, in fact the dominant styles, within the mainstream" (PDF p.15). This is the concept a dome review can borrow for the aesthetics of the immersive.
  • Realism "in itself" — "Capitalist realism is therefore not a particular type of realism; it is more like realism in itself" (PDF p.10). Realist means: compatible with capital.
  • The Real vs reality (via Lacan/Žižek) — the Real as the trauma or system-dysfunction that ideology cannot symbolise; environmental catastrophe is named as one such Real (PDF pp.23–25).
  • Reflexive impotence / immobilisation — the knowledge that things are wrong paired with the certainty that nothing can be done, especially among the young (PDF pp.29–36).
  • The consumer-spectator — the residual subject when belief collapses: "all that is left is the consumer-spectator, trudging through the ruins and the relics" (PDF p.10) — the one figure in the book that is directly useful for thinking about audiences in a spectacle apparatus.
  • Market Stalinism — compulsory performative targets, audits and anti-production in public institutions, a topic that includes the university and therefore artistic research (PDF pp.45–60).
  • Captured discontent — depression and anxiety as affect that has been privatised; the political task is to redirect it outward at capital (PDF p.86).

Quotes

  • "the widespread sense that not only is capitalism the only viable political and economic system, but also that it is now impossible even to imagille a coherent alternative to it" (PDF p.8 / printed p.2) — the definition; note that the scan prints “imagille” for “imagine”.
  • "Capitalist realism is therefore not a particular type of realism; it is more like realism in itself." (PDF p.10 / printed p.4) — short formulation of the concept: realism means compatibility with capital.
  • "Capitalism is what is left when beliefs have collapsed at the level of ritual or symbolic elaboration, and all that is left is the consumer-spectator, trudging through the ruins and the relics." (PDF p.10 / printed p.4) — usable for the audience position inside a spectacle apparatus.
  • "capitalist realism has successfully installed a 'business ontology' in which it is simply obvious that everything in society, including healthcare and education, should be run as a business." (PDF p.23 / printed p.17) — the term to borrow for institutions, funders and venues.
  • "their precorporation: the pre-emptive formatting and shaping of desires, aspirations and hopes by capitalist culture." (PDF p.15 / printed p.9) — the pre-emptive cultural-form line the review can borrow.

Extraction note: the first quotation reads "imagille" for "imagine" in this scan, and Children of Men is scanned as "Childrell of Mell"; the "Alternative"/"independent" line carries straight quotation marks in the source. Check every word against a printed copy before quoting Fisher in the review (see Coverage).

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

Fisher gives the review its why-now sentence and its most sceptical question: a fully enclosed, technically total, participatory image environment can be described as the cultural form of a condition in which no alternative can be imagined, and the immersive is one of the ready-made styles he calls precorporation. Borrowable terms: capitalist realism itself, business ontology (for the funding and packaging of immersive venues and dome projects), precorporation (for the aesthetics of participation), and consumer-spectator (for the dome audience position). It cannot carry anything about performance, screens or spectatorship from evidence — it has no performance examples and no medium theory.

Limits

No performance, theatre, projection or dome content at all: it is political-cultural criticism of film, television, popular music, public-sector management and mental health. It predates fulldome as a term and predates the mainstreaming of VR/360 video, so it has no account of image environments. Its prose is emphatic rather than evidential, and the book is short enough that several claims are asserted rather than argued. The word immersive never appears; the closest concept to immersive cultural form in the text is precorporation. The scan's OCR damage (see Coverage) means direct quotation from this file carries a real risk of transcribing corrupted words.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_The_Society_of_the_Spectacle_Guy_Debord_pdf — the older and more explicit theory of the spectacle-subject that Fisher's "consumer-spectator" silently leans on.
  • Books_Artificial_Hells_Claire_Bishop_pdf — Bishop's counterpart diagnosis of participatory art under neoliberalism; pairs directly with precorporation.
  • Books_Digital_Performance_Steve_Dixon_pdf — Dixon's optimism about a digital avant-garde is exactly what Fisher's frame would interrogate.
  • Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf — supplies the medium-relations vocabulary Fisher lacks.

José Esteban Muñoz, Cruising Utopia: The Then and There of Queer Futurity (2009)

Citation

Muñoz, José Esteban. Cruising Utopia: The Then and There of Queer Futurity. Sexual Cultures series. New York: New York University Press, 2009. ISBN-13 9780814757277 (cloth), 9780814757284 (paper). — taken from the title page (PDF p.1) and the Library of Congress Cataloging-in-Publication block on the copyright page (PDF p.2), which gives the series as (Sexual cultures) and the class mark HQ76.25.M86. Original PDF filename: Books_Cruising_Utopia_Jose_Esteban_Munoz_pdf.pdf. The title-page OCR runs the words together (CruisingUtopia / TheThenandThereofQueerFuturity / JoseEstebanMunoz); the CIP block is the cleaner source.

Coverage

Partial book — front matter + introduction + chapter 1 + the first four pages of the notes. 40 PDF pages, 13,848 words. Not a whole-book reading.

Present, in file order: - Title page, copyright/CIP page, Contents (PDF pp.1–3). The Contents page lists ten chapters, the introduction, the conclusion and the back matter (Notes p.191, Bibliography p.209, Index p.217, About the Author p.223). - Introduction, Feeling Utopia, printed pp.1–18 (PDF pp.4–21). PDF p.11 is a caption page for Warhol's Still-Life (Flowers). - Chapter 1, Queerness as Horizon: Utopian Hermeneutics in the Face of Gay Pragmatism, printed pp.19–32 (PDF pp.22–35). - Notes: Notes to the Introduction printed pp.191–192, Notes to Chapter 1 pp.193–194, and printed p.195 = the first page of Notes to Chapter 2 (PDF pp.36–40). The first line of PDF p.40 is inverted/mirrored OCR (it reads right to left in the extraction).

Missing: roughly 150 of the book's ~190 pages — chapters 2–10 (Ghosts of Public Sex; The Future Is in the Present: Sexual Avant-Gardes and the Performance of Utopia; Gesture, Ephemera, and Queer Feeling; Approaching Kevin Aviance; Cruising the Toilet: LeRoi Jones/Amiri Baraka, Radical Black Traditions, and Queer Futurity; Stages: Queers, Punks, and the Utopian Performative; Utopias Seating Chart: Ray Johnson, Jill Johnston, and Queer Intermedia as System; Just Like Heaven: Queer Utopian Art and the Aesthetic Dimension; A Jeté Out the Window: Fred Herko's Incandescent Illumination; After Jack: Queer Failure, Queer Virtuosity), the conclusion, the color illustrations, the bibliography and the index. Those chapter titles come from the Contents page only; none of those chapters was read, so nothing here can be said about Muñoz's case studies (Warhol's Coke bottles, Kevin Aviance, Amiri Baraka, Fred Herko, Judson dance) beyond what the introduction promises. The Contents page OCR is itself scrambled, so some chapter titles and page numbers there are uncertain.
