OCR/extraction damage (the file was re-OCR'd from a poor scan): dropped spaces throughout; straight/curly quote drift; and, more seriously, whole lines dropped mid-paragraph plus several lines rendered as mirrored or rotated garbage on PDF pp.23, 25, 30, 34 and the top of p.40. Sentences on those pages break off mid-clause. All eight quotes below come from passages that read cleanly; passages that run through a dropped line were not quoted.

Argument in brief

The introduction and chapter 1 set up a utopian hermeneutic for queer critique. Muñoz's claim is that queerness is not a present identity but an ideality — a horizon, something not yet here — and that the job of criticism is to read cultural texts for the not-yet-conscious and the not-yet-here rather than to describe what already exists. He takes the method from Bloch: the past is held as a no-longer-conscious that can be detonated against the present, and hope is treated as a critical affect and a hermeneutic rather than a mood. Concrete utopias, unlike abstract ones, stay tied to historically situated struggles. Against this he sets gay pragmatism — marriage equality, the military, assimilation into what he calls a corrupt and bankrupt social order (his named target is Evan Wolfson's Freedom to Marry) — and straight time, the naturalized temporality that says there is no future but the present, with reproduction as its only promised futurity. The chapter works by reading two texts from the past: a 1971 manifesto by Third World Gay Revolution demanding a new society, and James Schuyler's poem A photograph. From the manifesto he takes a futurity that does utopia, a speech act rather than a description; from the poem, an ecstasy that steps out of linear time and invokes future generations. The aesthetic, on this account, is where a culture stores blueprints of a future it cannot yet inhabit.

Key ideas

  • queerness as horizon — not an identity people possess in the present but a modality of perception; we are not yet queer, and queerness is visible only in the horizon. This keeps the term ontologically humble and non-ossified: fixing queerness in the present loses its critical force to neoliberalism and assimilation.
  • not-yet-here / not-yet-conscious — the future that is the object of critique; queerness and the collective we of the manifesto are both addressed as future formations, not as current populations.
  • the no-longer-conscious — Bloch's term for the past as an active site: the past does things, it is performative rather than static, and it can be pulled into the present to arm a critique of the present.
  • concrete utopia vs abstract utopia — abstract utopias are untethered from any historical consciousness and akin to banal optimism; concrete utopias are relational to historically situated struggles and are the realm of educated hope.
  • educated hope / hope as hermeneutic — hope is a method of reading (Bloch, The Principle of Hope) and an act of critical negation of the present, not a sentiment and not banal optimism.
  • straight time — the self-naturalizing temporality that presents the here and now as inevitable, with reproductive heterosexuality as its only futurity; queerness's time is ecstatic, a stepping out of it.
  • anticipatory illumination / cultural surplus — Bloch's aesthetic term: the surplus of affect and meaning in a work that points past itself, the blueprints and schemata of a forward-dawning futurity that art can carry.
  • the performative past and doing utopia — following Austin, the manifesto is a speech act that calls a future into being (a call to a doing in and for the future); the past is performative because it does things rather than lying fixed.
  • gay pragmatism / homonormativity — the assimilative politics of the present (marriage, the military, freedom as inclusion in a corrupt order), which Muñoz treats as antiutopian and retrograde; he cites Biddy Martin's being ordinary as a more nuanced version of the same pragmatism. The target is vernacular pragmatism, not the philosophical tradition of Peirce, James and Dewey.
  • anti-antirelationality / being singular plural — his quarrel with Bersani and Edelman's No Future: against romances of the negative, he argues for queerness as relationality and collectivity, borrowing Nancy's being singular plural and Sedgwick's reparative reading.

Quotes

  • "QUEERNESS IS NOT yet here. Queerness is an ideality. Put another way, we are not yet queer. We may never touch queerness, but we can feel it as the warm illumination of a horizon imbued with potentiality." (PDF p.4 / printed p.1) — the book's opening thesis; the cleanest statement that queerness is a horizon rather than a present identity.
  • "The here and now is a prison house. We must strive, in the face of the here and now's totalizing rendering of reality, to think and feel a then and there." (PDF p.4 / printed p.1) — the present as confining and insufficient; source of the book's title phrase the then and there.
  • "The aesthetic, especially the queer aesthetic, frequently contains blueprints and schemata of a forward-dawning futurity. Both the ornamental and the quotidian can contain a map of the utopia that is queerness." (PDF p.4 / printed p.1) — the line that licenses reading an art form as a mechanism for holding open a future.
  • "Abstract utopias falter for Bloch because they are untethered from any historical consciousness. Concrete utopias are relational to historically situated struggles, a collectivity that is actualized or potential." (PDF p.6 / printed p.3) — the distinction a dome or immersion argument needs if it wants to claim concrete rather than escapist utopianism.
  • "At the center of Cruising Utopia there is the idea of hope, which is both a critical affect and a methodology. Bloch offers us hope as a hermeneutic, and from the point of view of political struggles today, such a critical optic is nothing short of necessary in order to combat the force of political pessimism." (PDF p.7 / printed p.4) — hope as method, not sentiment; usable framing for a practice-based method section.
  • "A turn to the no-longer-conscious enabled a critical hermeneutics attuned to comprehending the not-yet-here. This temporal calculus performed and utilized the past and the future as armaments to combat the devastating logic of the world of the here and now, a notion of nothing existing outside the sphere of the current moment, a version of reality that naturalizes cultural logics such as capitalism and heteronormativity." (PDF p.15 / printed p.12) — the direct counter-formulation to capitalist realism: a present closed to alternatives is named and then attacked.
  • "The present is not enough. It is impoverished and toxic for queers and other people who do not feel the privilege of majoritarian belonging, normative tastes, and “rational” expectations." (PDF p.30 / printed p.27) — blunt statement of the refusal of the present as sufficient; pairs with the critique of gay pragmatism at printed pp.27–31.
  • "To see queerness as horizon is to perceive it as a modality of ecstatic time in which the temporal stranglehold that I describe as straight time is interrupted or stepped out of." (PDF p.35 / printed p.32) — the chapter's closing formulation, defining the horizon concept in temporal terms.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the review's theoretical counterweight to Debord's spectacle and Fisher's capitalist realism. Where those sources say the image and the present absorb all alternatives, Muñoz supplies the vocabulary for the opposite claim: a cultural form (here a poem and a manifesto) can be read as carrying blueprints and schemata of a forward-dawning futurity, and criticism can be conducted as hope rather than as the exposure of false consciousness. For a dome or performance-lecture argument the usable moves are: queerness-as-horizon as a model for a form that is not yet finished or fully inhabited; the no-longer-conscious / not-yet-here pairing as a way of saying a work addresses a spectator who does not yet exist; and the present is not enough as the rhetorical hinge against pragmatic, presentist justifications of immersive media.

What it cannot carry: anything about domes, planetaria, projection, immersion, screens, spectatorship, liveness or practice-based research method — none of those terms appears in this file. Nor can it carry Muñoz's own performance cases; his chapters on performance, intermedia and the utopian performative are all in the part of the book not present here, so the performance-studies weight of the book must be cited from the full text or not at all.

Limits

  • Introduction-and-chapter-one-only reading. The book's actual archive — Warhol, Amiri Baraka, Kevin Aviance, Fred Herko, Judson dance, queer intermedia — is absent from this file; every quote above sits on printed pp.1–18 or pp.19–32.
  • The utopian method is asserted and demonstrated only on a manifesto and a lyric poem. Nothing here shows how it would be applied to a staged, projected or time-based work, which is exactly the application a fulldome review would want.
  • Bloch is used instrumentally, and Muñoz says so: he takes Bloch not as orthodoxy but to open a line in queer thought, and he acknowledges Bloch's poor record on gender and sexuality and waves it past.
  • The political target is US same-sex-marriage politics circa 2009; the pragmatism discussed is vernacular, not the philosophical tradition of Peirce, James and Dewey, which Muñoz explicitly disclaims.
  • Some of the most quotable anti-pragmatism sentences sit on pages where the scan drops lines (pp.23, 25, 30, 34). They can be read but should be quoted only from a good copy of the book.
  • No bibliography, index or chapter notes beyond printed p.195 are present, so the book's own sources (Bloch, Marcuse, Jameson, Nancy) can be checked only through the four surviving note pages.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_The_Society_of_the_Spectacle_Guy_Debord_pdf — the spectacle thesis the utopian hermeneutic is set against; both describe a present that closes off alternatives.
  • Books_Capitalist_Realism_Mark_Fisher_pdf — a near-contemporary diagnosis of a present with no imaginable outside; Muñoz supplies the affirmative counter-vocabulary.
  • Books_Being_Singular_Plural_Jean_Luc_Nancy_pdf — the ontology Muñoz borrows to make queerness relational and collective rather than singular.
  • Books_Artificial_Hells_Claire_Bishop_pdf — the sceptical case on participatory and socially engaged art, i.e. the counter-argument to reading art as anticipatory illumination.
  • Fulldome_Articles_005_Towards_a_Fulldome_Manifesto_final_proof_pdf — a fulldome manifesto that reads well against Muñoz's account of a manifesto as a call to a doing in and for the future.
  • Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf — immersion as an art-historical category, useful for testing whether dome immersion is utopian or merely spectacular.

Victor Turner, The Ritual Process: Structure and Anti-Structure (1969)

Citation

Turner, Victor W. The Ritual Process: Structure and Anti-Structure. Chicago: Aldine Publishing Company, 1969. Cornell Paperbacks edition, Ithaca, NY: Cornell University Press, 1977 (seventh printing 1991). ISBN 0-8014-9163-0. The Lewis Henry Morgan Lectures, 1966, University of Rochester. [Copyright page and cover of the scanned PDF; the copyright page carries the publication history — first published 1969 by Aldine, first Cornell Paperbacks printing 1977, seventh printing 1991, Foreword to the Cornell Paperbacks edition copyright 1977.] The scan's final page is stamped "Bilkent University Library". The original PDF filename is not recoverable from the text layer; the slug is the record.

Coverage

Book (complete): 223 PDF pages, 86,450 words. Cover, series list, dedication, copyright, Foreword to the 1977 Cornell Paperbacks edition (PDF pp.5–7), Contents, five chapters, Bibliography (from PDF p.213), Index. The body ends on PDF p.212 (printed p.203) — the text stops on the Hell's Angels and Vice Lords material, not on a flourish. On the pages quoted below the printed folio is the PDF page minus 9 (checked against the folios visible on the dumped pages), so both numbers are given.

Text layer: OCR of a library scan. Legible throughout but damaged in patches, in three ways. (a) Letter-spacing inside words is frequent and heavy (T h e, V a n Gennep, c o m m u n i t a s); I have closed these spaces up in quoting and changed nothing else. (b) Locally illegible lines, where underlining, marginal marks or two-up page edges defeated the OCR — worst on the chapter 4 opening page (PDF p.141), where the first two lines of the chapter come out as a string of stray characters, caret marks and underscores with only a few real words recoverable, and similarly mid-page on PDF pp.176, 180 and 202. No quote below crosses such a span. (c) A scatter of wrong letters inside otherwise clean sentences; the one that falls inside a quote is left exactly as the text has it and marked. Front matter (title page, series page, contents) is noisy but readable enough to fix the citation.

Argument in brief

The five Lewis Henry Morgan Lectures of 1966 move from detailed Ndembu ritual (the Isoma rites for a woman who is barren or miscarries; the twinship rite Wubwang'u) to a general model of society as a process. Turner takes Van Gennep's three-phase account of rites de passage — separation, margin (limen, "threshold"), aggregation — and makes the middle phase, liminality, the analytic centre. In the threshold condition the ordinary classifications of status, property and role are suspended: the initiates are betwixt and between, levelled, passive, and bound to one another by an intense egalitarian comradeship he calls communitas. Communitas is not a place or an institution but a modality of social relationship, unstructured and undifferentiated, a moment in and out of time, and he distinguishes three versions of it — existential or spontaneous, normative, ideological. The central claim is that structure and communitas need each other and that the liminal interval is productive rather than merely disorderly: it is society's subjunctive mood, in which suppositions and possibilities become legitimate and new models of how people might live together are generated, models that can later displace the arrangements governing ordinary life. The argument is built comparatively — Ndembu material anchors it, but Tallensi, Ashanti, Nyakyusa, Bemba, European millenarianism and Franciscan history, South African independent churches (Sundkler), Bengali Sahajiya and Baul devotionalism (Dimock), Shakespeare's Tempest, Buber, Morgan, Chicago gangs and the Hell's Angels, Halloween and the British Army's Christmas dinner are all pulled in — and it rests on symbols being multivocal, carrying many meanings and working at several psychobiological levels at once. Chapter 5 adds a second type of liminality, status reversal (ritual dominance by inferiors in cyclical rites that end by reinforcing the structure), and extends the model to religions of humility founded by high-status figures. The book closes by claiming that society is a dialectical process of successive structure and communitas phases and that there is a human need to participate in both — asserted there, not demonstrated.

Key ideas

  • state / transition — state covers any type of stable or recurrent condition that is culturally recognized, more inclusive than status or office; passage between states runs through separation, margin (limen, threshold), aggregation (PDF p.103 / printed p.94).
  • liminality, liminal personae — the threshold condition of threshold people: ambiguous, neither here nor there, slipping through the network of classifications that locates positions in cultural space; likened to death, the womb, invisibility, darkness, bisexuality, the wilderness, an eclipse. Its imagery is negative (possessing nothing, no insignia of rank) and its behavioural rules are passivity, humility, silence, implicit obedience, sexual continence, near-nakedness or nakedness (PDF pp.104, 112–113). He describes the neophyte as a blank slate on which the knowledge and wisdom of the group is inscribed, in those respects that pertain to the new status (PDF p.112).
  • communitas — the unstructured or rudimentarily structured and relatively undifferentiated comitatus, community, or even communion of equal individuals that emerges in the liminal period; a modality of social relationship rather than an area of common living, and therefore not the same thing as intimacy between people who already know each other (PDF pp.105 / 96, 146 / 137).
  • the three modalities — existential or spontaneous communitas (immediate, unique, socially transient, the winged moment as it flies); normative communitas (existential communitas organised under time, the need to mobilise resources and to hold the group under social control, so that it becomes a perduring social system); ideological communitas (Utopian models that describe the outward form of an inward experience and prescribe the conditions that would let such experience flourish). Normative and ideological communitas are already within the domain of structure, and every spontaneous communitas in history is fated to what most people see as a decline and fall into structure and law (PDF pp.141 / 132, 143 / 134). The normative definition falls on one of the illegible lines and is summarised here, not quoted.
  • structure — used in the British social-anthropological sense (an arrangement of mutually dependent institutions and the institutional organisation of positions and actors), not in Lévi-Strauss's sense of logical categories; in liminal phases the first is simplified or eliminated while myth and ritual are elaborated (PDF pp.175–176 / 166–167).
  • anti-structure — the book's title term, but the term itself belongs to the 1977 Foreword rather than the 1969 body, whose vocabulary is structureless, undifferentiated, structural inferiority. There antistructural liminality is defined as the reflexivity of the social process and as its subjunctive mood (PDF p.6).
  • liminal vs liminoid — not drawn in this volume. Liminoid appears nowhere in the text (zero hits). The distinction between obligatory liminality and the optional, leisure, consumer liminoid of industrial societies is made in Turner's later essay "Liminal to Liminoid, in Play, Flow and Ritual" (1974) and in From Ritual to Theatre (1982). A review that needs the optional or aesthetic version of liminality must take it from those sources. Same caution for the often-quoted line about liminality as a realm of pure possibility: that is The Forest of Symbols (1967), not this book.
  • multivocality of symbols — ritual symbols mean many things at once, sit at nodal points where several classificatory sets intersect, and move people at many psychobiological levels simultaneously; they are said to incite men to action as well as to thought, and in isolation their multivocality is the striking feature while holistically each sense exemplifies a single principle (PDF pp.51 / 42, 138 / 129).
  • status elevation vs status reversal — two types of liminality: the irreversible movement of a novice to a higher position, and the cyclical or calendrical rites in which those who habitually occupy low positions exercise ritual authority over superiors who accept their own ritual degradation. Both reinforce structure: in the first the gaps between positions are reaffirmed, in the second inversion is a new perspective on structure, not anomie (PDF pp.176 / 167, 209–210 / 200–201).
  • the powers of the weak / structural inferiority — the hypothesis that persons or principles falling in the interstices of structure, on its margins, or on its lowest rungs carry sacred power: neophytes, court jesters, subjugated autochthones, holy mendicants, millenarian movements, monastic orders, small nations (PDF pp.118 / 109, 134 / 125). From the standpoint of those maintaining structure, he notes, sustained communitas appears dangerous and anarchical and has to be hedged with prescriptions and prohibitions (PDF p.118 / 109).
  • physical setting — ritual space is made by bounding: branches are bent into a ring around the whole scene of activity to make the sacred space, with a clearing hoed out (mukombela), so that a small realm of order is created in the formless bush; the ring (chipang'u) is the same word as the fence round a chief's dwelling. A seclusion hut is built for the patient outside the ring of huts that constitutes the village. The animal's burrow supplies the reference point of orientation for the spatial structure of the sacred site, and Isoma's oppositions are laid along longitudinal, latitudinal and altitudinal planes of ritual space (PDF pp.30 / 21, 32 / 23, 48 / 39).
  • institutionalisation of liminality — in complex large-scale societies liminality itself becomes a religious or quasi-religious state and re-enters structure with its own roles and positions: where tribal society had the seclusion lodge, complex society has the church (PDF p.176 / 167).
  • the gathered body — the text's references are to the whole community and the community's responsibility for what falls outside the norm, to people assembled to witness an installation, to a mother of twins dancing and soliciting before the onlookers, and (through a colonial-era account he quotes) to a king-elect surrounded by a crowd that abuses him before the enthronement (PDF pp.54 / 45, 58 / 49, 114 / 105, 180 / 171).
  • the human condition — structure and communitas as immortal antagonists that together constitute what he calls the human condition as regards a person's relations with fellow human beings; even the simplest societies distinguish them, and the distinction gets symbolic expression in the attributes of liminality, marginality and inferiority (PDF p.139 / 130).

Quotes

  • "More than once I have been accused of overgeneralizing and of misapplying concepts like \"liminality\" and \"communitas.\"" (PDF p.5, 1977 Foreword) — Turner's own statement of the charge against him, made by the author in the edition most libraries hold; the frame for how far the concepts may be borrowed.
  • "The antistructural liminality provided in the cores of ritual and aesthedc forms represents the reflexivity of the social process, wherein society becomes at once subject and direct object; it represents also its subjunctive mood, where suppositions, desires, hypotheses, possibilities, and so forth, all become legitimate." (PDF p.6, 1977 Foreword) — the compact statement that liminality is productive rather than merely disorderly; the source text has the OCR error "aesthedc" for "aesthetic", left as extracted. Note that this is the 1977 Foreword: in the 1969 body the argument is carried by structureless and undifferentiated, not by anti-structure.
  • "to create a sacred space that rapidly achieves structure. To ring something around is a persistent theme of Ndembu ritual; it is usually accompanied by the process of making a clearing (mukombela) by hoe. In this way a small realm of order is created in the formless milieu of the bush." (PDF p.32 / printed p.23) — the setting does the work: enclosure, not content, produces the sanctified place.
  • "Van Gennep has shown that all rites of passage or \"transition\" are marked by three phases: separation, margin (or limen, signifying \"threshold\" in Latin), and aggregation." (PDF p.103 / printed p.94) — the borrowed skeleton of the whole model, and the exact wording to cite when attributing it to Van Gennep (1909).
  • "The attributes of liminality or of liminal personae (\"threshold people\") are necessarily ambiguous, since this condition and these persons elude or slip through the network of classifications that normally locate states and positions in cultural space. Liminal entities are neither here nor there; they are betwixt and between the positions assigned and arrayed by law, custom, convention, and ceremonial." (PDF p.104 / printed p.95) — the definition of liminality; betwixt and between as the quotable phrase.
  • "The second, which emerges recognizably in the liminal period, is of society as an unstructured or rudimentarily structured and relatively undifferentiated comitatus, community, or even communion of equal individuals who submit together to the general authority of the ritual elders." (PDF p.105 / printed p.96) — the definition of communitas as a model of society, set against the structured, differentiated, hierarchical model.
  • "The wisdom (mana) that is imparted in sacred liminality is not just an aggregation of words and sentences; it has ontological value, it refashions the very being of the neophyte." (PDF p.112 / printed p.103) — the strongest thing in the book for the performance-lecture and plain-lecture end of the spectrum: instruction inside the liminal frame is claimed to constitute the person rather than transmit content.
  • "makes a ritual fence around the new chief's dwelling, and prays as follows to the shades of former chiefs, before the people who have assembled to witness the installation" (PDF p.114 / printed p.105) — one of the very few places where an assembled body is described as witnessing; note that it is the community assembled to witness an installation, not spectators at a show.
  • "Each of these productions has a multivocal character, having many meanings, and each is capable of moving people at many psychobiological levels simultaneously." (PDF p.138 / printed p.129) — multivocality as the reason a symbol (or an image) can address a group that does not share one reading.
  • "existential or spontaneous communitas—approximately what the hippies today would call \"a happening,\" and William Blake might have called \"the winged moment as it flies\" or, later, \"mutual forgiveness of each vice\"" (PDF p.141 / printed p.132) — the first of the three senses, in the book's own terms and tied by Turner himself to the Happening; the enumeration given here is on a legible line, but the definitions after it run onto illegible lines, so the normative and ideological senses are paraphrased above rather than quoted.
  • "if liminality is regarded as a time and place of withdrawal from normal modes of social action, it can be seen as potentially a period of scrutinization of the central values and axioms of the culture in which it occurs" (PDF p.176 / printed p.167) — time and place together, and the productive reading of withdrawal as scrutiny of a culture's axioms: the most directly transferable sentence in the book for a dome argument.
  • "The Bauls, like St. Francis, were \"troubadours of God,\" and it might be fitting to close this chapter with one of their songs, which clearly indicates how the spirit of Vaisnava communitas has persisted into the world today" (PDF p.174 / printed p.165) — flagged: the sentence immediately following this one is Turner's verdict on the devotional song, and it is where the argument stops testing and starts affirming.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the corpus's account of what happens to a group of people in a bounded space during a shared event, which is precisely the social claim a dome performance makes: Turner supplies the mechanism vocabulary (liminality, communitas in its three senses, anti-structure, the subjunctive interval, the multivocal symbol, status reversal) and the argument that such an interval is productive rather than merely a lapse from ordinary life. He also gives the review its most useful statement about the physical setting: the bounded, cleared, ringed site is what makes the state possible, which is a claim about enclosure rather than about content — the sentence a dome argument wants, with the enclosure doing the work. What it cannot carry is spectatorship. In this book the gathered body is the community that already owns the ritual; there is no audience distinct from the participants, no paying public, no author or performer separated from those assembled, no image, screen, projection or apparatus, and no aesthetic theory of any kind. The word audience barely occurs in the text at all. A review citing Turner for a dome audience is therefore importing a claim about a community's obligatory ritual into a claim about voluntary spectatorship, and should say so.

Limits

Method and its cost: this is comparative anthropology built on Turner's own Ndembu fieldwork (Northern Rhodesia, later Zambia, 1950s), delivered as the Lewis Henry Morgan Lectures at Rochester in 1966, with the comparison running through the sources of British social anthropology at that moment — Frazer, Tylor, Durkheim, Van Gennep, Monica Wilson, Max Gluckman, Lévi-Strauss — plus Shakespeare, Buber, Lewis Henry Morgan, Sundkler on South African separatism, Dimock on Bengali Vaisnavism, Norman Cohn on millenarianism, Hunter S. Thompson on the Hell's Angels, and a Rochester colleague's unpublished Chicago gang manuscripts. He states his rules for evidence: no ritual was ever staged for the anthropologist, and interpretations were cross-checked with ritual specialists and non-specialists until they were mutually consistent, which he takes to constitute the standardized hermeneutics of Ndembu culture rather than individual eccentricity (PDF pp.17–19 / printed pp.8–10). But the book is not a report of a controlled study; his informants' claims about medicines, shades and curses are data about belief, and the generalisations about all societies outrun what the data support. The 1977 Foreword answers critics who say liminality and communitas over-generalise with the book's citation record, not with evidence — worth noting before borrowing.

The theological ending: from chapter 4 onward the argument moves from describing communitas to valuing it. He writes that spontaneous communitas is very frequently associated with mystical power and is regarded as a charism or grace sent by the deities or ancestors (PDF p.147 / printed p.138). The chapter then ends by presenting the words of a Bengali devotional song, sung by the Bauls, as the authentic voice of spontaneous communitas, and two pages earlier reads a Bob Dylan record sleeve, with the singer flanked by Bauls, as the convergence of Eastern and Western communitas-bearers (PDF pp.173–174 / printed pp.164–165). Chapter 5's religions of humility and the closing claim of a human need to participate in both structure and communitas (PDF p.212 / printed p.203) are asserted rather than demonstrated. That is a position — a quasi-religious valuation of togetherness — not a finding, and a review that borrows communitas borrows the valuation with it: it should say which parts it is using as description and which as theology.

Other limits: written 1969 (this scan is the 1991 printing with the 1977 Foreword), and the idiom shows it — men for people throughout, the hippies and the Hell's Angels as contemporary reference points, millenarian and cargo-cult theory as it then stood. There is no account of mediation, no media audience, no reception research and no measurement of any kind; nothing on image, projection, screen or architecture, and the single gesture towards modern media is a one-line list of rituals, carnivals, dramas and films in the 1977 Foreword (PDF p.6). The late chapters (Halloween, fraternity hazings, the Vice Lords, motorcycle gangs) stretch the model to industrial leisure groups without the term that would later do that work; treat them as the seam where liminality is being forced, not as settled theory.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf — the folder's direct borrower of this concept: quoting Turner's Forest of Symbols on liminality as a realm of pure possibility, it renames the immersant a liminar who is in time yet timeless, and carries the liminoid term forward to immersive work.
  • Fulldome_Articles_005_Towards_a_Fulldome_Manifesto_final_proof_pdf — the field's own version of the same social claim, describing domes as causeways for beings to come together in physical and virtual space, in the round, as civilizations have gathered for thousands of years around new ideas: the claim Turner's vocabulary would be borrowed to underwrite.
  • Books_Spheres_Bubbles_pdf — Sloterdijk on bounded interiors as the form of shared life; the other route to a theory of the dome as an inside, without ritual and without community.
  • Expanded_White_Audience_Participation_in_Theatre_pdf — the corpus's explicit spectator-participation typology, i.e. the empirical audience material Turner does not have.
  • Expanded_Goffman_Forms_of_Talk_pdf — the lecture as a situated gathering with its own participation framework; the plain-lecture end of the spectrum that the claim about wisdom imparted in sacred liminality would have to meet.
  • Books_Cruising_Utopia_Jose_Esteban_Munoz_pdf — the utopian performative, performance studies' later descendant of the communitas claim; check Muñoz's own citations before attributing the link to him.

Quotes below are verbatim from the txt with only line-break whitespace repaired.

Argument in brief

The book stakes out the public realm — the social territory a city is made of — as a proper object of sociology, against a long tradition (Spykman, Simmel, Wirth, Milgram read as "stimulus overload") that treated public life as empty, formal and asocial. Lofland's counter-claim is that what passes between strangers is as thoroughly social as what passes between intimates, and that it has its own norms, culture and pleasures. She calls the realm a regio incognita and blames its unmapped state on three things: the assumption that public activity has no content, the fact that most scholars only crossed the realm on their way somewhere else, and a disciplinary contempt for the subject. She organises the book in two parts: characteristics of the realm, then its opponents (antiurbanism, privatism, hostile architecture). In the fragment here she gets as far as defining her terms and mapping the realm into three — private, parochial, public — and arguing that realms are social rather than physical territories.

Key ideas

  • Public realm — her provisional definition: "those areas of urban settlements in which individuals in copresence tend to be personally unknown or only categorically known to one another." She treats "public realm" and "urban public space" as near-synonyms at the outset, then separates them: public space is a legal/accessibility category (Webster's: "accessible or visible to all members of the community"), whereas the public realm is a sociological one, and the two come apart.
  • Stranger — used in the ordinary dictionary sense of biographical stranger, "a person with whom one has had no personal acquaintance". She explicitly sets this against the Simmelian tradition (via Karp and Yoels) that reserves "stranger" for the cultural stranger, "those who occupy symbolic worlds different from our own". Her point: the realm holds both, since its inhabitants often share neither acquaintance nor symbolic world.
  • Private / parochial / public realms — a trichotomy taken from Albert Hunter (1985), replacing her earlier private/public dichotomy: private = household and kin/friend networks (ties of intimacy); parochial = neighbourhood, workplace, acquaintance networks (a sense of commonality); public = strangers and the street. Cities are, in her terms, the only settlement form that routinely and persistently contains all three; tribes, villages and small towns contain only the first two.
  • Realms are social, not physical, territories — whether a given physical space contains a realm, and which realm, follows from "the proportions and densities of relationship types present", not from legal or cultural designation. An empty residence contains no realm; a legally public street in a dense-acquaintance small city may be almost entirely parochial. Realms can therefore be "out of place" — she extends Anselm Strauss's locations (communal) vs locales (stranger) distinction to make this point.
  • Categorical knowing — knowing someone only "in terms of occupational or other nonpersonal identity categories (for example, bus driver-customer)". This is the second half of the public realm's definition and matters as much as outright strangerhood.
  • Civil inattention — named once, as a cross-reference: "When we speak of norms like 'civil inattention' (see Chapter 2)" — a mutual willingness to concede a thin layer of private space around the body in shared nonprivate space. Chapter 2, where the norm is actually developed, is not in this file.
  • Pioneers of the public realm — Gregory Stone, Jane Jacobs, Erving Goffman, William H. Whyte, the four she credits with breaking the "asocial" consensus. She notes that the phrase "public realm" appears nowhere in their work and that three of the four wandered into the realm only in passing.
  • Regio incognita — the realm as unmapped territory. Her three reasons for the gap: the realm was assumed barren; scholars were "passing through"; and the subject is dismissed as unimportant or immoral, which she calls a discouragement built into social science's reward system.

Quotes

  • "you can begin to think of urban public space or the public realm as a kind of kingdom, one that is inhabited importantly, though not entirely, by persons who are unacquainted with one another: a "world of strangers" as it were" (PDF p.16 / printed p.xv) — the opening frame; the plainest statement that the realm is composed of strangers.
  • "the city provides, on a permanent basis, an environment composed importantly of persons who are personally unknown to one another-composed importantly of strangers" (PDF p.12 / printed p.xi) — strangerhood as a standing structural feature of the urban environment, not an incident.
  • "I am here using stranger in the quite straightforward, everyday, and dictionary sense of "a person with whom one has had no personal acquaintance"" (PDF p.28 / printed p.7) — the definition of stranger as a biographical, not a cultural, relation.
  • "The public realm is constituted of those areas of urban settlements in which individuals in copresence tend to be personally unknown or only categorically known to one another." (PDF p.30 / printed p.9) — the core definition to cite.
  • "the private realm is the world of the household and friend and kin networks; the parochial realm is the world of the neighborhood, workplace, or acquaintance networks; and the public realm is the world of strangers and the "street."" (PDF p.31 / printed p.10) — the three-realm map in one sentence.
  • "realms are not geographically or physically rooted pieces of space. They are social, not physical territories." (PDF p.32 / printed p.11) — a room's realm character depends on who is in it, not on the building.
  • "as when its rooms are inundated with strangers who have paid for a "house tour."" (PDF p.33 / printed p.12) — the file's only concrete instance of strangers paying to convert private space into public realm; the nearest thing here to an audience entering a venue (the sentence begins on PDF p.32).
  • "When we speak of norms like "civil inattention" (see Chapter 2) we are speaking about a mutual willingness to concede that there is a thin layer of private space around the bodies of the people with whom we are sharing nonprivate space." (PDF p.33 / printed p.12) — civil inattention as bodily privacy conceded in shared space; the only treatment of it in the file.
  • "Goffman demonstrated eloquently and persuasively that what occurs between two strangers passing on the street is as thoroughly social as what occurs in a conversation between two lovers" (PDF p.25 / printed p.4) — the explicit debt to Goffman, and the sentence that carries her whole position.
  • "a goodly portion of urban public space activity consists of humans responding "immediately" and apparently "unreflectively" to others' bodily movements and expressions (for example, when crossing through a busy intersection or standing in a crowded subway car)" (PDF p.17 / printed p.xvi) — unreflective bodily response as the mode of managing co-presence without interaction.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This source can carry the premise of a dome audience: that a room of mutually unacquainted people is a specific social formation with its own definition, not a null case — and that the relevant distinction is between the personally unknown and the categorically known. It can also carry the argument that a venue's realm character is a function of who is present rather than what the building is, which is directly useful for describing what happens to a space when a paying audience of strangers files in (her "house tour" example is the closest thing in the file to a ticket-buying audience). Its private/parochial/public trichotomy gives a vocabulary for the mixed state of a dome audience: strangers to each other, embedded in parochial clusters (school groups, colleagues, festival pass-holders) and carrying private bubbles in with them. It cannot carry anything about how such an audience behaves together — no material on crowds, spectatorship or gathering is in the fragment.

Limits

  • It is a preview, not the book. No Chapter 2, so no "Principles of Stranger Interaction" and no developed account of civil inattention. No Chapter 3 ("The Relational Web"), so no treatment of weak ties or of the connections the realm affords. No Chapter 4 on pleasures. No Chapters 5–7 on antiurbanism, privatism and architectural assault, so no historical-erosion thesis in the file at all — only the chapter titles in the Contents. No Chapter 8 ("Uses of the Public Realm") and no Epilogue.
  • Search results that confirm absence, not presence: "weak tie" — 0 hits; "civilit" — 0 hits; "erosion/erod" — 0 hits; "spectat", "theatr" and "perform" — 0 hits; "audience" — 1 hit, and it is inside a block quote from Spencer Cahill about whether other scholars* take the field seriously; "crowd" — 1 hit, in "crowded subway car". So the file says nothing about audiences, crowds, spectatorship or performance.
  • No dome, planetarium, immersion, projection or screen anywhere in the file.
  • Writing is US-centred and the 1998 date predates the networked/digital public realm literature; her data are American cities plus historic European examples.
  • The extraction damage listed under Coverage means the few quotes here should be checked against a clean copy before they go into print, above all the ones containing a "!" where an "I" is meant.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Expanded_Goffman_Forms_of_Talk_pdf — Goffman's own interaction order; the primary source Lofland defers to for civil inattention and stranger interaction.
  • Expanded_Kendon_Conducting_Interaction_pdf — co-presence, body movement and interaction management in face-to-face gathering.
  • Expanded_White_Audience_Participation_in_Theatre_pdf — what audiences do, which this file does not address.
  • Expanded_Bligh_Whats_the_Use_of_Lectures_pdf — the lecture as address to a gathered room.
  • Expanded_Lynch_The_Image_of_the_City_pdf — the city as a perceived environment; pairs with her claim that realms are social rather than physical territories.
  • Books_Culture_as_Performance_Erika_Fischer_Lichte_pdf — performance as a frame for social situations, including public ones.

If the research needs White, it needs the book. A chapter-only or review-only reading of this file supports the map, not the territory.

Argument in brief

The review argues that White's book fills one specific gap in the literature of the "social turn": it studies the invitation to participate as the linchpin of participation rather than the participant's response, and treats that invitation as something crafted and aesthetic. It reports the book chapter by chapter. Chapter 1, "Process and Procedure", borrows from Goffman and Bourdieu to map the distances and differences between performer and participant across which exchanges happen. White then takes Janet Murray's digital-design concept of "procedural authorship" to claim that crafting a participatory performance yields "material of a different kind"; the reviewer's gloss is that the audience becomes the manipulated medium - though he stresses White is more concerned with participants' contradictory experience than with formulas or the craft of the procedural author. The book's key term is the "horizon of participation", built on Gadamer and Jauss: the existing understanding a participant brings, which fixes what they perceive as possible and therefore the reach of their agency, and which is established primarily by the invitation. Chapters two and three cover risk and (ir)rationality; chapter four, "Accepting the Invitation", works phenomenologically through feedback loops and the blurred line between spectating and acting; the concluding chapter reframes Ranciere's emancipated spectator and answers that a participant can be a spectator to their own actions, because distance opens up once subjectivity has become aesthetic material. The reviewer's judgements: the book is exhaustively expansive yet holds its range of examples together with unifying principles; its scope is deliberately left open; and two things are missing - the digital participatory forms its use of Murray implies, and any reflexive unpacking of the book itself as a delivery format. It closes as "a provocative invitation in itself".

Key ideas

(The reviewer's summaries of White's terms, not White's own definitions, except where the reviewer quotes him.) - the invitation - asking spectators to participate, treated as the moment worth theorising; the reviewer calls it "a heuristic linchpin to participation, yet something surprisingly under-examined in this emerging field" (PDF p.1). - procedural authorship - from Janet Murray's digital design theory; crafting a participatory performance as authored material, so that the audience becomes "the manipulated medium" (reviewer's words, PDF p.1). - horizon of participation - from reception theory (Gadamer, Jauss): the participant's existing understanding and its assumptions, described by White as "a limit and range of potentials within that limit, both gaps to be filled and choices to be made" (White p.59, via PDF p.2). - horizon as agency and control - the perceived horizon shows both the possibility of the participant's agency and the procedural author's control over them, since the invitation establishes the horizon (White p.60, via PDF p.2). - risk and (ir)rationality - the material of chapters two and three as the reviewer lists it (PDF p.2). - feedback loops / blurred spectating and acting - the phenomenological machinery of chapter four, "Accepting the Invitation", applied to the intersubjective limits and potential of participatory theatre (PDF p.2). - the emancipated spectator reframed - White returns to Ranciere and asks whether a participatory spectator can be an emancipated one; his answer turns on subjectivity having become "an aesthetic material" (White p.206, via PDF p.2). - "the continuity of the participant's social being" - the reviewer's pick of what will occupy theorists and practitioners (PDF p.2). - the social turn - the review's frame for the field, with Bishop's Artificial Hells, McConachie's Engaging Audiences and Machon's Immersive Theatres as immediate neighbours (PDF p.1). - invitation as delivery format - the reviewer's reflexive question about the book itself: "How does an invitation to participate manifest itself in a book?" (PDF p.3).

Quotes

All six are the reviewer's words. Where White's words appear inside them, they are White's as the reviewer quotes him, with the reviewer's page references and his own bracketed ellipses marking his omissions - not quotations from the book held in this folder.

  • "White concentrates on the invitation of participatory actions, a heuristic linchpin to participation, yet something surprisingly under-examined in this emerging field." (PDF p.1 / printed p.356) - the reviewer's statement of the gap White fills: the invitation rather than the response.
  • "In other words, the audience becomes the manipulated medium." (PDF p.1 / printed p.356) - the reviewer's own summary sentence for procedural authorship; usable for the claim that the audience is authored as medium, but attribute it to the reviewer.
  • "Another helpful term White offers is the ‘horizon of participation’, developed from the field of reception theory - building on Gadamer and Jauss, to articulate the existing level of understanding a participant might possess – and all the assumptions therein." (PDF p.2 / printed p.357) - the horizon of participation and its reception-theory pedigree, as reported.
  • "‘The horizon that participants perceive’, White contends, ‘maps out the possibility of their agency in the event […] It will also serve to show the control that the procedural author has over the participants, as the horizon is established primarily by the invitation to participate, and by the relation between the event and its context […]’ (p.60)" (PDF p.2 / printed p.357) - White quoted by the reviewer (his p.60) on agency and the author's control; second-hand, and check against the book before citing White directly.
  • "Although White’s highly commendable work here can indeed apply across many disciplines, his somewhat understated focus on western – particularly British – forms of participation reflect a narrow cultural and critical lens." (PDF p.2 / printed p.357) - the reviewer's main critique, and the reason to read White's examples as culturally narrow.
  • "White’s book provokes a productive reconsideration of the dynamics and aesthetics of participatory art forms beyond theatre, so I also found myself questioning how White’s chosen delivery format might be more reflexively unpacked. How does an invitation to participate manifest itself in a book?" (PDF p.3 / printed p.358) - the reflexive critique of the book as a format that may or may not itself invite; the most transferable item here for work on delivery formats.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

It can carry the participatory-vocabulary strand - invitation, thresholds, procedural authorship, horizon of participation, feedback loops, the emancipated spectator - and it can carry the claim that the invitation, not the participant's reply, is the under-theorised hinge of audience participation. For the performance lecture, its most transferable content is the last quote: the question of whether a format is itself an invitation, which applies directly to a lecture or lecture-performance addressed to a seated dome audience. It also places the theatre-side literature against Machon's Immersive Theatres, the bridge to immersive and dome practice. It cannot carry White's own position, his case studies, or the aesthetics of the invitation themselves; those require the book.

Limits

A 1,188-word review, second-hand throughout, and the only representation of White in this folder. The account of the book is compressed to roughly a paragraph per chapter area, no production is named, every quotation from White is the reviewer's transcription, and none of the page numbers can be checked against anything held here. It has no dome, planetarium, projection or lecture content: nothing on immersion as a medium, on screens or scenography, on pedagogy or address. Its immersive-theatre link is only the comparison with Machon. Its critique of White's Western, particularly British, scope is one reviewer's opinion. Beyond the bibliographic head and the signature it carries no apparatus, there is no bibliography of White, and the journal's name is absent from the file. It must not stand in the bibliography as White 2013.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_Artificial_Hells_Claire_Bishop_pdf - Bishop, named here as what White responds to and builds on for the "social turn"; the review is a shorthand for that relation, not for Bishop.
  • Expanded_Goffman_Forms_of_Talk_pdf - Goffman, the sociology the review says underpins White's Chapter 1 account of distances between performer and participant.

  • Not in this folder: Josephine Machon, Immersive Theatres (2013), the book this review measures White against, and White's Audience Participation in Theatre itself - both need acquiring.

Christian Heath, review of Adam Kendon, Conducting Interaction: Patterns of Behavior in Focused Encounters (1992)

Citation

Heath, Christian. Review of Conducting Interaction: Patterns of Behavior in Focused Encounters, by Adam Kendon. American Anthropologist 94 (1992): 705–706.

Book under review (as given in the review's own header): Kendon, Adam. Conducting Interaction: Patterns of Behavior in Focused Encounters. Studies in Interactional Sociolinguistics, 7. New York: Cambridge University Press, 1990. 304 pp. $54.50 (cloth), $16.95 (paper).
