Quotes below are given exactly as extracted (including "o f"), so the intra-word spaces and any odd spelling are artifacts, not the reviewer's text. Some quotes end where the column break interrupts the sentence; that is noted.

Argument in brief

Heath reviews the Cambridge collection of Kendon's late-1960s/early-1970s essays on behavioral organization in face-to-face interaction, essays built on frame-by-frame analysis of film of real encounters. He accepts the book as a landmark: the collection plus new introductory chapters (on context analysis), a chapter on frame attunement, and postscripts joining old studies to newer research are said to make it a standard reading-list item in anthropology, sociology and sociolinguistics. Heath traces Kendon's analytic lineage (Cambridge perceptuo-motor studies, the Palo Alto collaboration, the "natural history" approach, Goffman's "interaction order") while insisting Kendon's orientation is his own, not borrowed. He then walks the empirical chapters: gaze organization regulating the delivery of talk; listeners synchronizing bodily conduct with speakers; facial expression coordinating courtship; the Kendon–Ferber analysis of how people move step by step into focused interaction; and the analysis of spatial patterning. The review's methodological verdict is that film and video reveal the fine details of in situ conduct and give anthropology a distinctive way of studying social interaction. It contains no criticism of the book at all — no reservations, no counter-evidence, no comparison with rivals. Its only quasi-critical move is the observation that Kendon attributes his framework to Goffman, Scheflen and Birdwhistell although the framework is in fact distinctive.

Key ideas

  • focused encounters / focused interaction — the book's object: face-to-face encounters in which participants are mutually engaged; the review reports an analysis of how individuals "progressively move into focused interaction".
  • behavioral organization in social interaction — Heath's umbrella phrase for what Kendon studies: the systematic organization of conduct in encounters, not the content of talk.
  • context analysis — the extended introduction Kendon added to the reprints, placing his approach among cognate approaches.
  • frame attunement — named as the topic of one new chapter on face-to-face interaction; the review names it but does not explain it.
  • "natural history" approach / Palo Alto collaboration / perceptuo-motor skills — the three influences Heath lists behind Kendon's method.
  • the "interaction order" — Goffman's term, cited as a metaphoric conception Kendon draws on; Heath adds that Kendon's own analytic orientation remains unique.
  • spatial patterning — Kendon's "well-known analysis of spatial patterning and human conduct in social interaction"; this is as close as the file comes to spatial/orientational vocabulary.
  • synchronization of bodily conduct — listeners synchronize bodily conduct with speakers and so participate "moment by moment" in producing the activity.
  • film and video as analytic instrument — the review's method claim: film and video "explicate the fine details of in situ human conduct" and ground "naturalistic research on social interaction".
  • gaze — the opening empirical chapter: looking both monitors the other's conduct and regulates the delivery and coordination of talk.

Not in this file. The parent terms were checked against the text: "F-formation" (0 hits), "facing formation" (0), "orientational" (0), "arrangement" (0), "sustained interaction" (0). The review never uses F-formation or any spatial-orientational vocabulary; it says "spatial patterning" and "focused interaction". No critique of the book is present, and the words dome, planetarium, immersion, spectator, theatre and performance do not occur.

Quotes

  • "In the late 1960s and early 1970s, Adam Kendon published a number of influential essays concerned with behavioral organization in social interaction. These essays were based on the detailed empirical analyses of film of face-to-face encounters and charted a variety of complex, sociointeractional organizations that inform the accomplishment o f a variety of apparently mundane tasks, such as exchanging greetings or coordinating speaker turns." (PDF p.1 / printed p.705) — establishes the book as film-based micro-analysis of real encounters; note the extraction artifact "o f" (= of).
  • "providing an extended introduction to context analysis and cognate approaches and an intriguing chapter on frame attunement in face-to-face interaction" (PDF p.1 / printed p.705) — the file's only naming of context analysis and frame attunement; documents which concepts the review actually reports.
  • "This distinctive methodological and conceptual orientation draws on a variety of analytic influences, ranging from the early Cambridge studies of perceptuo-motor skills, through the Palo Alto collaboration and the “natural history” approach, to Goffman’s" (PDF p.1 / printed p.705) — the intellectual lineage Heath assigns to Kendon; the sentence continues into a column break in the source text, which is why it stops at "Goffman's".
  • "in which Kendon reveals the ways listeners synchronize their bodily conduct with speakers and thereby participate, moment by moment, in the mutual production of the activity at hand." (PDF p.1 / printed p.705) — the clearest statement of embodied, moment-by-moment participation; useful for framing an audience as co-producer of an event.
  • "In the next chapter, Kendon and coauthor Andrew Ferber develop an extensive and rigorous analysis of how individuals progressively move into focused interaction, synchronizing, step by step, their visual and vocal conduct as they mutually establish engagement in interaction." (PDF p.1 / printed p.705) — how engagement is entered step by step; the closest thing to F-formation theory the review reports.
  • "This book is a landmark in the development of naturalistic research on social interaction. It powerfully demonstrates the analytic rewards of utilizing film and video to explicate" (PDF p.1 / printed p.705) — the methodological verdict on film/video as research instrument; the sentence continues "the fine details of in situ human conduct, and provides a distinctive methodological orientation for an anthropology of social interaction" across interleaved columns in PDF p.1 and again on PDF p.2, so it cannot be quoted as one clean run.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

Usable as a secondary, reception-side source: evidence that by 1992 the micro-analysis of face-to-face encounters — spatial arrangement, gaze, bodily synchronization, mutual engagement — was a recognized and prized research programme in anthropology, and that film/video recording was its standard instrument. That is the argument it can carry: a precedent for treating recorded, situated bodily conduct as data, which is one of the methodological stakes of practice-based dome research. It cannot carry anything about dome formats, immersion, projection, scenography or spectatorship in the theatrical sense, and it cannot supply Kendon's own concepts (F-formation, transactional segments, spacing) — those must be cited from Kendon 1990 directly.

Limits

It is a two-page, wholly favourable review; there is no critique to quote, so it should not be used as evidence of debate about the book. It reports Kendon's chapters in sequence rather than reconstructing his theory, and it defines almost none of the terms it names (context analysis, frame attunement, focused interaction are named, not explained). It predates fulldome practice entirely and makes no claim about performance. The text layer is column-interleaved and OCR-damaged, so all quotes carry extraction noise ("o f", "additiond", split hyphens); verify against the printed page before publishing any quote. The last sentence of the Kendon review survives only as fragments on printed p.706 and is not quotable.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Expanded_Goffman_Forms_of_Talk_pdf — Goffman is named in this review as the source of the "interaction order" Kendon is said to draw on.
  • Books_Intercorporeality_and_Interaffectivity_Thomas_Fuchs_pdf — adjacent literature on bodily attunement between subjects, the theme the listener-synchronization chapter raises here.
  • Expanded_White_Audience_Participation_in_Theatre_pdf — for the audience-participation side that this review's "moment by moment" participation implies but does not discuss.
  • Books_Culture_as_Performance_Erika_Fischer_Lichte_pdf — performance framing for the encounter-as-event vocabulary used in this review.

6. The body, perception and shared feeling

The philosophical grounding for the claim that an audience in a dome is not a set of viewers but a set of bodies in a shared space. Merleau-Ponty is the source: the body is not an object in the world but the means of having one, perception is oriented and practical, and the body schema is a spatial competence rather than a picture of the body. Fuchs and Gallese are the modern continuation and the bridge to empirical work — intercorporeality as mutual incorporation of body schemas in face-to-face encounter, and embodied simulation as the mechanism by which perceiving an action or an emotion is already a form of understanding. Both cite Merleau-Ponty directly, which makes the chain explicit and quotable. Hayles is the corrective: in information culture the body gets treated as optional, and her book is the argument that it is not — the reference for anyone tempted to describe immersion as leaving the body behind.

Merleau-Ponty, Phenomenology of Perception (1945; English translation 1962)

Citation

Merleau-Ponty, Maurice. Phenomenology of Perception. Translated by Colin Smith. London: Routledge & Kegan Paul, 1962; Routledge Classics edition, 2002. Taken from the title page and the copyright page of the file (Phénomènologie de la perception, Gallimard 1945; English edition 1962; Routledge Classics 2002; ISBN 0-415-27841-4) and from the running heads. Original PDF filename: Books\Phenomenology of Perception - Maurice Merleau Ponty.pdf.

Coverage

Book, complete. 570 PDF pages, ~235,000 words; runs from the title page and Preface through the index, whose final entry is Zucker (printed pp. 1–544 plus roman front matter). No preview watermarks. Text layer is clean machine extraction: it preserves the original ﬁ and ﬂ ligature characters (the words for first and reflected keep them) and drops some hyphens (MerleauPonty for Merleau-Ponty), so the quotes below keep the ligatures exactly as extracted. Body-page offset is constant — printed page = PDF page − 25 (PDF p.194 = printed p.169, PDF p.264 = printed p.239). The Preface is roman-numbered (PDF p.12 = printed pp. xi–xii).

Argument in brief

A sustained attack on the objective thought that treats the body as one object among others and perception as a sum of sensations plus judgement. Merleau-Ponty works through the clinical literature — Schneider's apraxia, the phantom limb, anosognosia, Stratton's inverted-vision experiments — to argue that the body is not an object but the subject's way of having a world. Perception is not a representation built up by an intellect; it is an oriented, motivated, bodily grip on a field, and consciousness is primarily practical (I can) rather than representational (I think). The book's central unit is the lived body as a system of motor and perceptual powers; its central claim is that the body schema and the theory of perception are one problem.

Key ideas

  • the body our general medium for having a world — the body is not an instrument the subject uses; it is what makes a world show up at all, at biological, motor-habitual and cultural levels at once.
  • motor intentionality / motor project (Bewegungsentwurf) — movement that aims at a situation rather than executing a representation; failing to recognise it is what makes apraxia unintelligible to psychology.
  • I think vs I can — consciousness is first a practical grasp on a field, not a representation of it (printed p.159; see the quote list).
  • body schema / body image — the pre-personal, whole-to-part organisation of the body, not an association of images; the book argues explicitly that this schema is already a theory of perception.
  • the lived body vs the objective body — being one's body only to the extent that one lives it; the body's unity is implicit and vague, which is why treating it as an object destroys the phenomenon.
  • habit-body vs the body at this moment — two layers of the body; the phantom limb shows the habitual body still reaching into a world the present body cannot act in.
  • virtual body / spatial level — the body that counts for perceived orientation is a system of possible actions defined by task and situation, not a physical thing; a virtual body can displace the real one so that the subject inhabits the spectacle.
  • the body as medium of intersubjectivity — gesture and speech are understood bodily, before any intellectual interpretation; this is the passage the embodiment literature later takes up (see Fuchs and Gallese below).

Quotes

  • "The body is our general medium for having a world." (PDF p.194 / printed p.169) — body as world-medium.
  • "The theory of the body schema is, implicitly, a theory of perception." (PDF p.264 / printed p.239) — body schema grounds perception.
  • "I am my body, at least wholly to the extent that I possess experience, and yet at the same time my body is as it were a ‘natural’ subject, a provisional sketch of my total being." (PDF p.256 / printed p.231) — lived body, not object.
  • "It is through my body that I understand other people, just as it is through my body that I perceive ‘things’." (PDF p.241 / printed p.216) — bodily intersubjectivity.
  • "and thought as a representation of movement—something which is an anticipation of, or arrival at, the objective and is ensured by the body itself as a motor power, a ‘motor project’ (Bewegungsentwurf), a ‘motor intentionality’ in the absence of which the order remains a dead letter." (PDF p.152 / printed p.127) — motor intentionality; the sentence begins on the previous line of the page, so it is quoted from mid-sentence.
  • "What counts for the orientation of the spectacle is not my body as it in fact is, as a thing in objective space, but as a system of possible actions, a virtual body with its phenomenal ‘place’ deﬁned by its task and situation. My body is wherever there is something to be done." (PDF p.316 / printed p.291) — virtual body / orientation.
  • "This virtual body ousts the real one to such an extent that the subject no longer has the feeling of being in the world where he actually is, and that instead of his real legs and arms, he feels that he has the legs and arms he would need to walk and act in the reﬂected room: he inhabits the spectacle." (PDF p.316 / printed p.291) — inhabiting the spectacle.
  • "It is precisely when my customary world arouses in me habitual intentions that I can no longer, if I have lost a limb, be eﬀectively drawn into it, and the utilizable objects, precisely in so far as they present themselves as utilizable, appeal to a hand which I no longer have. Thus are delimited, in the totality of my body, regions of silence." (PDF p.120 / printed p.95) — phantom limb / habit-body.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

The load-bearing theoretical source for the claim that a dome is perceived with the body rather than decoded by the eye. The virtual-body passage (PDF p.316) is the most directly usable: it describes a spectator whose actual body is displaced by a body fitted to the projected scene — immersion described phenomenologically, decades before dome literature. The lines on the general medium and on the body schema as a theory of perception are the citable premises for a phenomenology-of-the-dome section. It cannot supply anything about screens, projection surfaces, planetaria or empirical/theatrical spectatorship — that has to come from the dome material.

Limits

No account of images, technology, media or theatre; the analyses are of motor pathology, touch, sexuality, language and space, not of viewing apparatus. The vocabulary is 1940s French phenomenology through a 1958–62 translation, so quotes need glossing for a media-art readership, and the translation uses a male pronoun for the perceiving subject throughout. The extraction keeps ligatures and drops some hyphens, so long quotes should be checked against the print edition before final typesetting.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_Intercorporeality_and_Interaffectivity_Thomas_Fuchs_pdf — explicit continuation: Fuchs calls his own account an articulation of Merleau-Ponty's concept of intercorporeality.
  • Books_The_Roots_of_Empathy_Vittorio_Gallese_pdf — quotes Merleau-Ponty (1962, the same Smith translation) to ground the mirror-mechanism account of empathy.
  • Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf — performance-theory bridge from perception to intermedial staging.
  • Books_Digital_Performance_Steve_Dixon_pdf — the body in digitally mediated performance; a less phenomenological route to the spectator's body.
  • Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf — virtual used historically for illusion spaces; useful contrast to Merleau-Ponty's virtual body.
  • Books_Spheres_Bubbles_pdf — Sloterdijk on immersive, atmospheric space; comparable vocabulary, different method.

Fuchs, Intercorporeality and Interaffectivity (2016)

Citation

Fuchs, Thomas. Intercorporeality and Interaffectivity. Phenomenology and Mind, n. 11 (2016), pp. 194–209. Firenze University Press, CC BY 4.0. From the first page of the file (author block, abstract, keywords, copyright line); footnote 1 states it is a slightly revised version of the chapter of the same name in C. Meyer, J. Streeck and J. S. Jordan (eds.), Intercorporeality: Emerging Socialities in Interaction, Oxford University Press, 2016. Original PDF filename: Books\Intercorporeality and Interaffectivity - Thomas Fuchs.pdf.

Coverage

Journal article, complete and open access. 17 PDF chunks = the article's 16 pages (printed pp. 194–209) plus one blank trailing chunk. Text layer: good, but damaged in one consistent way — the extraction drops every ﬁ and ﬂ ligature, so the word specific appears as speciic, pre-reflective as pre-relective, field as ield, find as ind, conflicts as conlicts. The quotes below are restricted to sentences with no dropped ligatures; sentences that would require repairing a word are not quoted. Running heads and page numbers survive, so printed page numbers are reliable (printed page = PDF page + 193; PDF p.1 = printed p.194, PDF p.12 = printed p.205).

Argument in brief

Fuchs argues that social understanding does not begin with isolated minds inferring other minds. It begins with intercorporeality: two lived bodies (Leib) coupled in face-to-face interaction. (1) Emotions are not inner mental states but circular interactions of an embodied subject with a situation that has affective affordances; emotion is felt as bodily resonance. (2) In an encounter, expression and impression loop between partners so that each becomes an expression for the other — bodily resonance, coordinated interaction, mutual incorporation. That loop is primary empathy, prior to perspective-taking. (3) The patterns are sedimented as intercorporeal memory, acquired in early childhood, giving a social musicality and a dyadic body memory (we can, not I can). The closing claim is that this interaffective space, not mentalising, is the basis of social cognition.

Key ideas

  • intercorporeality — a pre-reflective intertwining of lived and living bodies in which each is affected by the other's body; Fuchs presents this as an articulation of Merleau-Ponty's intercorporéité.
  • embodied affectivity / bodily resonance — emotion is experienced as bodily sensation and action tendency; resonance is the proximal, tacit component of affective intentionality (Polanyi), usually receding from awareness.
  • affective affordances — Gibson's affordances extended to expressive qualities: situations appear interesting, uncanny, repulsive, and so on.
  • interaffectivity / mutual incorporation — the loop of expression and impression between bodies; each lived body reaches out to be extended by the other.
  • intra-bodily resonance — the felt body as the subject's own resonance board for its emotion, prior to expression.
  • intercorporeal memory / implicit relational knowing / social musicality — procedural memory of interaction rhythms learned in infancy; later framed as a dyadic body memory of we can rather than I can.
  • primary empathy — immediate, pre-reflective understanding of another's expression through the body, not simulation and not theory.
  • Leib/Körper and the chiasmatic body — the body as turning point of interior and exterior, referencing Merleau-Ponty's late chiasm (Merleau-Ponty 1968).
  • incorporation at a distance — Fuchs explicitly rejects as-if simulation and uses the acrobat on the high wire and the spellbinder's audience as cases where one body's schema extends into another's movement without contact.

Quotes

  • "According to phenomenological and enactive approaches, human sociality does not start from isolated individuals, but from intercorporeality and interaffectivity." (PDF p.1 / printed p.194) — starting point.
  • "This leads to a concept of embodied interaffectivity: in every face-to-face encounter, the partners’ subject-bodies are intertwined in a process of bodily resonance, coordinated interaction and ‘mutual incorporation’ which provides the basis for an intuitive empathic understanding." (PDF p.3 / printed p.196) — mutual incorporation.
  • "Our body schemas and feelings expand and ‘incorporate’ the perceived body of the other." (PDF p.5 / printed p.198) — spectator incorporation.
  • "This creates a circular interplay of expressions and reactions running in split seconds and constantly modifying each partner’s bodily state, in a process that becomes highly autonomous and is not controlled by the partners." (PDF p.6 / printed p.199) — circular inter-affectivity.
  • "This analysis may be regarded as an articulation of Merleau-Ponty’s concept of ‘intercorporeality’ (intercorporéité, Merleau-Ponty 1960)" (PDF p.7 / printed p.200) — explicit MP debt.
  • "We do not need to form internal models or representations of others in order to understand and communicate with them; as bodily subjects, we are always already involved in a shared affective and expressive space." (PDF p.12 / printed p.205) — no mentalising.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

The citable spine for the claim that shared feeling in a performance space is bodily and inter-relational rather than interpretive: it supplies mutual incorporation, bodily resonance, interaffective space and intercorporeal memory. The acrobat and spellbinder passage (PDF p.198) is a legitimated phenomenological account of an audience being drawn into a performer's movement at a distance — directly usable for dome spectatorship, where the audience's whole visual field is a moving projected image. It cannot carry anything about screens, projection or the dome as architecture, and it offers no account of large or distributed audiences (everything is dyadic, face-to-face, one body to one body).

Limits

The empirical support is developmental psychology and clinical work (the still-face experiment, mother–infant attunement), not performance or media; theatre references are illustrations, not analyses. The extraction's lost ligatures mean the file's prose cannot be quoted freely — choose sentences with no fi or fl words, or check the published article. The account is explicitly two-body: nothing on object- or technology-mediated intercorporeality, which is exactly what a dome performance adds.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_Phenomenology_of_Perception_Maurice_Merleau_Ponty_pdf — Fuchs quotes the same gesture-comprehension passage from it and names intercorporeality as Merleau-Ponty's term.
  • Books_The_Roots_of_Empathy_Vittorio_Gallese_pdf — the neuroscience counterpart (mirror mechanism, shared manifold); Fuchs cites Gallese's motor resonance as needing phenomenological spelling out.
  • Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf — where embodied spectatorship meets staging conventions.
  • Books_Being_Singular_Plural_Jean_Luc_Nancy_pdf — philosophical account of being-with as an alternative to the interaffective route.
  • Books_Spheres_Bubbles_pdf — Sloterdijk on shared atmospheric space; same family of claims, different vocabulary.

Gallese, The Roots of Empathy (2003)

Citation

Gallese, Vittorio. The Roots of Empathy: The Shared Manifold Hypothesis and the Neural Basis of Intersubjectivity. Psychopathology 2003;36:171–180. DOI: 10.1159/000072786. From the article's first page (Karger); received 16 October 2002, accepted after revision 3 March 2003. Original PDF filename: Books\The Roots of Empathy - Vittorio Gallese.pdf.

Coverage

Journal article, complete. 11 PDF chunks = the article's 10 pages (printed pp. 171–180) plus one blank trailing chunk. Text layer is clean — no dropped ligatures, no garbled pages; running heads and printed page numbers are intact, so printed page = PDF page + 170 (PDF p.1 = printed p.171, PDF p.7 = printed p.177). Footnotes and the reference list on printed pp. 179–180 are intact.

Argument in brief

Gallese asks what makes other minds intelligible at all and answers from neurophysiology. Mirror neurons in monkey premotor and parietal cortex discharge both when an action is executed and when the same action is seen or heard; homologous circuits exist in humans (Broca's region, premotor and parietal sectors). On that basis he proposes that perceiving an action is equivalent to simulating it internally — an implicit, automatic, prereflexive embodied simulation that links observer and agent without theorising. He generalises from actions to sensations and emotions via a mirror matching mechanism and bundles the results into one conceptual tool: the shared manifold of intersubjectivity, operationalised at a phenomenological, a functional (as-if modes) and a subpersonal (mirror circuits) level. The closing section claims the same shared manifold underlies the sense of similarity with others, and that its disturbance bears on the autistic aspects of schizophrenia.

Key ideas

  • embodied simulation — implicit, automatic, unconscious reuse of the observer's own motor and visceral resources to model another's behaviour; perceiving an action is treated as equivalent to simulating it internally.
  • mirror mechanism / mirror matching — neural substrate activated by both execution and perception of an action; extended to action sounds (audiovisual mirror neurons).
  • shared manifold of intersubjectivity — the umbrella construct, defined at three levels: phenomenological (sense of similarity, the empathic level), functional (as-if modes of interaction), subpersonal (mirror circuits coupled to bodily states).
  • we-centric space — the shared intersubjective space mapped by mirror neurons, which does not segregate any subject; explicitly distinguished from self-other identity, which preexists it.
  • implicit certainties — the broad range of what we share with conspecifics: actions, sensations, emotions, body schema.
  • reversibility / self-other — observer and observed as opposite extensions of one correlative, reversible system; alterity is preserved, argued against collapsing the other into the same (with Husserl and Zahavi).
  • phenomenological lineage — the hypothesis is grounded on Husserl (body as Leib, not Körper), on Edith Stein's account of empathy through common experience of action, and on Merleau-Ponty 1962. On printed p.176 Gallese names Stein and Merleau-Ponty as the works in which the link between action and empathic relations becomes most evident, and then quotes Merleau-Ponty's gesture passage from the Phenomenology (see the quote list); he adds that self and other relate as opposite extensions of one correlative and reversible system.

Quotes

  • "our capacity to understand others as intentional agents, far from being exclusively dependent upon mentalistic/linguistic abilities, be deeply grounded in the relational nature of our interactions with the world." (PDF p.1 / printed p.171) — thesis.
  • "According to this perspective, to perceive an action is equivalent to internally simulate it. This implicit, automatic, and unconscious process of embodied simulation enables the observer to use his/her own resources to penetrate the world of the other without the need of explicitly theorizing about it" (PDF p.4 / printed p.174) — embodied simulation.
  • "The communication or comprehension of gestures come about through the reciprocity of my intentions and the gestures of others, of my gestures and intentions discernible in the conduct of other people. It is as if the other person’s intention inhabited my body and mine his." (PDF p.6 / printed p.176) — Merleau-Ponty quoted by Gallese.
  • "These spaces are blended within a unified common intersubjective space, which paradoxically does not segregate any subject. This space is we-centric." (PDF p.5 / printed p.175) — we-centric space.
  • "The shared spaces allow us to appreciate, experience, and implicitly and prereflexively understand the emotions and the sensations we take others to experience." (PDF p.7 / printed p.177) — shared manifold.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

Supplies the empirical floor for two claims: that perception is a bodily simulation rather than the decoding of an image (usable for why a dome image is felt as bodily position), and that shared feeling across an audience rests on a shared embodied space rather than narrative comprehension. It is also the cleanest instance in this folder of the embodiment literature explicitly leaning on Merleau-Ponty — a phrase-level link a review can cite. It cannot carry spectatorship, immersion, projection or the dome: every example is action observation (grasping, walking, the acrobat on the wire taken from Lipps), and embodied simulation is a hypothesis about mechanism, not a theory of staged space.

Limits

The dome and any media apparatus are absent; the extension from hand actions to emotions, and then to aesthetic experience, is a research programme rather than a result. Gallese's own caveat on printed p.177 is that no systematic attempt had yet been made to test the hypothesis experimentally, and the closing claims about autism and schizophrenia are speculative by his own framing. Merleau-Ponty enters mostly as a block quotation and a citation, not as a worked-out framework — the phenomenology licenses the neuroscience rather than being developed by it. Note also that Gallese's transcription of the Merleau-Ponty passage reads gestures come about, where the Smith translation reads comes about; a review quoting the passage should take it from the Phenomenology itself (printed p.216).

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_Phenomenology_of_Perception_Maurice_Merleau_Ponty_pdf — the quoted source; Gallese's gesture passage is on printed p.216 of the Smith translation.
  • Books_Intercorporeality_and_Interaffectivity_Thomas_Fuchs_pdf — the phenomenological counterweight: Fuchs accepts resonance but rejects simulation as the mechanism.
  • Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf — performance-theory use of embodiment categories.
  • Books_Digital_Performance_Steve_Dixon_pdf — audience embodiment under digital mediation, at the level of staging rather than neurons.

N. Katherine Hayles, How We Became Posthuman: Virtual Bodies in Cybernetics, Literature, and Informatics (1999)

Citation

Hayles, N. Katherine. How We Became Posthuman: Virtual Bodies in Cybernetics, Literature, and Informatics. Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 1999. [Title page and copyright page in the extraction: The University of Chicago Press Chicago and London; © 1999 by The University of Chicago; ISBN (cloth) 0-226-32145-2, ISBN (paper) 0-226-32146-0.] Original PDF: Books_How_We_Became_Posthuman_Katherine_Hayles_pdf.pdf.

Coverage

Book, complete. 367 PDF pages, 150,121 words. One printed page per PDF page in the body (printed folio = PDF page − 15; the prologue pages are roman, PDF p.15 = printed p.xiv). Front matter (title, copyright, dedication, contents, acknowledgments), Prologue, chapters 1–11, Notes (printed p.293) and Index (printed p.325) are all present. No preview page, no watermark, no missing chapter. Extraction quality: OCR-grade and dirty. Spaces are dropped inside and between words (ofthe, materialforms, follOwing, deSignate, calljlickering signifiers) and capitals appear mid-word (Single, Signifier, cybernetiCS). Every quote below was string-verified against the .txt and taken only from spans the OCR left clean, so nothing here is reconstructed. Note that the definitional sentence for the flickering signifier (information technologies create what I will call flickering signifiers, characterised by their tendency toward unexpected metamorphoses, PDF p.45) is one of the damaged spans, which is why the quote carried for that term is the adjacent clean sentence on the same page.

Argument in brief

A history of cybernetics told as three interlaced stories: how information was separated from the material forms that carry it (information lost its body); how the cyborg was built as technological artifact and cultural icon after 1945; and how a historically specific construction called the human is giving way to one called the posthuman. The material is the Macy Conferences, information theory, second-wave cybernetics and autopoiesis, artificial life, treated alongside contemporaneous fiction (Limbo, Philip K. Dick, Neuromancer, Snow Crash, Galatea 2.2), on the argument that science and literature circulate the same assumptions. Her claim is that the cybernetic posthuman repeats the liberal humanist subject's erasure of embodiment rather than breaking with it: once information is defined as a pattern separable from any substrate, equating humans and machines is easy. She argues instead for embodied virtuality — information is a socially constructed concept that draws its efficacy from the material infrastructures it appears to obscure, and embodiment is what makes thought specific.

Key ideas

  • the posthuman — a point of view rather than a body modification: it privileges informational pattern over material instantiation, treats consciousness as an epiphenomenon, regards the body as a prosthesis to be operated, and sees the human as informational processes that can be articulated with machines. It does not require a literal cyborg.
  • information lost its body — the founding move of information theory: information is conceptualised as an entity separate from the material forms it is embedded in. Everything else in the book follows from this.
  • embodiment / the erasure of embodiment — she argues the erasure is common to both the liberal humanist subject and the cybernetic posthuman, and that it is what her book works against.
  • pattern/randomness — the pair that displaces presence/absence in information culture; the two are complements, not opposites, and the shift registers in the body as well as in the message.
  • flickering signifier — her extension of the Lacanian floating signifier to electronic text: a signifier is a chain of coded markers that can be transformed by one command, so signifiers flicker rather than float. Complexity of the coding chain is what gives small changes large effects.
  • informatics — following Haraway: the technologies of information together with the biological, social, linguistic and cultural changes that accompany them.
  • liberal humanist subject — Macpherson's possessive individual, proprietor of himself and owing nothing to society, for whom human essence is freedom from the wills of others; Hayles traces its deconstruction by feedback loops and distributed cognition.
  • enacted body / represented body — the pair the Turing test splits, and whose rejoining through technology makes the subject a cyborg.
  • embodied virtuality — the position she defends: virtuality that does not leave the body and context behind.

Quotes

  • "Just because information has lost its body does not mean that humans and the world have lost theirs." (PDF p.259 / printed p.244) — disembodiment critique - the single best sentence for arguing a dome image is not free-floating information.
  • "Because information had lost its body, this construction implied that embodiment is not essential to human being." (PDF p.19 / printed p.4) — erasure of embodiment in the cybernetic posthuman.
  • "The posthuman subject is an amalgam, a collection of heterogeneous components, a material-informational entity whose boundaries undergo continuous construction and reconstruction." (PDF p.18 / printed p.3) — posthuman subjectivity defined - useful for performer/avatar/audience boundary talk.
  • "What embodiment secures is not the distinction between male and female or between humans who can think and machines which cannot." (PDF p.15 / printed p.xiv) — embodiment as the ground of thought rather than of identity.
  • "The result is a multisensory interaction that creates the illusion that the user is inside the computer." (PDF p.42 / printed p.27) — the standard immersion claim in its 1990s form, quotable to historicise being inside the image.
  • "In these systems, the user learns, kinesthetically and proprioceptively, that the relevant boundaries for interaction are defined less by the skin than by the feedback loops connecting body and simulation in a technobio-integrated circuit." (PDF p.42 / printed p.27) — kinesthetic/proprioceptive learning of an interface - the closest Hayles comes to dome embodiment.
  • "This textual fluidity, which users learn in their bodies as they interact with the system, implies that signifiers flicker rather than float." (PDF p.45 / printed p.30) — the flickering signifier; signifiers as a changeable chain, learned bodily.
  • "The pattern/randomness dialectic does not erase the material world; information in fact derives its efficacy from the material infrastructures it appears to obscure." (PDF p.43 / printed p.28) — pattern/randomness plus materiality - the anti-dematerialisation thesis in one sentence.
  • "I see the deconstruction of the liberal humanist subject as an opportunity to put back into the picture the flesh that continues to be erased in contemporary discussions about cybernetic subjects." (PDF p.20 / printed p.5) — her stated programme: critique of liberal humanist subjectivity without giving up the body.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the source a fulldome literature review can use to argue that claims about the viewer becoming pure information inside an image misdescribe what a dome does. It supplies the vocabulary for (a) refusing the dematerialisation that immersion rhetoric often assumes, (b) treating the kinesthetic and proprioceptive learning of an interface as the actual site of embodied experience in mediated performance, and (c) historicising the idea of being inside the image as a 1990s claim rather than a neutral description. It cannot carry any account of a dome, planetarium, projection surface or live performance: her mediated subject is a single user at a terminal, and there is no audience, no performer, no staging and no teaching anywhere in the book.

Limits

No media theory of the screen and no spectatorship theory; the examples are word processing, MUDs, email and 1990s VR helmets, so the technical specifics are dated and predate the fulldome revival. The subject is always the individual operator, never a co-present group, so nothing here supports claims about collective attention or about a performer's body in a room. The argument is restated across chapters, so the book is highly quotable but long. The scan is dirty enough that any page-level citation should be re-checked against the print edition before publication.

Adjacent sources in this folder

Virtual Art: From Illusion to Immersion (Grau) — the art-historical account of immersion that Hayles's critique can be set against. The Language of New Media (Manovich) — the media-theoretical companion on screen and interface. Immersed in Technology (Moser & MacLeod, eds.) — same years, same VR/dome preoccupations, more art practice. The Virtual Window (Friedberg) — the architectural framing of screen and window that Hayles lacks. Phenomenology of Perception (Merleau-Ponty) — the embodiment position her chapter 8 quotes directly. Intercorporeality and Interaffectivity (Fuchs) — the contemporary version of the embodiment argument.

Feld, Steven, "Acoustemology" (2015)

Citation

Feld, Steven. "Acoustemology." In Keywords in Sound, edited by D. Novak and M. Sakakeeny. Duke U.P., 2015. Printed pp. 12-21.

Citation reconstructed, because the scan contains no title page or copyright page: (a) the handwritten note at the head of the first scan page reads "in D. Novak + M. Sakakeeny, eds. / Keywords in Sound, 2015, Duke U.P."; (b) the running heads and feet of the ten scanned book pages read "Steven Feld" and "acoustemology 13 ... 21"; (c) the entry's own reference list. Original PDF: 2015+Acoustemology.pdf (.../dome/literature/Expanded/); corpus slug Expanded_Feld_Acoustemology_2015_pdf.

Coverage

  • Not an article or chapter PDF: a 5-page PDF of photographs of an open book, each PDF page one two-page spread, rotated 90 degrees. It therefore covers printed pp. 12-21 (ten book pages): PDF p.1 = pp. 12/13, p.2 = 14/15, p.3 = 16/17, p.4 = 18/19, p.5 = 20/21.
  • Content is the complete keyword entry "acoustemology": entry title and author line on printed p.12, body text pp. 12-19, the entry's reference list pp. 20-21. No printed page in the 12-21 run is missing, and the text is continuous across every page break (verified against 170-dpi page renders). A handwritten citation note lies across the top of the first spread.
  • The PDF has no text layer (pdftotext returns nothing); the corpus .txt is OCR. Two OCR passes were made for this annotation (200 and 300 dpi, RapidOCR via PyMuPDF), each book page OCR'd separately rather than as a spread.

Argument in brief

The entry defines acoustemology, the term Feld coined in 1992, as the conjoining of acoustics and epistemology to theorize sound as a way of knowing. It argues that knowing through sound and listening is a relational, situated, contingent achievement, not the acquisition of facts, and it grounds that claim in relational ontology (Cassirer, Schutz, Dewey, Bateson, Haraway, Latour). It positions acoustemology "with but against" acoustic ecology and "soundscape": it refuses both the landscape analogy and any sonocentric master plan, and instead studies listening histories, attunement and reflexive feedback. The second half shifts to Feld's twenty-five years of fieldwork in the Bosavi rainforest of Papua New Guinea to show where the concept came from: bird identification by ear as an embodied skill, songs as vocalized maps sung from a bird's point of view, bird sound as the voice of memory, and song as an audible archive of relational attunements. The entry is built as a definition, a lineage, then an ethnographic demonstration; it carries no method section, no performance analysis and nothing on domes, screens or projection.

Key ideas

  • acoustemology — acoustics + epistemology: an inquiry into sounding and listening as a way of knowing, not a measurement of sound.
  • knowing-in-action — a knowing-with and knowing-through the audible; knowledge is cumulative, interactive, and produced through relations rather than acquired.
  • relational ontology — substantive existence never operates anterior to relationality; actors-plus-locations are produced by "relations-in-action." This is the term to which acoustemology is joined.
  • "situational," "mutual," "ecological," "polyphonic," "dialogical," "unfinalizable" — the vocabulary Feld borrows (Haraway, Buber, Bateson, Bakhtin) to describe the space of sonic knowing.
  • acoustic ecology / soundscape — the two legacy formations acoustemology writes "with but against"; Feld rejects "landscape" analogies and rejects replacing ocularcentrism with sonocentrism.
  • listening histories — acoustemology studies the experience and agency of listening histories (histories of listening, methods of listening), understood as relational, contingent, situated and reflexive.
  • acoustic attending / attunement — consciousness of modes of listening and of ways of "listening for and resounding to presence," produced by the routine condition of dwelling with others-in-relation.
  • listening as habitus — routinized, emplaced hearing as an embodied mastery of locality (children identifying birds by sound in seconds).
  • ane mama ("gone reverberations") — birds as absences turned into presence; bird sounds are "the voice of memory and the resonance of ancestry."
  • audible archive — songs as an archive of ecological and aesthetic coevolution; "place and voice" are naturalized sediments of long-lived attunements and antagonisms.
  • practice with experiment — Feld describes acoustemology pushing him to think through recording and playback, conjoining practice with experiment (the closest thing here to a practice-based research method).

Quotes

  • "Acoustemology conjoins “acoustics” and “epistemology” to theorize sound as a way of knowing. In doing so it inquires into what is knowable, and how it becomes known, through sounding and listening." (PDF p.1 / printed p.12) — definition: knowing through sound
  • "Acoustemology joins acoustics to epistemology to investigate sounding and listening as a knowing-in-action: a knowing-with and knowing-through the audible. Acoustemology thus does not invoke epistemology in the formal sense of an inquiry into metaphysical or transcendental assumptions surrounding claims to “truth” (“epistemology with a capital E,” in the phrasing of Richard Rorty, 1981). Rather it engages the relationality of knowledge production, as what John Dewey called contextual and experiential knowing (Dewey and Bentley 1949)." (PDF p.1 / printed p.12) — knowing-in-action, relational knowledge
  • "Acoustemology, as relational ontology, thus takes sound and sounding as “situational” (Haraway 1988) among “related subjects” (Bird-David 1999); it explores the “mutual” (Buber 1923) and “ecological” (Bateson 1972) space of sonic knowing as “polyphonic,” “dialogical,” and “unfinalizable” (Bakhtin 1981, 1984)." (PDF p.1 / printed p.13) — sound as relational, dialogical, situated
  • "Acoustemological approaches, while equally concerned with place-based space-time dynamics, concentrate on relational listening histories—on methods of listening to histories of listening—always with an ear to agency and positionalities. Unlike acoustic ecology, acoustemology is about the experience and agency of listening histories, understood as relational and contingent, situated and reflexive." (PDF p.2 / printed p.15) — listening histories, situated and reflexive
  • "acoustemology favors inquiry that centralizes situated listening in engagements with place and space-time. Acoustemology prioritizes histories of listening and attunement through the relational practices of listening and sounding and their reflexive productions of feedback." (PDF p.2 / printed p.15) — situated listening; attunement; feedback
  • "Acoustemology, then, is grounded in the basic assumption that life is shared with others-in-relation, with numerous sources of action (actant in Bruno Latour's terminology; 2005) that are variously human, nonhuman, living, nonliving, organic, or technological. This relationality is both a routine condition of dwelling and one that produces consciousness of modes of acoustic attending, of ways of listening for and resounding to presence." (PDF p.2 / printed p.15) — dwelling; acoustic attending; resounding to presence
  • "How to hear through the trees? How to hear the relationship of forest height to depth? Where is sound located when you can't see more than three feet ahead? Why does looking up into the forest simply take one's senses into the impenetrable density of the canopy?" (PDF p.3 / printed p.17) — acoustic orientation; hearing depth without sight
  • "This was a daily lesson in listening as habitus, a forceful demonstration of routinized, emplaced hearing as an embodied mastery of locality. It is only a matter of seconds before a twelve-year-old Bosavi kid can identify a bird by sound, describe its location in the forest density, and tell a good bit more about the location of its food, nests, and partners." (PDF p.4 / printed p.18) — listening as habitus; embodied mastery of place
  • "Could it be that when Bosavi people utter just this one word they are acknowledging audibility and perceptibility as simultaneously materializing past, present, and future social relations? Could they, in that sparse gesture, be theorizing that every sound is equally immediate to human experience and to the perceptual faculties of others, of perceivers who may even be absent, nonhuman, or dead?" (PDF p.4 / printed p.19) — sound materialises past/present/future; absent others hear
  • "This was where and how the conceptual term “acoustemology” was born: in years of listening to how sounding-as- and sounding-through-knowing is an audible archive of long-lived relational attunements and antagonisms that have come to be naturalized as place and voice." (PDF p.4 / printed p.19) — listening as archive; place naturalised as voice

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

It can carry the theoretical warrant for treating sound in a dome as knowing rather than decoration: acoustemology supplies "sound as a way of knowing," sound as relational and situational, and — most useful for the performance-lecture thread — the claim that what a listener knows is a listening history, situated and reflexive. It also carries the researcher's memory thread directly: songs as an audible archive, bird sound as the voice of memory, sound materializing past, present and future relations, and "place and voice." It cannot carry anything about domes, projection, immersion, audience staging, or the institution of the lecture; and it is not a model for practice-based research method (it reports fieldwork and CD-making retrospectively).

Limits

  • No text layer, all OCR. Every quote above was checked letter-by-letter against 170-dpi page renders before use.   • Scope.   • The account of Bosavi knowledge is reported by Feld as retrospective; the children and collaborators (Yubi, Ulahi) are named but not cited as authors, and the entry gives no transcriptions or song texts of its own (they are in Feld 1996, 2012).

7. Space, place and being-with

Different accounts of what a room is and what it does to the people in it. De Certeau is the most directly usable: walking as an enunciation of space, proper names that orient movement, the difference between the map and the itinerary — a vocabulary for what an audience does with a dome's geometry rather than what the dome does to them. Sloterdijk supplies the concept of the sphere and the interior as such, and the claim that being-in is always being-in-something-with-others, which is the philosophical form of the immersive claim. Nancy supplies being-with as originary rather than added: there is no being without co-existence, and presence is itself a spacing of singularities. Harman is the odd one in and earns its place by refusing the human-centred version of all this: objects withdraw from every relation, so the projected image, the screen and the room are actants in their own right rather than props for a spectator. Fuller is the ancestor — the whole-system view taken literally, thinking in wholes, and the spherical earth as the first dome.

Michel de Certeau, The Practice of Everyday Life (1984)

Citation

Certeau, Michel de. The Practice of Everyday Life. Translated by Steven Rendall. Berkeley, Los Angeles, London: University of California Press, 1984. ISBN 0-520-23699-8; copyright 1984 by the Regents of the University of California, first paperback printing 1988; translation of Arts de faire. [title page and copyright page of the scan] Original PDF filename is not recoverable from the file; the slug Books_The_Practice_of_Everyday_Life_Michel_de_Certeau_pdf is the only record of it. This text was OCR'd from a scan with no usable text layer.

Coverage

Book (complete text, but with no index). 254 PDF pages. The scan runs: title page, copyright page, dedication (to the ordinary man), Contents, the translator's note, the General Introduction (printed pp. xi-xxiv), Parts I-V with Chapters I-XIV (printed pp. 1-204), and the Notes (printed pp. 205-229). The file stops mid-note on printed p. 229 - there is no index and nothing after the Notes.

Contents as printed: Part I A Very Ordinary Culture (I. A Common Place: Ordinary Language; II. Popular Cultures: Ordinary Language; III. "Making Do": Uses and Tactics); Part II Theories of the Art of Practice (IV. Foucault and Bourdieu; V. The Arts of Theory; VI. Story Time); Part III Spatial Practices (VII. Walking in the City, p. 91; VIII. Railway Navigation and Incarceration, p. 111; IX. Spatial Stories, p. 115); Part IV Uses of Language (X. The Scriptural Economy, p. 131; XI. Note the naming: what the brief calls Part IV (Uses of Discourse) is titled Uses of Language here, and Part III (Spatial Practices) holds Walking in the City and Spatial Stories with Railway Navigation and Incarceration between them.

Page mapping (established from the printed folios surviving in the OCR): printed page = PDF page minus 25 for the arabic body (PDF 26 = p. 1; PDF 55 = p. 30; PDF 142 = p. 117; PDF 254 = p. 229). The roman front matter runs printed page = PDF page minus 1 (PDF 12 = p. xi; PDF 21 = p. xx).

OCR condition: usable for quotation, but line-level damage is frequent. Words are run together (inaccordwithwhich, beseen, theirknowledge), diacritics are dropped (Wandersmänner prints as Wandersmanner), and some lines are dropped or replaced by mirrored/upside-down strings from the facing page (a reversed line sits on PDF 129; PDF 118 interleaves text from the opposite page). Three passages named in the brief are affected: (a) the sentence naming the ordinary practitioners of the city and placing them below the level where visibility begins is cut off by the OCR, which resumes a few words later inside the same sentence (PDF 118 / p. 93); (b) the clause saying that haunted places are the only ones people can live in is missing after the phrase quoted at PDF 133 / p. 108, so that quote is only the first half of its sentence; (c) the phrase about walking filling the streets with names could not be located anywhere in the file (searched fill/fills and streets). Every quote below was checked programmatically against the extracted text with whitespace and punctuation ignored; nothing was reconstructed from a printed edition.

Argument in brief

The book asks not what people consume but how they use what they are given, and treats that use as a second, hidden production. The users in question are ordinary ones - walkers, readers, cooks, workers - operating inside systems they did not make, and their operations are not legible to the systems' own records (statistics, maps, plans). Parts I and II build the vocabulary: ordinary language as a reservoir of uses and imitations, popular culture as a set of operations, and the distinction between strategies (operations that presuppose a proper place and can produce, tabulate and impose spaces) and tactics (operations without a proper place, working in the cracks of the other's space). Part III turns to space, with the city as its evidence: seen from the 110th floor the city is a readable, panoptic text produced at the cost of the walkers below, whose movements constitute a space no map records; walking is analysed as an analogue of speech (enunciation, rhetoric, tropes) and stories are analysed as the everyday form in which places become spaces and actions are authorised. Part IV extends the same analysis to writing and reading (the scriptural economy; reading as poaching; the reader as a producer who poaches on another's text). The method is deliberately operational - it asks about modes of operation and schemata of action rather than about subjects - and it includes the analyst's own proper place, the vantage from which the city is read, inside the object of study.

Key ideas

  • place (lieu) - a stable order in which elements are distributed in relations of coexistence; it excludes two things being in the same spot, is a configuration of positions, and implies stability and a proper law.
  • space (espace) - what appears when direction, velocity and time are taken into account; produced by the operations that orient, situate and temporalise it, so that space is like the word when it is spoken - it has none of the univocity or stability of a proper place.
  • pedestrian speech act / walking as enunciation - walking appropriates the topographical system (as speaking appropriates language), actualises some possibilities, invents others, establishes a here and a there, and amounts to a rhetoric of turns, detours and figures (synecdoche, asyndeton).
  • Wandersmänner - the walkers whose bodies write an urban text they cannot read; their knowledge of the spaces they use is blind, like that of lovers in each other's arms.
  • long poem of walking - walking as a poem that manipulates spatial organisations, inserting its references and citations into them without receiving its identity from them.
  • strategy - the calculation that presupposes a proper place as its base (a business, an army, a city, a scientific institution); it produces, tabulates and imposes spaces, and its power is the power to see the whole and to predict.
  • tactic - a calculated action determined by the absence of a proper locus, operating in the space of the other, on the wing, seizing the cracks that open in the surveillance of the proprietary powers; it wins nothing it can keep, and de Certeau calls it an art of the weak, whose mode is legerdemain and ruse (la perruque, bricolage, the ruses of consumption).
  • the city as readable text / the solar Eye - the panorama-city seen from the summits is a theoretical (visual) simulacrum; the fiction of knowledge is bound to the lust to be a viewpoint and nothing more, and the walkers below, who cannot see down below, are what that view leaves out.
  • legibility versus the act - maps, route surveys and statistics fix the trace and lose the act of passing by; these fixations are procedures for forgetting, substituting the mark for the practice (PDF 122 / p. 97).
  • spatial stories - narratives that transform places into spaces and open a legitimate theater for practical actions; every story is a travel story.
  • proper names - names create a nowhere in places and change them into passages; walking is determined by semantic tropisms, so it is attracted and repelled by nominations rather than by cartographic reason.
  • memory and haunting - memory is an anti-museum, not localisable; places are composed by displacements among fragmented strata, and the reliquary of absence is what makes a place habitable and walkable (PDF 133 / p. 108).
  • the user as producer - de Certeau rejects the image of the consumer as a passive receiver or spectator-voyeur, and calls it unacceptable in principle as well as untrue in fact (PDF 191 / p. 166; PDF 192 / p. 167; PDF 56 / p. 31).
  • the analyst's proper place - theory and the human sciences also work from a proper place, from which the ordinary practices they depend on are excluded; the theory must be returned to the operations it denies (Part II).

Quotes

  • "Moreover, the question at hand concerns modes of operation or schemata of action, and not directly the subjects (or persons) who are their authors or vehicles. It concerns an operational logic whose models may go as far back as the age-old ruses of fishes and insects that disguise or transform themselves in order to survive" (PDF p.12 / printed p.xi) - the book's method: analyse operations, not subjects. The clearest single warrant for a practice-based study.
  • "strategies are able to produce, tabulate, and impose these spaces, when those operations take place, whereas tactics can only use, manipulate, and divert these spaces." (PDF p.55 / printed p.30) - the strategy/tactic distinction as a difference in what each can do to space.
  • "His elevation transfigures him into a voyeur. It puts him at a distance. It transforms the bewitching world by which one was "possessed" into a text that lies before one's eyes. It allows one to read it, to be a solar Eye, looking down like a god." (PDF p.117 / printed p.92) - the god's-eye view from the World Trade Center: the dome's own vantage, named and diagnosed.
  • "They walk -an elementary form of this experience of the city; they are walkers, Wandersmanner, whose bodies follow the thicks and thins of an urban "text" they write without being able to read it. These practitioners make use of spaces that cannot be seen; their knowledge of them is as blind as that of lovers in each other's arms." (PDF p.118 / printed p.93) - the walkers below; the legibility gap between writing and reading a city.
  • "It thus seems possible to give a preliminary definition of walking as a space of enunciation." (PDF p.123 / printed p.98) - walking as speech act; the basis for treating a walk as an utterance.
  • "The long poem of walking manipulates spatial organizations, no matter how panoptic they may be: it is neither foreign to them (it can take place only within them) nor in conformity with them (it does not receive its identity from them). It creates shadows and ambiguities within them. It inserts its multitudinous references and citations into them" (PDF p.126 / printed p.101) - walking produces meaning inside a panoptic order without obeying it.
  • "proper names carve out pockets of hidden and familiar meanings. They "make sense"; in other words, they are the impetus of movements, like vocations and calls that turn or divert an itinerary by giving it a meaning (or a direction) (sens) that was previously unforeseen. These names create a nowhere in places; they change them into passages." (PDF p.129 / printed p.104) - names, not coordinates, orient movement; directly usable for asking audiences which way is north.
  • "There is no place that is not haunted by many different spirits" (PDF p.133 / printed p.108) - memory and haunting as constitutive of place. The OCR loses the rest of the sentence.
  • "Every story is a travel story-a spatial practice." (PDF p.140 / printed p.115) - narrative as a spatial operation, the shortest form of the argument.
  • "A place (lieu) is the order (of whatever kind) in accord with which elements are distributed in relationships of coexistence. It thus excludes the possibility of two things being in the same location (place)." (PDF p.142 / printed p.117) - the definition of place.
  • "In short, space is a practiced place. Thus the street geometrically defined by urban planning is transformed into a space by walkers." (PDF p.142 / printed p.117) - the hinge of Part III: no space without practice.
  • "This founding is precisely the primary role of the story. It opens a legitimate theater for practical actions. It creates a field that authorizes dangerous and contingent social actions." (PDF p.150 / printed p.125) - stories as the license for action; the everyday means of legitimating a space.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the corpus's main theoretical instrument for the space/place pair and for the audience as producer rather than receiver. It can carry: the argument that a dome's space is produced by the movements and acts of those inside it rather than by its projection geometry; the critique of the god's-eye, all-seeing vantage (the panoptic eye that de Certeau locates on the 110th floor is the same figure a dome resists or reproduces); the treatment of walking and looking as enunciation, which is what allows a performance lecture's walk-around, or an audience's guesses about north, to count as propositional rather than decorative; the naming argument, since orientation by proper name rather than by coordinate is exactly what an audience is asked for when asked which way is north; and the methodological warrant that studying operations is as legitimate as studying texts. It cannot carry: anything about domes, projection, screens, planetaria, image technologies or immersion as such (none of these appear in the book); no empirical audience work and no data; no theory of the theatre event or of liveness; and no notion of practice as research by practitioners - de Certeau is analysing other people's practices from a scholarly position, which is a limit the researcher should state rather than hide.

Limits

The French original is 1974-1980 and the translation 1984: it predates digital immersion, virtual environments and fulldome by decades, and its media examples are print, television, newspapers and the street. The spatial analysis is a reading of maps, stories, legends and the built city (Paris, New York) rather than field observation, and it is dated at the level of detail - the World Trade Center passage reads the tower as a monument of Western urbanism, before 2001 and before the towers' destruction, so citing it as the dome's analogue slides two very different apparatuses together. Part III is only three chapters inside a five-part book whose centre of gravity is textual (Part IV on writing and reading), and the argument is spread across essays that shift register between semiotics, psychoanalysis and historiography. The consumer-as-producer thesis is asserted rather than demonstrated with cases. And the present text is OCR from a scan without a text layer: quotations are individually checked, but the file drops lines and merges words throughout, so any further quotation from this source should be re-checked against a printed copy.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_Phenomenology_of_Perception_Maurice_Merleau_Ponty_pdf - the geometrical/anthropological space distinction de Certeau cites directly on PDF p.142.
  • Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf - space and place inside performance rather than in everyday walking.
  • Books_The_Virtual_Window_pdf - the window/frame as a spatial figure, the optical apparatus de Certeau keeps at arm's length.
  • Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf - the history of immersive viewing spaces, i.e. the dome's own lineage.
  • Books_Immersed_in_Technology_Moser_and_MacLeod_pdf - presence and embodiment in immersive systems.
  • Fulldome_Articles_005_Towards_a_Fulldome_Manifesto_final_proof_pdf - the field's own claim-making, where the panoptic/immersive vantage is argued rather than diagnosed.

So the volume does divide roughly as the project expected — introduction, dyad/mother-child material (ch. 4–5), God material (ch. 8 and the theological excursuses), and The Sirens (ch. 7) — but the parts are interleaved with excursuses and the God/Trinity block is the longest single stretch.

Extraction damage (important). OCR from an image-only scan: spaces inside words are frequently dropped (throughthesuper-sensualmost real, existenceisalways), whole lines are dropped mid-paragraph (the sentence that defines a sphere provisionally as an orb in two halves is cut off at PDF p.22; Chapter 8's Trinity discussion runs into a picture caption on PDF p.297), running heads and figure captions are spliced into the body text, and several folios are misread. The extraction yields roughly 1,800–3,700 characters per spread (about 150 words per printed page), around half what a 664-page text of this density should give, so losses are general, not only where visible. Every quote below was string-checked against the .txt after whitespace normalisation; nothing was quoted from a visibly broken span, and damaged passages are described in prose instead of quoted.

Argument in brief

Sloterdijk's thesis is that being human means being inside something shared, and that philosophy and psychology have systematically misdescribed this. He rejects the isolated subject — the substantial ego units that, in his account, modern individualism treats as interacting voluntarily, like members of a liberal club — and replaces it with the pair as the minimal unit: a sphere is an orb in two halves, a shared, polar space that exists only because those inside it make and keep it. He calls his discipline microspherology, the study of small, intimate, shared interiors, and insists these are not metaphors and not the Freudian unconscious: they are vessels that human beings generate out of closeness, incorporation and resonance. The book is built as an essayistic tour rather than an argument with evidence — Introduction, then eight chapters each followed by an excursus — moving from breath and the breathed commune, to faces and hypnotic rapport, the womb, the primal companion (his With), angels and twins, the Sirens, and finally the Trinity as the model of a shared inside with no outside. The method is morphological and speculative: he reads theology, myth, psychoanalysis, poetry and philosophy for figures of roundness, enclosure, breath and resonance, and treats those figures as the real forms of things, not as decorations (PDF p.30). The tone is essayistic and deliberately immodest, including a proposed entrance inscription for the trilogy that refuses entry to anyone unwilling to praise transference and to refute loneliness (PDF p.6). There is no data, no case study, and no engagement with performance, media or built enclosure.

Key ideas

  • sphere — not a metaphor for community but the name of the containing form a pair or group inhabits: "The sphere is the interior, disclosed, shared realm inhabited by humans — in so far as they succeed in becoming humans." (PDF p.14). Spheres are produced rather than given, and they lapse without upkeep.
  • microspherology — the name of Volume I's project: the analysis of small, intimate, plural interiors. It is defined negatively as well as positively — not the introspection of a single consciousness, not depth psychology's apparatus, not the auditorium (PDF p.49) — and the intimate is specified as divided, consubjective, inter-intelligent interiors that exist only to the extent that human individuals create them as their own vessels, through closeness, incorporation, invasion, interfolding and resonance (PDF p.49).
  • the twin bubble / primal pair — the sphere's basic form is two-poled: two inhabitants facing one another in polar kinship, so that the shared subtlety they live in is the sphere itself (PDF p.22). The primal scene is therefore not a subject but a pair, and the second is not added to the first: where the second does not enter, the first was not given either (PDF p.21).
  • the With (primal companion) — his name for the pre-subjective partner of the intrauterine and newborn phase, coined to avoid object-relations vocabulary. It is what first gives the foetus and then the child a sense of their lasting Here; he describes it as a dark little brother placed beside us so that the fetal night would not be too lonely (PDF p.179). Excursus 6, on spheric mourning, is his account of losing it.
  • immune structures / morphological immunology — the reason a shared interior matters is protection: spheres are constructs of morphological immunology, and only in immune structures that form interiors can humans carry on their generational and individuating processes (PDF p.23). He also speaks of political immunology (PDF p.32). The term co-immunism does not occur anywhere in this volume — it belongs to his later work; the nearest vocabulary here is immune system, immune structures that form interiors, morpho-immunological, and political immunology.
  • the shared inside (intimate interior against an outside) — the interior is defined by contrast with a non-interior world that presses on it from the start, so that it must continually restore and extend itself (PDF p.23). The last chapter is presented as a theological preparation for a theory of the shared inside (PDF p.539ff); its model is a communion of persons whose shared interior has no outside, with no externalisation or estrangement inside it (PDF pp.297–299).
  • being-with against the monad — modern psychology dissolves the individualistic semblance of self-sufficient egos (PDF p.42); for Sloterdijk Heidegger's analysis of where Dasein is requires that every suggestion and mood of essential loneliness be bracketed before the structures of accompanied existence can be reached, and Heidegger's own turn to the who-question leaves, in his words, a lonely, hysterical-heroic existential subject (PDF p.170).
  • breath, voice, ritual, air conditioning — a shared space is held together by practice as much as by architecture: prayer, liturgy, gesture, myth, mutual attention and repeated symbolic work. The symbolic conditioning of the air of a shared space is, in his formula, the primal production of every society (PDF p.23); ritual and self-stimulation create social fabric even from resistant material (PDF p.29); and people work constantly on their accommodation in imaginary, sonorous, semiotic, ritual and technical shells (PDF p.42).
  • the sonosphere / voice as a sphere-forming medium — sound cannot be faced as an object, only entered: listening to music is being-in-music, he says, and the archaic mother–child exchange of vocal messages is the perfect realisation of an intimate acoustic bipolar sphere (PDF p.148). Chapter 7 reads the Sirens as the media-theoretical case — a song that is the listener's own and draws them into the space it opens.
  • celestial domes and their loss — the book's own dome history: the two-thousand-year doctrine of the celestial domes, the sky as the former immune system, its collapse after Copernicus and Digges, and the modern substitution of networks, insurance and an electronic medial skin for it (PDF pp.11–14). What is no longer contained must now build its own covering — on artificial continents under artificial skies and domes (PDF p.12).
  • method: morphology and transference — the study is offered as a morphological tool aimed at effective forms of the real (PDF pp.30, 33); transference is named as the formal source of the creative processes that carry humans into the open (PDF p.6), and the Preliminary Note's proposed entrance sign to the trilogy refuses anyone unwilling to praise transference and to refute loneliness (PDF p.6).

Quotes

  • "The sphere is the interior, disclosed, shared realm inhabited by humans — in so far as they succeed in becoming humans." (PDF p.14 / printed p.28) — the operative definition of a sphere; the first sentence a dome argument would reach for.
  • "Because living always means building spheres, both on a small and a large scale, humans are the beings that establish globes and look out into horizons. Living in spheres means creating the dimension in which humans can be contained. Spheres are immune-systemically effective space creations for ecstatic beings that are operated upon by the outside." (PDF p.14 / printed p.28) — humans as sphere-builders, plus the containment-and-protection claim in three sentences.
  • "But now that God’s shimmering bubbles, the celestial domes, have burst, who could have the power to create prosthetic husks around those who have been exposed? To oppose the cosmic frost infiltrating the human sphere through the open windows of the Enlightenment, modern humanity makes use of a deliberate greenhouse effect: it attempts to balance out its shellessness in space, following the shattering of the celestial domes, through an artificial civilizatory world." (PDF p.12 / printed p.24) — the most directly usable passage for a fulldome review: enclosure as a prosthetic immune shell produced after the loss of the cosmological dome. The same page goes on to say that what is no longer contained must create its satisfaction on artificial continents under artificial skies and domes.
  • "In its basic form the sphere appears as a twin bubble, an ellipsoid space of spirit and experience with at least two inhabitants facing one another in polar kinship. Living in spheres thus means inhabiting a shared subtlety." (PDF p.22 / printed p.45) — the sphere as the primal form of coexistence: a pair before it is a collective. (The preceding sentence, his first provisional definition of a sphere as an orb in two halves, is cut off by a dropped OCR line and is not quoted.)
  • "In this sense, spheres are by definition also morpho-immunological constructs. Only in immune structures that form interiors can humans continue their generational processes and advance their individuations." (PDF p.23 / printed p.46) — the immune argument for shared interiors, in the vocabulary this volume actually uses.
  • "Spheres are air conditioning systems in whose construction and calibration, for those living in real coexistence, it is out of the question not to participate. The symbolic air conditioning of the shared space is the primal production of every society." (PDF p.23 / printed p.46) — how a shared interior is made and maintained: continuous, participatory symbolic labour, not architecture alone.
  • "With the very first lines it draws, then, modern psychology dissolves the individualistic semblance, which attempts to understand individuals as substantial ego units that voluntarily interact with others like members of a liberal club — after the fact, arbitrarily and revocably, as befits the ideology of the individualistic contract society." (PDF p.42 / printed p.85) — the critique of the monadic, isolated subject.
  • "The intimate spaces of microspherology are neither the majestic auditoriums nor the cave-like hiding places of the individual conscious, which interacts with itself to create spatial images suitable for understanding the nature of its own position, spread out between the largest and the smallest." (PDF p.49 / printed p.98) — the demarcation of his own project; note that the auditorium appears only in order to be excluded from it.
  • "This latter term designates the kind of Being which an entity has when it is “in” another one, as the water is “in” the glass, or the garment is “in” the cupboard." (PDF p.167 / printed p.334) — Heidegger quoted on the literal sense of Being-in, which Sloterdijk then re-reads as habitation, the world being inside in a verbal-transitive sense (same page). This is the file's key passage for a claim about physically being inside something.
  • "Voices produce acoustic coverings of spheric-presentist expansion, and the only mode of participation in vocal presences can be described as being-in within the current sonosphere" (PDF p.148 / printed p.297) — the claim that the only way to be with a sound is to be inside it; the chapter's mother–child case follows on the same page.
  • "It is like a dark little brother placed by our side so that the fetal night would not be too lonely; a little sister who, at first glance, is merely there to sleep in the same room with us."
