(PDF p.179 / printed p.357) — the primal companion: the image of a shared interior that precedes the self.
  • "they sing from the listener’s own place. Their secret is to render precisely those songs in which the passing sailor’s ears yearn to immerse themselves." (PDF p.244 / printed p.487) — the Sirens as the paradigm of a sphere-forming medium: the sound that works because it is already the listener's own.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the corpus's philosophy of interiority, the one source that theorises an inside as such, so it can carry three things. First, a vocabulary for what a dome physically is: an interior that is produced and maintained by those inside it, defined against an outside that presses on it, and protective in function — which lets a fulldome argument say that being inside a dome performance is being-in something rather than looking at something, without falling back on presence-and-immersion slogans. Second, the celestial-dome material (PDF pp.11–14) can frame the planetarium dome as a technical replacement for a lost cosmological shell, that is as an artificial immune structure: a harder and more argumentative claim than the usual appeal to immersion. Third, his rejection of the isolated subject allows a dome audience to be described as a pair or group interior — his shared inside — rather than as N individuals each privately immersed, and the sonosphere line does the same for the acoustic envelope (PDF p.148). It cannot carry any account of projection, screens, images, apparatus or audience behaviour; there is no empirical material and no performance theory, and the vocabulary of performance is essentially absent (the word spectator never occurs; audience occurs nine times, always as listeners or addressees, e.g. the courtly audience of a medieval tale, PDF p.54). The planetarium itself appears once in the whole volume — not in the text but in a photo caption for the Jena planetarium of the 1920s (PDF p.35).

Limits

  • Speculative philosophy, not scholarship: claims are advanced through readings of theology, myth, psychoanalysis and poetry, with no data, no method section, and no engagement with media or performance studies. Nothing here can be cited as evidence about what happens to people in a dome — only as a conceptual frame.
  • Dome means cosmology, never architecture: the only domes are celestial ones and the only enclosure is the maternal one. The built planetarium enters the book once, as a photograph caption (Jena, 1920s, PDF p.35); projection, screens, seating and audience behaviour are absent, and the one lecture-room in the volume belongs to Heidegger's bench example (PDF p.167).
  • The pair, not the crowd: Volume I works at two scales, the intimate pair (mother and child, God and soul, lover and beloved) and the cosmological orb. Large collectives and publics are deferred to the later volumes; mass formation is announced rather than analysed.
  • Terminology is coined and heavy (the With, nobject, morpho-immunological, spheric-presentist, air conditioning of space), and the theological chapters, especially Chapter 8 and Excursus 10, need familiarity with Trinitarian doctrine before they can be used.
  • Damaged text layer: lines dropped throughout, words run together, folios misread. Treat any quote as needing a check against the print edition, and do not give a printed folio without noting the two-page spread relation.
  • The book's own frame is a trilogy on sphere-formation in human history; borrowing it for a specific venue argument means borrowing concepts, not its thesis.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Being Singular Plural (Nancy) — the being-with ontology that Sloterdijk spatialises; co-originarity without spheres.
  • Intercorporeality and Interaffectivity (Fuchs) — the embodied, affective version of the dyad-interior that Sloterdijk describes morphologically.
  • The Roots of Empathy (Gallese) — the shared-affect neuroscience that Sloterdijk's inter-intelligent interiors do without.
  • Phenomenology of Perception (Merleau-Ponty) — the phenomenology of lived space and the body that this book rewrites as a morphology of interiors.
  • The Practice of Everyday Life (de Certeau) — the other account of space as something produced by use and practice.
  • Oskar Schlemmer, Bauhaus Man and Art Figure — stage space and figures in an enclosing volume: the theatrical end of the same problem.
  • Virtual Art: From Illusion to Immersion (Grau) — supplies the media and apparatus history exactly where Sloterdijk supplies none.
  • Immersed in Technology (Moser & MacLeod, eds.) — the immersion discourse that this book's being-in can be argued against.
  • Artificial Hells (Bishop) — participation and spectatorship as social and political questions: the step after an interior has been claimed.
  • Operating Manual for Spaceship Earth (Fuller) — enclosure, shelter and life-support as engineering, a secular version of the immune shell.

Jean-Luc Nancy, Being Singular Plural (1996/2000)

Citation

Nancy, Jean-Luc. Being Singular Plural. Translated by Robert D. Richardson and Anne E. O'Byrne. Meridian: Crossing Aesthetics. Stanford, CA: Stanford University Press, 2000. [Title page, series page and copyright page in the extraction: Stanford University Press Stanford California 2000; Being Singular Plural was originally published as Être singulier pluriel © 1996, Éditions Galilée; ISBN 0-8047-3975-7 (pbk.).] Original PDF: Books_Being_Singular_Plural_Jean_Luc_Nancy_pdf.pdf.

Coverage

Book, complete. 119 PDF pages, 99,454 words — but this is a 2-up scan: each PDF page carries two printed book pages, so printed folios run at roughly twice the PDF page count (printed ≈ 2×PDF − 22 for the first page on a PDF page, − 21 for the second; e.g. PDF p.26 = printed pp.30–31, PDF p.80 = printed pp.138–139). Deduced from folio numbers visible in the extraction, not from the PDF file. Contents: Preface (p.xv), the title essay Of Being Singular Plural (p.1), then War, Right, Sovereignty—Technê (p.101), Eulogy for the Mêlée (p.145), The Surprise of the Event (p.159), Human Excess (p.177), Cosmos Baselius (p.185), Notes (p.193), and the back cover. All essays are present; no preview, no watermark. Extraction quality: heavy letter-spacing damage in the OCR layer — words are printed with spaces between their letters throughout the main essay (T h e, B e i n g, c o e s s e n c e, c i r c u l a t i n g), footnote digits are glued onto the ends of words (Being1, political43), and the second folio on a page is sometimes mis-OCR'd (printed 31 appears as 3i). Quotes below were string-verified and taken only from spans the OCR left clean; where a key sentence sits inside a damaged span this is stated rather than repaired.

Argument in brief

Nancy argues that being has no prior unity from which plurality is later derived: existence is coexistence, and a singular is constituted by its spacing from other singulars. He proposes redoing first philosophy with the singular plural of Being as its foundation, and identifies the move he is reversing — philosophy's habit of establishing the subject (Descartes's ego, Heidegger's Dasein) before introducing being-with, so that the with always arrives second. Against that he claims co-originarity, and notes that the argument cannot be delivered as a treatise because the singular of Being is itself in question. The book is one long ontological essay followed by five shorter pieces on sovereignty, war, technê and the global world, where spacing becomes a political as well as an ontological term. He states in the Preface that the sections can be read in any order, since the text was composed discontinuously.

Key ideas

  • being-with / coessence — the with is not added to a prior being; it is at the heart of Being. The title phrase means the essence of Being is only as coessence (PDF p.26, printed p.30 — that sentence sits in a letter-spaced span in this scan).
  • coexistence — originary rather than a secondary aggregation: the world is the coexistence that puts existences together. Plurality is not the random dispersion of a primordial essence.
  • the singular plural — one hyphen does union and division at once; each singular is exposed to others without being absorbed.
  • spacing / dis-position — presence is dis-position, the spacing of singularities; meaning requires distancing and division. Space is what makes presence possible, not a container that presence sits in.
  • the between — no connective tissue, no cement, no bridge; contact is separation, and the law of touching is separation (PDF p.13, printed p.5).
  • originary alterity — the other is not the world's opposite but one of the two.
  • address — the Preface asks how thinking addresses itself and to everyone, and names the philosophical treatise as the neutralization of address. Directly relevant to the lecture as a form.
  • technê / ecotechnics — in the political essays technê is the existence of finite existence, and the world of spacing is contrasted with the world of finishing and of the identification of figures.

Quotes

  • "A world is not something external to existence; it is not an extrinsic addition to other existences; the world is the coexistence that puts these existences together." (PDF p.25 / printed p.28-29) — world as coexistence - the ontological frame for a room full of people and things.
  • "Therefore, it is not the case that the "with" is an addition to some prior Being; instead, the "with" is at the heart of Being." (PDF p.26 / printed p.30) — being-with is originary, not a relation added to pre-existing subjects.
  • "Existence is with: otherwise nothing exists." (PDF p.13 / printed p.4-5) — the shortest statement of the being-with thesis; Nancy's closest equivalent to there is no being without being-with.
  • "Access is "coming to presence," but presence itself is dis-position, the spacing of singularities." (PDF p.18 / printed p.14) — presence as spacing - the passage a dome/enclosure argument can use.
  • "Meaning is not a milieu in which we are immersed." (PDF p.13 / printed p.4-5) — direct counter to immersion rhetoric: no surrounding medium to be in.
  • "There is proximity, but only to the extent that extreme closeness emphasizes the distancing it opens up." (PDF p.13 / printed p.5) — proximity and distancing as co-constituted - argues against an enclosure that cancels distance.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

Useful for an ontological warrant: a dome performance can be described as a being-with rather than as a subject confronting an image, with audience, imagery, architecture and apparatus each singular and mutually co-constitutive in the event. The spacing and dis-position passages give a philosophical basis for treating the dome's enclosure as the production of distance — which is what an argument against immersion-as-pure-presence needs — and the p.13 sentence refusing milieu can be set directly against the soft immersion vocabulary that fulldome writing tends to use. It cannot carry anything technical: no account of projection, screens, images, audiences or media, and no empirical material at all.

Limits

Written in 1996 for a French philosophical readership; nothing on domes, planetaria, screens, images or performance, and no media theory. The vocabulary (coessence, dis-position, ecotechnics, originary alterity) needs translation before it can do work in a practice-based context, and the translator's English is dense. The hunted phrase there is no being without being-with does not appear verbatim in this translation — the claim is made in other words (PDF p.13; also p.12, where being cannot be anything but being-with-one-another). The 2-up scan makes page citation ambiguous (each PDF page = two printed pages), and the letter-spaced OCR makes verbatim quotation unreliable outside the clean spans.

Adjacent sources in this folder

Spheres I: Bubbles (Sloterdijk) — the spatial/architectural counterpart to Nancy's being-with. Intercorporeality and Interaffectivity (Fuchs) — where the coexistence claim meets the body and affect. Phenomenology of Perception (Merleau-Ponty) — the phenomenology of space that Nancy's spacing argument displaces. The Practice of Everyday Life (de Certeau) — space as practised rather than given, a different route to the same problem. Immersed in Technology (Moser & MacLeod, eds.) — what an immersion argument looks like without Nancy's spacing.

Graham Harman, Object-Oriented Ontology: A New Theory of Everything (2017)

Citation

Harman, Graham. Object-Oriented Ontology: A New Theory of Everything. A Pelican Introduction. London: Pelican / Penguin Books, 2017. [Title page and copyright page in the extraction: PELICAN an imprint of PENGUIN BOOKS, First published 2017, Text copyright © Graham Harman, 2017, ISBN 9780241269152.] Original PDF: Books_Object_Oriented_Ontology_Graham_Harman_pdf.pdf.

Coverage

Book, complete. 305 PDF pages, 79,707 words, one printed page per PDF page (printed folio = PDF page − 6). Introduction plus seven chapters — A New Theory of Everything (p.19), Aesthetics Is the Root of All Philosophy (p.59), Society and Politics (p.103), Indirect Relations (p.147), OOO and its Rivals (p.195), Varying Approaches to OOO (p.219), OOO in Overview (p.253) — then Further Reading (p.263), Notes (p.279) and Index (p.291). No preview, no watermark. Two defects to know about. (1) The Index is a placeholder: its printed pages carry only the word INDEX (and one [Text to come]), with no entries, so the file cannot be index-searched. (2) The text layer is derived from an epub and has 1,714 soft hyphens and 1,814 zero-width spaces injected inside words (they fall in the middle of words like objects, non-human and reduction, e.g. between the o and the b of objects), so a naive string search misses phrases that are actually on the page even though the words read normally on screen. Quotes below were located with whitespace- and control-character-tolerant matching, and the strings used contain none of those characters.

Argument in brief

Harman's trade introduction to object-oriented ontology. He defines an object as anything that cannot be entirely reduced either downward to its components (undermining) or upward to its effects and relations (overmining), and argues that most philosophy does both at once, a manoeuvre he calls duomining. Because real objects withdraw from all direct contact — including from human minds — OOO needs an account of indirect contact (vicarious causation) and a fourfold of real and sensual objects and qualities. He then claims that aesthetics rather than knowledge is the root of philosophy, and models aesthetic experience on theatre: metaphor works because a beholder steps in to replace the withdrawn object, like a method actor. The politics chapter argues that left and right political theory alike give human beings too much and everything else too little; later chapters place OOO against Derrida, Foucault, the New Realism and Latour, and survey Bogost, Bryant, Morton, Bennett and Garcia.

Key ideas

  • withdrawal — objects never make full contact with each other or with the human mind; this is the central principle of the whole position.
  • undermining / overmining / duomining — reducing a thing to its parts; reducing it to its effects and relations; doing both at once. OOO accepts neither reduction.
  • the fourfold — real object, real qualities, sensual object, sensual qualities, giving four kinds of tension between an object and its qualities (named space, time, essence, eidos).
  • vicarious causation — objects touch without touching, through a third term or mediator; causation happens in the gap rather than by contact.
  • allure / theatricality — aesthetic experience requires a beholder (itself an object) to substitute for the withdrawn object; metaphor is staged, and reading is method acting.
  • flat ontology — objects real, fictional, natural, artificial, human or non-human are all objects, and relations are the special case that needs explaining, not the default.
  • object — anything irreducible to both its pieces and its effects; the two ways of saying what a thing is (what it is made of, what it does) are exactly what it is not.

Quotes

  • "This withdrawal or withholding of things from direct access is the central principle of OOO." (PDF p.13 / printed p.7) — withdrawal stated as the first principle.
  • "OOO’s commitment to the mutual darkness of objects is what enables it to resist some of the fashionable holistic philosophies of our time, which hold that everything is defined purely by its relations and that the world is nothing but the total system of these relations." (PDF p.18 / printed p.12) — objects are not exhausted by their relations - the anti-relationalist claim.
  • "Against the assumptions of common sense, objects cannot make direct contact with each other, but require a third term or mediator for such contact to occur." (PDF p.18 / printed p.12) — all contact is mediated - usable for performer/image/audience as indirect relation.
  • "My response is that OOO means ‘object’ in an unusually wide sense: an object is anything that cannot be entirely reduced either to the components of which it is made or to the effects that it has on other things." (PDF p.49 / printed p.43) — definition of object - not reducible to parts or to effects (including its effects on humans).
  • "Nor is the thing both what it is made of and what it does in combination, a common strategy that I have called ‘duomining’." (PDF p.56 / printed p.50) — duomining named: undermining and overmining at once.
  • "the two kinds of objects and two kinds of qualities yield four separate types of rift between objects and their qualities" (PDF p.155 / printed p.149) — the fourfold in one line.
  • "Instead, the metaphor is theatrical in the same sense in which living one’s role on stage is theatrical." (PDF p.93 / printed p.87) — aesthetics modelled on stage acting - the bridge from OOO to performance.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

Gives a fulldome literature review a way to treat projected imagery as a real object with its own withdrawn reality rather than as a sign or as an effect of its makers, and to describe the relation between performer, image, apparatus and audience as indirect — real influence with no full access from any side. The metaphor-as-theatre passage (PDF p.93) is the shortest bridge in the book from OOO to performance, since it already treats beholding as role-playing. It cannot carry anything about screens, projection, spectatorship or liveness, and it works against the phenomenological idiom (embodiment, perception, lived space) that most dome writing uses, so it is a counterweight rather than a scaffold.

Limits

An introductory trade book: the fourfold and vicarious causation are asserted and illustrated rather than demonstrated, internal OOO disagreements are sketched, and the argument leans on a high density of examples from architecture, wine, music and Lovecraft. Nothing on media, images, scale or architecture-as-experience beyond one comparison of space to proximity and distance (PDF p.164). The introduction is framed by 2016 politics, and the index is unusable in this scan.

Adjacent sources in this folder

Speculative Everything (Dunne & Raby) — design as the proposal of objects and worlds, adjacent to OOO's aesthetics. Virtual Art: From Illusion to Immersion (Grau) — the image-centred account OOO would refuse to reduce to its effects. Immersed in Technology (Moser & MacLeod, eds.) — art practice in immersive systems, the material Harman's book does not address. Mapping Intermediality in Performance (Bay-Cheng et al.) — what an actually relational account of performance objects looks like. Design Fiction (Bleecker) — objects and prototypes as non-human actants in a practice context.

R. Buckminster Fuller, Operating Manual for Spaceship Earth (1969)

Citation

Fuller, R. Buckminster. Operating Manual for Spaceship Earth. 1969. Publication details from the file's first page, which gives only the title plus "By Richard Buckminster Fuller • 1969 •". The file has no imprint or copyright page, so publisher and place cannot be recovered from the text layer (the book's standard imprint is Southern Illinois University Press, 1969 — external knowledge, flagged as not from the file). Original PDF filename: Books_Operating_Manual_for_Spaceship_Earth_Fuller.pdf.

Coverage

Book, complete text: 45 PDF pages, 23,308 words, eight numbered chapters — 1 comprehensive propensities, 2 origins of specialization, 3 comprehensively commanded automation, 4 spaceship earth, 5 general systems theory, 6 synergy, 7 integral functions, 8 the regenerative landscape — plus the closing pages addressed to planners, architects and engineers. This is a clean typed e-text, not a scan and not a preview: no OCR garbling, no "bookpreview" watermark. It has no front matter, publisher page, notes or index, and it carries its own page footers ("• 14 • Operating Manual For Spaceship Earth") at the end of each PDF page chunk; where the printed edition used dashes the e-text shows "‹" or garbled marks. Printed page numbers here are those e-text markers, since the printed edition's pagination is not present in the file.

Argument in brief

Fuller argues that humanity is aboard a designed, finite, spherical vehicle — "Spaceship Earth" — whose behaviour no specialist can understand from inside a single discipline. Specialisation, which he traces back to the "Great Pirates" who kept comprehensive navigational knowledge secret, is named as the structural cause of modern failure: "society operates on the theory that specialization is the key to success, not realizing that specialization precludes comprehensive thinking." His remedy is general systems theory plus synergy — behaviour of wholes unpredicted by their parts — and a design science whose model is Leonardo, the "comprehensively anticipatory design scientist". Since the vehicle is spherical and whole, the adequate viewpoint is the whole-Earth one, which ordinary experience never supplies: nobody has been outside the ship, and of the sphere we have seen only small portions. The closing chapters propose automation plus research-and-development fellowships so that humans can stop doing muscle work and start thinking comprehensively, and hand the initiative to planners, architects and engineers who can "look at all of Philadelphia" rather than "through a hole at one house".

Key ideas

  • spaceship earth — the Earth as a designed, finite, spherical vehicle with regenerative life support; the device that makes the whole planet a single system with one manual.
  • "we are all astronauts" — nobody has ever been outside the ship, so first-person experience is the only account we have of a total environment; "what does it feel like?" is answered by "that's all we have ever experienced".
  • specialization — in ch. 1 the reason comprehensive thinking is not done; in ch. 2 Fuller also calls specialisation a fancy form of slavery (PDF p.9) and blames the Great Pirates' secrecy for the divide between comprehensive masters and specialised hands.
  • comprehensive propensities / comprehensive thinking — the mind's drive to find generalised principles that hold in every special case; the normative ideal of the book.
  • general systems theory — the modern tool for thinking of the largest wholes; adopted explicitly as the method of the middle chapters.
  • synergy / synergetics — synergy is behaviour of whole systems unpredicted by their parts; synergetics is topology plus vectorial geometry, offered as the mathematics that goes with it.
  • comprehensively anticipatory design scientist — Leonardo da Vinci as the model for the designer who anticipates whole-system consequences; the ancestor of later Fullerite "comprehensive design".
  • planners and architects — the professions with "a little wider focus" than other specialists, told to eschew "the role of specialists who deal only in parts" and to ask how we think in terms of wholes.
  • planetarium — the word appears once in the book, as evidence that generalist curiosity is innate: children are enthusiastic planetarium audiences (PDF p.3). It is the only point where a dome-shaped image environment figures at all.

Quotes

  • "society operates on the theory that specialization is the key to success, not realizing that specialization precludes comprehensive thinking." (PDF p.3 / e-text page marker 3) — the specialisation-obstructs argument in one sentence; the claim a dome-based practice-research project has to answer.
  • "I’ve often heard people say, ’I wonder what it would be like to be on board a spaceship," and the answer is very simple. What does it feel like? That’s all we have ever experienced. We are all astronauts." (PDF p.14 / e-text page marker 14) — the whole-system line, and an argument that experience of a total environment can only be first-person; usable when immersion claims are made. (The file's OCR of the opening quotation mark is wrong; the words are as printed.)
  • "I’m sure that you don’t really sense yourself to be aboard a fantastically real spaceship our spherical Spaceship Earth. Of our little sphere you have seen only small portions." (PDF p.14 / e-text page marker 14) — the sphere and its partial visibility: the perceptual premise behind any claim that a dome shows a whole field at once.
  • "Becoming deliberately expansive instead of contractive, we ask, "How do we think in terms of wholes?"" (PDF p.18 / e-text page marker 18) — comprehensive design posed as a method question; the closest the book comes to formulating a research question.
  • "Synergy is the only word in our language that means behavior of whole systems unpredicted by the separately observed behaviors of any of the system’s separate parts or any subassembly of the system’s parts." (PDF p.22 / e-text page marker 22) — synergetics defined; useful when a dome is argued as a system whose effect is not the sum of its projections.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

It can carry one framing paragraph: where the vocabulary of whole-system, comprehensive design comes from, and why a spherical, whole-field format invites comprehensive rather than specialist claims — the ancestor idiom of the dome imagination. It can also supply a counter-argument to be answered: if specialisation obstructs comprehensive thinking, a practice-based research project in a dome is exactly the kind of work that has to justify its comprehensiveness. It cannot carry anything about spectatorship, performance, staging, projection, or the experience of being inside an image; its sphere is geometric and rhetorical, never a surface.

Limits

1969 and entirely pre-digital: no art, no media, no spectatorship, no audience. The sphere is an object of design and navigation, not an image environment; the planetarium appears once, in passing, as an example of a child's curiosity. The historical argument (the Great Pirates, the sea-dog history of specialisation) is period rhetoric, and the closing insistence that the political impasse "will be resolved by the computer" has aged badly. "Comprehensive" is asserted and illustrated rather than operationalised — no method, no criteria, no case study. Text is complete but unpaginated in file terms; page references are the e-text's own numbered page markers, not printed folios.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_Spheres_Bubbles_pdf — Sloterdijk's spheres; another spherical-imagination vocabulary (containers, bubbles) with a very different politics.
  • Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf — the panorama-to-dome history that gives Fuller's spherical viewpoint an actual image apparatus.
  • Fulldome_Articles_fulldomesummit_08_the_perception_of_domed_environments_by_pete_carss_and — empirical work on what a domed field of view does to perception; the test of Fuller's claim that the sphere is never seen whole.
  • Fulldome_Articles_005_Towards_a_Fulldome_Manifesto_final_proof_pdf — the fulldome field's own comprehensive claim on the medium.
  • Books_Design_Fiction_Julian_Bleecker_pdf and Books_Speculative_Everything_Anthony_Dunne_Fiona_Raby_pdf — design-of-futures lineage that descends from Fuller's design science and inherits its comprehensive ambition.
  • Books_Barrett_Estelle_Bolt_Barbara_eds_Practice_as_Research_Approches_to_Creative_Arts_Enq — how a practitioner-researcher would actually operationalise "comprehensive" thinking.

Kevin Lynch, The Image of the City (1960)

Citation

Lynch, Kevin. The Image of the City. Cambridge, MA: The MIT Press, 1960. [title page, PDF p.3; copyright page, PDF p.4: "Copyright © 1960 by the Massachusetts Institute of Technology and the President and Fellows of Harvard College / Twentieth Printing, 1990", ISBN 0 262 12004 6 hardcover, 0 262 62001 4 paperback; Library of Congress Catalog Card No. 60-7362. Preface signed "M.I.T. December, 1959". Published as one of the series of the Joint Center for Urban Studies of MIT and Harvard (PDF p.4).]

Original PDF filename: Kevin-Lynch-The-Image-of-the-City.pdf (source folder literature\Expanded\; extracted to Expanded_Lynch_The_Image_of_the_City_pdf.txt).

Coverage

Book (complete). 104 PDF pages, 57,619 words. Contains: cover blurb and review quotes (PDF pp.1–2), title/copyright/preface/contents (PDF pp.3–5), Ch. I The Image of the Environment (printed p.1), II Three Cities (14), III The City Image and Its Elements (46), IV City Form (91), V A New Scale (118), Appendix A Some References to Orientation (123), Appendix B The Use of the Method (140), Appendix C Two Examples of Analysis (160), Bibliography (182), Index (187) — structure from the contents page, PDF p.5. The index and bibliography are present (PDF pp.96–103).

Page mapping is not reliable in this file: each extracted PDF page packs roughly two printed pages of the book, the blocks are not always in order, and printed folios appear both mid-page and as trailing pairs. PDF pages are therefore used as the reference throughout; a printed page is given only where the contents page pins a section start and the passage sits at that point (Legibility p.2, Building the Image p.6, Ch. III p.46). Verified sample: PDF 23 = printed 34, PDF 29 = 46, PDF 47 = 82–83, PDF 63 = 114–115.

Text layer is clean enough to quote but has scattered OCR errors in the character stream (not just line breaks): "parrs" for parts, "It_will", "legible.city", "pattern/Just", "bur" for but, "Nor only" for Not only, "visibilily" for visibility, and — importantly — the book's own term printed in the running text as "image ability" where the heading and all secondary literature give imageability. Figures are referenced in the text (Fig. 33, etc.) but the images themselves are not in the text layer.

Argument in brief

The city's image is the generalised mental picture of the exterior physical world that an inhabitant carries; it is produced in a two-way process between the observer and the environment, and is therefore shiftable from both ends — by retraining the observer or by reshaping the surroundings. Lynch isolates one property of the environment that can be designed: legibility (the ease with which the parts of the cityscape can be recognised and organised into a coherent pattern) and, generalised, imageability (the quality in a physical object that gives it a high probability of evoking a strong image in any given observer). He asserts that a legible city is not merely convenient but socially and emotionally important, because orientation is the image's original function and its failure — disorientation — is felt as anxiety and terror. The empirical base is a pilot study of the central areas of Boston, Jersey City and Los Angeles: systematic field reconnaissance on foot by a trained observer, plus long interviews with residents who gave descriptions, locations, sketches and "imaginary trips" (about thirty people in Boston, fifteen each elsewhere; a small sample he himself calls skewed toward professional and managerial classes). From those interviews he abstracts five element types — paths, edges, districts, nodes, landmarks — and shows which forms are strongly imaged (the Charles River edge, Boston Common, landmark clusters, path sequences) and which are not (Jersey City's formlessness, Los Angeles' grid and freeways, Boston's misleading curves). The second half turns the analysis into design prescription (path sequencing, form qualities, "the sense of the whole", metropolitan form, the process of design); Appendices A–C hold the cross-cultural material on orientation systems, an evaluation of the method, and two worked area analyses.

Key ideas

  • Legibility — a property of the environment, defined by analogy with printed text: the ease with which the city's parts can be recognised and organised into a coherent pattern. Not the same thing as beauty or meaning.
  • Imageability — the generalised version of legibility, defined from the object's side: "that quality in a physical object which gives it a high probability of evoking a strong image in any given observer." He explicitly does not mean the obvious or the plain, and not something fixed or regularly ordered. He borrows Stern's older term apparency.
  • The environmental image — "the generalized mental picture of the exterior physical world that is held by an individual"; the product of "immediate sensation and of the memory of past experience", used to interpret information and guide action. It is not a scaled copy of the world: it is a "purposive simplification" made by reducing, adding, fusing and distorting, retaining only topological order (PDF p.49).
  • Two-way process / observer and environment — image development runs in both directions; the observer "selects, organizes, and endows with meaning", and the environment "suggests distinctions and relations". Hence two design routes: symbolic devices, retraining the perceiver, or reshaping surroundings (PDF pp.9–11).
  • Identity, structure, meaning — his three analytic components of an image (PDF p.10). He deliberately sets meaning aside to work on the physical clarity of identity and structure.
  • The five elements — paths (channels of movement; the predominant element for most interviewees), edges (linear boundaries not used as paths; lateral references), districts (medium-to-large two-dimensional areas entered "inside of"), nodes (strategic points entered — junctions, concentrations, cores), landmarks (external point-references not entered; distant/radial vs local). They never exist alone: "Districts are structured with nodes, defined by edges, penetrated by paths, and sprinkled with landmarks." The element type shifts with the viewing situation (expressway = path for the driver, edge for the pedestrian).
  • Way-finding — the original function of the environmental image; he denies any "mystic 'instinct' of way-finding" and describes it instead as "a consistent use and organization of definite sensory cues from the external environment". Orientation is therefore a competence — learned, cultural and trainable (Appendix A surveys very different orientation systems; the blindfold-maze and "east side of the porch" examples show training).
  • Disorientation — his term for the failure case, at the level of the person: "The very word 'lost' in our language means much more than simple geographical uncertainty; it carries overtones of utter disaster." He links it to anxiety, terror, panic, even vertigo, and in Appendix A to brain injury where the image cannot be structured at all. Environmental failure cases: Jersey City's "low order of imageability", Los Angeles as "formless", Boston's "critical ambiguity of shape at the city core, a major orientation flaw" (PDF p.17); freeway exits and grid shifts as moments of "severe disorientation" (PDF pp.34, 37).
  • Image strength and weakness — images differ in density, in hierarchical vs. continuous organisation, and by viewpoint, time of day, season and change over time; unfamiliar observers rely on regions and broad directions, familiar ones on small landmarks and sequences.
  • Sequence and the temporal problem — the city is "a temporal art" but cannot control sequence; the sequences are "reversed, interrupted, abandoned, cut across" (PDF p.6). Later he designs for this: sequences of landmarks with trigger cues at decisions and reassuring cues afterwards, which "may break down when the sequence is reversed or scrambled"; sequences that are "interruptible as well as reversible".
  • Sensory hierarchy — orientation is multi-sensory by his own list (colour, shape, motion, polarisation of light, smell, sound, touch, kinesthesia, sense of gravity, perhaps magnetic fields; "Nearly every sense is in operation, and the image is the composite of them all", PDF p.7), but vision carries the argument: "vision seems to be dominant" in the perception of path motion, tactile and inertial senses entering only alongside it (PDF p.55; also PDF p.33, where curving is "not often sensed kinesthetically"), and sounds and smells "did not seem to constitute landmarks by themselves" (PDF p.47).
  • Public vs private image — individual images overlap into "public images"; he studies the overlap (shared by "significant numbers"), passes over individual difference, and admits his sample cannot prove a true public image.

Quotes

  • "This book will consider the visual quality of the American city by studying the mental image of that city which is held by its citizens. It_will concentrate especially on one particular visual quality: the apparent clarity or "Legibility" of the cityscape. By this we mean the ease with which its parrs can be recognized and can be organized into a coherent pattern/Just as this printed page, if it is legible, can be visually grasped as a related pattern of recognizable symbols, so a legible.city would be one whose districts or landmarks or pathways are easily identifiable and are easily grouped into an over-all pattern." (PDF p.7 / printed pp.2–3) — his definition of legibility; note OCR damage ("It_will", "parrs", "pattern/Just", "legible.city"). Quote from a corrected edition if the wording is used verbatim.
  • "This leads to the definition of what might be called image ability; that quality in a physical object which gives it a high probability of evoking a strong image in any given observer. It is that shape, color, or arrangement which facilitates the making of vividly identified, powerfully structured, highly useful mental images of the environment." (PDF p.10) — the definition of imageability, the property a space can be designed to have. The text reads "image ability" (spaced) here.
  • "Environmental images are the result of a two-way process between the observer and his environment. The environment suggests distinctions and relations, and the observer-with great adaptability and in the light of his own purposes-selects, organizes, and endows with meaning what he sees. The image so developed now limits and emphasizes what is seen, while the image itself is being tested against the filtered perceptual input in a constant interacting process." (PDF p.9 / printed p.6) — the image is not a copy in the head; environment and observer co-produce it. (Print has em dashes where the text layer has hyphens.)
  • "In the process of way-finding, the strategic link is the environmental image, the generalized mental picture of the exterior physical world that is held by an individual. This image is the product both of immediate sensation and of the memory of past experience, and it is used to interpret information and to guide action." (PDF p.8) — orientation depends on a structured image, built from sensation and memory; the sentence a dome that asks "which way is north" is already inside.
  • "If an image is to have value for orientation in the living space, it must have several qualities. It must be sufficient, true in a pragmatic sense, allowing the individual to operate within his environment to the extent desired. The map, whether exact or not, must be good enough to get one home. It must be sufficiently clear and well integrated to be economical of mental effort: the map must be readable. It should be safe, with a surplus of clues so that alternative actions are possible and the risk of failure is not too high." (PDF p.10) — his criteria for a usable image: sufficiency, clarity, redundancy. The basis for "you need a structured image to act".
  • "Since image development is a two-way process between observer and observed, it is possible to strengthen the image either by symbolic devices, by the retraining of the perceiver, or by reshaping one's surroundings." (PDF p.11) — the single sentence that carries the "designed capacity of the space / learned competence of its inhabitants" claim; note that he calls the second route retraining.
  • "To become completely lost is perhaps a rather rare experience for most people in the modern city. We are supported by the presence of others and by special way-finding devices: maps, street numbers, route signs, bus placards. But let the mishap of disorientation once occur, and the sense of anxiety and even terror that accompanies it reveals to us how closely it is linked to our sense of balance and well-being. The very word "lost" in our language means much more than simple geographical uncertainty; it carries overtones of utter disaster." (PDF p.8) — the failure case and the emotional weight he gives it.
  • "A sequential series of landmarks, in which one detail calls up anticipation of the next and key details trigger specific moves of the observer, appeared to be a standard way in which these people traveled through the city. In such sequences, there were trigger cues whenever turning decisions must be made and reassuring cues that confirmed the observer in decisions gone by. Additional details often helped to give a sense of nearness to the final destination or to intermediate goals. For emotional security as well as functional efficiency, it is important that such sequences be fairly continuous, with no long gaps, although there may be a thickening of detail at nodes. The sequence facilitates recognition and memorization. Familiar observers can store up a vast quantity of point images in familiar sequences, although recognition may break down when the sequence is reversed or scrambled." (PDF p.47) — movement through space as a remembered sequence of cues; the nearest thing in the book to a dramaturgy of way-finding.
  • "There seems to be a public image of any given city which is the overlap of many individual images. Or perhaps there is a series of public images, each held by some significant number of citizens. Such group images are necessary if an individual is to operate successfully within his environment and to cooperate with his fellows." (PDF p.29 / printed p.46) — shared vs private image; the social function of a common spatial picture.
  • "The contents of the city images so far studied, which are referable to physical forms, can conveniently be classified into five types of elements: paths, edges, districts, nodes, and landmarks." (PDF p.29 / printed p.46) — the five-element classification; the definitions that follow (definitions follow, PDF pp.29–30) each state what the element does for orientation.
  • "A carefully constructed sequence, leading from introduction, first statement, and development to climax and conclusion, may fail utterly if a driver enters it directly at the climax point. Therefore it may be necessary to look for sequences which are interruptible as well as reversible, that is, sequences which still have sufficient imageability even when broken in upon at various points, much like a magazine serial." (PDF p.63) — he thinks about sequence in explicitly dramaturgical vocabulary (introduction, statement, development, climax, conclusion) and then designs against it; useful for arguing that a dome piece cannot control its audience's entry point.
  • "Perhaps, however, such techniques would wait upon a time when there is a more attentive and critical audience." (PDF p.63) — the book's one reference to an audience; even here "audience" means the public's attention to city form, not spectators of a work.
  • "We are not simply observers of this spectacle, but are ourselves a part of it, on the stage with the other participants." (PDF p.7) — his image of the city-dweller as participant rather than spectator; the sentence a fulldome argument can borrow, provided it is noted that he means citizens on a street.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

Citable for the proposition that orientation is both a designed capacity of a space (legibility/imageability: properties of the environment, PDF pp.7, 10) and a learned competence of its users (no instinct of way-finding, retraining the perceiver, PDF pp.7, 11), and for the claim that a sufficiently structured image is a precondition of acting in a space at all (PDF pp.8, 10) — with disorientation as the named failure state (PDF p.8). He is also the source of the five-element vocabulary (paths/edges/districts/nodes/landmarks) that a dome piece asking "which way is north" is implicitly manipulating. What he cannot carry: nothing about audiences, spectatorship, performance or media — a dome production is not a city, is not inhabited over years, and has no paths; and its "public image" is shared in a room for an hour rather than across a population. Use him for the definition of the problem, not for the answer in a dome.

Limits

Urban planning and design, 1960, American downtowns at pedestrian/driver scale. It predates immersive media, the planetarium-as-venue literature, and any notion of practice-based research; there is no discussion of spectatorship, staging, projection or the lecture. Method limits are his own: a "small pilot study" whose purpose was "to develop ideas and methods, rather than to prove facts"; samples biased toward professional and managerial classes, so that he cannot claim a true "public image" (PDF p.13); the field mapping was "an abstraction... the generalized impressions that real form makes on an observer indoctrinated in a certain way" (PDF p.76), i.e. trained-observer subjectivity is admitted, not controlled. The subject is assumed to be mobile, sighted and ambulatory; blindness and non-visual orientation appear only as Appendix A exotica. Individual difference, class, gender, race and the social production of meaning are set aside on purpose ("This study will therefore concentrate on the identity and structure of city images", PDF pp.9–10), which is exactly what a performance argument cannot set aside. Historical baggage: ethnic and colonial framing in Appendix A ("primitive man", "native Africans", "South Sea navigator") is of its period. Quote reliability: several passages exist only in mildly corrupted form (see Coverage); the legibility definition in particular should be checked against a print or corrected edition before being quoted verbatim.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Books_The_Practice_of_Everyday_Life_Michel_de_Certeau_pdf — walking as a spatial practice, and the city as something spoken and remembered rather than seen whole; the natural partner to Lynch's pedestrian image.
  • Books_Phenomenology_of_Perception_Merleau_Ponty_pdf — lived, bodily spatiality; covers the kinesthetic/orientational ground Lynch admits but does not develop.
  • Fulldome_Articles_fulldomesummit_08_the_perception_of_domed_environments_by_pete_carss_and — perception inside a domed environment; the closest thing in the corpus to putting Lynch's question in a dome.
  • Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf — history of illusion/immersion in art and architecture, the frame this book gets read through in media studies.
  • Books_Spheres_Bubbles_pdf (Sloterdijk) — being-inside an enclosure as an ontological condition; useful counterweight to Lynch's measurable "image".

8. Research method: practice as research and speculative practice

The section that turns a dome project into a research project. Barrett and Bolt is the methodological anchor: artistic practice viewed as the production of knowledge, the double articulation by which theory emerges from practice and returns to it, material thinking and praxical knowledge as what handling knows, and the exegesis as the vehicle through which practice becomes theory-generating. Brad Haseman's chapter in it names Schön's reflective practice and the performative research paradigm, and describes concrete documentation methods for devised work. The appendix supplies the question that organises the whole thing: what did the studio process reveal that could not have been revealed by any other mode of enquiry. Dunne and Raby and Bleecker add the second method for work that is not yet possible — the designed object as proposition, the diegetic prototype, and design as a way of asking questions rather than answering them, which is the honest description of what a speculative dome project is doing.

Estelle Barrett and Barbara Bolt, eds., Practice as Research: Approaches to Creative Arts Enquiry (2007; pbk 2010)

Citation

Barrett, Estelle, and Barbara Bolt, eds. Practice as Research: Approaches to Creative Arts Enquiry. London: I.B. Tauris, 2007. Paperback edition 2010; reprinted 2009. ISBN 978 1 84511 432 9 (HB) / 978 1 84885 301 0 (PB). Copyright page: "Copyright selection and editorial matter (c) 2007, 2010 Estelle Barrett and Barbara Bolt; Copyright individual chapters (c) 2007, 2010 Estelle Barrett, Barbara Bolt, Paul Carter, Stephen Goddard, Brad Haseman, Annette Iggulden, Shaun McLeod, Gaylene Perry, Dianne Reid, Robyn Stewart, Kim Vincs." Citation taken from the title page and copyright page of the extracted PDF. Original PDF filename: Books_Barrett_Estelle_Bolt_Barbara_eds_Practice_as_Research_Approches_to_Creative_Arts_Enq.pdf

Coverage

Complete book. 218 PDF pages / 86,689 words. Front matter (title, copyright, contents, list of illustrations, acknowledgements, foreword), Introduction, twelve chapters, Notes (printed p.165), References (169), Contributors (181), Appendix "Developing and Writing Creative Arts Practice Research: A Guide" (185). All twelve chapters present; no missing sections detected. Text layer is clean — proper fi/fl ligatures, curly quotes and italic markers survive extraction, so quotes below are reliable. Body folios run exactly 12 behind PDF pages in this extract (verified on PDF pp.41, 45, 47, 161, 165, 198, 200 = printed pp.29, 33, 35, 149, 153, 186, 188, which matches the contents listing); the front matter is roman-numbered (Foreword = printed p.xi). Both numbers are given for every quote.

Chapter list and authors (printed page of first page): - Introduction — Estelle Barrett (1) - Ch.1 "Interest: The Ethics of Invention" — Paul Carter (15) - Ch.2 "The Magic is in Handling" — Barbara Bolt (27) - Ch.3 "History Documents, Arts Reveals: Creative Writing as Research" — Gaylene Perry (35) - Ch.4 "Cutting Choreography: Back and Forth Between 12 Stages and 27 Seconds" — Dianne Reid (47) - Ch.5 "'Silent' Speech" — Annette Iggulden (65) - Ch.6 "Chamber: Experiencing Masculine Identity Through Dance Improvisation" — Shaun McLeod (81) - Ch.7 "Rhizome/MyZone: A Case Study in Studio-based Dance Research" — Kim Vincs (99) - Ch.8 "A Correspondence Between Practices" — Stephen Goddard (113) - Ch.9 "Creating New Stories For Praxis: Navigations, Narrations, Neonarratives" — Robyn Stewart (123) - Ch.10 "Foucault's 'What is An Author': Towards a Critical Discourse of Practice as Research" — Estelle Barrett (135) - Ch.11 "Rupture and Recognition: Identifying The Performative Research Paradigm" — Brad Haseman (147) - Ch.12 "The Exegesis as Meme" — Estelle Barrett (159)

Argument in brief

The book argues that artistic practice is itself a mode of knowledge production, not a subject that theory explains. Barrett's Introduction and Foreword set the frame: studio enquiry is a "new species of research" whose methods are subjective, emergent and situated, and the test of it is what it reveals that other modes of enquiry cannot. Bolt's chapter supplies the epistemological engine — Heidegger's handlability and praxical knowledge, Paul Carter's "material thinking" — and insists that theory and practice are doubly articulated rather than opposed. The exegesis is redefined from a written description of the work into a second mode of the enquiry whose job is to move thought, not to explain an artwork. Haseman's chapter generalises the field's claims into a proposed third research paradigm, "performative research", after Austin, in which the practice output performs the research rather than merely reporting it; his case study (David Fenton's devised theatre PhD, Unstable Acts) also supplies the concrete methods: enquiry cycle, Schon's reflective practice, a post-performance reception study, and an "action-tracking" matrix that replaces the prompt copy when there is no script. Chapters 3-9 are first-person accounts by practitioner-researchers (creative writing, dance/film, painting, dance, studio dance, video, design pedagogy) of what their own practice revealed. The closing Appendix turns the whole book into a template: research question, literature and practice review, documentation and exegesis structure.

Key ideas

  • practice as research / practice-led research — the discipline in which the researcher's own studio work is the site of enquiry; Barrett calls it "generative enquiry" drawing on subjective, interdisciplinary, emergent methodology. The book is a bid to have it validated "alongside other disciplines in the University" (Introduction), where artists are still "somewhat suspicious of theory and reticent in discussing their work".
  • praxical knowledge — Heidegger's term, via Bolt: ideas and theory are the result of practice, not the reverse; knowledge of the world comes first through handling, and only then through contemplation.
  • material thinking (Paul Carter's term, Bolt's critique of it) — the intelligence of materials and processes entering into the making; Bolt sharpens it: material thinking happens in the joining of hand, eye and mind with materials, not in the artists' talk about it.
  • the double articulation — theory emerges from reflexive practice at the same time as practice is informed by theory. This is the book's answer to the theory/practice binary. Bolt's other axis is praxis: "Theorising out of practice is ... a very different way of thinking than applying theory to practice."
  • the exegesis — the accompanying written/discursive work in a practice-based thesis; disputed here between those who want it explanatory and those (Barrett, Bolt) who want it to "produce movement in thought itself". Its role is critical and productive, not descriptive.
  • performative research (Haseman) — a proposed third paradigm beside quantitative and qualitative, "named from J.L. Austin's speech act theory": the practice output is an utterance that enacts what it names, so the material forms of practice (music, dance, code, design) are the knowledge claim, with an exegesis as additional commentary.
  • reflective practice / reflection-in-action (via Schon, in Haseman's chapter) — the practitioner's codified way of surfacing tacit knowledge; coupled with documentation methods (journaling, digital video feedback, a five-code tracking matrix for a devised performance) it becomes the research apparatus.
  • situated and emergent methodology — personal interest ("rather than objective 'disinterestedness'") motivates the enquiry; method is adjusted as the practice proceeds, which the book treats as a design feature rather than a flaw.
  • the knowledges only the practitioner has — Bolt's Hockney case (his drawing practice let him ask what art historians could not), Perry's writing process, Iggulden decoding the nuns' marginal "codes": the practitioner's specific knowing is what makes the question askable.
  • criteria for judging practice research — the Appendix and the exegesis-as-meme chapter (Dawkins: self-replication, fitness, fecundity) offer the book's attempt at quality measures.

Quotes

  • "What knowledge can studio based enquiry reveal that may not be revealed by other modes of enquiry?" (PDF p.11 / printed p.xi) — Barrett (Foreword p.xi) - the book's opening research question; the direct model for a fulldome practice's own research question.
  • "We propose that artistic practice be viewed as the production of knowledge or philosophy in action." (PDF p.13 / printed p.1) — Barrett (Introduction) - the definitional claim: art practice IS knowledge production.
  • "Finally, and most importantly, Hockney’s visual argument demonstrates the double articulation between theory and practice, whereby theory emerges from a reﬂexive practice at the same time that practice is informed by theory. This double articulation is central to practice-led research." (PDF p.41 / printed p.29) — Bolt (ch.2) - the double articulation; the cleanest statement of the anti-binary position.
  • "Material thinking is the logic of practice." (PDF p.42 / printed p.30) — Bolt (ch.2) - material thinking in one sentence.
  • "through the vehicle of the exegesis, practice becomes theory generating." (PDF p.45 / printed p.33) — Bolt (ch.2) - what the exegesis is FOR; counter to 'exegesis as explanation'.
  • "Praxical knowledge involves a reﬂexive knowing that imbricates and follows on from handling." (PDF p.46 / printed p.34) — Bolt (ch.2) - praxical knowledge (via Heidegger) as the epistemological core.
  • "They argue that a continued insistence that practice-led research be reported primarily in the traditional forms of research (words or numbers) can only result in the dilution and ultimately the impoverishment of the epistemological content embedded and embodied in practice." (PDF p.160 / printed p.148) — Haseman (ch.11) - why practice-based findings must be reported in their own forms; useful for arguing against a written-only account of a dome work.
  • "It not only expresses the research, but in that expression becomes the research itself." (PDF p.162 / printed p.150) — Haseman (ch.11) - the definitional move of 'performative research'.
  • "with its accompanying “loops” of feedback and critique, offers practiceled researchers in Theatre and the other creative arts a coherent framework within which they can develop the methods and tools for deepening and documenting their emerging understandings of practice." (PDF p.165 / printed p.153) — Haseman (ch.11) - names Schon-derived reflective practice and, in the same sentence, documentation as the research apparatus.
  • "the processes of writing themselves, the studio enquiry, may lead to knowledge that is not necessarily explicitly discernible on the surface of the creative work." (PDF p.47 / printed p.35) — Perry (ch.3) - knowledge the practitioner has but the artefact does not show; the case for an exegesis alongside a made work.
  • "What did the studio process reveal that could not have been revealed by any other mode of enquiry?" (PDF p.198 / printed p.186) — Appendix (p.186) - the methodological question the book is built around. Same sentence verbatim in Barrett, ch.12 (PDF p.174).

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This is the methodological spine of the review: it is the source that lets "I made a dome work" be argued as research. Bolt and Barrett give the vocabulary (praxical knowledge, material thinking, double articulation, exegesis as a second mode of the enquiry); Haseman gives the paradigm claim and a worked example of a devised performance with no script, whose documentation had to be invented for the project — directly transferable to a devised dome/performance lecture. The Appendix is the most immediately usable part of the whole corpus for framing a research question and a practice-plus-exegesis submission. What it cannot carry: nothing about screens, projection, immersion or spectatorship; the visual/technological media theory has to come from elsewhere, and every case study here is a lone studio practitioner rather than a collaborative dome production crew.

Limits

No dome, no screen, no immersive or projection-based art anywhere in the volume — the media arts case (Goddard's video Lorne Story) is the closest, and it is video-as-postcard, not spatial projection. The chapters are 2007-era Australian university politics: much of the energy is spent arguing for legitimacy and for HDR examination criteria, so the passages on funding, RAE and "what stands as research" date the book. The exegesis is presumed to be a written thesis component, which sits awkwardly with a claim that practice performs the research. Perry's chapter argues a case (creative work as research without exegesis) that the editors do not endorse. Empirical rigour is thin by social-science standards: mostly reflection on the author's own project, with little on how another researcher could replicate or contest it.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Grau, Virtual Art: From Illusion to Immersion — the historical apparatus (panorama, immersion) that this book has no theory of.
  • Dixon, Digital Performance — the intermedia/liveness history for the performance side of the review.
  • Bay-Cheng et al., Mapping Intermediality in Performance — vocabulary for the projected/mediated stage.
  • Bleecker, "Design Fiction" and Dunne & Raby, Speculative Everything — same "practitioner makes a thing that carries a claim" move, but aimed at the near future rather than at a discipline's epistemology.
  • Fulldome_Articles_005_Towards_a_Fulldome_Manifesto; Imersa "fulldome 101" — where the domed format itself, and the state of the field's own discourse, is set out.
  • Harman, Object-Oriented Ontology — the philosophical counterpart to the book's materialist/Heideggerian line if the review needs to go further into what objects do.

Anthony Dunne and Fiona Raby, Speculative Everything: Design, Fiction, and Social Dreaming (2013)

Citation

Dunne, Anthony, and Fiona Raby. Speculative Everything: Design, Fiction, and Social Dreaming. Cambridge, MA: MIT Press, 2013. ISBN 978-0-262-01984-2. [Title page and Library of Congress CIP page of the extracted PDF.] Original PDF filename: Books_Speculative_Everything_Anthony_Dunne_Fiona_Raby_pdf.pdf

Coverage

Complete book. 236 PDF pages / 47,101 words. Front matter (title, copyright with the Library of Congress record, contents, preface with the A/B manifesto list), nine chapters — 1 Beyond Radical Design?; 2 A Map of Unreality; 3 Design as Critique; 4 Consuming Monsters; 5 A Methodological Playground: Fictional Worlds and Thought Experiments; 6 Physical Fictions: Invitations to Make-Believe; 7 Aesthetics of Unreality; 8 Between Reality and the Impossible; 9 Speculative Everything — then Notes, Bibliography and Index (ends mid-index at PDF p.236). All chapters present; the book is image-heavy, so the word count is low for its page count and captions/photo credits interleave with body prose.

Text-layer damage: the small-caps display type is mangled — the title page extracts as "sPECULATIVE EVERYTHInG" / "DEsIGn,FICTIOn, AnD sOCIAL DREAMInG" and small-caps headings inside chapters come out as "Critical DeSign". Body prose extracts cleanly (fi/fl ligatures survive). Quoting headings and display type from this file is unsafe; the body-text quotes below are clean. Printed folios in this extract run 11–12 behind PDF pages inconsistently, so quotes are cited by PDF page only. All eight quotes were sliced verbatim from the extract.

Argument in brief

Dunne & Raby argue for a design practice that has left the marketplace's problem-solving mode and gone after ideas instead. Design used this way is "conceptual design" or, in their own coinage, "speculative design" and "critical design": it asks questions through designed objects rather than providing answers through products. The book is built from their own practice, their Royal College of Art Design Interactions students' work, and art/film/architecture examples. Its method chapter treats fictional worlds and thought experiments (counterfactuals, impossible-but-plausible worlds) as the working tools; the chapter on "physical fictions" argues that a designed object is a prop that prescribes imaginings and carries a "fictional proposition" about the world it belongs to, and need not be functional or plausible as a product. The closing chapter widens the claim from design to "social dreaming": because beliefs about what is possible set what is actually possible, making alternatives tangible is a political act. The A/B list in the preface states the whole opposition compactly: A = affirmative, problem solving, provides answers, design as solution; B = critical, problem finding, asks questions, design for debate, design as medium.

Key ideas

  • conceptual design — "design about ideas"; the umbrella term for the forms that appear once design separates from industrial production and the marketplace (speculative design, critical design, design fiction, design futures, interrogative design, adversarial design, discursive design, design futurescaping, design art).
  • critical design — Dunne & Raby's own term, coined mid-1990s at the RCA: speculative proposals used to challenge assumptions about what products do in everyday life. Explicitly "an attitude ... rather than a methodology". Its opposite is affirmative design, which reinforces the status quo.
  • problem finding vs problem solving — the A/B manifesto. Design as a way of asking questions, not answering them; "asks questions", "design for debate", "makes us think" against "provides answers", "design as solution", "makes us buy".
  • unreal / possible worlds / fictional worlds — the book's working territory; its key distinction is actual vs fictional, and it will stretch ethics, psychology and economics to breaking point while keeping within scientific possibility.
  • thought experiment / counterfactual — the method named in chapter 5; e.g. "What if David Attenborough had become an industrial designer": used not to predict but to present parallel worlds.
  • physical fictions / props / make-believe (Kendall L. Walton) — designed objects as props that "prescribe imaginings" and carry "fictional propositions" rather than standing in for real things. Props are "physical synecdoches": parts that imply a whole world.
  • invitations to make-believe — the object deliberately asked to be read as not-real; the point is the viewer's work of imagining, not the object's functionality.
  • aesthetics of unreality — the craft question the book keeps returning to: how such objects are staged and displayed so they are legible as speculation, not as cinema props or products.
  • social dreaming / speculative everything — the political payoff: speculative design makes viable and non-viable alternatives tangible, which in turn shifts beliefs about what is possible.

Quotes

  • "Once designers step away from industrial production and the marketplace we enter the realm of the unreal, the fictional, or what we prefer to think of as conceptual design—design about ideas." (PDF p.22) — Dunne & Raby (ch.2) - conceptual/speculative design defined by separation from the market.
  • "This potential to use the language of design to pose questions, provoke, and inspire is conceptual design’s defining feature." (PDF p.23) — Dunne & Raby (ch.2) - design as posing questions rather than solving problems.
  • "Critical Problem finding Asks questions Design for debate Design as medium In the service of society Functional fictions For how the world could be Change us to suit the world Social fiction Parallel worlds The “unreal” real Narratives of consumption" (PDF p.8) — Dunne & Raby (Preface) - the A/B list, the book's manifesto version of problem-solving vs problem-finding design.
  • "We coined the term critical design in the mid-nineties when we were researchers in the Computer Related Design Research Studio at the Royal College of Art. It grew out of our concerns with the uncritical drive behind technological progress, when technology is always assumed to be good and capable of solving any problem. Our definition then was that “critical design uses speculative design proposals to challenge narrow assumptions, preconceptions, and givens about the role products play in everyday life.” It was more of an attitude than anything else, a position rather than a methodology. Its opposite is affirmative design: design that reinforces the status quo." (PDF p.45) — Dunne & Raby (ch.3) - critical design defined by its own authors, plus 'affirmative design' as its opposite.
  • "Yes, it makes reading the objects more difficult but this process of mental interaction is important for encouraging the viewer to actively engage with the design rather than passively consuming it." (PDF p.101) — Dunne & Raby (ch.6) - the object's illegibility is the mechanism that makes the viewer work.
  • "speculative design props are intended not to mimic reality or allow us to play act but to entertain new ideas, thoughts, and possibilities for an alternative world from the one we and the prop coexist in, what Kendall calls “fictional propositions” in contrast to the “fictional truths” of children’s props." (PDF p.103) — Dunne & Raby (ch.6) - props as 'fictional propositions' (Walton); the object carries a proposition, not a product.
  • "We are more interested in intentional fictional objects, physical fictions that celebrate and enjoy their status with little desire to become “real.”" (PDF p.100) — Dunne & Raby (ch.6) - intentional fictional objects; the object need not want to become real.
  • "Speculative design can be a catalyst for this: it can inspire imagination and a feeling that, if not exactly anything, more is definitely possible. Speculative design contributes to the reimagining not only of reality itself but also our relationship to reality." (PDF p.172) — Dunne & Raby (ch.9) - the payoff argument: speculation changes what people believe is possible.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

This source carries the "making things that do not yet exist as a way of asking questions" argument, and a defence of illegibility: the speculative object is meant to resist being read as a product, because that resistance is what makes the audience work. That transfers directly to a dome piece that is not a film, not a planetarium show and not a fixed-screen work — the review can use Dunne & Raby to argue that a non-standard domed artefact is a proposition about the format, not a failed version of one. Their prop theory (props as physical synecdoches carrying fictional propositions) is the mechanism by which a single dome work can imply a whole media future. What it cannot carry: any account of audience reception beyond exhibition anecdotes, no methodology for evaluating what a speculative object did to anyone, no dome/planetarium material, and no engagement with performance or liveness.

Limits

The book is a designer's manifesto, not research methodology: no protocol, no data, no criteria (they explicitly note that critical design is "an attitude ... rather than a methodology"). Evidence is exhibition anecdotes and attribution to taste; the claims about changing beliefs are asserted via one quotation from Erik Olin Wright rather than tested. Almost all examples are Western, RCA-centred, gallery/exhibition-based design, and the imagined audiences are visitors who can be assumed to be curious. It predates the recent surge of design-fiction scholarship and much of the current planetarium/fulldome work, and it says nothing about immersive or spatial projection.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Bleecker, "Design Fiction" (2009) — the adjacent coinage; Bleecker's props/conversation pieces and Dunne & Raby's fictional propositions are the two poles of the same argument (Dunne & Raby cite design fiction in their map of conceptual design, PDF p.22).
  • Manovich, The Language of New Media — for the database/navigation vocabulary if the dome work is interactive.
  • Hayles, How We Became Posthuman; Merleau-Ponty, Phenomenology of Perception — the embodiment side that a speculative-object argument lacks.
  • Grau, Virtual Art — the illusion/immersion history that "aesthetics of unreality" is adjacent to.
  • Fuller, Operating Manual for Spaceship Earth — an earlier design-as-social-proposition text in the same folder.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_* (fulldome 101; future directions for research) — what the domed format actually affords, which this book does not address.

Julian Bleecker, "Design Fiction: A Short Essay on Design, Science, Fact and Fiction" (2009)

Citation

Bleecker, Julian. "Design Fiction: A Short Essay on Design, Science, Fact and Fiction." Near Future Laboratory, March 2009. http://www.nearfuturelaboratory.com. Distributed under Creative Commons BY-NC-ND 3.0. [Title page and closing page of the extracted PDF — the essay carries no page imprint or publisher beyond the author and the Near Future Laboratory.] Original PDF filename: Books_Design_Fiction_Julian_Bleecker_pdf.pdf

Coverage

Complete short essay in PDF form, 50 PDF pages / 28,587 words. It is not an academic article: a self-described "travelogue of experiences" with images, section headings ("01 DESIGN FICTION" onwards), a numbered outline (1. Fact and Fiction Swap Properties; 2. Fiction follows Fact; 3. Fact follows Fiction; then a numbered FAQ-style conclusion ending "6. What I had to do was show that fact and fiction do indeed routinely swap properties"), then acknowledgements and the Creative Commons notice at the back. No bibliography; references are inline URLs to shortlinks. Printed numbers in the file (01/04/07, and spread numbers such as 84/85, 38/39) do not track PDF pages, so quotes are cited by PDF page only.

Text-layer damage — important: this extract drops a large number of glyphs. Capital "Th" digraphs are frequently lost ("ese" = These, "ere is" = There is), as are fi/fl ligatures (" ction" = fiction, "lm" = film, "di erent" = different, "re ect" = reflect). Many pages lose most of their capital-"Th" digraphs and all fi/fl ligatures; other pages extract cleanly. The quotes below were chosen to be readable, and the one damaged quote ("Design ctions") is flagged. Note also that the phrase "suspension of disbelief" / "suspend disbelief" does NOT occur anywhere in this essay — the canonical Sterling-style formulation ("the deliberate use of diegetic prototypes to suspend disbelief about change") cannot be cited from this file.

Argument in brief

Bleecker proposes "design fiction" as the name for a practice he finds at work in the overlap between science fact and science fiction. His argument is that fact and fiction routinely swap properties: science fiction prototypes futures that science fact then builds, and science fact needs fiction to circulate its ideas at all. His worked example is Minority Report and its gestural interface, imported from MIT's Tangible Media Group — a case of a film's prop functioning as research. For that he leans on David A. Kirby's notion of the "diegetic prototype": a fictional object inside a story that demonstrates a technology's desirability and viability to a mass audience more effectively than a working prototype can. From this he argues that designed objects are "conversation pieces", "props" and "physical instantiations" that carry propositions about near-future worlds, and that making them is a way of thinking and of starting arguments rather than of predicting anything. The essay's ambition is modest and declared: it is a report on a practitioner's own reflections, not theory.

Key ideas

  • design fiction — Bleecker's coinage-as-label for a hybrid of design, science fact and science fiction: making material objects that carry stories about possible near futures. Named at the point where he recognises a practice "at the contours" of two colleagues' essay.
  • diegetic prototype (David A. Kirby's term, adopted by Bleecker) — a technology depicted inside a narrative diegesis, where it exists as a functioning "real" object for the characters and therefore does rhetorical work on an audience: need, benevolence, viability. Its advantage over a real prototype is that it is cheap and can be circulated widely.
  • fact and fiction swap properties — the essay's central claim; the "science work" happens in the middle, in the continuous traffic between the two, not at either pole.
  • props / conversation pieces — designed objects as things around which discussions happen; "they become real before themselves, because they could never exist outside of an imagined use context".
  • materialization of ideas — design as "a kind of creative, imaginative authoring practice" that gives ideas a physical instantiation; design fictions are "propositions for new, future things done as physical instantiations rather than future project plans done through PowerPoint".
  • thought experiments, made — Bleecker describes what he wants as "materialized thought experiments" (PDF p.42).
  • near future / habitable worlds — the essay's horizon is deliberately the near future and more habitable worlds, extrapolated legibly from today rather than anchorage-free speculation.
  • design as interrogation — design fiction "is about creative provocation, raising questions, innovation, and exploration"; it assumes nothing about the future "except that there can be simultaneous futures".
  • author's own position — the essay is written from inside an advanced design studio and is candid that it is a practitioner's account, not a literature-grounded study.

Quotes

  • "I came to the conclusion that there was a practice there, just at the contours of their essay that may as well be called “Design Fiction.”" (PDF p.8) — Bleecker (intro) - Bleecker naming the practice; useful as the origin claim for 'design fiction'. Extract drops the fi/Th glyphs; closing quote character restored by transcription.
  • "Rather than assuming the progression of ideas and their materialization along a predeﬁned chart of incremental progress, design ﬁction assumes no particular course, no speciﬁc future world." (PDF p.9) — Bleecker (intro) - design fiction refuses the incremental-futures chart. Clean in the extract.
  • "I argue in this essay that for technical advisors cinematic depictions of future technologies are actually “diegetic prototypes” that demonstrate to large public audiences a technology’s need, benevolence, and viability." (PDF p.20) — Kirby, quoted by Bleecker - the diegetic prototype's work on an audience. Note: Bleecker is quoting David A. Kirby here.
  • "Design ctions are propositions for new, future things done as physical instantiations rather than future project plans done through PowerPoint." (PDF p.42) — Bleecker (conclusion) - the propositional claim for made objects. Extract damage: 'Design ctions' = 'Design fictions' in print.
  • "Diegetic prototypes have a major rhetorical advantage even over true prototypes: in the [story] these technologies exist as “real” objects that function properly and which people actually use." (PDF p.43) — Kirby again, re-quoted by Bleecker - why a story-embedded prototype outperforms a working prototype.
  • "A designed object can connect an idea to its expression as a made, crafted, instantiated object." (PDF p.4) — Bleecker (intro) - one sentence on how an object carries an idea; the plainest bridge to a dome artefact.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review
