This is the shortest and most directly quotable statement of the claim the review needs: a made thing can hold and circulate a proposition about a future, and a story-embedded prototype does rhetorical work that a working prototype cannot. Kirby's "diegetic prototype" gives the review a precise way to describe a domed performance that shows a world rather than a product — the dome as a diegesis in which a proposed future is, for the duration, the case. Bleecker also supplies the vocabulary of props and conversation pieces for the audience's post-show talk. What it cannot carry: no method, no evaluation criteria, no evidence about what these objects actually do to audiences, and nothing on immersion, spatial projection or performance.

Limits

This is an essay by a practitioner, explicitly "less a theoretical statement than a travelogue of experiences" — there is no method section, no data, no criteria, and the argument leans almost entirely on one case study (Minority Report) plus Kirby's forthcoming paper. It is 2009 and pre-dates the academic consolidation of design fiction; it does not contain the common later formulation about "suspending disbelief about change". The extract's dropped glyphs mean some of the best lines are unusable or need flagging, and any quotation should be checked against print. It says nothing about performance, the body, or the dome.

Adjacent sources in this folder

  • Dunne & Raby, Speculative Everything (2013) — the book-length version of the same argument, with the props/fictional-proposition theory; they cite design fiction in their map of conceptual design.
  • Fuller, Operating Manual for Spaceship Earth — earlier design-as-planetary-proposition text.
  • Grau, Virtual Art; Books_The_Virtual_Window — the illusion/window history that "diegetic" objects live inside.
  • Fulldome_Articles_Imersa_* (fulldome 101; the perception of domed environments) — the format-specific counterpart to Bleecker's generic "near future".
  • Barrett & Bolt, Practice as Research — supplies the research-method framework that this essay lacks.

Bligh, What's the Use of Lectures? — extract of chapters 1-2 (9 PDF pages)

Citation

Bligh, Donald A. What's the Use of Lectures? Extract containing chapter 1, Opinions of what lectures achieve, and chapter 2, Evidence of what lectures achieve (complete as far as the appendices to the tables). 9 PDF pages, 5,102 words.

Where the citation comes from, stated plainly. This file carries no title page, no cover, no copyright page, no author name and no publisher — the string Bligh does not occur once in the text layer, and there is no bibliography, reference list, preface or index. Identification rests on two things only: the running head WHAT'S THE USE OF LECTURES? (PDF pp.2, 4, 6, 7, 8, 9) and the source filename Expanded_Bligh_Whats_the_Use_of_Lectures_pdf. The chapter headings in the file are 1.Opinions of what Lectures Achieve (PDF p.1) and 2.Evidence of what Lectures Achieve (PDF p.3).

Standard citation for the volume (checked outside this corpus; not verifiable in the file): Bligh, Donald A. What's the Use of Lectures? San Francisco: Jossey-Bass, 2000 (first U.S. edition of a book first written in 1971 at the University of London Teaching Methods Unit; earlier editions Penguin 1972, Intellect editions later). The edition in this file cannot be confirmed, but its in-text citations run to 1996-1997 (Kowalski 1987; Isaacs 1994; Lesniak 1996; LeBlanc 1996; Benson 1996; Gunzburger 1993; Lesniak 1996), so it is a late, 2000-era printing and not the 1971/72 original. Confirm the edition against a copy before citing page numbers in a published review.

Coverage

Book, incomplete. This is a 9-page extract of one continuous run of printed pages - printed pp.6-23 - covering chapter 1 and most of chapter 2, with everything before and after missing. Present: the chapter-1 heading and the whole of Opinions of what lectures achieve; the chapter-2 heading and Evidence of what lectures achieve including Tables 2.1-2.8, the four generalisations, the discussion of each, and the chapter's closing page. The last line of the file stops mid-sentence where the scan window ends, still inside the discussion of Table 2.8 and its appendix.

Structure of the scan, which governs how it can be quoted. Each of the 9 PDF pages is a scan of two facing printed pages, and the text layer was laid out by position across the whole width, so lines from the left and the right leaf are interleaved line by line. The evidence is direct: two consecutive folios appear inside single PDF pages - 12 and 13 (PDF p.4), 14 and 15 (p.5), 16 and 17 (p.6), 18 and 19 (p.7), 20 and 21 (p.8), 22 and 23 (p.9); PDF p.2 carries the folio 8. So the run is printed pp.6-23 on 9 PDF pages, with PDF p.N = printed pp.2N+4 / 2N+5 for N≥2 (p.1 = printed 6/7 and p.3 = printed 10/11, where no folio survives). Three consequences for use:

  1. Any sentence whose text crosses a line boundary of either leaf is broken in this file by a line belonging to the other leaf. Long contiguous runs do exist, but they are runs from one leaf, not from the reading text of one page. Every quote below was tested against the extracted text; where one ends in an ellipsis the sentence continues in the source but the file cuts it off at an interleaved line.
  2. It is not possible to say which of the two leaves a given quote sits on. Quotes are located by PDF page; the printed-page figure in brackets is the spread that PDF page holds, not a single printed page. Do not turn those into exact citations.
  3. The interleaving also scrambles the tables: the figures in Tables 2.1-2.8 cannot be relied on from this file (cells appear as loose lines mixed with body text), though numbers Bligh states in his prose can.

OCR damage. Spaces are lost almost everywhere (Inastudy, thelecture, isnotobvious, openingup), and the running heads come out as WHAr'STHEUSEOFLECTURES? or OPTNIONSOFWHATLECTURESACHIEVE. Word-level errors also occur: inelude (include), giuen (given), samlectures (same lectures), intereted (interested), persauding (persuading), eriterion (criterion), c0operative, and PSl (capital I) for PSI in places. Correction, added after a leaf-aware re-OCR. The first three consequences above describe the text file this annotation was first written from, in which the two leaves of each spread were interleaved line by line. That file was afterwards re-OCR'd reading each half of the spread on its own (.dome-work/ocr4.py), and the corpus text now holds the left leaf followed by the right leaf for every PDF page. What that changes:

  • Sentences are no longer cut at the interleaving, and all 15 quotes below were re-checked against the corrected text: 15 quotes, 0 flagged — unchanged.
  • A quote's leaf is now readable from its position in the page: the first half of a PDF page is the even (left) folio, the second half the odd (right) one.
  • The printed folios now stand at the head of each leaf: PDF p.2 = printed 8 / 9; p.3 = 10 / 11 (no folio survives); p.4 = 12 / 13; p.5 = 14 / 15; p.6 = 16 / 17; p.7 = 18 / 19; p.8 = 20 / 21; p.9 = 22 / 23; p.1 = 6 / 7. Quotes below are still located by PDF page; the bracketed printed figures can now be narrowed to a single leaf by the position rule, and any passage not at the head of a leaf needs that check before it is given a printed folio.
  • Table figures remain unreliable — but for the original reason (they were never laid out as tables in the text layer), not because of the interleaving.

Argument in brief

Bligh asks which of four kinds of objective a lecture can actually serve - the acquisition of information, the promotion of thought, changes in attitudes, behavioral skills - and answers by separating what people believe about lectures from what has been measured. Chapter 1 collects the opinions of faculty and of students (Hale 1964, Isaacs 1994, Kowalski 1987, Saunders et al. 1969, Stones 1970, McLeish 1976, and his own surveys) and then refuses to let those opinions count as evidence: where a question seeks a generalisation, personal experience is a weak instrument, and the lecture's indispensability is an assumption, not a finding. Chapter 2 sets the lecture against discussion, reading, enquiry, independent study and television in the experimental literature (Dubin and Taveggia's ninety-one studies plus the comparisons he tabulates himself) and states four broad generalisations: the lecture transmits information as effectively as other methods but no more effectively; most lectures are less effective than discussion at promoting thought; changing students' attitudes should not normally be a lecture's major objective; lectures are ineffective for behavioural skills. His reservations - above all programmed learning and PSI, the Keller Plan - are deferred to Chapter 3, and he insists throughout that these are broad generalisations rather than laws. The consistent target is not lecturing as such but the traditional expository lecture: unbroken exposition by one speaker to a seated audience, in which the students' role is passive and thought is not demanded. Where lectures fail, Bligh usually blames the method's passivity rather than the individual lecturer - except on one point, where he says the fault is the lecturer's: students' complaints about poor preparation and about lectures that repeat standard textbooks.

Key ideas

  • four kinds of objective - acquisition of information, promotion of thought, changes in attitudes, behavioral skills; Bligh's organising scheme for the whole book, and the reason his verdicts differ by objective (PDF p.1).
  • acquisition of information - the one objective he concludes the lecture can serve: as effective as other methods for transmitting it, but not more effective (PDF p.3).
  • the traditional expository lecture / continuous exposition - his specific target: uninterrupted exposition in which the lecture does not demand an active mental response (PDF pp.5-6).
  • information input vs information processing - what students' experience and expectations of the lecture format fit them for; the lecturer who wants to promote thought must overcome that conservatism (PDF p.6).
  • the activity principle - active methods (discussion, practice, role play, simulation) change attitudes and skills because the learner acts; presentation methods do not, whatever their novelty value (PDF pp.7, 9). Presentation methods versus methods requiring student activity is the classification behind all his comparison tables (PDF pp.3-4, 9).
  • instructor-centred vs student-centred - the lecture as one chosen authority in an authoritarian social situation, compared with discussion methods described as more democratic (PDF p.2); students rank lectures highest for obtaining information, low for inspiring ideas and for efficiency, and first of all for frequency (PDF p.8).
  • the folklore of academe - his label for the belief that lecturers inspire students; students do not report being inspired with timetable regularity (PDF p.2).
  • Bloom 1953, the study he reports on what students think about during lectures (PDF p.5): passive thoughts about the subject and simple comprehension occupy 36.8% of lecture time against 20.3% during discussion; irrelevant thoughts 31% against 14.5%; attempting to solve problems and synthesise information 8.3% during discussion against 1.0% during lectures. All differences statistically significant; the method is tape-replay recall, checked later against independent measures.
  • PSI and programmed learning (Keller Plan) - the exception where a comparison method beats the lecture; he returns to it in Chapter 3 (PDF pp.3-4).
  • reservations - the caveats he holds against his own four generalisations, declared in Chapter 3 and not in this extract (PDF pp.3, 9).

Quotes

Fifteen quotes, all clean in the text layer except for OCR's lost spaces, which are restored. A quote ending in an ellipsis is cut off in this file by a line from the facing leaf of the scan, not by Bligh; a quote beginning mid-sentence begins where the extracted line begins. No word or letter has been changed. Page references are PDF pages, with the printed spread the PDF page holds.

  • "These included 'introducing and opening up a subject and the provision of a framework for reading'. Especially in science subjects it was claimed that an oral method is clearer and less wasteful of time when dealing with complex material." (PDF p.1 / printed 6-7) - the reported faculty case for the oral form (Hale 1964): the claim a practitioner should test rather than assume.
  • "Lecturing is still the most common method when teaching adults." (PDF p.1 / printed 6-7) - the persistence of the form across decades of surveys, and its spread outside education (he adds: in politics they are called speeches, in churches sermons).
  • "the lecture method is regarded as instructor-centred and providing an authoritarian …" (PDF p.2 / printed 8-9) - the lecture room's social arrangement, in Bligh's words; the sentence continues in the source by contrasting student-centred discussion, described as more democratic, quoting McKeachie in Gage 1963.
  • "McLeish (1976) claims the lecture 'is undoubtedly the most economical method by which the individual can present in a personalized and continuous argument the general framework for understanding the fundamentals of a particular subject, and involving the audience in reflective thought that moves in time with the on-going performance'. But I do doubt it." (PDF p.2 / printed 8-9) - the best formulation in the extract of the lecture as a performed form in time, quoted by Bligh in order to doubt it; the two-sided problem a lecture-performance inherits.
  • "However (a) and (b) are frequently assumed and proclaimed. The idea that lecturers inspire their students any more than very rarely, is part of the folklore of academe." (PDF p.2 / printed 8-9) - the claim that inspiration is belief rather than finding; (a) and (b) are the acquisition of disciplinary values and interest in a subject.
  • "There is virtually no published evidence on faculty's opinions on the place of lectures when teaching behavioral skills. They may well believe it has no place at all." (PDF p.2 / printed 8-9) - what the evidence base itself does not cover, before any verdict is offered.
  • "The lecture is as effective as other methods to transmit information." (PDF p.3 / printed 10-11) - one of the seven conclusions listed together at the head of the evidence chapter.
  • "Most lectures are not as effective as discussion to promote thought." (PDF p.3 / printed 10-11) - the counterpart; the pair is the extract's central verdict.
  • "relatively passive. They sit listening: their activity usually consists of selecting …" (PDF p.5 / printed 14-15) - the seated audience of the traditional lecture, stated as the reason its role is passive.
  • "information from what is said, possibly translating it into their own words or 'shorthand …" (PDF p.5 / printed 14-15) - the only description of note-taking in the extract, and the continuation of the sentence above; the whole of what listening-plus-writing amounts to.
  • "and suggest that during discussion students are more attentive, active and thoughtful …" (PDF p.5 / printed 14-15) - Bligh's own summary of the thought-content data (Bloom 1953); the sentence continues with a comparison against lectures.
  • "Passive reception of information will not fan the flame (unless there is an emotionally prepared mind)." (PDF p.6 / printed 16-17) - motivation as something the format cannot supply.
  • "focus of attention is on lecturers and what they say, not on the students." (PDF p.8 / printed 20-21) - the attention economy of the lecture room, in his treatment of personality and social adjustment objectives.
  • "There can be little doubt about the unpopularity of the lecture system amongst students." (PDF p.8 / printed 20-21) - the student-side finding that runs alongside the method's institutional persistence.
  • "professor, who wanted to teach me surveying, gave us a lecture; but we never handled the equipment or tried to survey a field. Verbal presentations present words; and words are what students get from them. If you want them to be able to do something put them in a situation where they practice doing it." (PDF p.9 / printed 22-23) - his own practice anecdote and the rule it yields; the extract's clearest statement of what a lecture is and is not.

Where it lands in a fulldome-performance literature review

It can carry the lecture-side baseline of the review: the empirical case that a seated audience listening to one speaker's continuous exposition is a strong format for transmitting information and a weak one for thought, attitudes or skills, and that the lecture room's arrangement - one chosen authority, no group, attention on the speaker - is a condition of the method rather than a neutral container. The McLeish passage, and Bligh's immediate doubt about it, gives the review both the strongest available description of the lecture as a form performed in time and the counter-argument a lecture-performance has to answer. It also supplies the vocabulary the review can borrow without importing it wholesale: information input versus information processing, the activity principle, presentation methods versus methods requiring student activity. It cannot carry anything about the dome, the planetarium, projection, immersion, screens or scenography, and it cannot support claims about lecture-performances that use a medium, because its unit of analysis is the conventional university lecture and its comparison groups are discussion, reading, enquiry and television. Attention appears here only as data on what students are thinking about; there is nothing on how long attention holds, on seating, acoustics or sightlines, or on the setting beyond its social relation.

Limits

The extract stops at printed p.23. Chapters 3 to the end, the appendices to Tables 2.1-2.8, the bibliography and everything Bligh says about technique - what to do with the lecture, formats, handouts, feedback, testing alternatives - are all absent, so nothing here can support claims about his recommendations. Attention is present only as the content-of-thought data of Bloom 1953; the extract contains no data on attention span or its decay across a session, and no physical description of the room (seating, sightlines, acoustics, screens) - the arrangement of the room appears only as the lecture's social arrangement. Table figures are unreliable as extracted; the counts must be read from a copy of the book. Authorship and edition are not verifiable inside the file, and four in-text dates put it after 1997. The evidence base is dated: pre-2000 psychological and educational research, much of it 1940s-1970s, with lecture-versus-television comparisons from the 1960s, and one voice (his own preferred methods: discussion, PSI, practice) throughout. The criterion is achievement on tests of course content for undergraduate teaching, judged by comparison with other teaching methods; liveness, embodiment, the performer's presence and the performer's own research are outside its frame, and the only mention of the audience's body is that it sits and writes. Bligh is not an opponent of the lecture - he is an opponent of the expository default and of arguments from personal experience, which is a distinction worth keeping when this chapter is cited against a performed lecture.

Adjacent sources in this folder

Pointers only - none of the following was examined for this annotation. - Expanded_Goffman_Forms_of_Talk_pdf - Goffman on talk as a situated, focused gathering; the room-and-interaction side of what Bligh states only as a social arrangement. - Expanded_Kendon_Conducting_Interaction_pdf - the spatial organisation of bodies in interaction, for the physical setting Bligh leaves out. - Expanded_White_Audience_Participation_in_Theatre_pdf - the invitation to participate and what can be asked of a seated audience; the Theatre-side vocabulary for the lecture-performance's two-way problem. - Books_Culture_as_Performance_Erika_Fischer_Lichte_pdf - performance as communication with feedback loops; the frame in which a lecture can be treated as performance rather than delivery. - Books_Mapping_Intermediality_in_Performance_Sarah_Bay_Cheng_pdf - performance formats and their medial mixtures; the lecture as one such format. - Books_Virtual_Art_From_Illusion_to_Immersion_Oliver_Grau_pdf and Books_Immersed_in_Technology_Moser_and_MacLeod_pdf - attention, immersion and the spectator's body from the media-arts side, against Bligh's classroom-only evidence. - Fulldome_Articles_005_Towards_a_Fulldome_Manifesto_final_proof_pdf - the dome-side position statement to be set against Bligh's lecture-side baseline.

Cross-cutting themes

Six themes run across the corpus. Each one is a place where the dome literature and the imported theory actually meet, and each is usable as a section of a literature review. Quotes are given with PDF page; where the source's printed folio was recoverable it is in the entry above.

1. The frame is what fulldome defines itself against

The dome's own literature treats the rectangle as a historical restriction of vision, and the art-history literature supplies the same claim with the evidence. Grau's account of the panorama is the strong form: the image space stops being something you look at and becomes something you are inside, and the presentation apparatus shuts the rest of the world out.

  • "The panorama installs the observer in the picture." — Grau, Virtual Art, PDF p.72
  • "The intention is to install an artificial world that renders the image space a totality or at least fills the observer's entire field of vision" — Grau, PDF p.28
  • "Building on the traditions and mechanisms of illusionistic landscape spaces, the panorama developed into a presentation apparatus that shut out the outside world completely and made the image absolute." — Grau, PDF p.74
  • "Then came the dark ages. This was a time of caged images—a time of broadening distance between vision and viewer: the frame age!" — Remann & Francis, PDF p.4
  • "Like the frame of the architectural window and the frame of the painting, the frame of the moving-image screen marks a separation—an 'ontological cut'—between the material surface of the wall and the view contained within its aperture." — Friedberg, PDF p.20
  • "These plans—whether with multiple mobile projectors and a spectator encircled by the screen, or a theater with walls, ceiling, and an adjustable main screen as projection surfaces—were designed to abolish the proscenium frame and allow the spectator to be lost in the imaginary space of the screen: not in distraction but immersion." — Friedberg, PDF p.185

Schlemmer, writing in 1925, gives this argument its Bauhaus form — and names the framed picture as one historical option among several rather than the natural condition of theatre:

  • "The history of the theater is the history of the transfiguration of the human form. […] The materials involved in this transfiguration are form and color, the materials of the painter and sculptor. The arena for this transfiguration is found in the constructive fusion of space and building, the realm of the architect. Through the manipulation of these materials the role of the artist, the synthesizer of these elements, is determined." — Schlemmer, PDF p.4 / printed p.17
  • "This confrontation of passive spectator and animate actor preconditions also the form of the stage, at its most monumental as the antique arena and at its most primitive as the scaffold in the market place. The need for concentration resulted in the peep show or 'picture frame,' today the 'universal' form of the stage." — Schlemmer, PDF p.5 / printed p.18
  • "Man, the human organism, stands in the cubical, abstract space of the stage. Man and Space. Each has different laws of order. Whose shall prevail?" — Schlemmer, PDF p.9 / printed p.22
  • "Invisibly involved with all these laws is Man as Dancer (Tanzermensch). He obeys the law of the body as well as the law of space; he follows his sense of himself as well as his sense of embracing space. […] whether he is on the bare stage or in a scenic environment constructed for him, […] the Tanzermensch is the medium of transition into the great world of the theater" — Schlemmer, PDF p.12 / printed p.25

The theoretical version of the same claim is in Sloterdijk, and it is the corpus's hardest statement of what a dome does. The celestial dome was once a real immune shell; once it burst, enclosure had to be manufactured:

  • "The sphere is the interior, disclosed, shared realm inhabited by humans — in so far as they succeed in becoming humans." — Sloterdijk, PDF p.14 / printed p.28
  • "Because living always means building spheres, both on a small and a large scale, humans are the beings that establish globes and look out into horizons. Living in spheres means creating the dimension in which humans can be contained. Spheres are immune-systemically effective space creations for ecstatic beings that are operated upon by the outside." — Sloterdijk, PDF p.14 / printed p.28
  • "But now that God's shimmering bubbles, the celestial domes, have burst, who could have the power to create prosthetic husks around those who have been exposed? To oppose the cosmic frost infiltrating the human sphere through the open windows of the Enlightenment, modern humanity makes use of a deliberate greenhouse effect: it attempts to balance out its shellessness in space, following the shattering of the celestial domes, through an artificial civilizatory world." — Sloterdijk, PDF p.12 / printed p.24 — the planetarium as a prosthetic immune shell rather than an entertainment venue, and the strongest frame the corpus offers for the claim in the manifesto chapter
  • "Spheres are air conditioning systems in whose construction and calibration, for those living in real coexistence, it is out of the question not to participate. The symbolic air conditioning of the shared space is the primal production of every society." — Sloterdijk, PDF p.23 / printed p.46 — note the resonance with Gaston's actual ventilation and loudspeaker data; the corpus's grandest claim and its most mundane finding describe the same thing

Two counterweights in the same corpus keep this from being a manifesto position. The dome literature admits that its own furniture reinstates the frame, and the cine-poetics paper argues that the dome's institutional home does the same thing culturally: - "Most seating arrangements in planetariums and dome theaters have a regretful tendency to reintroduce the limitation of the field of vision that the 360-degree projection technology has overcome." — Remann & Francis, PDF p.25 - "There is no standard dome tilt or sweep for fulldome video theaters, but content producers often wish to present viewers with a 'gravity level horizon'." / "Because the viewer is immersed in the frame, a non-gravity level horizon can make the viewer feel off-balance." — Thompson, PDF p.2 - "Because of the technical impediments of the medium – one requires a dome and the specialized projection system - fulldome is heavily restricted to planetaria and hence both technically and socially normalized to the culture of the science museum" — von Chamier-Waite, PDF p.7

2. Immersion works by hiding its apparatus

This is the most load-bearing claim in the corpus for a practice-based argument, because a performance lecture necessarily exposes apparatus — a person, a keyboard, a poll, a question. Grau states the mechanism; Carss and Hazelden state the measurable consequence, and propose presence as the term worth using instead of immersion:

  • "The examples discussed here demonstrate that a constant characteristic of the principle of immersion is to conceal the appearance of the actual illusion medium by keeping it beneath the perceptive threshold of the observer to maximize the intensity of the messages that are being conveyed. The medium becomes invisible." — Grau, PDF p.355
  • "This organization of perception must be construed as a countermodel to image worlds that address all the senses to form a sphere of illusion and, with the aid of intuitive interfaces and anthropomorphic agents, curtail the inner distance of the observer." — Grau, PDF p.231
  • "In technological practice, immersion is a much-overused phrase - the ubiquity of use has rendered it quite meaningless in an academic context. Instead, the term presence carries more value." / "The degree of presence in a given environment inversely correlates with the degree of awareness of the mediation." — Carss & Hazelden, PDF p.2
  • "It is well-nigh impossible to perceive it as an autonomous aesthetic object. If media competence results from the faculty or learned ability to objectify a given medium, then this mechanism is diminished in virtual installations." — Grau, PDF p.217

The break is what makes it theatre. Fischer-Lichte supplies the argument that the manipulation thesis fails anyway, which matters if a project claims its audience was affected in a particular way:

  • "For it has become clear that no individual or group of individuals/specialists will succeed in manipulating the 'innocent' participants of such events according to their intention via minutely planned and calculated strategies of staging." — Fischer-Lichte, PDF p.6

3. The audience: inhabitant, co-creator, or isolated spectator

Four incompatible accounts of the same room sit in this corpus. A literature review can use the disagreement rather than resolve it.

Inhabitant — the immersive-media position:

  • "Virtual environments replace the spectator with the inhabitant, and consequently the terms are reversed: the spectator does not suspend disbelief, but rather the spectator-become-inhabitant is held suspended within the arms of unbelievable space." — Moser & MacLeod, PDF p.320
  • "While, historically, theatre audiences observed a constructed world in actual space, immersive environments, actual and virtual, allow us both to see space and move into and through it. In respect of virtual environments, however, the space is not already there, as it is in theatre buildings and actual site-specific environments, but is created in the process of our moving through, and playing within, it." — Bay-Cheng, PDF p.20
  • "ODV is a human-machine interface that leads to unprecedented levels of presence and intimacy as well as novel ways of mixing and experiencing different levels of reality. By means of a headmounted display, the spectator is afforded a surround video environment and becomes a user or an 'immersant'." — Bay-Cheng, PDF p.71

Co-creator — the performance-studies position:

  • "In this sense performances are generated and determined by the actions and behavior of all participants, no matter whether they are performers or spectators. All participants act as co-creators of the performance which, in many respects, remains unpredictable and spontaneous to a certain degree." — Fischer-Lichte, PDF p.4
  • "We will argue that in intermedial performances spectatorship in itself becomes a self-reflective act and in this process of becoming, is able to entail a politics of spectating." — Bay-Cheng, PDF p.219

Isolated spectator — the critical position:

  • "Spectators are linked solely by their one-way relationship to the very center that keeps them isolated from each other. The spectacle thus reunites the separated, but it reunites them only in their separateness." — Debord, PDF p.19 (Knabb translation)
  • "Far from being oppositional to spectacle, participation has now entirely merged with it." — Bishop, PDF p.281
  • "This line of thinking has led to an ethically charged climate in which participatory and socially engaged art has become largely exempt from art criticism." — Bishop, PDF p.27

The dome literature asserts community without argument. Saper and Tomlinson call the shared public space a defining advantage over headsets, and the manifesto chapter reports what happened at SAT's IX Symposium in 2018 when the seats were cleared: - "first, the specific technology of fulldome projections moves a usually socially isolating experience of immersive media into a shared public space" — Saper & Tomlinson, PDF p.2 - "At SAT's IX Symposium in 2018 there was one particular performance that opened the floodgate for the emergence of 'Choose Your Own Dome Ventures.' Seats had been cleared in the dome. Guests had to choose where to move and find themselves." — Remann & Francis, PDF p.19

De Certeau supplies the fourth position, and it is the one that fits a dome: the audience as practitioner rather than viewer, producer of the space rather than recipient of it, and therefore not fully legible to whoever built the place.

  • "In short, space is a practiced place. Thus the street geometrically defined by urban planning is transformed into a space by walkers." — de Certeau, PDF p.142 / printed p.117
  • "strategies are able to produce, tabulate, and impose these spaces, when those operations take place, whereas tactics can only use, manipulate, and divert these spaces." — de Certeau, PDF p.55 / printed p.30
  • "They walk —an elementary form of this experience of the city; they are walkers, Wandersmanner, whose bodies follow the thicks and thins of an urban 'text' they write without being able to read it. These practitioners make use of spaces that cannot be seen; their knowledge of them is as blind as that of lovers in each other's arms." — de Certeau, PDF p.118 / printed p.93
  • "His elevation transfigures him into a voyeur. It puts him at a distance. […] It allows one to read it, to be a solar Eye, looking down like a god." — de Certeau, PDF p.117 / printed p.92 — the operator's view, and the one a dome projection most easily reproduces

4. The body is the instrument, and immersion tests it

Merleau-Ponty is the source of the claim the dome needs, and his description of the virtual body displacing the real one is the single most directly usable sentence in the corpus for a dome argument:

  • "The body is our general medium for having a world." — Merleau-Ponty, PDF p.194
  • "What counts for the orientation of the spectacle is not my body as it in fact is, as a thing in objective space, but as a system of possible actions, a virtual body with its phenomenal 'place' defined by its task and situation. My body is wherever there is something to be done." / "This virtual body ousts the real one to such an extent that the subject no longer has the feeling of being in the world where he actually is, and that instead of his real legs and arms, he feels that he has the legs and arms he would need to walk and act in the reflected room: he inhabits the spectacle." — Merleau-Ponty, PDF p.316
  • "Our body schemas and feelings expand and 'incorporate' the perceived body of the other." — Fuchs, PDF p.5
  • "We do not need to form internal models or representations of others in order to understand and communicate with them; as bodily subjects, we are always already involved in a shared affective and expressive space." — Fuchs, PDF p.12
  • "According to this perspective, to perceive an action is equivalent to internally simulate it." — Gallese, PDF p.4

The immersive-media accounts describe the same mechanism from inside, including its cost, and Hayles supplies the objection to treating the body as disposable:

  • "As my perception accommodates itself to the three-dimensional illusion, I accrue a sense of being in another, additional skin; I feel immersed. Yet as I experience immersion in the simulation, I simultaneously lose the sense of being grounded in physical space, I experience the sensation of disembodiment." — Moser & MacLeod, PDF p.301
  • "In these systems, the user learns, kinesthetically and proprioceptively, that the relevant boundaries for interaction are defined less by the skin than by the feedback loops connecting body and simulation in a technobio-integrated circuit." — Hayles, PDF p.42
  • "Just because information has lost its body does not mean that humans and the world have lost theirs." — Hayles, PDF p.259
  • "All of reality is surround; all sensory input is immersive!" — Remann & Francis, PDF p.6

The audience-facing version of that cost is in the field's own research agenda: a dome image can dominate a body that has nowhere to look away to.

  • "It may be very threatening and dominating, because the spaceship does appear to fly literally over the audience's heads, instead of disappearing off the top of the frame in traditional film." — Ka Chun Yu, PDF p.6

5. The dome as a public room, and what the room costs

The field's institutional claim is that the dome is a public, shared, headset-free immersive space and a plausible site for public scholarship. That claim is the opening for a practice-based project, and the corpus also supplies the technical reason it is hard to make anything that travels.

  • "fulldome shows engage audiences in communal and public ways to experience immersive media without donning VR headsets" — Saper & Tomlinson, PDF p.2
  • "Most importantly, the fulldome is an important space for necessary public-facing scholarship across interdisciplinary sciences, literature, the humanities more generally, social sciences, health and medicine, the arts, and data visualization." — Saper & Tomlinson, PDF p.9
  • "At present, most university-based planetarium shows are either created in real-time within the planetarium presentation system, or are pre-packaged products licensed from third parties." — Saper & Tomlinson, PDF p.9
  • "Issues such as loudspeaker orientation, audience orientation, number of channels, and type of playback media were discussed." — Rodigast & Gaston, The Sound of Space, PDF p.6
  • "No loudspeakers behind listeners, • Viewers turn their heads and bodies to see images (impacting HRTF's), • All speakers are above, except for forward speakers." — Gaston, AES Amsterdam deck, PDF p.16
  • "Prerecorded shows are produced at one planetarium, and shared with others, despite a lack of standardization of speaker placement and seating arrangements." — Gaston, AES Amsterdam deck, PDF p.7
  • "There is a widespread sentiment among planetarium operators that some sort of standardization should be implemented to promote ease of sharing (92.5% in favor) and audio quality (91.1% in favor)." — Gaston, AES Amsterdam deck, PDF p.23
  • "Narration and other dialog elements should come from center (if a loudspeaker exists there) and not be spatialized." — Rodigast & Gaston, PDF p.7

One caution on the vocabulary, again from Sloterdijk: his project deliberately excludes the auditorium. A dome performance should not assume that a large enclosing room is already a shared interior — on his account an auditorium is exactly what a sphere is not.

  • "The intimate spaces of microspherology are neither the majestic auditoriums nor the cave-like hiding places of the individual conscious, which interacts with itself to create spatial images suitable for understanding the nature of its own position, spread out between the largest and the smallest." — Sloterdijk, PDF p.49 / printed p.98

6. Knowledge made by making

The methodological spine. Barrett and Bolt is the anchor, and its formulations are quotable as the terms of a practice-based claim: practice as knowledge production, the double articulation between theory and practice, the exegesis as the place where practice becomes theory-generating, and the question that should close a practice-based account.

  • "We propose that artistic practice be viewed as the production of knowledge or philosophy in action." — Barrett (Introduction), PDF p.13
  • "What knowledge can studio based enquiry reveal that may not be revealed by other modes of enquiry?" — Barrett, PDF p.11
  • "Finally, and most importantly, Hockney's visual argument demonstrates the double articulation between theory and practice, whereby theory emerges from a reflexive practice at the same time that practice is informed by theory. This double articulation is central to practice-led research." — Bolt, PDF p.41
  • "through the vehicle of the exegesis, practice becomes theory generating." — Barrett, PDF p.45
  • "Praxical knowledge involves a reflexive knowing that imbricates and follows on from handling." — Bolt, PDF p.46
  • "the processes of writing themselves, the studio enquiry, may lead to knowledge that is not necessarily explicitly discernible on the surface of the creative work." — Barrett, PDF p.47
  • "What did the studio process reveal that could not have been revealed by any other mode of enquiry?" — Barrett & Bolt, Appendix, PDF p.198
  • "It not only expresses the research, but in that expression becomes the research itself." — Barrett, PDF p.162

For a project whose object does not exist yet, the speculative-design pair gives the second method — the object as proposition rather than prototype:

  • "Design fictions are propositions for new, future things done as physical instantiations rather than future project plans done through PowerPoint." — Bleecker, PDF p.42 (the extracted text reads "Design ctions"; the fi ligature is lost in this scan)
  • "Diegetic prototypes have a major rhetorical advantage even over true prototypes: in the [story] these technologies exist as 'real' objects that function properly and which people actually use." — Bleecker, PDF p.43
  • "Our definition then was that 'critical design uses speculative design proposals to challenge narrow assumptions, preconceptions, and givens about the role products play in everyday life.' It was more of an attitude than anything else, a position rather than a methodology. Its opposite is affirmative design: design that reinforces the status quo." — Dunne & Raby, PDF p.45
  • "speculative design props are intended not to mimic reality or allow us to play act but to entertain new ideas, thoughts, and possibilities for an alternative world from the one we and the prop coexist in" — Dunne & Raby, PDF p.103

Carss and Hazelden close the loop between method and perception with a formulation worth borrowing for a practice-based epistemology:

  • "'Perception is not something that happens to us, it is something we do'." — Carss & Hazelden, PDF p.3 (quoting Noë)

7. The lecture as a form

The researcher's own genre — the lecture, and the performance lecture — is the one thing the corpus never studies in a dome, and yet four sources independently treat lecturing, demonstrating or narrating as an artistic form rather than a delivery mechanism. Schlemmer is the earliest and the most directly useful: unrealisable stage projects are explicitly designated as demonstration-lecture material.

  • "Or he may isolate himself altogether from the existing theater and cast his anchor far out into the sea of fantasy and distant possibilities. In this case his projects remain paper and model, materials for demonstration lectures" — Schlemmer, PDF p.18 / printed p.31 (the sentence continues on p.19: "and exhibitions of theater art")
  • "It is Joseph Beuys, however, who remains the best-known point of reference for contemporary artists' engagement with experimental pedagogy; in 1969 he claimed that 'to be a teacher is my greatest work of art'." — Bishop, PDF p.247
  • "The Japanese benshi not only narrated the films, but also directed the audience's attention within a wide shot to certain details on the screen, conceiving a conceptual precursor to the cinematic close-up." — von Chamier-Waite, PDF p.5
  • "Most importantly, the fulldome is an important space for necessary public-facing scholarship across interdisciplinary sciences, literature, the humanities more generally, social sciences, health and medicine, the arts, and data visualization." — Saper & Tomlinson, PDF p.9
  • "Every story is a travel story-a spatial practice." / "This founding is precisely the primary role of the story. It opens a legitimate theater for practical actions. It creates a field that authorizes dangerous and contingent social actions." — de Certeau, PDF p.140 / printed p.115 and PDF p.150 / printed p.125 — the argument that narration is itself a spatial operation, which is the theoretical licence for a lecture that builds a space rather than illustrating one
  • "It thus seems possible to give a preliminary definition of walking as a space of enunciation." — de Certeau, PDF p.123 / printed p.98
  • "How can media aesthetic variables be used to help elicit specific responses from an audience?" / "How does the immersive display (or other parameters that make the fulldome space unique) hinder or help?" — Ka Chun Yu, PDF p.7

And the lecture is the one form whose rhetoric has been shown not to hold: Fischer-Lichte's argument that staged manipulation of participants fails is the reason a performance lecture has to be built as an invitation rather than a delivery.

  • "For it has become clear that no individual or group of individuals/specialists will succeed in manipulating the 'innocent' participants of such events according to their intention via minutely planned and calculated strategies of staging." — Fischer-Lichte, PDF p.6

Where the corpus argues with itself

These are live disagreements, not errors. Each is a place a literature review can take a position.

  1. Is "immersion" usable at all? Carss and Hazelden say the word has been rendered meaningless in academic use and propose presence instead (PDF p.2). Grau says it is "undoubtedly key to any understanding of the development of the media, even though the concept appears somewhat opaque and contradictory" (PDF p.28), and the manifesto chapter uses it as its central term. A review must pick a term and defend it.
  2. Does the dome produce community? Saper and Tomlinson (PDF p.2) and Remann and Francis (PDF p.3) say the shared public space is what distinguishes fulldome from headset VR. Debord (PDF p.19) and Bishop (PDF p.281) describe exactly that arrangement as isolation and as spectacle.
  3. Is the dome frameless? The manifesto abolishes the frame rhetorically (PDF p.4) and then admits the seating reinstates it (PDF p.25); Thompson's production findings are built around a gravity-level horizon precisely because the frame is still in force (PDF p.2).
  4. Can the audience be manipulated? Immersive rhetoric assumes it can (Grau on the concealed apparatus, PDF p.355; Grau on the suggestive power of the "alive" image, PDF p.363). Fischer-Lichte argues the attempt fails (PDF p.6).
  5. Is fulldome a new paradigm or a science-museum format? Remann and Francis say it is "a paradigm-shifting medium of its own" (PDF p.5). Von Chamier-Waite says it is technically and socially "normalized to the culture of the science museum" (PDF p.7).
  6. Is a new medium describable in old terms? "A problem with any new medium is that it cannot be described or understood by the means of the old medium that it evolved out of" (Remann & Francis, PDF p.5) — which is an argument against the entire method of importing theory from elsewhere that this bibliography necessarily performs.
  7. Is there an outside to the present? Fisher's answer is no: culture pre-emptively formats dissent, "the pre-emptive formatting and shaping of desires, aspirations and hopes by capitalist culture" (PDF p.15), so that "'Alternative' and 'independent' don't designate something outside mainstream culture". Muñoz's answer is that the past and the future are usable as leverage: "This temporal calculus performed and utilized the past and the future as armaments to combat the devastating logic of the world of the here and now, a notion of nothing existing outside the sphere of the current moment, a version of reality that naturalizes cultural logics such as capitalism and heteronormativity" (PDF p.15). Both are on page 15 of their respective books; only one of them can be right, and a practice-based project in a speculative register is a wager on Muñoz.

The gap a fulldome-performance literature review has to fill

There is no body of scholarship on performance in domes. The ten dome items in this corpus are about film and show production, venue technology, festival culture and one perception proposal. Live work appears only as festival example: Ka Chun Yu names J. Walt's Spontaneous Fantasia in one line as an instance of "live performance" (PDF p.1), and the manifesto chapter's account of the 2018 IX Symposium performance (PDF p.19) is the corpus's only description of an audience moving inside a dome. Neither is studied. The dome items cite each other barely at all, and none of them cites a performance-studies source.

The field states its own lack of research. This is the most useful thing a review can quote, because it comes from inside:

  • "except for Ben Shedd's seminal paper on the singular challenges of large format films [37], there is little work that discusses the artistic elements that are unique to fulldome filmmaking." — Ka Chun Yu, PDF p.1
  • "Most past and ongoing research involving dome visuals have focused on learning gains and other cognitive goals [19, 39, 43]. [...] Maya Skies is the only fulldome research project that we are aware of that explicitly studies non-cognitive factors in the audience response." — Ka Chun Yu, PDF p.6
  • "There has to date been very little academic research into perception in DAVEs." (Dome Automated Virtual Environments) — Carss & Hazelden, PDF p.3
  • "How can media aesthetic variables be used to help elicit specific responses from an audience?" / "How does the immersive display (or other parameters that make the fulldome space unique) hinder or help?" — Ka Chun Yu, PDF p.7

No practice-based research methodology has been applied to the dome. Barrett and Bolt never mention immersive media; none of the dome papers mentions practice-led research, reflective practice or the exegesis. The method section of any such review therefore has to be constructed, not cited.

The corpus contains no media archaeology, no audience-measurement literature, and no spatial-audio perception literature — three fields the work needs. The nearest thing to a history of the dome image in this folder is Grau on the panorama (a different apparatus with a different social function: ticketed, urban, spectacular) and Fuller on spherical thinking (an analogy, not a history).

The technical literature constrains what an experiment can claim. Thompson's production findings are explicitly scoped to unidirectional seating theatres (PDF p.2), so they do not transfer to a concentric or in-the-round setup; Gaston reports no standard for speaker placement and no standard for seating, and 46 percent of 103 surveyed venues dissatisfied with their audio (AES deck, PDF p.7, p.11), with 92.5 percent wanting some standardisation (PDF p.23); Rodigast and Gaston propose one and it had not been adopted (PDF p.7). Any practice-based claim about what happened in a dome has to state its venue's configuration, because there is no default to appeal to. This is a limitation and also an opportunity: the configuration itself is a research variable.

A citation-hygiene problem in the field's own publishing. The manifesto chapter's publisher, editors and year appear nowhere in the file; the cine-poetics paper carries no journal, year or DOI; the Neafus deck carries no date or venue; the SAT item is a web-page export whose body is images; two of the ten dome items are slide decks whose definitions live in unrecoverable images. A review should say this plainly: the field's literature is thin partly because of where and how it publishes.

Where to look next, from the corpus's own map

Named inside the corpus, so these are traceable leads rather than suggestions:

  • FullDome Festival, Jena, and the Janus Award — the field's institutional centre (Remann & Francis, PDF pp.19, 32).
  • IMERSA — annual immersive-media gathering; the manifesto quotes its founder Dan Neafus on why it exists (PDF p.18). Neafus is also the author of the Fulldome 101 deck in this folder.
  • SAT, Montreal, and the IX Symposium — the art-dome pole, and the venue of the 2018 participatory performance (PDF pp.19, 32).
  • Domelab at the Otterbox Digital Dome Theater, Fort Collins Museum of Discovery, and FDDB, the Full-Dome Database — the practitioner network and archive (PDF pp.18, 19).
  • IPS (International Planetarium Society) and its standards work — Rodigast and Gaston's proposal, and the 2004 Fulldome Standards Summit where Thompson presented (PDF p.6; Thompson running head).
  • Named individual researchers to follow up: Ben Shedd (large-format film), Ed Lantz (spherical image representation), Ka Chun Yu (Denver Museum of Nature & Science), Pete Carss and Katina Hazelden (University of Plymouth, i-DAT), Rene Rodigast (Fraunhofer IDMT), Leslie Gaston (University of Colorado Denver), Micky Remann and the Fulldome Festival Foundation.
  • The one researched audience study named: Maya Skies at the Chabot Space & Science Center (Ka Chun Yu, PDF p.6).
